LINES from EINSTEIN

An Intellectual Odyssey

by

Viv Pope

Acknowledgements

This book was written at the suggestion of my editorial colleagues, A. E. Chubykalo and R. Smirnov-Rueda, following our collaboration on the compilation of papers entitled Instantaneous Action at a Distance in Modern Physics, “Pro and Contra” (in the series CONTEMPORARY MODERN PHYSICS (1999), Series Editor: Valeri V. Dvoeglazov, Nova Science Publishers, INC. New York., ISBN 1-56072-698-9).

My thanks are due to these colleagues who, while fully aware of the difficulties the work would inevitably encounter in the matter of publication (due to its unconventional and therefore unplaceable genre), nevertheless vigorously encouraged me to write the book, ‘It’s a story that needs to be told,’ they said, ‘so just do it and be damned’. This had followed their reading of the record called ‘Philosophical Glimpses’ (op. cit.) on which the book is based and it echoed what a French professor once commented on that record: ‘Mon Dieu, but this is dynamite!’.

My thanks are due also to friends, family members and colleagues for their helpful advice and comments on the early rough drafts of the book. Particular pleasure was gained from the fact that the greatest help and support for this account of the ‘odyssey’ came, in the end, from some very old friends, some of whom had been most negative towards the venture in its early days following the letter from Einstein. I must also thank those younger friends and family members who have dragged me screaming out of my familiar world of wires and pliers into a magic-wand world of computers, with their inscrutable modules, spells and incantations.

Not least, of course, as anyone who reads the book must soon realise, are thanks due to my wife. These are not only for her steadfast support in the adventure, right from its start (during our teens) and for gently providing the education that was necessary for articulating these ideas; they are also for providing, throughout, the secure practical and emotional background from which one sallied forth against the Educational Establishment and to which, often beaten and discouraged, one so thankfully returned.

Above all, however, are my thanks to her for allowing, without any hint of complaint, her husband to employ to the full his energies in the pursuit of despised philosophical truth rather than of riches and social acclaim.

In the early 1950s, while hitch hiking his way back to college, a Physics student was given a lift by a lorry full of telephone linemen. After the exchange of a few pleasantries, the men resumed what had obviously been a heated argument. As he listened, the student began to realise that the subject they were so vigorously discussing was Einstein’s Theory of Relativity. Quite unselfconsciously, these unlikely debaters were bandying about phrases like ‘Fitzgerald contraction’, ‘Lorentz transformation’ and so on in language liberally sprinkled with expletives and even obscenities. Most astounding of all was the fact that the argument was centred on a letter which one of these unprepossessing workmen had received from Einstein himself.

The distinctly unacademic work that these men were doing was taking down dangerous telegraph poles (as can be seen in the picture, opposite, where scaffolding has been placed around a pole too unsafe to climb). Their discussion of a subject which was generally considered too erudite for ordinary people to understand was, in their case, as completely spontaneous and unaffected as though they were discussing a soccer match.

In later years, the recipient of the letter, mindful of the ability of his former untutored colleagues to understand the basics of a theory so notoriously abstruse, devoted himself, as a mature student at university, to developing —or, rather, refurbishing— a commonsense approach to natural philosophy, which he called Normal Realism. This was dedicated to removing from physics and philosophy what, in their customary vernacular, these unassuming and unpretentious gangers had nicely described as ‘bullshit’. By this means, he proved that with this academic obfuscation removed, what Einstein was saying and what its implications were for commonsense philosophy could be understood by just about anyone who was honest and forthright enough to refuse to allow Education to stand between himself and nature.

RELATIVITY THEORISTS

The telephone linemen whose vigorous work-time arguments over Einstein’s letter helped to inspire an Intellectual Odyssey.

The men in this picture are, from left to right and top down, David (Dai) Rasbridge, Derek Thomas (driver), Jack Thomas (leading hand), Frank Rees and Viv Pope. The foreman, Matthew Hayman (“Mad Matt”) can be glimpsed just in front of and behind the latter. The work of this gang was to take down dangerous telegraph poles. This one was rotten and had to be scaffolded before being cut down in bits in that built-up area. The man hanging from the pole, on the title page, is Donald (Don) Williams, a trainee who was also a party to later discussions.

This book is dedicated to Albert Einstein, but for whose encouraging response to a letter, the events here reported could never have happened.

The wind blows and the rain falls where it will.

The same goes for ideas. If what one person discovers about nature is true, then others, in all walks of life, may discover that same truth in relatively different ways.

This is what relativity is all about.

FOREWORD

Modern physics and classical physics are very different. In classical physics our observations of things were thought to be no more than incidental to their physical reality. In modern Relativity and Quantum theory, physical reality is centred on observation.

However, that philosophical shift in physics from absolutism to relativism is by no means clear-cut. The present situation is like that of the time when about as many people believed the earth to be flat as believed it to be round. In this transitional phase, the once confident pronouncements of physicists now give place to questions as to what, fundamentally, physics is all about. At conferences, people gather from all over the globe to deliver theories on fundamental issues of space and time, with scarcely anyone agreeing with anyone else.

Meanwhile, in schools and universities, Physics continues to be taught according to a curriculum of graduation which is maintained like the rules of class-segregation on the decks of the sinking 'Titanic'. When the most suitable relativistic instruction might well be 'Every man think for himself!' the order is still for all observers to think and observe in the same standard way.

This book is an account of how, in this state of confusion, one observer was forced to make up his own mind on the subject. From his completely relative, personal standpoint he critically observed not only the world as professional physicists have interpreted it but also the history and sociology of the Educational system which is charged with standardising our perceptions. With the advantage of starting without formal qualifications in either maths or physics, and with just a little bit of help from Albert Einstein (in a short correspondence just before Einstein's death) he managed, by methods of pure commonsense logic, to rediscover what Einstein had discovered about space and time but in an altogether different way. This was in ordinary graphical (engineering-drawing) terms without any recourse to the usual mathematics. More significantly, as it turned out —especially from the quantum physics point of view— this did not involve Einstein's problematic 'separability' interpretation of the constant $c$ as the 'speed of light in vacuo', according to which we can never observe anything directly but only at second hand and —especially in astronomical cases— long after the event.

The writer makes no apology for the style in which this book is written, because it is based on true events of the sort which are indicative of the confusion it describes. As more and more scientists are saying, now is not the time for conformity —in theoretical physics, at any rate. So it is a paradox that when the call is for conceptual innovation, anyone who seeks to be innovative encounters negative reactions —not only from professional physicists but also from the community at large. 'Who do you think you are, another Einstein?' is the typical 'put-down' applied to anyone questioning the system.

But of course, there are bound to be those who, for purely egotistical reasons, crave to be 'another Einstein', just as there are those who aspire to be another Elvis Presley or another Jesus Christ. However, if every attempt to reproduce Einstein's results from a relatively different perspective were inhibited by fear of such reactions, then the intellectual effort that has led physics thus far would surely lead no further.

The need for impartiality also requires that polite conventions against 'name-dropping' need to be waived in this book. This is because the entirely extramural one-to-one sort of discussion which began with Einstein was continued with many others, including Alfred Ayer, John Bell, David Bohm, Herman Bondi, Karl Popper, Gilbert Ryle and Victor Weisskopf, whose widely diversified inputs were vital to the development of the thesis described [1]. This is not to say that the value of these inputs had anything to do with the fame of the contributors, since some of those who contributed most to the final result are the least well-known.

Whether this book will be seen as an account of some personal 'ego-trip' or as a genuinely disinterested search for answers to questions which were left to go begging with Einstein's death, the reader will of course judge for himself.

[1] These discussions are recorded in seventeen volumes of more than ten thousand pages entitled Philosophical Glimpses, a private collection from 1954 to the present time. It is kept at West Glamorgan Archives, County Hall, Swansea, UK, ref.: D/D NVP/1-17. Archives website: http://www.swansea.gov.uk).

Copyright N.V and A.M. Pope, 17/05/00 E:\ODYSSEY.DOC

‘Lines from Einstein’

(An Intellectual Odyssey)

by

Viv Pope

All the keys hang not at one man’s girdle Anon.

‘Llys Alaw’. 10 West End, Penclawdd, Swansea, SA4 3YX, U.K.

E-mails: Viv_Pope@compuserve.com n.v.pope@swansea.ac.uk

INTRODUCTION

This book is as much about sociology as about science—as if the two could ever be separated. For it is a strange irony that scientists who are among the first to see ‘relativity’ in nature are among the last to see it as it applies to science itself. Such people are inclined to see ‘relativity’ as an absolute theory, with ‘Einstein’ as its only true mediator and advocate, so that any attempt to criticise the theory or any part of it is seen as tantamount to a denial of nature itself.

However, it is plain commonsense that if what any one person may discover about nature is true, then others may discover that same truth in relatively different ways. Indeed, to be verified by such independent means is usually regarded as the essential criterion for judging natural truth. For Science to put a unique stamp of approval on any one person’s insight into nature to the exclusion of all others is like demanding that everyone must view the world from the same spot and through the same pair of spectacles. Different viewpoints provide different perspectives and focuses—which is surely what relativity is all about. It provides an objectivity that cannot be gained by any other means.

Nor do those whose views are selected for such social preferment necessarily welcome such attention. Einstein was not delighted that society used his famous mass-energy formula to wipe out whole cities. Nor is it likely that he would have been flattered to know that after his death, his personal ‘relativistic’ insight into nature would put a virtual block on all other insights—that Education would take his own personal insight as the only pinhole through which students would be allowed to peep. The fact, however, as shall be seen, is that Einstein did not consider himself so exalted as to reserve his attentions for members of his academic peer-group. The following record shows that although his reluctance to answer letters was legendary, he was not above corresponding with ordinary people expressing different and sometimes altogether naïve insights.

This lends point to the old saying: ‘He thanks his teacher not at all who forever remains his pupil’. Like any true teacher, Einstein would have hoped that at least some crumb of instruction given to someone, somewhere, would stimulate that person to seek insights into nature further than his own. In one case, at least, this did happen. What would have surprised Einstein, however—or then again, perhaps not—was the friction that his piece of instruction would create between its recipient and the Educational Establishment. This is where the sociological dimension comes into play. In these pages it may be seen just how that happened—that is to say, how a short, ‘out of school’ instruction given by Einstein to an ordinary non-academic enquirer would set that enquirer completely at odds with formal Education. With no tutors to channel his ideas, this young, non-academic enquirer had taken Einstein’s relativity literally as meaning that if what Einstein had discovered about space and time was true, then there should be as many different routes to that same truth as there are different observers and thinkers. In this innocent frame of mind our impudent inquisitor did actually discover another logical route to relativity, one which was corroborative of Einstein’s but derived from different premises and with implications far less paradoxical than those of the accepted theory. The difference, in short, was that in this alternative approach, Einstein’s fundamental axiom of ‘the constant speed of light in vacuo’ was jettisoned in favour of a direct-connection account of light-interaction accompanied by an axiom to the effect that ‘observational lengths and times are related in the constant ratio of units $c$’. This is much in the way that inches, in the old Imperial measure, and metres in the modern Standard International measure are related in the ratio of 39.37 which, like $c$, is constant in all systems of reference whether relatively moving or stationary. The ‘relativism’ thus expressed turned out (as this enquirer subsequently discovered) to be more in line with the relationism of Einstein’s philosophical mentor, Mach $^{[2]}$ than with the ‘relativity’ of Einstein which, as is well known, Mach regarded as philosophically incoherent. For instance, as Mach wrote $^{[3]}$:

I gather from publications which have reached me, and especially from my correspondence that I am gradually becoming regarded as the forerunner of relativity. ... I must, however, as assuredly disclaim to be a forerunner of the relativists as I withhold from the atomistic belief of the present day.

Our enquirer, in his own natural and unforced way, came, independently and unknowingly, to the same conclusion as Mach, that neither the local realism (Remote Realism $^{[4]}$) of Einstein nor the reductionism of the atomists can have any place in a fully relationist synthesis of physical experience. This is where, right at the start, the philosophical dimension of the enquiry comes into play. In that same spontaneous manner, the boundaries of Sociology, Psychology, Theology and other restricted areas of expertise were crossed and recrossed unwittingly, due to the explorer being uninformed that this was academically forbidden.

$^{[2]}$ Ernst Mach: German physicist-philosopher (1838 - 1916). His ideas formed the foundation of the Logical Positivist movement of the Vienna Circle philosophers.

$^{[3]}$ Mach, E. (posthumous publ. 1921): Prinzipien der physikalischen Optik. Quoted in Wolters, G. ‘Ernst Mach and the Theory of Relativity’, Philosophia Naturalis, (Transl., S. Gillies) 21, 630-641, 1984.

$^{[4]}$ So called due to the Einsteinian interpretation of light as the finite-speed separator and mediator of observation from physical sources regarded as real although inscrutable (i.e., unknowable by any means other than via influences travelling at or lower than that finite speed).

PART 1

INCEPTION

This is an account of the spontaneous emergence of a thesis which began as a completely naïve, commonsense attempt to understand how time can vary in the manner described by Einstein. Being a non-mathematician, the author of this thesis succeeded in replicating Einstein’s results in a novel and entirely intuitive way, by means of engineering-type scale drawings. As later confirmed by professional mathematicians and physicists, these results were quantitatively identical to Einstein’s. Philosophically, however, they were very different.

I Einstein and the ‘Clock Paradox’

In 1954 a young telephone lineman wrote a letter to Albert Einstein. The letter was very scrappy and naïvely written, and all record of it has been lost. The gist of it, however, was that in an evening lecture at the local Astronomical Society, the audience had been told that in Einstein’s theory, time is not the same everywhere but is different for different timekeepers moving relatively to one another at different speeds. What the lineman wanted to know was this: for just two observers, A and B, in otherwise empty space, for A to say that B travels away from him at a speed $v$ is obviously the same as for B to say that A moves away from him at that same speed. So if, as the lecturer had explained, motion slows down the time of those observers relatively to each other, then the slowing of time for both observers should be the same. And since the slowing was the same in both directions, if the away-motion was reversed so as to bring A and B back together, the overall effect would be nil, in which case, time would have flowed steadily on in the usual way, regardless of the theory.

In his reply (the record of which survives) Einstein kindly complimented the writer on the clarity of the question and explained, in effect, that the situation described in the theory was not, as the questioner had assumed, necessarily the same for both A and B. For the two observers to return together after moving away, one or the other of them — if not both — would physically have to make a reversing action. Assuming that this reversing action is undertaken by just one of the observers, say B, with the time-frame of the non-reversing observer, A, remaining the same throughout, that of B would be split into two: one being his time-frame on the outward leg of the motion and the other his time-frame on the inward leg. So, on arriving back together the observer B who had reversed his motion would have clocked-up a total of his outwards-time plus his inwards-time, whilst the non-reversing observer A would have recorded the time of the motion from start to finish in a single frame. According to the theory, therefore, Einstein explained, the total time of the out-and-back observer and that of the stay-put observer were, mathematically, not the same.

So far as the lineman was concerned, if that was the correct answer to his question, then being a complete non-mathematician he would have to take Einstein’s word for it, because Einstein’s reply had been written in mathematical language which he could not understand. Later, however, Einstein’s explanation would be satisfactorily explained to him in further correspondence (of the same extramural character) with Professor Herman Bondi, then at Cambridge. In the meantime, however, something happened which he found most intriguing. Being completely non-academic, he had not appreciated quite how famous his correspondent, Einstein, was to people ‘in the know’. The fact that he had corresponded with the Great Man created quite a stir. The first intimation of this was when, in an open day of the Astronomical Society, at the request of the secretary of the society, Einstein’s letter had been put on display. Spotted by a visiting newspaper journalist, the lineman was interviewed, with the result that his letter appeared in the local newspaper under the heading ‘Swansea Telephone Lineman’s Lines from Einstein’ [5].

He then received the first of many lessons in practical sociology that were to follow from his temerity in contacting Einstein. This was when it got him into trouble with his employers. Having read this piece in the paper, the area head of what was then the Post Office Engineering Department put him ‘on the carpet’ where he was told, in no uncertain manner, that having published his letter without the Post Office’s permission, he had embarrassed them by connecting it with his job as a telephone lineman. ‘People will wonder,’ said the Area Engineer, ‘why a man who can hob-nob with Einstein is employed by the Department in such a menial capacity as lineman’. In reply, the lineman told the

[5] South Wales Evening Post, circa 1961, Phil. Glim. op. cit., vol. 1, p. 15b.

Area Engineer, in no less uncertain terms, that being a ‘menial lineman’ had been good enough for this man’s father and so was good enough for him. His father, by then deceased, as well as being employed as a ‘menial lineman’ for the Post Office had been, out of working-hours, Vice Chairman and a highly intelligent, well-respected founder-member of the local Astronomical Society, which has flourished to this time of writing. He had lived just long enough to be astounded to find that his son had corresponded with the great Einstein. Refusing, therefore, to change course towards those ‘higher things’ offered in the employ of the Post Office—for which he would have to swap his contemplative freedom for highly competitive programmes of industrial training—the ‘menial lineman’ parted with his boss, not on the friendliest of terms. Nor did those terms improve in the Department, from that day on, as shall subsequently be revealed.

This was not the only disconcerting result of his short correspondence with Einstein. Friends and relatives who knew nothing about Einstein except, vaguely, that the man was famous for having done or said something highly intelligent, reacted badly to the publicity the letter engendered. For them, what could all this ‘Einstein’ thing possibly be but some kind of ‘ego trip’, a blatant piece of cheap publicity-seeking! ‘Who do you think you are?’ was the general response. ‘You don’t have a degree or anything like that and you know next to nothing about science and mathematics. So what’s your game? Are you seeking to be some kind of intellectual or what? How can you, a mere telephone lineman, presume to talk about Einstein when, as everyone knows, what Einstein says can be understood only by elevated and highly qualified people?’

However, this demeaning charge of ‘trying to be clever’ did not prevent him from puzzling over what was for him at that stage, the still unexplained problem of how a person as famous as Einstein could claim that time is the same yet different for different observers. In search of an answer he began to make a nuisance of himself by taking the opportunity, whenever he could, of visiting the local university and quizzing various off-duty academics about it. There, his enquiries became the more dogged and aggressive as the lecturers were more and more off-putting—invariably, on account of his ‘lack of qualifications’. But what, he wondered, should ‘qualifications’ have to do with it? What qualifications did one need to discuss a question which, as Einstein had stated, had been so ‘well-formulated’? And how could so many people talk about the ‘greatness’ of Einstein when they obviously had no clue as to what the man had done. As our inquisitor saw it, ‘mathematics’ or no, the consequence of Einstein’s theory was plain enough for anyone to see, namely, that it was claiming that time was both different and the same for different measurers. How, he wondered, could people possibly accept without question something so plainly paradoxical? ‘We leave it to the experts’, his friends replied when he probed them, adding—by implication if not statedly—and if you were honest and unassuming, like us, you will do the same and stop bothering about it’.

The fact that these honest enquiries of his into the fundamentals of time and space had made him feel, socially, like a cockroach in a cream bun, inflamed his curiosity about human psychology and sociology. Little did he know, at the time, that these promptings would lead him eventually to study these subjects at university. However, as already stated, from the beginning of those philosophical enquiries into which he had been spurred by Einstein—to whom he could no longer write because the Great Man had since died—he was never able to keep these scientific, psychological and sociological (in a word, philosophical) factors channelled in the way that was obligatory for students entering higher education. This meant that when he would eventually get to study these subjects at university, as a mature student in 1966, his inability to keep them securely contained in their allotted compartments would become, as we shall see, a source of increasing academic friction.

Long before that, however, the original pure-physics problem of the apparent paradoxicality of time being both the same and different in Einstein’s theory had been solved, as already intimated, in further extramural contacts with various academics; in particular, the aforementioned Professor Bondi and Professor P. M. Davidson (Swansea). This alerted him to the curious sociological fact that like Einstein, well-known people of science and philosophy who were the true pathfinders of these subjects were by no means so concerned about ‘qualifications’ as were their followers on the lower rungs of the academic ladder. All this, however, was for later. At the time, after some long and vigorous discussions which must have tested almost to destruction the patience of these venerated professors, it suddenly dawned on our enquirer what they—and Einstein—had been saying all along. The course which this ‘Eureka’ experience took is explained in the following chapter.

II The Clock Non-Paradox

As it transpired, what our lineman’s question to Einstein had failed to take into account was something to which Bondi eventually succeeded in calling to his attention. This is that if, in a telescope, say, one actually counts the ticks of a standard clock travelling away and back in the manner of A and B already described, it turns out that, as a matter of fact, at the end of the motion that count does not tally with that of the ticks of the observatory clock over the same interval. The reason was plain commonsense. As A and B travel away from one another, so the ticks of the other’s clock that each observes in his telescope are slowed relatively to his own due to the extra time it takes for the light to travel the increasing distance. To return together, some change in the motion has to be made. Supposing that this is a reversing action made by B, B counts the slowed ticks from A up till the time when he stops and reverses his motion relative to A. From that moment on, the slowed ticks from A which he sees in his telescope are replaced by faster ones until he arrives back at A. Meanwhile, that turnaround of B will not be seen by A immediately (as it will, of course, for B) but only after a time-lapse which is the distance of B from A divided by the speed of light. The situation for A and B is therefore plainly not symmetrical, because A counts slower ticks from B for a longer period than B does from A and counts faster ticks from then on for a correspondingly shorter period. So, at the end of the motion, the sums of the observed slow ticks and fast ticks in the two telescopes do not tally, and it would be a miracle if they did. That is to say, in A’s telescope there are more slower-than-faster ticks from B, and in B’s telescope there are more faster-than-slower ticks from A.

To think about it was to see, by pure commonsense, that clocks A and B, moving away from one another and back in the way Einstein and Bondi had described could not possibly record the same times. And what was the case for clocks must obviously be the same for all other processes, such as heartbeats, ageing-rates—in fact, the whole time-systems of the respective observers. Since there are more of these events in A’s life than in B’s, between their departure and return, then at the end of the exercise, A would have aged faster than B and B would have aged more slowly than A, with there being no suggestion whatever of a paradox. And that, our lineman suddenly realised, was the answer to the question he had put to Einstein. He now saw, with a rush of euphoria, that this was what Einstein had meant by relativity. Since an asymmetrical to-and-fro motion could make time different for different observers moving away and then ending-up on the same spot, then time could not be the same for everyone everywhere in the way he had supposed. So what everyone, including himself, had imagined prior to Einstein to be a continuous universal flow of time was obviously wrong. Having read about the revolutions in thinking which people like Columbus, Copernicus and Galileo had initiated, he was well aware that this kind of disconcerting thing could happen. So, he told himself, what’s new! Here we have, with Einstein, another conceptual revolution. It’s like the shift from flat-earth to round earth thinking and from an earth-centred to a sun-centred solar system. It happens! But what an exciting prospect! What a great privilege, he felt, to be in on something so absolutely intriguing and adventurous!

Then he began to wonder about all that nonsense he had been given about having to be qualified in mathematics before one could understand Relativity when it obviously required nothing more, in the end, than just a bit of diligently applied commonsense. And, since what Einstein was saying was so eminently commonsensical, why had so many people made of it such a priestly mystique? As soon as he had savoured to the full this new commonsense/scientific realisation, his thoughts began to drift again in the lateral direction of human psychology and sociology. Recalling the trouble he had got into on account of his pursuit of that new understanding, he could not help marvelling at the numbers of those who could genuflect to the name of Einstein without being in the least bit curious as to what it was that the man had done. They simply had no feeling for what Einstein had actually subscribed to our common understanding of the space and time which are so absolutely basic, not only to science but to their own and everyone else’s very existence.

For instance, some friends and acquaintances to whom he explained this relativistic time-effect doggedly insisted that it was nonsense, that it had to be no more than apparent, a ‘trick of the light’ due to the use of telescopes in the viewing of those events by the moving observers. ‘Behind the scenes’, they said, the clocks of the two travellers had to tick together in the ordinary way so as to remain synchronised in ‘real time’ throughout. But how, he asked them, could the effect be ‘merely apparent’ when, brought together at the end of the motion, the clocks recorded the different times on the same spot? There was something absurd, to say the least, in seeing the two clocks placed alongside each other in that way, reading different times and insisting that the difference was ‘merely apparent’, that ‘really’ they were reading the same time! Those who in response to that didn’t simply shrug and walk away argued that in that case it must be only the clocks that were affected. The difference in the clock-readings, they said, must be due to some physical effect on the clock mechanisms caused by the forces of braking and accelerating during the turnaround. When it was pointed out that the time-differences observed in the respective telescopes were the same not only for all clocks, regardless of differences in their design and construction but for all processes, mechanical, physical, chemical, biological, psychological ... no matter what, then these others also shrugged and walked away.

In any case, as, many years later, he would demonstrate in full detail [6], the same effect would occur if the accelerations were done away with altogether. For instance, instead of braking and then accelerating towards A for the return journey, B might travel on indefinitely with his outward motion uninterrupted. Then, at the point where he would have reversed his motion, he might pass his time-baton, as it were, to another observer C moving towards A at the same speed that he, B, would have travelled on his way back. The effect, as it turned out, was demonstrably the same with or without involving forces of braking and acceleration. It was purely kinematical, with no dynamics having to be considered.

For the most part, however, this reasoning fell on deaf ears. It was assumed that despite the appearances, properly regulated identical clocks just had to read the same, no matter what. This seemed to him a completely recalcitrant notion, which militated against any true realisation of what Einstein was saying. It formed a complete block on the intellectual ability of certain kinds of people to appreciate the revolutionary transition Einstein had accomplished from absolutism to relativism in our dealings with nature. It reminded him of the tale about the psychiatrist who sought to convince a patient that he, the patient, was not dead. ‘Do you agree,’ the psychiatrist said, ‘that dead men don’t bleed?’ ‘No doubt about that,’ replied the patient. The psychiatrist pricked the patient’s finger and showed him the blood. ‘Well I’m damned!’, exclaimed the patient, ‘Dead men do bleed!’

There was no recourse, then, our enquirer decided resignedly, but to leave these recalcitrant thinkers to their own devices and carry on without them. These frustrations could not detract from his realisation that for himself, having understood Einstein had been a complete revelation. It was the point of departure for a whole new world-outlook, a whole new philosophy, in effect, to the exploration of which he was to become more and more dedicated. For the moment, however, he remained

[6] This was the subject of a talk he would give at ANPA (the Alternative Natural Philosophy Association), at Cambridge. See Appendix 1.

preoccupied with the much more practically focused question of how to deal in a natural way with what Einstein had discovered and which did not require the study of mathematics that those 'in the know' held to be obligatory. The method he chanced upon, during a quiet spell following an industrial injury, was by means of a measuring ruler and a pair of dividers. Prior to his being employed as a telephone lineman he had been trained as an engineering draughtsman, which made it seem natural to him to solve problems of motion in a graphical, or engineering-drawing sort of way. So, from a point on a piece of paper he drew a horizontal line to the right to represent one second of time registered by a moving body. Then, from the end of that line he drew a vertical line to represent the distance travelled in that time by the body, relative to the observer of the motion, in units of 186,000 miles. He then joined the end of this second line to the beginning of the first, which formed the right-angled triangle shown in Figure 1, below.

Now of course, as usually understood, spaces and times are not thought of as combining in the way lengths do in engineering-type drawings. But, he wondered, what if that traditional understanding was wrong? It was no more than an assumption, after all, that the distances and times represented in traditional motion-graphs are independent measures. With a rush of inspiration he then said to himself 'What if spaces and times do combine in the same way that the dimensions length, breadth and depth do in engineering drawings?' If so —and he could not see how that possibility could logically be excluded— then perhaps the geometrical combination of the two lines representing distance and time (their resultant signified by the dotted line in the diagram) would give the stretching of time due to distance-travelled that Einstein had called 'time-dilation?' It made good sense, because what else but an infusion of the distance-time that A and B observed in each other's telescopes into a normal time-interval could cause the time to be 'dilated' in that way?

But of course, it all depended on his choice of the units 186,000 miles and seconds in scaling the diagram. Having tried all sorts of different units in constructing the triangle he found that nothing worked except that particular choice of 186,000 miles for distance and seconds for time. This made him consider the distinct possibility that up till then he had been completely wrong in his understanding of length and time. These he had always thought of as independent measures like length and temperature. Now he was forced to consider the likelihood that they were, like vertical measures in inches and horizontal measures in metres, dependent measures, related in the constant ratio of units he had used in his diagram. In all the other diagrams that he constructed with those same units, the 'dilations' worked out exactly. With a rush of excitement he dashed in from the garden where he had been working on these diagrams and mystified his wife by shouting, triumphantly, 'I've just discovered the length of a second'.

He now languished in the comfortable realisation that he had a means at his disposal of working out the time-dilational effects of relative motion without having to crawl through the usual morass of mathematical mystification surrounding 'Einstein' and 'Relativity'. Plainly, anyone with a ruler, a pair of dividers and a bit of applied commonsense could do it. And the reason for the effect was perfectly simple. If you accept that every 186,000 miles of space is equal to a second of time (as the Danish astronomer Olaus Römer had discovered in 1676), then combining these length and time measures in the way one naturally does in describing the motion of a body produces a composite space-time result which is indistinguishable from that described by Einstein. He was immensely grateful to Einstein for putting him on that track.

Some time was then spent on experimenting with this ‘engineering-drawing’ approach to both relativity and science in general, to see what other mysteries might be found to underlie our commonplace experience. Diagrams and hieroglyphics of all kinds began to appear all over the house, in massive notebooks retrieved from the rubbish-bins of places like shipping offices; and thus commenced a process which his fastidiously tidy wife exasperatingly named ‘creeping blight’. Unlike her husband—who, at school, had excelled in nothing so much as truancy—she had been an excellent scholar, a favourite for Oxbridge until she met her future husband. With her prompting, help and encouragement, he began to write-up his discovery for the benefit, as he saw it, of ordinary enquirers like himself. If time differs in the way Einstein had discovered—and as he himself had corroborated—then surely, the academics shouldn’t be allowed to keep that to themselves! For the sake of democracy, everyone should know about it, since that knowledge was fundamental, not only to science but to just about every aspect of our life’s experience. How, for instance, since consciousness occupied only a tiny moment in time, could we be sure that the consciousness of a person we were communicating with on one occasion would be ‘in there’ on the next occasion if, in the meantime, our living-times were changed in the manner of A and B as explained in the letter from Einstein. Surely, he felt, such things needed thinking about. And in his attempts to get his friends and family to think about these things he all but completely alienated them.

Something of a social balance was maintained, however, in plebeian pursuits well suited to the more rumbustious side of his nature—such as, for example, motor racing and other competitive activities conducive of gregariousness among young men of similar bent. Meanwhile, his assault on the academics continued apace. At one stage he took his drawings to the Mathematics Department of the local university, where he showed them to a young Ph.D. This time, the response was most encouraging. For once, it seemed, he had found a sympathetic soul who was prepared to give serious consideration to what he had to offer. After some discussion he was told, ‘I think you’ve got something here!’ This was followed by a contemplative silence, after which it was suggested that he should ‘nip off for some lunch’, leaving him (the Ph.D.) to ponder over the drawings—on which he had forgotten to make any explicit reference to the units of length he was employing. On returning from the refectory, our lineman found his new friend in a barely concealed state of excitement, with the drawings plus some pages of mathematics set out before him. ‘Just one thing I want to be sure of,’ said the Ph.D., ‘is what units you are using for length.’ ‘Units of 186,000 miles,’ came the reply. ‘Well that’s it, then,’ breathed the Ph.D., throwing down his pencil with a gesture of finality, ‘With your drawings you’ve replicated Einstein’s equations!’

The mathematical proof of this was, of course, lost on our lineman, who sought hard to look intelligent while it was enthusiastically explained to him. However, the tenor of the conversation was definitely encouraging, and it ended with an invitation for him to return during the following week, so as to leave some time, as the Ph.D. told him, ‘for me to show this to my colleagues’. However, when he did return, the mood had changed completely. Evidently, the older colleagues of this young Ph.D. had curbed his enthusiasm by persuading him that any upstart could take Einstein’s equations, make geometrical representations of them and then, by ‘deducing’ the equations from those drawings, claim that he was another Einstein. And that is where that seemingly promising avenue ended—in that particular direction, at any rate.

This was yet another incident in his scientific enquiry which provided grist to the mill of our lineman's burgeoning philosophical curiosity. For what possible reason, he wondered, would someone like me want to be 'another Einstein'? I am what I am, and happy with it. Why would I want to be someone else, least of all a mathematician! (He would have much preferred to pursue his hobby of motor racing, despite many incidents in that sphere which could have put an end to all his interests.) But still he had his diagrams; and now it had been confirmed—though he was yet to learn quite how—that they did, in fact, exactly represent, in his geometrical terms, the situation that Einstein had described mathematically. However, it soon began to percolate through to him that what he now regarded as his 'Theory' and Einstein's were not exactly the same. The difference—and it turned out to be a vital one—was that in his diagrams there was no line that could be drawn representing the 'speed of light' that was such a common feature of all the textbook explanations of Einstein's theory. The constant $c$, which everyone else was calling the 'speed of light in space' was, for him, no more than just that: a constant, giving the ratio between conventional length-units and time-units in drawing relativistic diagrams. It was analogous to the constant ratio of 39.36 inches to the metre in the vertical and horizontal measures he had thought about earlier. To say that $c$ was the same for all observers in relative motion or rest was, in that case, no mystery of the sort it had so notoriously been, he learned, for the physicists Michelson and Morley in their abortive attempts, in the 1880s, to establish the objective reference-datum for $c$ in what they had thought of as the 'luminiferous ether' $^{[7]}$. The mystery this failure had occasioned was, as he saw it, as though someone had made some great mystery of the fact that there are 39.36 inches to the metre for all relatively moving and stationary measures! The real mystery, so far as he was now concerned, was not 'how can $c$ be the same for all observers' but 'how could we have imagined that it would be otherwise!'

Some to whom he put this proposition assumed that since all velocities of motion are distances divided by time, the fact that $c$ is distance divided by time made it necessarily a velocity of motion. They did not thank him for pointing out that by the same reasoning, since all Welshmen are human beings, all human beings are necessarily Welshmen. For himself, however, the fact that there was no logical necessity to think of $c$ as the velocity of anything travelling opened up whole new vistas of conceptual possibility. So it was that on this point of whether $c$ is a velocity or simply a dimensional constant these new ideas of his and those he had inculcated as a telephone engineer began logically to diverge. Had he realised it, what was happening was that his interests were expanding away from engineering and astronomy. He was becoming what Sir Karl Popper would (much later) tell him was 'a true philosopher'. In an informal discussion at the Astronomical Society, he tried to explain to someone that he could no longer think of the constant $c$ as the speed of 'electromagnetic' processes, such as light- and radio-signals travelling on their own in an objective space. So far as he was concerned, light was not some quasi-substance, taking ages to travel in its own time from stars and so on to the eye of the observer. It was simply what we see; and the constant we call $c$ was as much a part of that visual perception as are colour, parallax and perspective.

His changed conception of $c$ was therefore, at the time, that it had to be a constant relating the dimensions of things in perception itself. And since, as his 'telescopes' example with the observers A and B had shown, visual perception involved not only optical (kinematical) but also dynamical phenomena—in fact, all processes in the time-systems of the respective observers—the result seemed to be that not only was beauty 'in the eye of the beholder' but so, also, was everything else including all that he had so far regarded as most material and substantial. This he found disturbing, to say the

$^{[7]}$ On the traditional assumption that the constant $c$ was the speed of light in the ether, in 1887 two scientists, A. A. Michelson and E.W. Morley, sought to establish the speed, with respect to that datum which they conceived as the 'luminiferous ether', of the earth-borne observatory in the earth's various astronomical movements around the sun, the galaxy and so on. The completely negative results of this and other repeated experiments led to the conclusion that $c$ was no ordinary velocity but was a constant of observation for all observers regardless of their motions relative to one another, which led to the Einsteinian Theory of Relativity (see Enc. Brit. 1961, 15: 417b).

least, but intriguing nonetheless. The fact that this perception-based view of things was not unknown within the annals of Western philosophy did not occur to him. This, of course, was because to him at that time, 'Philosophy' was a more or less 'closed book'. Enough of his garbled account of his new ideas was, however, sufficiently appreciated by a very patient older man at the Astronomical Society, who told him that his argument was reminiscent of Bishop George Berkeley's eighteenth century philosophy which was based on the dictum 'Being (esse) is to be perceived or known' [8]. This had been followed with the well-judged advice to study the works of that famous philosopher, which advice our lineman accepted with alacrity. His reward was to discover that instead of his thoughts being 'way out on a limb' as he had been led to believe, they had, all along, been developing on well-respected —indeed quite illustrious— logical lines.

He was thus persuaded, by one means or another, that the way towards a true understanding of how nature works —the way towards a truly scientific understanding of the fundamentals, space and time— was not by learning to recognise and repeat the names of astronomical objects, nor by studying textbooks on electrodynamics, far less by studying mathematics. It was by studying what he now knew as Philosophy. Newly imbued with this philosophical spirit, he made a nuisance of himself at the university yet again. This time, however, it was with the philosophers rather than the mathematicians and physicists. But this was only to discover, to his infinite puzzlement, that the philosophers and the scientists of the establishment had been, for some time, 'at daggers drawn'.

III The Anti-Science of the 1960s Philosophers

The driving force of the local University College Philosophy Department was, at that time, an influential philosopher by the name of Rush Rhees. Our lineman was informed, in an interview with one of his students, that this Rhees had sat on the right hand of the 'Great Wittgenstein', whose name was familiar to everyone —except, it seemed, to our philosophically aspiring lineman. In a meeting the student had arranged for him with Rhees, at Rhees' home, he was roundly informed that Berkeley was now 'old hat', and had been 'overtaken' by Wittgenstein. Philosophy, he was told, was now all about language —or, to use its proper description, linguistic analysis. In the ensuing discussion, for all that it concerned the issue which had begun with the letter from Einstein, the lineman felt that the interview might just as well have been conducted under water. It would be some time before he would come to understand the true import of what these 'Wittgensteinians' were saying (see following).

Completely bemused by this reception, our lineman sought help in other directions, particularly from Drs. Gareth Evans and Mel Hopkins, of the university Maths and Physics Departments, respectively. Meanwhile, however, he maintained his contacts with what he came to think of as the Swansea 'Wittgenstein Society'. He had been invited to join that Philosophical Society by the Head of the Philosophy Department, Professor J.R. Jones, who somehow persuaded the Post Office to allow their employee (reluctantly on their part) to attend the College Philosophical Society on Thursday afternoons. This he did, in his working-clothes, spattered with bird-lime and smelling of creosote —so as the sooner to work up his hours on lineman's duties afterwards. It was when he informed his Maths and Physics associates of this new 'Wittgenstein' contact of his that he learned how deep was the antipathy between these scientists and the philosophers. So far as the scientists were concerned, Rhees' claim that 'the world is language' was completely ridiculous, and they were fulsome in expressing their rejection of that way of thinking.

[8] Berkeley, Bishop G., 'A Treatise concerning the Principles of Human Knowledge' (first published 1710), in A New Theory of Vision and Other writings, Dent, London (1950), p. 116.

Finding himself now caught between these two warring factions, our lineman became more and more bewildered. How on earth, he wondered, had that short correspondence with Einstein got him into this mess? All he had wanted were some simple commonsense answers to some simple commonsense questions about space and time. Some semblance of sanity was, however, maintained in correspondence with a very tall and patrician, yet extremely eccentric gentleman known affectionately to his students as 'The Noble Savage'. This was Dr. (later to become Professor) P. M. Davidson, of the Swansea Physics Department. He had been one of those with whom, together with Bondi, our lineman had corresponded in his puzzlings over what he had thought was a paradox in Einstein's theory. In these further correspondences, he was amazed to discover that there had been a whole phalanx of people, led by Herbert Dingle, who had contemplated this same paradox, which they named the 'Clock Paradox', but had been unable to accept what he now saw as the plain commonsense solution. Davidson had been as instrumental as Bondi in enabling him to reach this satisfactory explanation. Moreover, Davidson succeeded, where, previously, the young Maths Ph.D. had not, in explaining to him what was really the very simple mathematical connection between his own relativistic diagrams and Einstein's time-dilation formula. Davidson suggested that instead of using units of 186,000 miles for his distance-measures $s$ he should use standard metres. The hypotenuse of the right-angled triangle is then the resultant of the two dimensional components, $t_{\text{P}}$ and $s/c$ (where $t_{\text{P}}$ is the time registered by the travelling particle, or projectile, and $c$ is the constant $3 \times 10^8$ metres to the second). Since both of these measures are in seconds, then by ordinary schoolboy Pythagoras, the length, in seconds, of the observational resultant of those two components (call it $t_{\text{R}}$) is

$$t_{\text{R}} = \sqrt{[(s/c)^2 + t_{\text{P}}^2]} \quad (1).$$

Since $s$ in this formula is the distance $vt_{\text{R}}$ travelled by the object relatively to the observer, where $v$ is velocity $s/t_{\text{R}}$, $s$ it can be replaced by $vt_{\text{R}}$ in (1) to produce

$$t_{\text{R}} = \sqrt{[(vt_{\text{R}}/c)^2 + t_{\text{P}}^2]} \quad (2).$$

By just a bit of simple algebra (which the Professor explained in detail but which need not be reproduced here), this becomes

$$t_{\text{R}} = t_{\text{P}} / \sqrt{(1 - v^2/c^2)} \quad (3).$$

which is Einstein's formula precisely.

Notably, however, there was no mention, in this deduction, of the customary 'velocity of light'. The Professor had accepted without demur, that $c$, in this relativistic deduction, was no more than a scale-constant in the geometrical construction of the diagrams. The philosophical implications of this conceptual re-interpretation of $c$ had dropped completely out of account. Indeed, Bondi, who later also confirmed this deduction, claimed precedence for it and stated, in so many words, that for him, dropping the 'light-velocity' concept of $c$ in favour of interpreting it as what he called a 'conversion factor’ was a matter of pure convenience in simplifying the teaching of Relativity $^{[9]}$. As far as any philosophical implications were concerned he declared that he had no interest in them whatsoever $^{[10]}$.

But if the scientists, for their part, had no interest in the philosophy, the philosophers, conversely, had no interest in the mathematics. Much more, these philosophers declaredly had no interest in Science! Indeed, it soon became plain, in the Philosophical Society meetings which our lineman attended, that ‘scientism’ was a dirty word which Rush Rhees would often use as an epithet with which to flagellate any student daring to mention anything even suggestive of science. For Rhees, ‘Ordinary Language’ was sacrosanct and was not to be sullied by the ‘jargon-mongers’ of maths and physics. Indeed, it was said that on one occasion a physical confrontation occurred over some issue of this sort, between Rhees and another lecturer, on the steps of the College Library.

With the scientists, then, caring nothing for the logic, and the philosophers caring nothing for the maths —with ‘Einstein’ as irrelevant to the Wittgensteinians as ‘Wittgenstein’ was to the Einsteinians— his ‘Theory’, as he began to call it, was forced to continue its logical course in the way it had started, in a veritable no-man’s land between two completely hostile Educational factions. To get anyone to grasp what he was saying was impossible. As he put it to his wife, it was as though a comedian were forced to tell a joke in separate halves to separate audiences, so that the ‘penny dropped’ in neither case.

In an intermittent correspondence with Alfred Ayer, which lasted until just before Ayer’s death, the eminent philosopher told our lineman that he was unfortunately unable to assess the value of the thesis as a whole because, as he wrote, he ‘suffered from having been educated in Greek and Latin rather than in mathematics and physics’$^{[11]}$. Not long before he died, in a Newspaper article entitled ‘Things I Wish I had Known at Eighteen’$^{[12]}$, Ayer repeated that sentiment by declaring that he wished he had been able to study these subjects to a greater level. With this communicational breakdown sadly in mind the lineman tried to confide to his closest friends this first-hand discovery of the sorry state of the Education that they, like he, had been brought up to respect. He likened it to the way the priests of the Middle Ages supported themselves by selling to serfs assurances of places in heaven, and swanning around speaking in Latin, making it seem as though they knew everything when in fact they knew nothing. Some of these friends were galvanized for a while —some for many years, as shall be seen in following chapters— by these ‘Bolshevik’-like rantings of his; but for the most part they were unable to sustain this revolutionary enthusiasm and, understandably, not being directly involved, they subsided into watching their friend fighting ‘The System’ on his own.

The stress caused by this continual alienation eventually began to tell. His disenchantment increased, not only with what he saw as the stultified, scholastically confined state of modern Education but also with those, even among his friends and family, whom he saw as prepared to accept it all with sheep-like complacency. Angry exchanges, unprovoked aggression, speeding, traffic accidents and all manner of antisocial incidents became the order of the day. In a visit to a psychiatrist he was advised to drop all this ‘university’ nonsense and settle for doing his best to obtain promotion as a good and conscientious employee of the Post Office Engineering Department. To the psychiatrist he replied: ‘So if this were Nazi Germany and I came to you stressed at being forced to burn Jews, I

$^{[9]}$ ‘Any attempt to measure the velocity of light is ... not an attempt at measuring the velocity of light but an attempt at ascertaining the length of the standard metre in Paris in terms of time units.’ Bondi, H. Assumption and Myth in Physical Theory, C.U.P., 1965, p.28.

$^{[10]}$ Personal correspondence, Phil. Glim. Vol. 8, p. 2981.

$^{[11]}$ ibid. Vol. 12, p. 4515.

$^{[12]}$ Ayer, A. J., ‘Things I Wish I’d Known at Eighteen’, (The Sunday Express, Supplement. February 14$^{th}$, 1982, pp. 45 – 46).

suppose you would advise me to forget all this "humanity" nonsense and settle for being a good and conscientious Jew-burner!' The result of the interview was that he was diagnosed as 'mildly sociopathic', with medicines prescribed to restore passive conformity.

This was more grist, again, to his faster and faster grinding philosophical mill! 'Curiouser and curiouser', he told himself, like Alice in Wonderland. The Mad Hatter's tea party wasn't in it! Into the sky he would sometimes exclaim: 'Am I from this planet, or what?' His simple commonsense question to Einstein had led him into a social minefield. With his continually angry and aggressive behaviour, and rantings about things that most people couldn't care less about, he was becoming regarded as some special kind of egomaniac. Nor was this impression ameliorated by the second appearance, in the local newspaper, of the 'Einstein letter'. This was in response to a request made by a reporter to whom he was introduced at a party and who had asked for a photograph to accompany an article the reporter wished to write. Thinking that this might elicit some sensible responses to his discovery, our lineman naively agreed. The article duly appeared under the title of 'A Mini-Einstein Theory', followed by a highly enlarged and imaginative journalistic account of academic acceptance and support, with a portrait of the author in a pose like that of Rodin's thinker. To his horror, this was then followed, in the same paper a short time later, by an article reporting that this same 'thinker' had formed a vigilante group for neighbourhood protection against attacks by hooligans—a no less journalistically-imaginative tale 'full of sound and fury' spun from a chance bit of conversation the reporter overheard in a cafe on a slow newsday of the silly season.$^{[13]}$

For those who were incapable of seeing him as anything less than an egotist and publicity-seeker, this was clear confirmation of their assessment. What remained of his serious attempt, in agreeing to the first article, to promote his idea in a proper and dignified way was in ruins. Whatever scientific and/or philosophical merit the theory might have was now less likely than ever to be discerned behind this journalistically contrived and 'schizophrenic' public image.

And yet, through all this inexplicable social and psychological confusion there still ran, for him, that unbroken thread of pure rationality that stretched straight through from Einstein, via the young Maths Ph.D. and Bondi, to the final mathematical corroboration by Davidson. As for what went on at the Philosophical Society, what that had to do with anything was completely lost on him. Finally, he cancelled his membership of that group by getting up and walking out, as obtrusively as possible, on one of Rush Rhees' most pontifical pronouncements on the evils of 'Scientism'.

So who could he possibly approach for an honest appraisal of his uniquely different, geometrical approach to relativity theory. He certainly did not want any journalist to make another botch-up of it in the press. With his wife's invaluable assistance, he wrote what he fancifully described as his 'thesis'. This was an honest but unschooled attempt to present his unique theoretical insight with no attempt to separate the conventionally distinguished Physics aspect of the work from the Philosophical. The confusion this caused among those he invited to read it may be judged from the following incidents. The first was when, at the suggestion of his mother-in law, he contacted the vicar of the local church, who said he would be glad to read and comment upon what his parishioner had written. Some short time later, the vicar reported back that the work was not scholarly presentable because it had not mentioned the Holy Trinity. 'Leave out the diagrams and the formulae,' he was advised, 'and concentrate on that.' The second incident was when he contacted a local writer, known to his family, who had written books on the history of Swansea. After reading the work, the historian invited the lineman to his home where he kindly explained that in his considered opinion the work had been written because he, the lineman, had not succeeded in going to university like some of his friends (and, of course, the historian himself) and was making some sort of pathetic attempt to convince

$^{[13]}$ 'Herald of Wales', Swansea, July 4th, 1964. Also, Phil. Glim. Vol. 1, p. 40.

everyone that he was as good as they were. 'A perfectly understandable psychological reaction', he was kindly informed.

Following this there were numerous other attempts to get the idea assessed, but with similar and often even more bizarre results which it would be tedious to recount. Nowhere, it seemed, was there any Educational provision for examining such an innovative suggestion, especially one subscribed by an unqualified person, and far less one which combined both scientific and philosophical perspectives in that academically 'mixed-up' way. How, his inquisitors wondered, could anyone talk in the same breath about 'relativity', which is pure Physics, and 'perception' which is pure Psychology! In any discussion the scientists would stick to the one and the philosophers the other. 'We are not qualified,' they would grandly proclaim, 'to comment on ...' (the one aspect or the other). For them, Nature seemed to have fallen between those two completely different Educational stools. To seek natural knowledge in the manner of the original, unschooled enquirers was nowadays to fall into an academic limbo.

IV Into the Academic Limbo

The fact, nonetheless, which Bondi and Davidson had corroborated, that Einstein's relativistic equations can be derived in an entirely different way, simply by geometrically combining space with time and without involving the customary conception of 'the speed of light', continued to intrigue our lineman. The mistake he had made was to assume that this should excite the curiosity of any logically and fair-minded commonsense person. For instance, to interpret $c$ as a dimensional constant relating to objects and processes in perception instead of as a velocity in space, interesting and effective though it had turned out in itself, clashed very obviously with the current notion that distant objects such as the stars are seen, not as they are now but as they were long ago. It was in discussing this that he began to distinguish the difference between two very different types of mentality. Very few people, as he has soon discovered, were prepared to entertain any suggestion, regardless of how logical it might be, that in some subtle way we might be wrong about light-velocity in the way that people had once been wrong about the flat earth. It involved no logical contradiction to suggest that light may be simply what we see, not something travelling in space, and that those distance-time dimensions which we customarily conceive as its 'velocity' might be in the light as we see it, not of the light itself, in some 'void' underlying it. Yet the responses he received to this logical suggestion were puzzling in the extreme. They invariably took the form of complete and utter rejection, laced with ridicule. Time and again, he would go back over his thinking, to see if there was any flaw in the argument but, try as he might, he could find none. Was it, then, he was forced to wonder, simply a matter of sheer intelligence—or some peculiar kind of intelligence—which he unknowingly possessed and which others lacked? What else could it be, since he could find no other rational explanation for this completely dismissive attitude, on the part of so many people, towards something so plainly logical and mentally challenging?

But there was another explanation, the full realisation of which took a very long time to sink in. Again, it had nothing to do with the logic or the physics of his original enquiry, nor yet with 'intelligence', but with pure psychology. This realisation, in the end, would completely shatter his original, purist ideal of Science. The first step in this disillusionment was when it became more and more plain to him that 'Science' had not been, as he had assumed, a purely rational, far less purely logical, undertaking. What it seemed to be was some kind of glorified, mathematically projected version of 'Mechanical Engineering', in which everything had to be explained in terms of things either bumping into one another, exchanging particles or attracting and repelling one another at a distance by inscrutable happenings in vacuo. But this 'theorising', no matter how socially prestigious it might be, was not reason as he understood it, because what the nature of this mechanistically projected underworld of the ‘void’ or ‘vacuum’ might be could be anyone’s guess. A ‘void’, in the common-language sense of a Bell jar, a fault in cast metal or concrete, he could perfectly understand. However, in the special sense used by physicists to describe the spatial separation of fundamental particles and astronomical objects it was a different matter. In what sense could such a ‘void’, in and of itself, be said to exist? There was no possible way of getting anything into it to prove its existence because putting any sort of detector into the vacuum made it, at that precise detection-point, a non-vacuum. The whole notion of a void, other than in those straightforward engineering-type senses already mentioned was, whichever way you looked at it, a complete conundrum. This world that these theoreticians were concerned with in the name of ‘the vacuum’, or ‘the void’, conceived as something in itself apart from matter was, it seemed to him, a world so intellectually dark as to be beyond darkness itself.

Then came the second step in the disillusionment. These theoreticians were plainly prepared, like Bondi (see FN 9), to accept the relativistic equations simply as mathematical ‘tools of the trade’, in their professional pursuit of a ‘Science’ that had been strangely hedged about with (albeit unwritten) professional rules and regulations against exploring the logical implications any further. The reason for this curtailment of philosophical interest among the scientists, as it subsequently turned out [see Ch. VIII], was an incipient dread, based on nothing more than vague ‘intuitions’, that such enquiries, if pursued, would lead inevitably to ‘solipsism’. This was the notion which physicists imputed to philosophers in general and to Berkeley in particular, that oneself is the sole and ultimate observer, and that everything else, including other observers, are nothing but figments of one’s own imagination. The antagonism he had encountered from these mechanistic thinkers was due to their lurking suspicion, or plain superstition, that if these ‘relativistic’ implications were taken to their full philosophical conclusion, the good old solid, physical world would turn out, in the end, to be nothing but a dream.

Of course, the form this dismissive reaction would take was seldom overtly antagonistic. Mostly it would be that to regard any suggestion that the world as we perceive it might not be quite as real as it so ‘obviously’ is, was just downright silly. Also, in the way that, in men, fear often disguises itself as bravado, some of those friends and associates with whom he tried to discuss these things reacted with such an affronted machismo that it was as though, with his ‘idealistic’ arguments, he was inviting them to wear a frock. This is undoubtedly why someone had declared that the biggest enemy of true education is not ignorance but vanity. Nor had it escaped his notice in other spheres of experience, particularly among manual labourers, that the dignity which men feel they have to maintain in the face of their peers may keep them intellectually handicapped; and the more severely so as their dignity—and the masculinity in many cases—was the more fragile. For instance, if ‘Einstein’ was mentioned in such circumstances, someone might say something like ‘Who does he play for?’ Or a discussion might evoke buffoonery as a means of derailing the conversation, as in making statements like ‘I know another big word: “wheelbarrow”,’ and so on. He was intrigued to learn that this sort of reaction was by no means confined to his own era. He read that after being told about Berkeley’s theory, the great Samuel Johnson had contemptuously dismissed that theory by kicking at a stone, saying ‘Sir, I refute thee thus!’

Such, then, was the vanity which, he now clearly discerned, had been the ‘enemy of true education’ by polarising man’s naturally scientific intellect into ‘hard-headed physics’ and ‘effete philosophy’. Physicists fancied themselves as good honest, macho ‘realists’, in contrast to which they regarded philosophers as airy-fairy ‘idealists’. So intrigued was he by this psycho-social phenomenon of chronic split-mindedness that to trace its origins would become the aim of his postgraduate dissertation at university. His thesis would trace the history of that split right back to the distinction Plato had made, in his dialogue The Sophist, which described a battle between the ‘Gods’ and the ‘Giants’ among the natural philosophers of Plato’s time, presaging the hostility so often witnessed between the ‘Philosophers’ and ‘Scientists’ of our day. Plato’s ‘Giants’ were those who, as Plato described them, 'are forever trying to drag everything down to earth ... literally grasping rocks and trees in their hands,' or who 'lay hold upon every stock and stone and strenuously affirm that real existence belongs only to that which can be handled' [14]. Plato's 'Gods', on the other hand, were the pure reasoners, who —as the 'Giants' would see it— believed 'that unseen things are the true realities'. The thesis would track Plato's attempts to close this gap between the 'Materialists' and 'Idealists' of his time by introducing another species of thinkers Plato referred to as the 'Reformed Giants' (or 'God-Giants') These, the thesis proposed, were the precursors of our modern theoretical physicists (the self-styled 'Realists') who project into their own 'invisible (i.e., abstract) realms' of the void and 'hidden' atomic mechanisms, the tangible qualities that things manifest to us on the levels of ordinary observation. But in the same way that Plato had failed to bring together these two main factions of thinkers, whose followers remain separated today, so, ironically, the thesis failed —twice. This was because its prospects for fair assessment fell between those very same separate stools that it sought to bring together. It was as neat a case of 'Catch 22' as one might have imagined!

All this, however, was in the future. For the moment, he remained bemused by his discovery of the sheer decadence of what, in his childhood, his bright and sweet-smelling encyclopaedias had seduced him into thinking was 'Science'. Then, in a flash, it occurred to him that what Rush Rhees and his associates in the Swansea University Philosophical Society had been rejecting in the name of 'Scientism' must have been this mechanistically projected world of 'theory' and all the jargon that had become associated with it. The world these 'Wittgensteinians' wanted to describe was, ostensibly, the same world that he did, the world of undivided, ordinary commonsense experience, the world he knew, in which he, his friends and family lived, worked and communicated, as opposed to the world of these scientific theoreticians. Being well-versed in the vernacular and all its various shortcomings, however, he had not felt any need to idealise it in the way these academic philosophers sought to do in the name of 'Ordinary Language'. As he had experienced it, logic was one thing, and 'ordinary language' was, all too often, something else. Yet somehow, these academics had decided to place this 'Ordinary Language' on a pedestal and to protect it, uncritically, not only from what they saw as these 'Scientific' incursions but also, as it seemed to him, from all prospect of true scientific development. Where he and these 'Wittgensteinians' had failed to communicate was that in this conservationist reaction against their bogey of 'Scientism', when this 'engineer' appeared in their midst they lumped him together with the 'scientists', assuming that the language he was using was not of their world but of the inscrutable and detestable world described by the dreaded 'scientific' jargon.

But however justified might have been the rejection by these philosophers of the scientific jargon that was undoubtedly polluting and diminishing commonsense language, they had, he felt, by their ill-considered, over-reaction against it, lost all touch with true natural philosophy. So, as already mentioned, he left their company to seek elsewhere for answers to those questions which his brief correspondence with Einstein had raised. This was when he began to learn —though it would be many decades before he would realise it to the full— just how few people there were of a truly philosophical bent, even among accredited students and teachers of the subject. In a Staff-Student Philosophy seminar, at university, a fellow student would say to him, as if to demean him in front of his tutors: 'You don't seem to even like philosophy'. His reply took everyone aback, not least those who would be his examiners: 'No, I don't!' he said, simply. 'So why are you here studying it?' they asked him incredulously. 'Because I simply had to know,' he replied. 'And what do you now know?' someone else asked him. Bored and disenchanted by the stilted and pretentious level of the discussion he replied: 'I now know that seminars like these are the very last places where one is likely to find true knowledge!'

[14] 'The Sophist', Plato's Theory of Knowledge, J. M. Cornford, (Routledge, Kegan Paul) London, (1966), p. 228.

To continue, for a short space, this anticipation of what happened at university, what our lineman really needed to ‘know’ were logical answers to the questions that had been raised by what the newspaper had described as his ‘lines from Einstein’. In his dedicated concern for these things, it was not as though his interests were narrow. He was enjoying, to the full, the breadth of discussion provided by his choices of Psychology, Sociology, Political Theory and so on, that were ancillary to his main subject, Philosophy. Much of his interest in these particular subjects had been stimulated and augmented by peripheral experiences of people and situations in his dogged search for answers to the questions raised in his correspondence with Einstein. It had opened up a whole new world of further questions about the psychology of philosophers and men of science and about the sociology of those institutions. But, again, now right in the very heart of the Educational system, he was falling foul, as he had throughout, of those who regarded Education as a means towards money and social status, and who simply could not comprehend his own peculiar involvement in it. Just prior to graduation, he was asked by a careers adviser what he wanted to do when he left university. ‘I want to go back to my job as telephone lineman,’ he replied. ‘But wouldn’t it be more appropriate for you to want to return as a Telephone Manager?’ he was asked, incredulously. Thinking back to his disagreement with his erstwhile boss, over his ‘Lines from Einstein’, his answer was swift and decisive. ‘No thanks!’ he said.

V Direct-Observation versus Light-Separation

The trouble had been, all along, that ‘education’, so far as our lineman was concerned, was something to be pursued essentially for its own sake, as a means of understanding the world, in all its scientific, philosophical and social aspects, not simply for the sake of ‘getting on’, as he had so often heard it said. To recount, in detail, the events which had led to his appearance at university (as a mature student, aged thirty-five) would not be pertinent —not at this stage, at any rate— to the essentially scientific theme of this book. Suffice it to say, that what he did learn, as a result of his university studies, was, as he had been advised early on, that the logical course for exploring the implications of dispensing with the ‘light-velocity’ interpretation of relativity lay along a trail that had already been blazed by earlier thinkers such as Berkeley, Hume and Kant. This so-called ‘empiricist’ approach required having to contemplate a direct observational interaction with nature, as opposed to the indirect contact presumed to be mediated by light. But of course, the plain fact could not be ignored that experimental evidence definitely seemed to have shown that light takes a finite time to travel from A to B, so that nature is always observed secondhand.
