Yet it also seemed paradoxical to claim to know that there are things behind our observations which we can never know directly so as to compare with them our knowledge of them. To know that those things are there, behind all our observations of them, implies having some sort of direct and supernatural evidence of their existence, so that all talk of such ‘underlying realities’ becomes esoteric nonsense. So in a sense, all knowledge becomes, by default, observational —which, of course, was the empiricist stance of the philosophers, Berkeley, et al.

His studies of these philosophers, however, gave him no indication that any of them had ever even attempted to explain, in their purely observationalist terms, this experimental fact —which he felt that Kant, at least, should have known $^{[15]}$— of light-separation. But that omission was crucial, because how can our knowledge of the world be directly observational whilst mediated to us by the time-delayed action of light? That question had to be addressed, otherwise it could be legitimately claimed that the real world is not the directly observed world with which these empiricists were concerned but

$^{[15]}$ The astronomer, Römer discovered the distance-time constant $c$ in 1676, so that one would have expected that Kant (1724–1804) would have been aware of this discovery.

something lying inscrutably beyond it, and our purely observational world consists of no more than our own subjective impressions and fantasies. Little wonder, then, that with such a crucial omission, natural philosophy had become split in two, that the theories of the empiricist philosophers had been rejected by physicists as 'idealistic' in favour of the contrastingly 'realistic' theories of such people as Huygens, Hertz and Maxwell—and, of course, Einstein. It was doubtless also the reason why those logical enquiries that the likes of Berkeley had undertaken seemed to have precipitated them into the isolating self-centredness of the above-mentioned solipsism, or subjective idealism, which was about as airy-fairy, from the mechanistic-physics point of view, as it was possible in those days to get.

What was required, then, it seemed, in order for these philosophers and physicists to be able to take one another seriously and 'get their act together' was for the philosophers to come down from their 'idealistic' high ground and address the real problem of how to explain, in terms of their implied direct-action theory of observation, why light, the essential agency of interconnection between bodies and our perceptions of them, appears to be delayed in the manner described by Römer, et al.. They should, he felt, actually compete with the mechanists for explanations of these observational/experimental facts, instead of preoccupying themselves in cloistered isolation with, as someone disparagingly put it, 'writing footnotes to Plato'.

So how, he wondered, should these philosophers have dealt with that incontestable experimental fact of the time-delayed to-ing and fro-ing of light in space? There would be no need to know all that is now known about how light behaves in experiments of the sort conducted by Michelson, et al. Anyone who knows that light casts a shadow, that it is reflected by mirrors, that a stick looks bent in water, and so on, is led to suppose that light is some sort of process in space that can be interrupted en route from A to B. Such effects had been known to optical science from the very beginning. Not being a scholar (unlike his wife who, fortunately for him was, as already stated, an excellent scholar), he was reluctant to delve deep into books for answers to his questions. Notwithstanding his childhood interest in pictorial encyclopedias (usually read in bed, in times of sickness), while he was fit, the reading of books had been anathema to him. The only book he was interested in, as he frequently proclaimed to his wife when he met her first (whilst carrying her heavy satchel of books from school), was the Book of Nature. For himself, he would as soon sit in a library reading books as sit in the cockpit of a racing car studying the Highway Code.

Under his wife's influence, however, and following his advised reading of Berkeley, this unscholarly attitude had gradually changed. Although his studying-periods were brief and his reading cursory, he read enough, even in his pre-university days, to discover that in George Berkeley's two books, the aforementioned An Essay Towards a New Theory of Vision (1709), and Dialogues Between Hylas and Philonous (1713), the Irish Bishop had attempted to address the problem of how to explain, in terms of a direct-interaction theory of perception, this apparently mediatory role of light. The issue, it seemed, hung logically in the balance between two antithetically opposed ways of thinking. Are we seeing the world as it really is, or is what we see never more than a second-hand representation, mediated to us by light, of things lying forever behind and beyond our perceptions? Further readings, both prior to and at university, convinced our lineman that in seeking to decide this issue the philosophers had signally failed. Far from avoiding the dreaded 'solipsism', Berkeley, regardless of how 'misunderstood' he is now considered to have been, had fallen right into it simply by not addressing, in any convincing way, the problem of light-separation. Nor had his followers, Hume and Kant, so far as he could glean, done any better.

This issue did not excite the interests of those for whom Learning was simply a means for 'getting on' in the educational system provided. How could the threat of 'solipsism' concern them? What did they care, he would cynically reflect, whether life is real or just a dream? Best not to think about it! Make it as pleasant and undemanding as you can by whatever means are available, and avoid whatever it takes to turn that 'dream' into a nightmare. But why he himself could not settle for this

Epicurean solution he could no more explain than plants can explain why they seek the sun. It was plain to him that on this fundamental issue of whether physical reality resides in things as we perceive them or as they are in themselves behind and beyond our perceptions, just about everything else depended. What about morality, for instance? What can it matter how you treat your fellow beings if, in the end, they are no more than figments of a dream? Yet how can that 'dream' be avoided if all that one perceives are 'appearances' brought to us, by light, of things whose true natures as they might be behind the light can never be perceived other than in the light itself? And what about touch, sound, taste, smell? He knew well enough that the way we interact with our environment in these categories of perception all depend, in the last analysis, on the same sorts of energies that manifest themselves in light. So this fundamental question, as to whether, in light, we are perceiving the world immediately or at second-hand was absolutely vital and just had to be solved.

At other times he would reflect that what was required, for Berkeley's theory to be judged fairly, was to see if it really could produce a knock-down explanation as to how, in direct-action terms, light can behave in experiments of the sort conducted by Michelson, et al. In the first place, as he had discovered, instead of interpreting Römer's original observations of the variations in the eclipse-times of Jupiter's principal satellite as due to light travelling across the varying distance between Jupiter and Earth, one could interpret it much more simply as being due to the fact that there is no space as such nor time as customarily conceived but only observational space-time, in the ratio of conventional units $c$. This would be a phenomenalistic paraphrase of Einstein's conclusion as stated in the latter's Special Theory of Relativity and in his well-known General Theory.

This combining of space and time to form spacetime by direct observation seemed far more sensible and efficient than doing so by the usual esoteric route of messing about with 'electrodynamics' and 'light-velocity'. It was, as he confided to a friend, like counting horses direct, instead of by some strange convention of counting their legs and tails and dividing by five. And hadn't that plain observational interpretation of $c$ as a dimensional constant of perception rather than as a 'velocity in space' produced his simplified, commonsense-intuitive version of relativity that Bondi and Davidson had endorsed? (See Ch. II).

Well, at least, the fact that one could, without creating contradiction, shift from thinking of the constant $c$ as a 'velocity in space' to thinking of it as a constant of perception proved that the usually attested 'experimental evidence' that light 'travels' could not be taken unconditionally as a valid logical refutation of Berkeley's empiricism. However, this particular conclusion still seemed too abstract and airy-fairy—too 'philosophical', as one might say—to be scientifically convincing. What was needed was a much stronger argument, one which explained unequivocally, in terms of this new observationist interpretation of $c$, the delayed-action to-ing and fro-ing of light-signals in experiments such as the toothed wheel and revolving mirror experiments of researchers such as Fizeau and Michelson.

This took him some time to figure out. Eventually, however, it came to him. The answer was obvious! The measured 'velocity' of the light, in travelling from the source to the mirror and back, in these experiments, was simply the extreme, or limiting case of the 'travellers' A and B in his letter to Einstein. Recalling those equations which Davidson had derived from his original diagrams, and with whose maths he was now familiar, he looked once again at his original Pythagorean equation, (1), viz.:

$$t_R = \sqrt{[(s/c)^2 + t_P^2]} \quad (1)$$

This was when he realised that when $t_P$ in this equation is the intrinsic (or, as he later learned to call it, the 'proper') time of the transition of a light-signal from a source to a distant mirror is zero i.e., instantaneous), the time $t_R$ of that signal relative to the observer (to Michelson in his laboratory, say) is, from (1), the delayed-time $s/c$, which is 3.3 nanoseconds for every metre of distance from the source to the mirror and back in Michelson's apparatus. What Michelson had discovered, therefore, had been perfectly predictable on the basis, purely, of the information supplied by Römer in the seventeenth century. The result was incontrovertible. Berkeley's observational, direct-perception theory could, through these relativistic equations, have provided an explanation of the finite-speed to-ing and fro-ing of light-signals. The explanation was practically a tautology! The full intuitive follow-up of this result, in what he would call his 'cinematic model' would come much later (chapter XI).

Moreover, the fact that the proper-time t of the signal is instantaneous in both directions, while the relative time t_R is 2s/c, presented no more of a paradox for the to-ing and fro-ing of the light than in the case of the observers A and B discussed in the correspondence with Einstein. The time of the signal relative to itself, in the Michelson apparatus, is shorter than for Michelson, because its journey, like that of the traveller B in the original example, is broken by being reversed at the mirror, whilst that of Michelson and his laboratory is not. So, once again, there was no paradox in thinking of observation as a direct action. Everything was logically in order.

It seemed, then, that there was no true logical nor mathematical distinction between Berkeley's theory and that of his opponents regarding the issue of light. In light, our observational contact with nature is both direct and indirect, without this being in any way paradoxical. Besides, from our lineman's commonsense time-equation it followed, as from Einstein's identical one, that if time is relative, then so is mass. This was easy to understand in commonsense terms. Mass is, virtually by definition, the resistance to acceleration of a free-moving body. As the speed v/c of the body approaches unity in the relative time equation, (3), so the resistance to relative acceleration of the body increases with the relative time until, at theoretical unity, they both become infinite. Since the proportion of the relative increase in mass, m_R/m, is equal to that of the relative increase in time, t_R/t_P in equation (3), we can write

$$m_{\mathrm{R}}/m = t_{\mathrm{R}}/t_{\mathrm{P}} = 1/\sqrt{(1 - v^2/c^2)} \tag{4},$$

from which it follows that mass increases with velocity in the relation

$$m_{\mathrm{R}} = m/\sqrt{(1 - v^2/c^2)} \tag{5},$$

the same as in standard relativity. (More of this later, in chapter VII, section 1.)

So Johnson would have been confounded to have learned what we now know, that the mass—the very materiality—of the stone he kicked in order to refute Berkeley was as relative as its colour. Had this relativistic solution been available to Berkeley, reflected our lineman, things might have turned out very differently indeed! Our dichotomous conceptions of 'objective' and 'subjective' might have become merged into that of the relative, and the disastrous splitting of natural philosophy into materialistic 'Science' and idealistic 'Philosophy' might have been averted.

However, it had not been averted, and the artificial apartheid between the two disciplines was now endemic in the schizophrenic way we are all accustomed to think about nature. For the scientists, the philosophers have their feet planted firmly in mid air, and for the philosophers, the scientists have their heads planted firmly in the ground. Little wonder that with such a topsy-turvy history, the Education he had brushed with made such little sense. Indeed, just how little sense it made was to be confirmed, to his cost, when, later, at university, in proposing his post-graduate Philosophy of Science Dissertation he was warned that Philosophy of Science had to be purely descriptive. Its aim, he was told, was to describe and apply linguistic analysis to what physicists actually do, which, as Richard

Feynman once put it, was just about ‘what the hell they liked’. If the thesis was to succeed, then ‘in no way’, they said, ‘should it be made to seem prescriptive about science’. But where, other than in this obvious sop to the system, called Philosophy of Science, was he to present his thesis? Within the completely polarised Arts/Science of the academic administration, his thesis was a non-subject. There was no-one, he was told, who was ‘competent’ to deal with its academically mixed-up subject-matter. No supervisor, he was told, would touch it. Eventually, in desperation to get him off their backs, his tutors would suggest that he might contact the famous philosopher of science, Karl Popper. On this suggestion, he and his wife were to travel to London, where they would be met, on Popper’s behalf, by an underling Ph.D., the consequences of which meeting will be recounted later (Ch. VIII).

All this, of course, was still to come. For the moment he was left to carry on with his job as telephone-lineman. The job was well-suited to his temperament, being both physically and mentally fulfilling. It also gave him time to ruminate while driving—and often trudging—through the hills of Wales, where, through the kind of work he was doing, he was daily presented with problems that were widely varying and could not be fitted into neatly divided academic slots. Problems presented by nature, whether to a lineman, a physicist or a philosopher, were there to be solved, and that, ultimately, by applied commonsense logic. There was, for him, no more question of anyone, at the end of the day, ‘agreeing to differ’ over questions of science or philosophy than there was of linemen, at the end of the day, ‘agreeing to differ’ over whether a line had been repaired or not. Such practical employments of logic were far from academic, and in the more contemplative moments this occupation afforded he would reflect how strange it was that, as it seemed to him, physicists and philosophers had scorned this commonsense imperative by allowing themselves to become so academically divided. He likened them to the warring factions of Lilliputians in Swift’s Gulliver’s Travels, who were opposed over the issue of whether an egg should be cracked at the bottom or the top. In his own, academically unfettered approach to nature, he had offended just about everyone, right from the start, by attacking the ‘egg’ right in the middle.

A lineman in the hills of Wales (Winter 1962/3)

PART 2

DEVELOPMENT

The philosophical repercussions of the commonsense ‘engineering-drawing’ type of approach to relativity are explored in the more academic environment of an Arts course in Science-Philosophy at university. A way of re-uniting philosophy and physics in the context of modern commonsense philosophy is discovered. In this unifying approach, the opposing elements of philosophical subjectivism (Subjective Realism) and physical absolutism (Remote Realism) are jettisoned in favour of the commonsense relationism of such thinkers as the well-known science-philosopher and critic of Einsteinian relativity, Ernst Mach.

VI The Commonsense Philosophy of G. E. Moore

Our seeker after truth had now come to the conclusion that a true relativism should have merged the traditionally polarised 'objectivity' of science and the 'subjectivity' of philosophy within a new and completely unified philosophy of relationism. Why had Einstein's 'relativism' failed to do that? The answer was obvious. It was because Einstein had compromised his 'relativistic' theory by basing it on an absolutist interpretation of the constant ratio of dimensional units, $c$, as a 'speed'. This 'speed' was supposed to be that of a 'something-in-itself' (call it 'light', 'electromagnetic radiation or whatever') travelling independently of, yet somehow relative to, not only every object in space but also to the very space itself.

But what kind of 'relativism' was that? Little wonder that Mach had regarded Einstein's theory as no more than a 'transitory inspiration in the history of science' [16]. Little wonder, also, that despite its success in practical physics, from a conceptual point of view the theory had become a music-hall by-word for intellectual obscurity! He was yet to discover just how problematic, even in the scientific community, were the full implications of that theory regarding 'Einstein separation', 'EPR' and so on [17]. By contrast, his own time-equation, which had been mathematically proved equivalent to Einstein's had shown, quite unequivocally, that in the proper-time of light itself there is no such 'Einstein separation', hence none of those complications associated with it. Our perceptions of distant objects are both immediate (relative to the light itself) and time-separated (relative to the observer). These contacts between observers and objects—and, of course, between objects themselves—are both direct and indirect, 'objective' and 'subjective', as the time-formulae plainly show.

'But how can that be?' someone asked him. 'How can things be both together and apart in that way?' [18] 'Easily,' he replied, and by way of demonstration he cited the case of a printed sentence in which the characters are separated in the plane of the page but which collapse more and more together as the page is turned edgewise-on. In principle, then, he explained, separateness and togetherness can be a matter of dimensions (as his simple diagrams and equations had clearly shown). In its own proper-time, a light-interaction is instantaneous while in the time of the observer that same interaction is the delayed time consisting of the distance $s$ between the interacting bodies divided by the dimensional constant $c$.

There was, however, one great difficulty in this solution. It was that no matter how logically and mathematically irrefutable that unification of immediate and delayed action might be, it had nothing like the easy intuitive appeal of the familiar 'light-velocity' conception. There was, it seemed, no conceptual model of that situation which had anything like the facility of the one that had been supplied by the classical mechanists, from Empedocles to Einstein. He could say all he liked about the

[16] Mach, E, op. cit.

[17] 'EPR', from a well-known paper by Einstein, Podolski and Rosen (1935), is the name given to the still unresolved conflict in science between, on the one hand, Einstein's 'delayed-time' light-separation of objects from each other and from the observer and, on the other hand, the 'Standard-Model' quantum-theoretical view that all objects including the observer are instantly and continually connected by a universal non-local 'wave-function', as predicted by John S. Bell, is supposed to be verified in experiments by Alain Aspect and others.

[18] Correspondence with Basil Hiley, Dept. of Physics, Birkbeck College, London. December 27th, 1990. Philosophical Glimpses, Vol. 13, pp. 5041 a-d.

sheer illusoriness of that apparent facility of the ‘light-velocity’ model. With all his Socratic fervour—like a Welsh preacher, as someone described it—he would point out the sheer nonsense of the idea that light could be ‘waves in vacuo’. (For instance, in a vacuum, what was there to wave?) And, he would argue, if what travelled were Einstein’s light-particles or ‘photons’, then if they were real particles, in any ordinary understanding of real, they would have to have mass, in which case, at their ‘velocity c’ that mass would be relativistically infinite. But this was nonsense, since light has nothing like that mass. So the only alternative, if those particles were not to have infinite mass at ‘speed c’, would be for them to zero mass when stationary—and when were they ever stationary, since it was axiomatic in Relativity that their velocity had to be c in all systems of reference?

How, then, he would contend, could anyone possibly suppose that light ‘travelled’ in any physical form whatsoever? But of course, no-one would listen to him while, from a conventional point of view, for all its logicality, his own explanation as to how light behaved seemed, in some ways, even more abstruse than Einstein’s. ‘What the hell, they travel!’ had been the response of one professor to his best-presented arguments against travelling ‘photons’—as in the old saying, ‘We see and approve the better course but follow the worse’.

It would be some time before he would eventually discover that there is, in the nature of things, a familiar phenomenon capable of providing the sort of intuitive model he was seeking as a presentable alternative to the problematic ‘light-velocity’. Meanwhile, there occurred, in quick succession, a set of events which were to have a profound effect on the course of his philosophical enquiry. The first of these was a change in his personal circumstances. Contented with his immediate family and sufficiently rewarded in his job as telephone lineman, he nevertheless became more and more stressed in his relations with others outside that immediate workaday environment. Indeed, it would be fair to say that he began more and more to fit the contemporary stereotype of the ‘angry young man’. He could not understand how his friends could be so complacent about the shortcomings of ‘The System’ as he sought to describe it to them from his first-hand experience; and in his language and general behaviour, this impatience became plain. And, naturally, it generated reactions. Incipient though these were, for the most part, they were cumulatively sufficient to cause him great difficulties in coping with a social situation which he found more and more confining. Yet he could find no way out. He and his wife considered emigrating. The idea of being a lineman in the wilds of Canada appealed to him immensely. Family ties, however (his younger sister and widowed mother), eventually militated against this solution. He felt trapped.

Then, one day, in a particularly uneasy frame of mind, working near a little church by the sea, he wandered in and sat down. With no-one else around, the sheer tranquillity of that very old church, with its leaf-filtered light falling on ancient grey stonework, and no sound but that of the rhythmic wash of the sea on the rocks nearby, he lapsed into a state of near-catatonia. In that state he somehow, at some silent, subliminal level, made a solemn pact with something deep within himself—or around him, he couldn’t say. This was that no matter what it might take, he would dedicate his life to searching for the truth that was so evidently missing from Science as he had seen it—not through its magnificent portals, as bona fide students and professors do but through its most unprepossessing alley-way Entrance.

Emerging from that church into the sunlight, he felt at peace. This sense of peace lasted for a few weeks until the next event occurred. On a late-night emergency call-out, while pulling out at a junction, a motorcyclist skidded into the side of his van. There was no damage done, and pleasantries were exchanged with the rider, who then remounted and rode off. At that time, however, it was well known that anyone claiming damages in an accident with a ‘Crown vehicle’ was handsomely recompensed. The incident had occurred well after midnight. There were no witnesses, save one who had appeared nowhere at the time but only subsequently, in court, where it was claimed that as a result of the accident the old motorbike had been a ‘write-off’ due to carelessness on the van driver’s part. (The fact that the van had sustained no damage whatsoever in the ‘collision’ was mentioned by no-one, far less by the defending solicitor, who hadn’t even deigned to interview his client.) So the result was that our lineman’s name appeared in the newspaper yet again, this time under the headline ‘Hit and Run Driver’.

Although none of his colleagues believed that story, the publicity was too much for the Area Engineer, in further brushes with whom, following the original ‘Einstein’ publication, cordiality had virtually vanished. The result was that he was removed from lineman’s duties and relegated to an extremely boring job that was no more well paid than it was suited to his temperament.

Then came the third significant event. Two staff members of the local university Maths and Physics Departments, whose extramural evening lectures he had been attending, advised him, from their intramural standpoint, to ‘come in from the cold’ and pursue his interests ‘through the proper academic channels’. On recommendation from these lecturers he was interviewed at a Liberal Studies college in North Wales, where he was offered a place for the next session. Now that his income had been brought down to a level at which it compared favourably with the offer of a Local Authority grant, encouraged by his wife he decided to take up this offer.

The time from his experience in the church to his appearance at college was less than three months. He was now well and truly ‘in from the cold’, and far away from those social circumstances he had found so oppressive. In this new liberal atmosphere and idyllic setting in Snowdonia, he spent two terms enjoying studies of a wide-ranging kind and discussions of an unselfconscious sort that would have been unthinkable in his previous circumstances. During the second term, on the strength of his discovery of a unique sort of logic mnemonic, he was interviewed and duly accepted as a mature student for a degree course at the nearby University College of North Wales. This was to study Philosophy, with special emphasis on Logic and Philosophy of Science, with Psychology and Sociology as ancillary subjects.

Missing his wife and family, and financially stressed, he forewent the third term at the Liberal Studies College and returned to Swansea, where he and his wife decided to move themselves and their children to North Wales. He was still officially employed by the Post Office, but on ‘unpaid leave’ (to which his bosses had agreed under pressure from those authorities who had recommended him for attendance at the Liberal Studies college). His Local Authority grant, alone, would be barely enough to support his family, and their move to the North would mean his wife having to resign her teaching post in Swansea. But wanting to keep the family together (his time away at College had distressed the children in particular), they decided to take a chance on the possibility of her finding another teaching post in the university area. Fortunately, she did, so he spent the next three years studying whatever he could in avid preparation for presenting his thesis in what he had been told would be the ‘proper’ manner. This ‘proper’ presentation, however, was not to be. As a mature student with pre-formed ideas and an approach to discussion of the probing sort that got Socrates condemned, he was lucky to graduate as a second class Honours Bachelor of Arts.

Unfortunately, not only was this not good enough for him to go on to postgraduate Research, but also, at the age of thirty-eight, he was now too old for sponsorship. He had no recourse, therefore, but to return to his employment with the Post Office Engineering Department at Swansea. However, with the stigma of the ‘hit-and-run’ allegation still hanging over him, he could not resume his job of lineman. Moreover, the fact that he had gained his degree in Arts rather than in Science confounded his employers —signifying to them, as it did, that he was not interested in furthering his Post Office career in the accepted way.

Besides, he had been naïvely unaware of what a strange animal a ‘menial lineman’ with a Philosophy degree could be in the official pecking order of the Post Office, and it was only later that he would come to realise the sociological inevitability of what someone told him was virtually a ‘constructed dismissal’. (On these grounds, he eventually won a legal tussle with his employers over his superannuation rights.) So he finally parted company with the Post Office Engineering Department and returned to his family and to the North Wales university, where he took up a Post-graduate course in teaching, specialising in Adult Education.

He was still determined to present his thesis for examination, but now it would have to be presented externally, without the benefit of supervision. Prior to graduation he had written a preparatory dissertation, based on his studies of how Western Physics had begun. Physics, he learned, had its beginnings in an ancient dispute between the Greek monists (holists) of Elea and the pluralists (atomists) of Ionia. It appeared that this atomic theory had been adopted as a philosophical system by Epicurus (3rd century B.C.). It was then studied by the Roman poet Lucretius, in the last century B.C., who had encapsulated it in a poem, De rerum natura, which, during the Renaissance, had been imported to the West by travelling scholars. Thus it came to the notice of René Descartes (1596-1650), the French philosopher-scientist whose ideas were to become central to modern physics. He also learned that it was Descartes who, in the mechanistic tradition of Democritus, had arbitrarily split commonsense reality into the two distinct ‘realities’, named Mind and Matter [19]. It was on the basis, therefore, of the Cartesian philosophy that the modern sciences of Psychology and Physics, to the cost of common understanding, became and remained irreconcilably divided.

Undoubtedly, however, the biggest revelation he received as a result of these studies —though its relevance to his concerns about Physics was not at that time fully appreciated— was due to his reading of a short essay entitled ‘Refutation of Idealism’. This was written in 1903 by G. E. Moore [20], the Cambridge philosopher who is accredited with being the leader of the Commonsense revolt (in philosophical circles, at any rate), against Berkeleyan idealism. In that essay (for the ‘unnecessary complexity’ of which its author had felt he should apologise), Moore showed that Berkeley’s idealistic dictum ‘To be is to be perceived’ was pure nonsense. This demonstrated, in effect, that what Berkeley had done was to take on trust the artificial Cartesian (originally, Democritan) splitting of commonsense reality into the two different and entirely separate ‘realities’ and then cancelling the one (the ‘material’) to make the other (the ‘mental’) the one and only true reality. This was, of course, a catastrophic fallacy. The logic of it was as twisted as though Berkeley had decided that all humanity was split into ‘Irish’ and ‘non-Irish’, and then, by assuming that all but the Irish were non-entities, concluded that all of humanity was Irish. Through this essay of Moore’s, our erstwhile telephone lineman saw very clearly where he had gone astray in his advised following of Berkeley. It was now plain to him that Berkeley and his followers had merely compounded the earlier fallacy committed by Descartes. It was therefore a ‘double whammy’ of a fallacy, because there simply is no way in which our observations of physical objects can either be separated from those objects in the way Descartes had taught, or isolated from them in the ‘idealistic’ fashion of Berkeley, Hume and Kant. The ‘revelation’ he received

[19] Descartes arbitrarily divided the world into the two separate categories, Mind and Matter. This bequeathed to philosophers, thereafter, the so-called ‘Problem of Knowledge’ or the ‘Epistemological Problem’ of how the observer, in the first category (the mental), can have any assured knowledge of the existence of anything in the second (the material). Without Descartes’ highly questionable assumption that God created both mind and our sense-apparatuses which, because they are God-given, ‘cannot lie’, the problem becomes insoluble. Modern philosophers after Moore (see following) regard the Cartesian distinction as artificial and the problem as therefore non-existent. Descartes’ arbitrary splitting of ordinary commonsense knowledge into these two artificial categories is known as ‘Cartesian dualism’.

[20] G.E. Moore, ‘Refutation of Idealism’, 20th Century Philosophy, ed. Morris Weitz (Collier-Macmillan) London, (1966).

from Moore was that all the historical bickering over ‘objective versus subjective’, ‘real versus ideal’ and so on —between Physics and Philosophy, in effect— had scuppered academia. It had been a farce, a complete educational-administrational fiasco. In that one small passage in a short and under-rated essay, Moore had solved the main problem that had troubled our lineman for all those years.

That problem was, of course, the fundamental physics/philosophy problem of how things and our observations of them interrelate. Do physical objects exist in atomic realms which are forever inaccessible to direct observation, separated from us inexorably by the limitations of our sense organs and nerve-processes? Obviously not, since we are by no means compelled to observe things ourselves, through our own organic sensory equipment. We can also observe in the manner commonly called ‘objective’, by means of instruments which interact with things in all sorts of different ways. What we observe, therefore, are things as they appear not only to ourselves and our own observational instruments but also, corroboratively, to other observers and their instrument-systems and —which is more to the point— as those things manifest themselves to one another physically, as bits of matter. All of this, he now realised, disposed of ‘idealism’ (and, of course, ‘solipsism’) at a stroke.

It then struck him, forcibly, how unlikely it would have been for him to have received this revelation anywhere other than at university. And where else would that be but on an Arts course in Philosophy —for who would ever study, in sufficient depth, such an essay as Moore’s for pure entertainment; and what possible relevance could it be seen to possess for any Science course? It was plain to him that only in this unique and ‘unofficial’ way in which he had drifted upon them, could those academically severed aspects of nature, the ‘physical’ and the ‘Philosophical’, have been brought so fortunately together. He could scarcely believe his luck in having enjoyed that unique experience, which he could never have done if he had pursued his interests through the official channels. Hard on the heels of that feeling of euphoria came the sobering realisation that, due to the Educational apartheid that Descartes had initiated between the two disciplines, Moore’s philosophical refutation of idealism was virtually unknown in Physics circles. There, idealism persisted unchecked in the ‘Positivistic’ interpretation of quantum theory by Heisenberg and Bohr. In Physics, then, without the antidote supplied by Moore, in the disputes over ‘realism’ and ‘idealism’ (the issue of ‘EPR’), the virus of Cartesian dualism still ran rife. Compared to Moore’s logical refutation, the Draconian attempts of the reactionary ‘realists’ of physics to discredit their ‘idealist’ opponents were as philistine and ineffectual as Samuel Johnson’s stone-kicking attempt to refute Berkeley.

It was at this stage that he began to feel that what he had been seeking to solve was a gigantic jigsaw puzzle in which various pieces were now rapidly slotting into place. It was a portrait of Nature that was somehow, through his dedicated efforts, putting itself together right in front of his eyes. Thus far, he could clearly discern, as it were, an ‘arm’ here, a ‘leg’ there, and so on. There were still, of course, great gaps in between, where essential pieces were missing. Moore, he decided, had provided him with a whole ‘head and shoulders’. He next set out to find the ‘trunk’.

VII The Quantisation of Phenomena

The search for the remaining parts of this philosophical jigsaw puzzle fell, officially, so far as our lineman-turned-student's thesis was concerned, into the academically demarcated category of Physics, so that its appearance as an inseparable part of his Arts dissertation confounded its examiners. Prior to his reading of Moore's essay, he had been intrigued by the 'sense-datum' theory of the Vienna Circle philosophers [21]. For these philosophers, 'sense-data' were, quite literally, the data of sense—that is, as bits of human sensation conceived, in the idealist manner of Berkeley, as 'atomic ideas'. Now, due to the influence of Moore, he began to think of these sense data as the data of instrumentation, as known to experimental physicists. That is to say, for him, the ultimate data of scientific observation became the units of atomic interaction which physicists call quanta (i.e., units of Planck's action-constant h). In making this conceptual shift, he parted company with his tutors, who were astounded that any Arts-trained philosopher could think in this 'scientific' way.

These people had obviously failed to see that Moore's boringly written argument (which he had pored over for weeks in order to understand it, despite being warned not to spend too much time on it) was not just an exercise for students of Philosophy but should also have been seen as vitally important for the very fabric of physical science. What Moore was saying—although Moore would scarcely have stated it in these terms, or even been aware of it—was the same as what Einstein had been trying to convey (albeit somewhat ineffectually as Mach had judged). This was that physical reality is neither completely independent of observation nor completely dependent upon it. Materiality is neither entirely objective in the sense of Democritan realism (Remote Realism) nor entirely subjective in the sense of Berkeleyan idealism (Subjective Realism) but relative to observation, both direct and instrumental. What a 'pantomime', then, he thought, those centuries of academic separatism had seen! Descartes had chopped commonsense reality in two; and what Moore had done was simply to mend that split and restore to us the default state of commonsense as it had existed before its philosophical butchery at the hands of Descartes. This made it perfectly understandable why, in philosophical circles, Moore had been hailed as the 'champion of commonsense'. Would that it had been hailed so, he reflected, in circles of science!

So now he could see no excuse for the separatists of Physics and Philosophy failing to get their act together. This was when it occurred to him that since there seemed to be no administrative provision for this integration in modern Education there would, in the end, be no recourse for him but to appeal, over the heads of the scholars, to commonsense itself. In his thesis he sought to pursue this academically subversive theme.

VII.1 The spectroscopic combining of sense-data and instrument-data

In that thesis he explored some further consequences of this physicalising of the idealists' sense data. One of these consequences, he was delighted to discover, was that, as he might have expected from his theory, these 'sense-data' could be precisely identified with the action-packages which physicists call quanta. So here, he told himself, was another large 'trunk-piece' of the Jigsaw Puzzle. In both organic and instrumental observation, we are presented, not with what the Vienna circle 'Positivists' had fancied were 'atomic ideas' but—and no less directly—by light in little units of Planck's quantum h.

[21] The Vienna Circle was a group forming the organisational centre of logical positivism. It was developed from a study-group organised in 1922 by the Austrian philosopher and physicist, Moritz Schlick (1882-1936) at the Department of the Philosophy of the Inductive Sciences, Vienna University. Its allies included, for some time, L. Wittgenstein, K. Popper and A. J. Ayer.

So if he looked hard enough, he thought, he should find that these observational-instrumental 'sense-data' exhibited the distribution-characteristics of the quantised light spectrum. This, in fact, is what he did find [22]. It happened while he was doodling with some diagrams and formulae. In physics, he reflected, action is energy multiplied by time (that is, $a = mv^2t = et$). Since energy is a rate, then multiplying that rate by a time makes it a quantity, hence the name 'quantum' as applied to Max Planck's unit of action. Now the time he already had in his relativistic formula (see Ch. III), which is, once again,

$$t_{\mathrm{R}} = t_{\mathrm{P}} / \sqrt{(1 - v^2/c^2)} \tag{3}$$

So much, then, for the time. But where was the energy? Then he realised that $v^2$ in this formula is, dimensionally, the energy $e$ in above the expression for action, divided by mass. So, substituting that equivalent expression for $v^2$ in the formula, he obtained

$$t_{\mathrm{R}} = t_{\mathrm{P}} / \sqrt{(1 - e/mc^2)} \tag{6}$$

Rearranging this formula to express it in terms of $e$ finally produced

$$e = mc^2(1 - t_{\mathrm{P}}^2/t_{\mathrm{R}}^2) \tag{7}$$

The expression $e = mc^2$, here, looked particularly interesting since it was the same as Einstein's famous formula for interconverting mass and energy. What this equation (7) seemed to be saying, then, was that as the relativistic time-dilation of a body increases, so does its mass in the relation given by the formula. So, as the time dilation approaches infinity (i.e., as the fraction $t_{\mathrm{P}}^2/t_{\mathrm{R}}^2$ approaches zero) the energy $e$ approaches $mc^2$ as in standard relativity.

Could it be, then, he wondered, that atoms exist relatively to one another in this way as configurations of relativistic mass-energy and that light is the swapping, by those atoms, of bits of that relativistic mass-energy? It was a fairly commonsensical suggestion and therefore an idea worth exploring. For instance, the time $t_{\mathrm{P}}$ in (7) could easily be thought of as the period (call it $t_0$) of the smallest orbit of the electron in the standard schematic depiction of the simplest atom. That is the atom of hydrogen, with its one-proton nucleus and single planetary electron as envisaged by Niels Bohr [23]. The series $t_{\mathrm{R}}$ would then represent the periods of the larger orbits, in whole multiples $n$, of that smallest orbit (i.e., in ascending order $t_{\mathrm{R}} = nt_0$). Atoms might thus be conceived as little storehouses of relativistic mass-energy built up and released (absorbed and emitted) in discrete amounts according to the value of $n$ in the equation:

$$e = mc^2(1 - 1/n^2) \tag{8}$$

[22] The actual process of this discovery as expressed in the submitted thesis was quite tortuous. For the purpose of this book, the following account is much simplified and condensed.

[23] This is now referred to as the 'Old Quantum Theory' of the atom and of atomic interaction, which has been replaced by the 'wave-functional' theory of Heisenberg and Schrödinger. The 'Old Quantum Theory' is nevertheless valid enough, for the present stage in this demonstration.

To think in this way, of our observations of objects being direct transactions of bits of physical mass-energy between those objects and ourselves made much more sense, in commonsense terms, than thinking of them as constructions out of nothing more than the subjective 'atomic ideas' of the Vienna Circle idealists. Besides, this formula (8) reminded him of something he had seen somewhere before. And, sure enough, when he searched he found that it was virtually identical—in its form, at least—to the relation which people like Balmer and Rydberg had discovered in seeking to rationalise the lines in the spectrum of hydrogen [24]. In fact, as he later discovered, The Balmer Rydberg formula for all hydrogen-like spectra (that is, the spectra of atoms stripped of all but one of their electrons) can be derived far more directly and simply from the relativistic time-formula (3) than from the usual theories of electrodynamics (see 'Deriving Balmer from Einstein', Appendix 3.).

However, those physicists to whom, at the time, he showed this result dismissed it out of hand as 'coincidental'. As one of them expressed it, 'Why should this process give the spectrum of hydrogen rather than some other atom—or indeed, any spectrum at all?' To this, of course, the obvious answer was, why not? If it looks like a duck, walks like a duck and quacks like a duck, then perhaps it is a duck. At the very least, the possibility of its being a duck can scarcely be discounted! And that was all he was claiming, that perhaps—just perhaps—the usual electrodynamical description of the atom, with its traditional 'electric charges', 'fields' and so on was redundant. The possibility could not be discounted that it been no more than a useful theoretical 'flash-in-the-pan', and that there was some other way of describing the whole process of atomic interaction in these straightforward direct action terms which did not involve the customary conceptions of 'field-forces', 'electromagnetic propagation' and 'Einstein separability'. In his own philosophical pond, that 'duck' quacked naturally enough, and it was in that pond that he had invited commentators to view it, not as it floundered in their eclectic quagmire of 'electrodynamics'.

That 'pond', however, lay in Philosophical habitats whose denizens were the likes of Moore and Wittgenstein, being virtually out-of bounds for physicists. So it was understandable that other than in their philosophical context, these results had to be regarded as no more than coincidental because otherwise, the consequences for the existing 'electrodynamical' theory would be catastrophic. So it was not likely that the critics could be persuaded by an even greater 'coincidence'. This was that if the coefficient $mc^2$ in his formula was made equal to the full ionisation potential of the hydrogen atom, in mechanical units of joules [25], then these interactions with and between objects accorded not only formally but also quantitatively with the descriptions of atomic interactions as actually observed in spectroscopy. This account of the relation between objects themselves and our perceptions of them was therefore consistent not only with objective physics but also with phenomenalism, as propounded by Berkeley, Hume and Kant (with, of course, the anti-idealist correction supplied by Moore). It was also consistent with the general requirements of atomic theory concerning the way atoms interact. This was especially in view of the growing emphasis, in quantum physics, on direct action-at-a-distance as an essential requirement not only of the law of conservation of angular momentum but also of Newton's third law of instantly reciprocal action and reaction.

[24] The Balmer formula for ionisation energy, discovered simply by juggling numbers and matching them to the lines of the hydrogen spectrum was $v = R \left[ (1/2^2) - (1/n_1^2) \right]$, where $v$ signifies wave-number, $R$ is a wave-number constant called the Rydberg constant (see G. Herzberg, Atomic Spectra and Atomic Structure, Dover Publications, N.Y., 1944). This was for an intermediate series from level $n = 2$ as the fixed term of the series and $n_1$ as the running term from unity to infinity. For $n = 1$, the 'Balmer' formula becomes the even more basic Lyman series. In (8), with $n = 1$ and $R$ put equal to the empirical $mc^2/hc$ and then multiplying throughout by $c$ to express frequency, that formula and the Balmer formula become identical.

[25] This is $hcR = 2.1798 \times 10^{-18}$ J, where $hcR$ is the equivalent in joules of the ionisation energy in conventional volts, i.e., $I_0 = 13.595$ volts multiplied by the conventional 'electronic charge' $q$ in coulombs, where $q = 1.601 \times 10^{-19}$ C.

There was, however, something about this reasoning which worried him. It was that it held, so far, only for quantum transactions between bodies assumed to be relatively motionless. He was well aware, as the son of an amateur astronomer, that the spectral frequencies of moving bodies are Doppler shifted, and he could not quite see at the time how his irreducible quantum periods $nt_0$ could be affected by motion in that way. But despite many hours of racking his brains, he could not solve the problem. However, the results of his reasoning concerning this case of non-moving atoms seemed encouraging enough for him to set aside that technical problem for the time being, hopefully to be solved later (which he did, see Chapter XII, section 4) and to continue meantime with his more philosophical deliberations concerning Moore.

It seemed, then, that by purging the commonsense word 'observation' of the peculiarly idealistic meaning it had accrued in the two-and-a-half centuries of Philosophy since Berkeley, Moore had returned it to its original default meaning. And in so doing, he had made it tally with its meaning in contexts of modern Physics. It had also made those terminals of reductive analysis which the philosophers had conceived as 'sense-data' tally with what physicists call 'quanta'. In the ultimate analysis, therefore, our adventurer concluded, the subjective 'datum' of sense perception and the objective 'quantum' of instrumental perception were one and the same. In their approaches to nature, it seemed that modern philosophy and modern physics had, unbeknown to each other, been almost perfectly in accord.

VII.2 A paradigm switch from analog to digital in physical observation

It appeared, then, that another advantage which his relativism offered over Einstein's was in demonstrating how time and therefore frequency can be regarded as automatically quantised, so that the Balmer formula for the discrete energies in the light-spectrum becomes virtually a transform of the relativistic time-equation. Why had no one noticed this possibility? Why had quantum theory and relativity remained so notoriously at odds when this basic compatibility was plain for all to see? That was now, for him, a rhetorical question to which he already knew the answer. It was, of course, because of the separate false trails that had been followed by Physics and Philosophy for three whole centuries since Descartes had, albeit inadvertently, misdirected them.

The upshot of this discovery of the quantum discreteness of observational data was that he began to develop, at that stage, what he subsequently called his 'digital' approach to fundamental physics. This was as opposed to the traditional 'analog' or 'realist' approach. In this newly considered, digital approach (to be described more fully in due course), the hard and self-sufficient 'atoms' out of which, according to Democritus, everything is mechanically constructed, were replaced by events, these events being the similarly irreducible quanta of observational interaction which he had expressed in his formulae. In this way, the determinate-seeming 'mechanical causality' of processes on the ordinary macrophysical level gave way, on the ultimate microphysical level, to stochasticity (i.e., statistically directed probability).

Then he thought about 'language' as understood by the Linguistic philosophers such as Moore and Wittgenstein. To them, 'language' had meant ordinary human language in which the information conveyed is generally regarded as abstract and therefore unquantifiable. However, as he now began to see it, language is essentially information, and in modern information theory, information is as objectively quantifiable as any physical measure. This quantification is made in terms of probability, which is expressed as a number in the range of zero to plus one, where zero signifies that there is no chance whatsoever of the event occurring, and one indicates that the occurrence of the event is absolutely certain [26].

[26] Rosie, A.M., Information and Communication Theory, Van Nostrand, N.Y. (1973) p. 21.

He had already learned how probability applies in thermodynamics. He was aware, for instance, that the kinetic energy which molecules expend, against the face of a piston, say, in the form of heat and pressure obeys statistical laws, such as the law of entropy which ensures the irreversible downgrading of energy-availability. Entropy, he recalled, had been conceived by Boltzmann as the statistical probability of the number of those molecular events (as e.g., of steam molecules striking the piston) occurring at any one time. For Boltzmann, the statistical law which makes that pressure regular (Charles' law) was no more than an expedient way of dealing with what, if we were gods, we would see as the completely determinate motions of the molecules involved. Like the mathematician, Laplace, Boltzmann had imagined that a cosmical deity who knew precisely, at any instant, the positions and momenta of all the particles in the universe would be able to predict, mathematically, everything that is going to happen till the end of time. However, following Heisenberg's discovery of the impossibility, even in theory, of ever knowing in the same instance both the position and momentum of microphysical particles, the classical idea of those particles having well-defined tracks gave way to the idea that on those ultimate microphysical levels there are no such things as 'particles' in the old way of thinking, but only events. By the same token, the classical notion of mechanical determinacy had given way to statistical probability. In short, it had gradually become clear to physicists that these statistical, or probabilistic laws, far from being merely expedient and therefore 'subjective' ways of dealing with deterministic atomic causality, were completely sufficient in themselves for the objective explanation of causation. This meant that the emphasis had shifted, in physics, from atomistic reductionism towards holism. That is to say, it was no longer the atomic events that determined the law; it was the law that directed the events. So the need to postulate an underlying atomic determinacy, which people like Laplace and Boltzmann had felt, became redundant.

Such, at any rate, was what our researcher had come to understand. So now, in view of his own discovery of the basic discreteness, or quantisation, of the relativistic time-equation, his interest began to centre on this concept of holistically measurable probability. He had also learned that the law of entropy, as it applies in physics to probabilities such as those of molecular events in the cylinders of steam engines, applies in the same way to the information content of language [27]. For information theorists, following Claude Shannon [28], the entropy of a message or set of occurrences is the average information-content of that message or set. As he explained it to his wife, information is to communications theory as order is to thermodynamics. In thermodynamics, order is a concentration of, e.g., highly energetic particles which in itself would be statistically improbable, as, for instance, in a high-pressure steam cylinder compared to the outside atmosphere. So, in the levelling-out process which is entropy, as the piston moves and the cylinder expands, so the pressure inside it decreases towards atmospheric pressure and the distribution of the particles inside it becomes less improbable. In thermodynamical terms, as the entropy increases, in the way that statistically it must, so the cylinder expands and drives the engine.

As he continued to explain, the same applies to language. For instance, it would be highly improbable for a set of sounds of the sort which convey information in human or animal language to occur by pure chance in the wind or the sound of the sea. The maximum probability, in such cases, is the continual hiss called 'white noise', over which the cry of a gull provides clear information as to its presence. The background against which the information in human language occurs is, similarly, that of maximum probability, as in the sort of babble which characterises crowd-scenes in films. In a community where the level of day-to-day conversation may have settled to that of clichés and general bathos, news, as is well-known, 'travels fast'. Such pieces of information become quickly distributed,

[27] Ibid., p. 64.

[28] Shannon, C. E., 'A Mathematical Theory of Communication', Bell System Technical Journal, (1948), No. 27, p. 379.

spreading throughout the community like a stain in still water. When it is known all over, it is no longer news but 'yesterday's news' or, as someone might say, 'no longer news but history'. What makes news 'travel' in that way, he explained, is entropy, which drives social communication in the same way that in mechanics it drives an engine. And the same applies, of course, with signs and symbols, such as writings on sand, on cave walls or on otherwise blank sheets of paper. It was all, in the last analysis, he said, communication —hence, by definition, intelligence, whose undeniable presence in the scheme of things cannot be explained in terms of natures simpler than its own.

As he summarised it to her, his big 'Eureka!' experience had been to realise that in the order and information which characterise our contacts with physical things there is as much 'language', in the sense of information, as there is physical interaction. People communicate in the various forms of human language, spoken and written, and inanimate things communicate in physical language, which is a basic and universal 'language of nature' known to the Greek philosophers such as Heraclitus (see following) as logos. It was, he repeated, all communication, in which case the professional preoccupation of the Linguistic philosophers with 'language' was basically inseparable from that of the physicists with their physical interaction and experimental observation. What Moore, with his refutation of idealism, had done for philosophy and what de Broglie, Heisenberg and Schrödinger with their probability analysis had done for physics was, in effect, the same service. Had it not been for our bifurcated Educational system, he concluded, Moore and the quantum physicists might all have shaken hands. It was that coming together of physics and philosophy, in his own mind at least, that was his 'conceptual flipover'.

VII. 3 'Whether the pitcher strikes the rock or the rock the pitcher, it is bad for the pitcher'

It was at this stage in the development of his thesis that what he had learned about Psychology at university came into play. The part-completed shift from the old physics to the new [29] required a complete change of Gestalt, a total switch-over from one conceptual organisation to a completely different one (what Thomas Kuhn had called 'paradigms'). The confusion he had witnessed in his contacts with the establishment was now, he realised, a conflict of paradigms. It was a mismatch of Gestalten, like familiar 'impossible constructs' of the sort shown in Figure 2, or the buildings created by the artist, Escher (Figure 3), in which different parts of the building are seen inside out and upside-down [30]. Seeking to see the picture as a whole, in being forced to switch from the one view to the

[29] Pope, N. V. 'The Overdue Revolution', MENSA, April 1987, pp.28-29.

[30] See The Magic Mirror of M. C. Escher, Bruno Ernst, Tarquin Publications. Norfolk, England (1985).

other, the mind is bewildered. In the case of Escher’s pictures such confusions are entertaining. They are no more than jokes played on perception by the artist for purposes of amusement. However, in modern Education, the three-centuries-long ‘standing joke’ in which our perceptions of the world have been torn between the irreconcilable ‘Idealism’ of the Philosophers and the remote ‘Realism’ of the Physicists had been beyond a joke—in fact, a complete psycho-social disaster. That joke should have ended with relativism. That it had not was due to nothing more nor less than Einstein’s classically inhibited Escher-like rendering of relativity in terms of his unnecessary ‘absolute velocity in vacuo’ interpretation of the dimensional constant c. Remove that ‘velocity’ interpretation, his thesis urged, and all the bits in the jigsaw-portrait of nature fell properly into place.

Having completed his university teaching course, he and his family moved yet again, to a Midlands town where, at a Technical College, he had obtained a post as Lecturer in Liberal Studies and Adult Education. During this time, while continuing to work on his thesis, he became extremely—indeed, almost manically—busy. In addition to normal teaching he did work for the Open University as Counsellor and Study-Centre Coordinator. He marked Open University assignments and tutored in both Logic and Science History. He was also persuaded to stand as Candidate for the local Council. In 1972, the first version of his thesis, named ‘A Philosophical Re-Examination of Some Present Physical Concepts’ was submitted for examination to the University of Wales. With no supervisory feedback and with so many different rewrites that the argument became all but completely scrambled, it was badly presented and, predictably, failed. His frustration and anger at not being able to convey his philosophical insight to others sufficiently for them even to give a sensible opinion on it led him to seek for slogan-like encapsulations. These were of the sort he ineptly imagined might be presented for the judgement of an audience with a clapometer, or to anyone who might say, in the words of Omar Khayyám, ‘The secret, quick about it, Friend’. He was attempting the impossible. A resubmitted version of the thesis was so over-strenuously and over-defensively, not to say desperately, written as to be completely idiosyncratic. Thus, even more predictably, it also failed. He was heading, had he known it, for a nervous breakdown.

This breakdown occurred in 1976 with some uncharacteristically violent and aggressive, antisocial behaviour. It led to his voluntary admittance to a Psychiatric ward, where he was treated for an alcoholic condition exacerbated by the onset of diabetes. After three weeks of being thoroughly examined and ‘dried out’, he was discharged. Before leaving, he was called before a panel of medical students, presided over by the Professor of Psychiatry. ‘We have reached our diagnosis,’ said the professor, and then enquired, to the patient’s surprise: ‘What would you say if we said you are mad?’ The patient thought for a moment and then replied: ‘Well, at least it would be a logical reason for all that has happened.’ ‘Well,’ came the professor’s rejoinder, ‘I hate to disappoint you but you are as sane as any of us here. Unfortunately,’ he added, ‘to be sane in an insane society can be worse than being insane in a sane society. So,’ he said, rising and proffering a handshake, ‘now that you are rested and reassured that there is nothing mentally wrong with you, we shall just have to watch your career with interest.’ And with that, the dismissed patient returned to his family and his teaching duties in the Midlands.

VIII Discovery of the Cone Model

Back at work, in calmer mood, he ruminated objectively on the circumstances that had led from the Einstein letter in South Wales to his appearance as a patient in a psychiatric ward in the Midlands. These events had been so strange as to make him doubt his sanity, but now he had been told that there was no explanation of that sort. It was simply a case of, as the old saying had it, 'Whether the pitcher strikes the rock or the rock the pitcher, it is bad for the pitcher'. The fact that he had corresponded with Einstein was no delusion; the letter was there for anyone to see. The ideas that this letter had set off in his mind were both valid, and significantly different from Einstein's. That had been corroborated by the very best of mathematicians —the record of that was also available in his collection of correspondence. Nor had anyone discovered any logical flaw in his reasoning. The thesis remained what it had been from the start, a cogent proposal as to how the philosophical legacy that Einstein had contributed to the fund of natural philosophy might be continued and embellished after his death.

Yet those seeds that Einstein had planted and which had sprouted so rapidly in this telephone lineman's mind seemed incapable of germinating anywhere else. Despite all his efforts to stimulate and encourage their growth beyond the confines of his own head, those seeds might as well have been planted in reinforced concrete. 'Come in from the cold', he had been told. 'Present your ideas in the proper manner'. He had done all that, and now, after more than twenty years, he had failed abysmally in that respect. Fellow students who had entered university with no more feeling for philosophy than had Socrates' sophistic arch rival, Protagoras $^{[31]}$, had gone on to research and obtained their higher degrees with no trouble. In his own case, he heard that one of his tutors had written to someone else that the Department had 'had a hell of a time' with his thesis. Moreover, in their researches, as in their graduations, some of these fellow students had quite proudly stated to him that they had 'played the system' and, confessed that they'd gained no more of the true spirit of philosophy at the end of their course than they'd had at the start. 'Philosophy', for them, had been a 'soft option'. In that same cavalier spirit, with their Masters degrees and Doctorates in the subject they had gone on to do well in occupations like those of catering, accountancy, sales management, agriculture and so on. He was not complaining about that. They were all great friends, whose company he had much enjoyed, and he genuinely wished them the best of luck. But why, he wondered, as someone who actually did philosophy so dedicatedly, in the true spirit of the subject and for the very same reasons that the subject existed in the first place, had his ideas been so systematically spurned? It made no sense at all!

Or was it, he wondered, that those who had benefited from the subject had done so by accepting its provision without question, whereas he had sought, as the professionals might see it, to 'rewrite the constitution'. Using the conventions was one thing. Criticising them was something else!

But how, in our society, had things come to such a pass? He recalled how Descartes had artificially split commonsense into the dual academic disciplines of Physics and Philosophy, and how Moore, unbeknown to most people, had logically mended that split. Not appreciating what Moore had done, the exponents of these subjects were content to remain set in their artificially bifurcated ways. The 'hell of a time' which the Philosophy Department had had with his thesis was simply that there was no provision within the Educational system for dealing with it in the truly philosophical way which, by rights (puristically speaking), it should have been. The idea of appealing directly to commonsense for

$^{[31]}$ Protagoras was a Greek philosopher of the 5th century B.C. whose ideas were portrayed in Plato's dialogue 'The Sophist'. Protagoras' sophistic maxim was 'Such as appears for you is for you, and such as appears for me is for me'. See also The Sophists, W.K.C. Guthrie, C.U.P. (1971).

the restoration of its integrity after its virtual destruction at the hands of so many intellectuals began to burgeon in his mind.

Part of the problem that had led to his breakdown had been the fact that his ‘Socratic’ style of teaching, both in Philosophy and in Liberal Studies at this Midlands technical college, was not suited to the requirements of industry. What employers wanted from their employees, on day-release courses at the Technical College was, understandably, compliance and conformity. In his attempts to ‘liberalise’ his students in the truly philosophical way, he had been out on a limb. On more than one occasion, a parent, an employer, or even his colleagues, would complain that these workers were being given ‘ideas beyond their station’. As he conceived it, his job was to teach them to be free-range citizens, to provide for them the sort of mind-broadening experience he himself had enjoyed during those idyllic days at the North Wales Liberal Studies college. Indeed, it had been on the basis of that understanding, as he expressed it in his job-interview, that his employers had selected him for the post.

Afterwards, however, the emphasis in Education had gradually changed. More and more stress had been placed on ‘financial accountability’ for both the content of education and the use of educational resources. But the more the educational policies became centred on ‘training for industry’, the lower, it seemed to him, the standards of our democratic society were falling in comparison with those of the Golden Age of Greece. It was, he thought, as though in that illustrious era all the free philosophical dialogue had been diverted from teaching citizens towards teaching slaves how to obey their masters and to how to carry pails of water without spilling them.

In any event, the more the stress had fallen on ‘relevance to industry’ the more intolerable his job had become. It was his anger over some Draconian strictures of this cost-cutting kind, imposed by a new management at his college, that had sparked-off his ‘sociopathic’ episode. As he complained to his wife, ‘This is not why we left Swansea, for me to end up stuck in a classroom teaching people to use telephones and write job-reports!’ Knowing full well how true this was, she suggested to him that he might give up his lecturing job while she, in a teaching post she enjoyed at a local primary school, became the breadwinner. In any case, their combined income, with both of them teaching, had set them at the low end of the high income-tax bracket where they paid top rate for everything, including full fees for their three daughters now embarked on higher education. So after due discussion, they concurred that the best course would be for him to resign his lecturing post and pursue his research as he felt free. In this way there began a whole new phase of development of those ideas that had taken them from their home in Swansea to the situation in which they now found themselves.

This gave him his opportunity to put into effect his strengthening resolve to enlist the aid of commonsense in his struggle against what he saw as its own stagnation at the hands of the intellectuals. By a coincidence, this resolve was reinforced in yet another sojourn with the Educationally ‘unwashed’. After resigning from his teaching post, in order to qualify for unemployment benefit he had to make himself available for some kind of work. Having stipulated that he did not want anything academic, he eventually found himself working as a temporary general labourer and driver with a local council. The job involved tarmacking, unblocking sewers, grass-cutting, rubbish-collection, gullying and whatever else was required, and when he started he was warned not to let on to his fellow workmen that he was a college graduate. This he found amusing, considering his early background as a ‘menial telephone lineman’, which enabled him to fit very comfortably into this sort of company. Several of these men were, in the terms of that time, officially labelled ‘ESN’, meaning Educationally Sub-Normal’. However, it soon became plain that this was a travesty of a misnomer. These men were so well-versed in country ways (mostly in the spare-time occupation of poaching) that he found himself receiving from them an education in the ways of nature that was unmatched by anything he had studied at university.

One morning, while he and two others he was working with were enjoying a break, with his lorry, loaded with tarmac parked in a country lay-by, he was amazed to hear a dialogue which was about the best summary he had ever heard, of the process of philosophical thought from Berkeley and Hume to Moore and Wittgenstein. 'How do we know,' said the leading hand, 'that we didn't just dream that we were at the depot earlier on?' Then, after a long and thoughtful pause, he added: 'How can we prove that we're not dreaming that we go to different places? Perhaps everything's just a dream!' There was another, long contemplative pause, after which his workmate ventured: 'But how can it be a dream if we all dreamed the same thing?' The leading hand thought for a minute and then said: 'Maybe I'm dreaming you're saying that.' To this his mate replied: 'Well, if it's all a dream, then what does it matter, since that's all there is!' Another minute passed, while the three workmen drank their tea and stared out at the rolling Derbyshire countryside. The leading hand, an Educational reject, whose formative years had been spent mostly in prison, broke the silence. 'In that case,' he said, 'To even think that it's all a dream is just crap, because if there's nothing to compare it with, then how can it be a dream!' To this, his mate replied: 'So that takes us right back to where we started'. The leading hand slapped his thighs and rose to his feet. 'Yup!', he said, 'There's nothing for it but to just get on with it. Come on, you skiving bastards, that tarmac's going off!'

Our adventurer had listened in silence to this truly philosophical exchange, which would have been much the worse for being conducted college seminar. These 'ESN's had romped through the process by which philosophers like Moore and the later Wittgenstein had struggled to answer the idealism of Berkeley, reaffirming his belief that the ordinary man, left to think about it, was more than capable of understanding philosophical issues. Modern Education, he felt, had placed itself between these ordinary man and nature in the same way that the Church had put itself vicariously between man and the cosmos during the Middle Ages. He reflected on the fortunes of his own ideas that had been conceived and discussed in working situations such as these. Submitting them for academic approval and for publication in the standard journals was like submitting a thesis in support of Protestantism to the Church of Rome.

However, before resigning his lectureship at the Technical College, he had had an idea. He decided, that the only way of getting his ideas past the controls of the Establishment would be by becoming a publisher in his own right. With an almost Lutheran zeal, he therefore set out to print his articles of dissatisfaction and nail them, as it were, to Science's 'church-door'.

Whilst living in the Midlands he had remained in regular contact with two former colleagues from his days in the telephone industry. He had kept these friends informed of his growing dissatisfactions with 'Higher Education'. There was also another friend he had made at university who, as an adult student, had previously attended the same Liberal Studies college as he had. This friend, having attained a teaching post at another Midlands Technical College, had ended up with similar dissatisfactions with the industrialisation of Liberal Studies. It had been philosophical discussions with this man, whilst, as students, walking in the mountains of North Wales, that many of our ex-lineman's present ideas had been honed. He now discussed with these friends the feasibility of his proposed Commonsense enterprise. Highly enthused, they agreed to form a group. Together with his wife and their eldest daughter who was now a graduate in Philosophy and Psychology, it was decided, after consultation with a local accountant, to form a limited company. This, advised the accountant, was 'as a safeguard against financial comebacks' generated by what he clearly saw as the subversiveness of the enterprise. The company was duly formed and registered, in 1974 under the title of 'Phi Philosophical Enterprises'. Printing equipment was purchased, second-hand, and an office was set up on the top floor of his four-storey house overlooking the River Trent. There, some small books were produced and circulated, some privately and some in bookshops. A set of altogether six issues of a quarterly periodical was also produced (the seventh was compiled but not distributed). This periodical was registered in the copyright libraries under the title of 'Phi, the Philosophical Viewspaper' [32]. Some copies of these were sold but most were distributed, free of charge, to every university Philosophy department in the land.

In all of this, the appeal was to a commonsense realism of a sort which Moore and Austin [33] would surely have approved. To distinguish it from all the other 'realisms', such as 'Naïve Realism', 'Critical Realism', 'Remote Realism' and so on, the realism defined in the Viewspaper's Declaration of Intent was called Normal Realism. This title had been conceived during mountain walks with his erstwhile fellow student at university and the title caught-on with the group. Its intention was to encapsulate and re-establish the standards of ordinary, logical and critical commonsense. The criticality to which it appealed was Socratic and its logic was basically Aristotelian. The appellation 'Normal' was chosen so as partly to suggest 'normative', as opposed to the effete 'descriptivism' of so-called 'Philosophy of Science'. Mainly, however, it was used to signify the norms and standards of the everyday thinking person whose mind had not been funnelled by our Educational history. However, at this stage in that very same history, to find anyone not steeped in that same intellectuality, however informally, was as likely as finding a hay wain on a super-highway.
