So it soon became plain that far from being welcomed with open arms, this commonsense appeal, where it had not met with sheer apathy, met with mystification. So far as the academics were concerned, some idea of their reception of the Viewspaper may be gleaned from what happened when a lecturer from a prestigious West Country university visited the Midlands college and asked to meet our lineman-turned-lecturer. Feeling rather flattered that his fame should have reached so eminent an academic establishment, the ex-lineman made a jocular comment to that effect. The visitor's reply, no less jocular, was nevertheless distinctly unflattering. 'Well, actually,' he said, 'you're known in our Department as "the shit-stirring Welshman"!'

Plainly, 'Commonsense', as Moore might have understood it, had virtually vanished. The three-way split between Philosophy, Science and what was left of commonsense had become endemic. The ordinary person's view of the world had become so obscured by popularised and scarcely understood scientific jargon that it was, as he put it to his wife, 'like a view through the bottom of a stout bottle'. The Natural Philosophy of commonsense had been officially withdrawn and the totally confusing languages of 'light-velocity', and 'electrodynamics' had become common parlance. Regardless of how questionable these ideas might have been at their inception, they were now fixed and no longer negotiable. Cowed by the authority of established Science, ordinary people had been made to feel that they could no more question the conception of, say, a 'photon' or a 'force-field' than question the existence of a horse or a cow—or, as it might once have been, a winged angel.

Financially, also, the publishing enterprise was a disaster. It was under-funded, under-advertised and under-sold. When it folded, friends and acquaintances who had been entirely cynical about the whole operation—especially those who had never seen it as anything more than a strange financial venture—had a field-day. 'We told you so!' they said. They had not seen that, like the note nailed to the church door, even though it had ended up blowing in the wind, for the short while that it lasted the enterprise had at least succeeded in registering its essential 'message to humanity'. The copies of the Viewspaper that had been distributed to the universities (some had even been sent to universities abroad) had not all, presumably, been trashed. Besides, the essence of that Normal Realist approach—at least to the elementary level of development it had reached at the time—was now preserved in the copyright

[32] These publications were Phi, The Philosophical Viewspaper' nos. 1-7, ISSN 0305-5833, published quarterly throughout 1974 and 1975; Philosophy, 1974 (ISBN 0-9503790-0-X) and 2nd ed. 1975 (ISBN 0-9503790-1-8); Relativising Relativity, 1981, (ISBN 0-9503790-3-4), all by Phi Philosophical Enterprises Ltd. (Burton-on-Trent, UK.)

[33] J. L. Austin was another philosopher, after Moore, whose book Sense and Sensibilia, ed. G. J. Warnock, O.U.P. (1962), had been most influential in shaping these commonsense ideas.

libraries. Moreover, prior to the setting-up of Phi, there had been produced a set of small hand-bound volumes consisting of reprographed copies of correspondence, starting with the letter from Einstein. Copies were sent some of the correspondents, among whom was the eminent philosopher, Gilbert Ryle, now retired from his official teaching duties and from his editorship of the well-known Philosophical journal, Mind. Ryle responded with an angry letter saying that his permission should have been sought before publication of his letters, but a suitably apologetic reply from the offender mollified the professor sufficiently for a further, more friendly dialogue which lasted till the professor’s death in 1975. After his death, Ryle’s sister, finding our adventurer’s address among the professor’s papers, invited him to Ryle’s Memorial Service at Oxford. With his wife, he attended the service, at Kings College, where they sat with the great and good of the academic community. As they were leaving the chapel, they bumped into one of our ex-lineman’s former university tutors who had been a student of Ryle’s. The tutor was evidently taken aback to see his problem student among so many eminent people and curiously asked him what he was doing there. The ex-student’s wife took great delight in explaining that it was at the personal invitation of the late professor’s sister.

Another volume was sent to Karl Popper. This started a lengthy correspondence, beginning with Sir Karl’s expressions of dismay at the account given in the volume of the perfunctory way in which our lineman’s attempt to visit him had been sidelined in London by a junior lecturer (see Ch. V) who had not even informed Popper that he had a visitor. It also led to an invitation to Sir Karl’s seventy-fifth birthday party at the Austrian Institute in London. Sir Karl had not been able to attend this celebration because his wife was ill, so he later made good the lost opportunity of a meeting by inviting our ex-lineman and his wife to his home in Buckinghamshire, where they enjoyed tea and a most interesting discussion on the terrace with Sir Karl and Lady Popper. The little volume that Popper had read, and on the basis of which, on the terrace, he declared our lineman to be a ‘true philosopher’ (whilst warning him of the troubles this would bring upon him), was called ‘Philosophical Glimpses’.

Responses to those little volumes were many and various. In one instance, when one of them was shown by the ex-lineman’s brother-in-law to a friend whom he invited to comment on it, that friend had refused to comment because, as he said, ‘I don’t want to be included in your brother-in-law’s “Famous Academics I Have Been Rude To” book’. The fact was, however, that all these, mostly rejective, responses helped to swell the fund of correspondence following the circulation of that book. In this way it led, eventually to one of the Phi directors suggesting that all the papers and related previous and subsequent correspondence should be kept in files under that same title of ‘Philosophical Glimpses’ $^{[34]}$. So the failure was not complete. If nothing more ever happened to it, Normal Realism had not disappeared on the wind. It had been planted, for posterity to make of it whatever it might.

Meanwhile, under the auspices of Phi, seminars were arranged which, among other things, helped to develop the neo-relativistic ideas further. The large old house overlooking the Trent was ideal for this purpose and had been purchased with something like this in mind. These conferences, though not large, were attended by people from all walks of life, including academic physicists, mathematicians and astronomers, both national and international. Some contact was also maintained with MENSA through the editor of that organisation’s Space/Physics Newsletter $^{[35]}$ who was for some time a regular attender at the conferences. Some of the ideas arising from these discussions were published in that Newsletter. As a result, an article entitled ‘The Overdue Revolution’ appeared in the main MENSA

$^{[34]}$ Ibid.: Phil. Glim. (see FOREWORD, Fn. 1)

$^{[35]}$ Spacesignl, the Newsletter of SPACESIG, a Special Interest Group within British MENSA Ltd., Ed. C. Wagstaff, Vols. 24-45.

journal$^{[36]}$. Explaining the nub of the Normal Realist proposal of a shift from ‘analog’ to ‘digital’ as an approach to physics, it brought the most encouraging responses up till that time.

So, in a sense, the Phi enterprise had done its job. It secured contacts and helped to forge new ideas. It was wound up eventually, due, not only to financial difficulties but also (and mainly) to a philosophical dispute which split the enterprise into two factions of thought, reflecting once again the traditional Arts/ Science dichotomy in Education. The ‘equations’ and all the talk about ‘physics’ was not to the liking of the Arts faction of Phi any more than the discussions about Psychology and Religion were to the Science faction, especially to the professionals of the latter discipline. Moreover, discussions about ‘solipsism’ brought to light the fact that not everyone saw this centralising of the self as something to be avoided (see Ch. IV). Some actually felt that it should be embraced! As it transpired, there were some in the group who actually welcomed it, to the extent of seeking to make it a system. In short, it turned out that the unique importance that solipsism accords to the self was as satisfying to some as it was scary to others. In the group there were solipsists of both kinds. Some saw it, as one of them put it, as a ‘lobster pot’ from which, once inside, there was no way out. What Moore had done, it was argued, had all been done from inside the pot, so that there was no true escape by that fancied route. (This argument much resembled Wittgenstein’s ‘fly in the bottle’ argument, according to which, instead of buzzing about trying to get out, the fly should settle into accepting as inevitable that the bottle was world and that there was no ‘outside’ that anyone could sensibly speak of.) So, as this argument ran, instead of seeing oneself as just a tiny part of a gigantic outside universe, one should see the universe (as known to commonsense and science) as something existing inside Oneself and One’s ideas of it, making Oneself, in effect, the God of that universe. On that sophistic, not to say narcissistic, theme a university thesis was later presented and its presenter promptly awarded a Master’s degree. Meanwhile, another member of the group, failing to appreciate the difference between Normal Realism and solipsism became psychologically stressed by the enterprise to the extent that he declared the intentions of its founder to be evil and a threat to the sanity of ordinary people. So this person, who had originally been one of those on the inside of the Phi circle ‘pissing out’, now became someone on the outside ‘pissing in’ —as keenly in the latter mode as in the former.

To reconcile these various factions within the group therefore became impossible; so like the original ill-fated thesis, this ‘commonsense’ enterprise fell, ironically, into the very same Arts/Science gulf that the Company had been formed to bridge. The gulf was simply too huge to be spanned by such a perfunctory effort. He felt as though he had persuaded these friends of his to try to jump the Grand Canyon and had watched them dashed on the rocks below.

So although a rational solution to the Educational split was there for the taking, for sociological reasons it could not be even considered. Just how psychologically fraught were issues of supposedly objective reason in these circumstances he had discovered in two particular teaching instances. One was with a class of trainee welders, on day release from industry. For these he had prepared a particularly graphic demonstration of how great are the distances between the planets of our solar system compared to their size, and how much larger again, are the distances between the stars and galaxies. He realised from some unruly behaviour breaking out that the lesson was not going down particularly well, so he stopped at that point and confronted the students. Some direct and probing questions he put to them eventually elicited a response which surprised him. One of the more forthcoming students admitted, when pressed, that ‘all that stuff about how small we are compared to the size of the universe’ was frightening. ‘The last thing I want to think about,’ he said, ‘when I’m out with my girl at night, is what tiny insignificant bugs we are on these little bits of rock where our lives are so short and we are likely to be destroyed at any minute.’ Plainly, what these young people needed to learn in our Industrial society, was how best to cope with life at the level of occupation and

$^{[36]}$ MENSA, op. cit.

procreation. As for all that ‘size of the universe stuff’, no matter how true it might be, they simply didn’t want to know.

This confession on the part of these students started what was for him one of the most interesting and forthright discussions he had ever experienced. In this case it was he, the teacher, who did the learning and the students the teaching; and what they taught him was more than he had ever learned at university on the subject of psychological attitudes towards things of pure reason. He had, he realised, committed the ‘rationalist fallacy’ of supposing that society and its Educational institutions are, or even that they should be, based on reason.

The second instance of this kind was in an adult class in Philosophy. ‘Can you give us something for homework,’ asked one student, ‘so as to prepare for next week’s discussion?’ In response he set them the following question:

You are confronted by a terrorist with a gun. He gives you four sheets of writing paper on which to convince him that you are not just a figment of his imagination with which to amuse himself by blowing your head off. What do you write?

The next week’s class was decimated. So few turned up, from then on that the class became no longer viable. He was completely mystified until, some months later, he learned (from one of the absentees, whom he met in a pub) that the exercise had literally terrified them. One woman, for instance, had had to seek treatment for depression, and some of the men had felt they couldn’t turn up for work. The few who survived the exercise had done so by waxing jocular—as, for example, in the case of one of them who replied to the ‘terrorist’: ‘If I’m a figment of your imagination then you can safely give me that gun.’ Such people simply could not understand what had ailed the others. Indeed, when told about it later, they could not believe it. What was surprising, though, was that as a group, the ‘survivors’ in the class were those who had joined in order simply ‘to learn something about Philosophy’ without being the least bit interested in the subject in its truly subversive, Socratic sense.

These hard lessons in practical sociology were, he felt, invaluable. For who, knowing full well what he was doing would ever contemplate taking on such a philosophical task as he had for a living? And who would ever knowingly employ someone in that philosophical capacity? Indeed, who, knowingly, would ever want to do Philosophy at all? Was it therefore really surprising that he had now become officially unemployed—in fact, unemployable—a ‘kept man’, with no income of his own, having to ‘sign on’ every week at the Employment Office. He began to understand what Popper had been trying to tell him in that discussion on the terrace. It was something he came more and more to understand as time went on.

He could therefore well understand what had happened to the Phi Group. A search after truth for its own sake had nothing going for it socially. So far as any politically ‘streetwise’ person was concerned, only a complete fool would make that sort of philosophy his aim. What Phi had signally failed to do, of course, was to instil into such a commonsense enterprise anything like the confidence that would be necessary for it to compete with those whose caps and gowns and other badges of office signal their assurance of social acceptance and conformity. Like disturbing a beehive, to stamp around without that helmet of social assurance, making the sort of disturbance Phi was making, inevitably invited stings. To suffer such stings in the cause of Philosophy obviously required far more dedication than most people were prepared to give—or, at any rate, sustain. So, quite understandably, irrespective of how enthused they might have been at the start, few of the Phi group were prepared to dedicate themselves to the extent that was required for the enterprise to have any chance of success in the direction intended.

This realisation gave him an uncomfortable feeling that he had failed to provide, for these friends of his, any truly convincing way of avoiding solipsism. He himself had been convinced that Moore’s restoration of commonsense had made possible a logical unification of Philosophy and Physics in which that kind of subjective idealism had no place; but he had failed to convince his friends—and, needless to say, the Physicists—of this synthesis of Moore’s.

For himself, however, the interest continued apace, so he pushed that uncomfortable feeling into the background. Meanwhile, with some mathematical help from a former teaching colleague (another attendee at the Phi conferences) our ex-telephone lineman developed what was to become his ‘Cone Model’ representation of relativity. This was a more sophisticated development of those simple diagrams he had originally presented to Davidson and the young Ph.D. at Swansea. In one of the Phi publications$^{[37]}$ this was presented to a member of the Mathematics Department of a local university. Intrigued with the model, which, at the time was still somewhat naïve, one of the younger lecturers invited him to visit the university and there suggested collaborating on a paper for publication in a suitable journal. This was the beginning of a long and fruitful partnership. Through this new, well-qualified partner, our ex-lineman gained academic respectability. This enabled him to attend conferences at which his simplified pictorial version of relativity could be presented and politely received—even approved—without necessarily having to be understood, far less in anything like its full philosophical significance.

In one such conference, at Strasbourg, in 1986$^{[38]}$, he presented a highly graphic line-drawing depiction of his (then) rudimentary cone model. This caught the attention of a French professor of Physics, at the Université Louis Pasteur, with whom he remained in contact and who later showed him a hologram of the cone model which he used as a graphic ‘hands-on’ method of teaching his students relativity. This was, once again, in the philosophically uncommitted way that Bondi had done.

$^{[37]}$ The Phenomenon of Time-Dilation, Phi Philosophical Enterprises, 1983.

$^{[38]}$ EASST (European Association for the Study of Science and Technology) Conference, Palais de l’Europe, Strasbourg, October, 1986.

IX The Cone Model

In its essence, the cone model is as follows. As he needed to emphasise to all concerned, this cone-model was not to be confused with the 'light cone' of Eddington which, in its conception, was as different from his as chalk from cheese. Nor was it to be confused with his other model, the 'cinematic' or 'movie' model, which came later (Chapter XIII section 1). The jobs for which these models were intended, although logically complementary, were very different. For instance, the cone-model was purely mathematical, while the cinematic model was purely intuitive.

He could not quite remember when it started, but gradually he came to realise that through his own slight but significant modification of relativity, the classical 'Newtonian' graph of motion (see Figure 4) and an 'Einsteinian' graph of the same motion (see Figure 5) were not different graphs but different-dimensional or perspectival aspects of the same graph. Indeed, when the axes of those graphs were drawn to the same scale of dimensions, $c$, they became, like his original diagrams, scale-drawings of something that could no longer be called a 'graph' in the usual sense. What they became were more like the architectural end- and side-elevations of a three-dimensional construct, which turned out to be the cone shown in Figure 6.

The 'Newtonian' and 'Einsteinian' Aspects of Relative Motion

In Fig. 4 we see a series of regularly increasing velocities plotted as the distance-times travelled by bodies in some standard time $t_{\mathrm{P}}$ registered by those bodies themselves as viewed in the telescope of the observer. In Fig. 5 we see those same motions as the same distance-times travelled by the bodies in the relative time $t_{\mathrm{R}}$ of the observatory (i.e., the observed time of the motion minus the distance-time $s/c$). In the first figure, the limit of the velocities $s/t$ is infinity; in the second, the limit of the velocities $s/t_{\mathrm{R}}$ of those same motions is the 'finite speed' $c$.

The Pythagorean Space-Time Cone

A mathematical model demonstrating the integration of the two aspects of motion shown in Figs. 4 and 5. The 'Newtonian', or infinite-limit representation is the front view of the cone shown in Fig. 4; the 'Einsteinian', or hyperbolic, finite-limit representation is the side-view of the cone shown in Fig. 5. (The velocities which are here projected all in the same direction might, of course, have been projected in different directions, whence they could lie anywhere in the cone's surface.)

Figure 6

In his unusual Maths-Philosophy association with his new-found partner and collaborator the development of this cone-model representation of relativity became an official, ongoing research project at the university. Its first publication was in a journal dedicated to the teaching of Mathematics and Science [39]. So perhaps it was not altogether surprising that although the fact was stressed that this manner of deducing relativity did not involve Einstein's axiom of 'the speed of light in vacuo', the Citations Index described it simply as a 'modelling approach' to relativity, as though it had been presented as no more than some peculiar kind of teaching-aid. Its relevance to the issue of EPR (see Chapter VI) was far from having been automatically appreciated. It should have been plain, from this demonstration, that the two perspectives of time and motion, the classical and the relativistic, were combined a priori, so that the controversy between Einstein and Bohr was unnecessary. The model showed very clearly and mathematically precisely that quantum instantaneity and the 'finite speed c' are no more than different aspects of the same light interaction. But it seemed that the reviewers knew no more what to do with that demonstration than, as he put it to his partner, 'a cow with a musket'. Or, he wondered, was it that the philosophical implications of the model were all too plain and that the prospect of following them through to their logical conclusion was too disturbing to contemplate?

In any event, as with Bondi, Davidson and the French Physics professor, plus a growing list of others, while the practical advantages of this simpler and more conceptually economical approach to relativity were accepted, its philosophical significance was completely ignored. Seen purely as a teaching-aid, taking no account whatever of its philosophical connection, this 'modelling' approach to relativity was no more than a novelty. Einstein's approach had been a complete fait accompli. So as far as this new approach was concerned, it was as though, after its completion in 1994, someone were to suggest a simpler and more efficient way of digging the Channel Tunnel. And one might as well have suggested flying aeroplanes through that Tunnel as talk about the cone-model's philosophical advantages.

However, it had by now become clear that this lack of philosophical response to the published 'New Approach to Special Relativity' could not be taken as indicative of its being regarded as innocuous or trivial. For instance, at about this time, he became a member of the British Society for

[39] Pope, N. V. and Osborne, A.D., International Journal of Mathematical Education in Science and Technology, Vol. 18, No. 2 (March-April 1987), pp. 191-198.

the Philosophy of Science (BSPS) in the belief that this, surely, was the place in which to present his ideas on relativism. What a disappointment this proved to be! On one occasion, after attending several of the monthly meetings in London, he dared to announce the existence of Phi and offered a set of handouts for members to read. This resulted in the biggest snub he had ever received. He remembered the respect with which he had been treated among the men in his temporary labouring job. When this had come to an end his workmates had touchingly plied him with gifts of tools, albeit purloined from the works-depot stores; and one of them, concerned that he would now be ‘unemployed’, offered to recommend him for a job of ‘bouncer’ at a local night-club. ‘And if you get blood on your shirt,’ he was told persuasively, ‘they give you a new shirt!’

So comparatively demeaning, however, was his reception in this different milieu that another member, a fellow Welshman, after the meeting invited him to a pub to apologise for this boorish behaviour on the part of his fellow BSPS members. ‘At least,’ remarked his companion, ‘it showed that they had heard of you!’ And indeed, he reflected, how else could it have been, since there was no way in which they could have responded in that manner without some preconception as to what those handouts were about.

So it was prejudice by definition. Nor was there any more success when, later, this ‘upstart’ and his Establishment colleague submitted a paper to the Society’s journal. It was rejected with what his colleague described as a ‘most dreadful’ review. Despite this, he continued his membership for many more years but never again attended the meetings. That society, as he was told by a high-ranking former member, far from being a forum for free philosophical discussion, had become merely a showcase for professional proficiency in Philosophy of Science, an extension of the academic examination system in effect. From the copies of the Viewspaper which had been circulated, in which its philosophical intentions had been made plain, his appearing in their midst must have seemed, as he reported it to his family, ‘like finding something green in the bottom of a milk bottle’.

It was true that neither his accent nor physical appearance fitted the stereotype of an academic. ‘He dunna look like a philosopher!’ someone had remarked to his son-in law. There had been the time when he and his wife had been holidaying in a French wine-growing area. It was just after he had finished his stint of labouring for the local council. When they boarded the ferry, while his wife was directed toward the first class cabins, she was amused to find that her husband had been directed to the lorry-drivers’ saloon. A few days later, while out walking they got into conversation (at least his wife did) with a charming lady who invited them into her house for a glass of wine. Her husband was a vintner, she explained, and the wine they kept for themselves tasted far better than what they exported to Britain. Our odysseyist was unable to take part in the conversation, as his knowledge of French was very limited. He gleaned, however, that his wife had told their hostess that her husband was a teacher of philosophy. Soon they were joined by the vintner himself, and as the men shook hands it became obvious from the rasping sound of their palms that they were both, as their hostess commented, ‘laboureurs manuels’.

Something about the ensuing conversation made our odysseyist feel uncomfortable. This was especially when, more than a little tipsy from the wine, they were taking their leave. Their hostess nudged his wife and said, with a knowing grin, ‘L’amour, beaucoup d’amour, n’est pas?’ Some time later, after walking along in silence, he suddenly turned to his wife and said ‘Strewth! You know what she thinks! She doesn’t believe that I’m your ‘professeur de philosophe’. She thinks you’ve left him at home and picked me up as a “bit of rough”!’

This ‘manual worker’ image was to persist, even when the state of his hands returned to that of a writer, and it caused problems on more than one occasion. He and his wife had been long resigned to the fact that no-one would ever assume from his appearance or demeanour that he was an academic. ‘Never mind,’ he told her, ‘Socrates was a stone-mason, not an ascetic. And I reckon that if he’d been on that gangway with me, they’d have sent us both to the lorry-driver’s saloon!’

CONSOLIDATION

Some further implications for physics of the commonsense relativistic synthesis are discussed. A new conception is suggested as to how the conventionally disparate 'field-forces' traditionally held responsible for the 'gravitational', 'electric' and 'magnetic' motions of bodies may be logically unified.

X The Relativistic Recovery of Physical Substance

Following the 1987 publication of the cone model there were other publications of a similarly peripheral kind. There were two 'Noddy's guides' to both relativity and quantum theory in Schools Science Review [40]. These generated responses good and bad. Some were to the effect that, as one physics teacher put it, it was the first time that he had really understood relativity. Another wrote that it had been understood not only by his sixth form (pre-university) students but also, with his help in going through the equations, by his fourth form (fifteen-year-old) pupils. Other responses were of a nit-picking variety criticising the presentation of the argument with no thought whatever as to its possible significance, as if what had been presented was no more than a move in some competitive intellectual game of 'Trivial Pursuit'.

By now, our adventurer's wife had taken early retirement from her full-time teaching post and was working only part-time. This enabled her to act as her husband's research assistant. Whenever he spent the day working with his colleague at the university, she would go to the library and check through the journals. On one of these visits, she came across a call for papers for a conference at Imperial College, London, on 'Physical Interpretations of Relativity Theory'. Although the closing date for submissions had officially passed, she realised that this conference was virtually tailor-made for her husband. A late application was made to the organiser, which was accepted, and our ex-lineman duly appeared at the Conference where he read a paper entitled 'Instantaneous Relativistic Action-at-a-Distance'. In its anecdotal style this was well received by the audience and was published in the Proceedings of the conference [41]. One of those attending this talk was a well-known professor of Physics from SLAC (the Stanford Linear Accelerator Centre, California), who recommended the paper 'for wider publication', as he said, in a suitably modified style, in an issue of Physics Essays [42]. This same professor also invited him, on the strength of his 'discretist' approach to relativity, to join the Alternative Natural Philosophy Association (ANPA), based in Cambridge in the UK and in California, USA. This offer was accepted, so he became a member of that association, albeit mainly in the capacity of a critic of the prevailing view [43] —a Welsh 'Merlin', in the opinion of one member, and 'Curmudgeon to ANPA' according to another. His running battle with the founder members of the group was that although, like them, he was a discretist (which was why he had been enlisted), their discretism was essentially reductionist. It was a 'bottom-up' approach in which, as a friend of his nicely put it, 'in the beginning were the numbers'. These numbers were undefined, being pure numbers or binary digits, and everything was supposed to be constructed out of these, by automatic combinations in pure number theory. Beginning with dots, lines, triangles, squares, etc. and ending up (in intention at least) with space and whatever it contains on the macrophysical and even cosmical level, this method reminded him of nothing so much as that of the ancient Pythagoreans. His discretism, by contrast, was of a holistic, or 'top-down' kind, starting with what there is on the ordinary macrophysical level (i.e.,

[40] 'Relativity is Kids' Stuff', Schools Science Review, Vol. 70 (1989), No. 253, pp. 86-87 and 'The Quantum is Kids' Stuff', ibid. Vol. 72 (1990), No. 258, p. 161.

[41] Proceedings of Physical Interpretations of Relativity Theory (PIRT), London, September, 1990, pp. 460-488.

[42] 'Instantaneous Relativistic Action at a Distance', Physics Essays, op. cit. pp. 409-421.

[43] Namely, that of the 'Combinatorial Hierarchies' (CH), see Bastin, T. and Kilmister, C.W., Combinatorial Physics, World Scientific, Singapore, (1995).

empirically), and then 'drilling-down' to the ultimate or terminal irreducibles which, for him, were the action quanta $h$ and the angular momentum quanta $h$.

To return, however, to his well-received paper on 'Instantaneous Relativistic Action-at-a-Distance', some comments made by the publisher's reviewers on the first draft of that paper [44] brought to the surface some uncomfortable feelings he had harboured about his work for some time. He now realised that something had been amiss all along. It was that he had made it seem as though he had been arguing that light was everything, as though all the objective substance of the world was contained in the light; that in light were the masses of objects, the space between them, their physical characteristics, qualities, properties, etc., making it seem that the whole universe was nowhere but in the light. Indeed, he had published an article more or less to that effect, entitled 'The New World Synthesis' [45]. In that article he had argued that light-quanta were little pixel-like events on our observational 'video-screens', out of which we informationally project the world and all it contains. True, it had answered the Epistemological Problem (q.v.) by removing the 'Einstein separation' between objects and our observations of them, and between objects themselves in the way Mach would have wanted. Nevertheless, this begged the question of what Bohm [46] had referred to as the 'implicate' structure underlying the 'explicate' one of the received pixel-events. Without this question being addressed, he now realised, those 'light-pixels' offered little advance on the abortive 'sense-data' of Logical Positivism. Perhaps, he reflected, this was what had made his thesis seem so unacceptable. The trouble was that the 'video-world', projected entirely in terms of quantum light-pixels, could be taken as purely phenomenal, that is to say, phantasmagorical, as in the idealistic theories of Berkeley, Hume and Kant. What was missing, therefore, he decided, in order to 'clinch the deal' with Moore was the 'something' underlying the visual appearances, without which those appearances remain unsupported. It was not that his commonsense synthesis, following Moore, had been wrong; it was just that it had not been sufficient. To the extent he had pursued it so far, it still lacked the essential substantial ingredient. To avoid making a new kind of 'idealism' of commonsense, the relative appearances obviously had to be of something other than just themselves.

This did not mean having to revert to the problematic Remote Realism' entailed by Einstein's 'object-observer separability' interpretation of light nor the 'electrodynamic field-conduction' theory of Faraday and Maxwell. Far less did it mean adopting the wave-functional view of the Standard-Model quantum theory with its underlying uncollapsed wave-function—which, from any commonsense point of view (he felt at that time), was just pure nonsense. Such theories he regarded as being all very well as tools of the trade for the purely ad hoc purposes of practical physics, just so long as they were not taken as serious philosophical statements about the real world. Many true advances in practical physics had undoubtedly been secured by means of such theories and their purely practical spin-offs had obviously been of great benefit to mankind. In that sense, scientific theories were like designs for motor cars. If they worked, then that was good for everyone. But to give these theories the wider application of philosophical theories about nature was as absurd as for motor-manufacturers to claim, on the basis of the success of their designs, that nature was, in itself, 'Rolls-Royce', 'Mercedes', 'Mitsubishi', or whatever.

The fact, then, that there were people so committed to these special theories that they could not see them for what they were—that is, nothing more than working hypotheses for the purely expedient daily purposes of physics—amazed him. He could well understand the need for such working theories

[44] The most telling comment was that 'Pope and Osborne are actually proposing an ontology'. Reviewer's remarks on 'Instantaneous Relativistic Action at a Distance', letter dated Sept. 9, 1991, Philosophical Glimpses, op. cit. p. 6168.

[45] Pope, N. V., 'The New World Synthesis', Philosophia Mathematica (II), Vol.4, No.1, pp. 23-28.

[46] Bohm, D., Wholeness and the Implicate Order, Routledge and Kegan Paul, London, (1980).

and for science students to develop an intellectual ability to switch from one well-established theory to another in pursuit of their special subject, without being hung-up on any particular one of them. That could not be faulted. The trouble was that if these working theories were taken as statements about natural truth and reality, then the same facility of switching from one established theory to another in describing nature made it seem as though nature itself had no overall logic to it, which encouraged sheer sophistry. 'Truth' and 'reality', one such sophist once told him, were 'words which Educated people never use'. But that was just silly. When another sophist said to him, 'There's no such thing as truth, only opinion', he answered in truly Socratic style: 'Is that the truth, or only your opinion?' And that, typically, was where the conversation ended.

Still less could he understand the view which other science people seemed to have inculcated, that 'truth', in science, is whatever your Professor had taught and that to question it is, at best, a sign of social naïveté or, at worst, insanity. And least of all could he understand the view that whatever one chose to regard as 'truth' in science is purely personal, not to say sacrosanct, and that for one theoretician to refute another is as improper as attacking his religion or insulting his wife.

That there have to be theories, however, he now felt, was something which Moore, a typical Arts scholar, had not taken fully into account. What Moore had neglected to consider, it seemed—or, at least, had failed sufficiently to emphasise—was just how much more extensive and complex the world is, especially to the scientific mind, than can ever be comprehended at any one time by the sum-total of one's own and other people's knowledge of it. So no one can ever justly claim that commonsense is, as someone had put it, an oracle of truth and reality. Commonsense evolves, as much around successful scientific concepts as around those rustic conceptions of trees, churches and barns cited by the Linguistic philosophers. These philosophers could no more claim special dispensation for their Oxbridge-conceived 'commonsense' standards than could the lady who said, 'Why travel in planes and not in trains as God intended?'

True, Moore had done commonsense a great service in disposing of Berkeley's idealistic dictum which had made it seem nonsense to claim knowledge of the existence, even in theory, of anything lying beyond what we perceive. As Moore had demonstrated, there can be no such problem for commonsense because what exists unperceived by ourselves may be perceived and reported to us, described to us, named and so on, by others. As Ayer had put it [47],

We can identify a sense-field by reference not only to its own character but also that of its neighbours, and if necessary to that of their neighbours and so on.

Austin, another Oxford Arts scholar (q.v.) had further reminded us that things we do not see directly may be seen indirectly, by means of mirrors, microscopes, telescopes, cameras, etc. etc. What none of these scholars had stressed, however, was that some of these direct and indirect experiences of things may be those of scientists in their laboratories, with electron microscopes, radio telescopes, cyclotrons and other pieces of apparatus which are not, in general, available to the ordinary observer. For experiences gained by such means, the ready-made concepts and concept-categories of ordinary language may all too often prove completely inadequate. Scientists are thus thrown upon the urgent necessity of having, for that moment at least, to invent special kinds of language which may not fit the evolved common-language structure. Concepts such as, for instance, that of 'photons' (bits of particulate space-travelling luminosity); 'quarks', with their measures of 'strangeness', 'upness' and 'downness', 'beauty' and so on, useful though they might be for one specialist talking to another, have no connection whatever with ordinary language in the way the Oxbridge Linguistics conceived it. In ordinary circumstances, the tension between the existing language and these new, exotic languages would call for further adjustment of the language as a whole, either by adapting itself to these new

[47] Ayer, A.J., The Central Questions of Philosophy (London, 1973) p. 91.

registers or by modifying them so as to incorporate them within the range of our common understanding. The fact, however, that this natural process of linguistic adjustment had virtually ended with the Educational elevation of Physical Science to levels of ‘ivory tower’ inaccessibility was the main problem to which our researcher’s thesis was addressed.

However, as Austin had emphasised [48], it is plain commonsense that things not perceived or known to anyone may be discovered behind trees and rocks, in the sky, in the ground, under the sea, and so on. We may not know these things until we find them, which may be purely by chance. They may turn out to have characters, properties, qualities or whatever which we might never suspect. Nor does it offend commonsense in the least to say that they may have in themselves, as we speak, these objective features, even though neither we nor anyone else may ever know it. Nothing, therefore, relieves mankind of the necessity, either in science or in commonsense, of having to make guesses about the true reality of things. We may, indeed, make ‘educated guesses’ about things unknown by extrapolating from the things that are known. The leaders in this educated guesswork are, inevitably the scientists, whose educated guesses are the necessary theories of empirical science. None of this, he felt, should have been contested by the Linguistic philosophers. He blamed the Educational system with its artificial bifurcation of Arts and Science for the failure of those Arts philosophers to take full account of the sheer extent to which commonsense is —or, at least should be— continually involved with the theories of scientists. It was as though these Arts people had taken all the science involved in building the commonsense of their day as more or less completed, making it seem that the problems which scientists continually face in seeking to understand nature as it presents itself to them had little or no relevance. So they had ignored all need to square their philosophical ideas with what was currently going on in physical science contexts about how objects and our observations of them interrelate and interact. They had taken it for granted that Moore’s refutation of Berkeley was sufficient in itself and that it had made the problem of light-separation, as known to science, somehow vanish. But it had not. This, our explorer judged, had been a serious omission on the part of those philosophers. In failing to address that problem they had trapped our perceptions of things (as, indeed, he himself had, inadvertently) in the ‘retail department’ of light-interaction and had simply ignored the concern of physicists with the ‘production’ end. In this way, they had made as much a closed system of our commonsense descriptions of things as Berkeley and his idealist successors had made of our subjective ideas, sensations and impressions.

So that problem of the true nature of physical substance very clearly remained to be solved —as much in philosophy as in physics. But how, with the way things stood, was he to convince the Establishment that it was a problem in which physical science, philosophy and commonsense should all be closely and interactively involved. That would mean physicists and philosophers having to pool their resources in the manner of that combination of disciplines once known as natural philosophy, which had now been officially withdrawn. Academically unfeasible though it might be, however, such cooperation he now saw as essential; and he was determined, as he had promised himself in his sojourn in the church by the sea, to work as diligently as he could towards that unpopular and seemingly unreachable end.

Specifically, that would mean having to undertake, from the commonsense standpoint which philosophers such as Moore and Austin had established, theoretical work of the kind which, as Arts-educated people, they had professionally disdained. It would require, from their linguistic-analysis point of view, sorting out from the mishmash of scientific jargon those bits of language which are compatible with commonsense from the bits which are not. The essential difference, he decided, was that as a category, the theories of a commonsense science are essentially empirical as contrasted to so many modern, so-called ‘scientific’ theories that are non-empirical. Theories postulating the ‘real’

[48] J. L. Austin, op cit.

existence of things which have no more than an explanatory function and which we can never, even in principle, know directly but only from what are claimed to be their ‘effects’, could have no place in a truly commonsense ontology. For instance, there was a radical difference between saying, from a purely observational standpoint, that the moon affects the tides, that the orbit of one planet perturbs that of another, and so on, and saying that these phenomena are caused by an invisible ‘force of gravity’ stretching across space. By the same token, there is a radical difference between saying that illumination is quantised and saying that these quanta are little particles of illumination called ‘photons’ travelling through space at the ‘speed $c$’. The business of natural philosophy, as he had conceived it, should have been to analyse this sort of language, to winnow out the observational essence of these discoveries and incorporate it into common language, leaving out whatever specialised conceptions might, on that analysis, prove unnecessary and/or logically incoherent.

Otherwise, in presuming to see behind and beyond all possible perception, to things not only unknown but unknowable in any commonsense way—that is, things of pure nonsense such as, for example, the notion of a ‘photon in flight’$^{[49]}$—such theories, if taken as other than working theories (see above) had firmly to be ruled-out as not physical but metaphysical. The theories of ‘fields’, ‘uncollapsed wave-functions’, the simultaneous position and momentum of elementary particles, trajectories of light in vacuo, the insides of ‘black holes’, the invisible ‘forces’ of ‘gravity’, ‘electrostatics’, ‘magnetics’ ... and so on, none of which are directly verifiable, all fell, so far as he was concerned, into that non-empirical reject-category—like the beautiful colour-picture he had once, seen in a book on ‘Modern Science’, of a ‘black hole’ which, as the book explained, was a singularity from which no light nor anything else can emerge. That these sorts of working ideas and theories were so avidly and indiscriminately swallowed by such an over-credulous public seemed odd enough. That they were so often described as ‘realistic’, however, had seemed to him particularly strange. Being so obviously metaphysical, the word ‘physical’, which their proponents applied to them was and had always been, so far as he was concerned, a howling misnomer.

So now, given that there was this clear commonsense distinction between physical (empirical) and non-physical (non-empirical) theories, there should, he felt, no longer be any need for the customary dissociation between physics (as opposed to metaphysics) and commonsense. What was required in order to complete his projected synthesis of commonsense and science along the lines indicated by Moore et al., was now some theory—some commonsense/empirical theory—as to what it was that lay behind the light. This was, of course, without having to revert to Cartesian dualism (see Chapter VI) and its associated Remote Realism’. The commonly accepted theory was that the sources of the light are what Democritus conceived as ‘atoms’, a concept which, via Descartes, scientists had followed ever since. Now in atomic spectra, as our erstwhile lineman had shown (see chapter VII.1), once the unnecessary ‘Einstein separation’ is removed, the data out of which our perceptions of things are constructed and the data out of which those things themselves are constructed are seen to be one and the same. This at least supported the removal, by the Linguistic philosophers after Moore, of the epistemological ‘veil of perception’ that Descartes had drawn between physical objects and our perceptions of them. Nevertheless, despite the directness of those spectral contacts, the transacted mass-energies expressed in formula (8), chapter VII—the quantum ‘pixels’, as he had described them—were still no more than just bits of those atoms. The bulk of those atoms, as things in themselves behind the light, had been determined other than in purely optical ways (e.g., in measurements made by empirical scientists such as Dalton, of the comparative weights, chemical constitutions and so on, of the various chemical substances).

$^{[49]}$ Since a ‘photon’ is a quantum of energy and since a quantum is irreducible, there is no way in which that quantum can sensibly be thought to have energy to spare in manifesting itself in any way in the distance between A and B.

The theory he needed, therefore, was one which would provide a consistent commonsense account of these substantial sources, the knowledge of which was obviously not wholly obtainable from light itself. However, for his purposes he could not simply take the existing substance theories 'off the peg' because, as he saw it, those theories, with their references to self-sufficient 'atoms', invisible 'field-forces' and so on—in short, what physicists call 'hidden mechanisms'—fell for the most part into his non-empirical or metaphysical reject-category. The reviewers of the aforementioned Physics Essays paper had sussed that what its authors were doing was incipient ontology. If so, then it was a commonsense ontology in which everything had to be epistemologically 'up front'. There could be no 'hidden mechanisms' of the kind which, to use a nice phrase of Austin's, lay 'ticking over metaphysically' behind the scenes, such as the 'elementary charges' or 'electrons' of classical electrodynamics, envisioned as bits of 'static', frizzling away, on their own in complete emptiness and darkness when not interacting with anything. What he should be looking for, therefore, was not some new and academically approved 'elegant' or 'aesthetic' theory of the sort which theoreticians all too often vied with one another to produce. It occurred to him that, like Socrates he was claiming no theories of his own. His thesis was an exercise in pure conceptual analysis—not to say linguistic analysis—typical of the Socratic method in which all the ideas were those of others which survived this method of critical probing.

X.1 The True Meaning of 'Substance'

It was in applying this Socratic method to the existing theory of 'atoms' that he became convinced that something had gone terribly wrong, right at the start, with the customary notion of 'substance'. In a commonsense science, as he conceived it, substantiality had to be that of things in the world, not of things metaphysically behind and beyond it. Relativity had managed to rescue mass, or 'materiality' from that metaphysical underworld to which it had been consigned by Democritus and had restored it to the world of commonsense perception. To refresh his memory, he consulted his Dictionary of Philosophy [50]. The bit he was looking for was where it was stated that 'substance' is the traditional translation of the Greek ousia, which meant 'being' or 'entity'. In the sense understood by Aristotle, this meant either a particular concrete object, like Socrates or his horse, or as the form, or essence, which makes that entity the thing it is. Socrates is what he is, the Dictionary explained, because the flesh of which Socrates is made has taken on the form of a man and not, say, that of a horse.

What had gone wrong, then, as our enquirer analysed it, was that all those centuries ago, Democritus had made the fundamental mistake of artificially opposing 'substance' to 'void' and then identifying 'void' with space. To the Greeks, void meant, initially, what we would now mean by null and void. It could not—at least not without theoretical distortion—be identified with the space of commonsense experience. As Aristotle expressed it, if the space that separates objects is a void, then since 'void' means 'nothing', to say that the void separates bodies is to say that the bodies are separated by nothing, which is the same as saying that they are not separated. For Aristotle, therefore, there was no void as such. Everything that truly is, he would say, is substantial. In Aristotle's sense, even space is a substance insofar as it is the 'thing' to which experience ascribes properties, qualities and so on [51]. For instance, in a statement such as 'space is extended and empty of matter', the substance of that statement is space. In other words, substantiality meant literally whatever 'stands under' all the sensible properties and qualities used to define and identify something distinguished in perception..

[50] Lacey, A. R. A Dictionary of Philosophy, Routledge and Kegan Paul, London (1976) p. 209

[51] In Logic terms, the substance of a statement or proposition belongs to the subject of that statement or proposition, of which qualities or properties are predicated via a copula, which is some variant of the verb 'to be' (i.e., 'is', 'was', 'were', 'will be' ... etc.).

However, that original meaning of substance had been changed, by Democritus, to become 'material substance'. Aristotle mentions this different, Democritan meaning but dismisses it because, for him, any notion of 'matter' as what remains behind when all the properties and qualities by which an object is identified are removed was just pure nonsense. In contrast, for Democritus, those same properties and qualities which define an object were no more than 'conventions' of human perception, whereas 'matter' was its one and only true underlying reality or substance [52]. In that case, for Democritus, in addition to his 'material substance' there also had to be void—that is, a real nothing, or non-matter. Without that 'real' void, nothing would be able to move because there would be no room to allow either it or its parts to move, which makes motion or fluidity impossible. Everything material would be jammed solid. So, as Democritus reasoned, since motion obviously is possible, the void, or nothing, whether we like it or not, has to be real. So, having misinterpreted 'substance' in that materialist way, in order to accommodate motion he had to compound that original error by also misinterpreting 'void'. In other words, Democritus had to break that 'material substance' into bits, which he called 'atoms', and then introduce the notion of a real nothing with which to intersperse them. As our former lineman saw it, it was Descartes' contamination of the West with this Democritan notion of space as a void separating bits of matter, from each other and from us as observers, which had succeeded in making our science practically hyperactive whilst crippling it philosophically.

What was required, then, it seemed to him, in order to rectify this sorry state of affairs, was to complete the job that Einstein had already partly achieved, of restoring matter from its epistemological exile at the hands of these atomists to its original place in that realm of ordinary commonsense substance which Aristotle had defined. The part of the job that remained to be done was to complete that relativistic restoration for physics generally—that is, to reinstate the true meaning of substance in the 'immediately manifest' sense of Aristotle and Moore. Plainly, in this truly relativistic sense it was no longer feasible to think of mass as the 'material substance' which Democritus envisaged as something which bodies possess 'in themselves', independently of how they are manifest to one another and to us as observers. As Einstein had shown, mass is just one observational (i.e., relative) measure of an object which cannot be isolated from its other observational or relative measures such as speed, energy and so on.

Evidently, then, the substance he was looking for was not something in some artificially isolated dimensional category of 'mass'—nor of 'length' or 'time'. These measures mass, length and time, he clearly perceived, cannot be isolated from one another in any way other than in pure metaphysical imagination. From his contrasting commonsense-empirical point of view, what he was looking for, in the way of substance, was some substantial and inseparable combination of all three of those dimensions. And the only physical measure he could think of which combined those dimensions in that substantial way was what physicists call angular momentum. At school (when he attended) he had learned the law of moments, in which a pair of masses, m and M are balanced a distance apart as on a see-saw, such that the mass m multiplied by its distance r_m from the balancing point was equal to the product of the other mass M and its opposite distance r_M from that same point. The only difference with angular momentum, so far as he could gather, was that instead of the ends of the see-saw rocking up and down, these masses rotate around their common centre, separated by a length r (the sum of the two opposite moment-arms r_m and r_M), in a time t which is the period of the rotation.

Angular momentum, as he understood it, then, is an automatically paired relation between masses in rotational systems such as those of the planets and satellites of the solar system. It is also, he learned, a conserved relation. This meant that if the balance of any one pair of masses in a system is changed, then compensatory changes in the distributions of masses have to take place, immediately

[52] See Samburski, S. The Physical World of the Greeks, Routledge, London, (1960).

and automatically, elsewhere in the system (that is, instantly, since any delay would break the law of overall conservation). Angular momentum is also, as he already knew (see Chapter VII) ultimately quantised, in discrete units of Planck's constant $h$ divided by $2\pi$, which was signified in the nomenclature of Physics by $h$ (usually referred to as $h$-bar). The conservation of angular momentum thus held everything together, while the fact that it was ultimately quantised avoided the unwanted consequence a cosmical 'gridlock'. This, he felt, made it the prime candidate for consideration as the true substance of nature in place of the metaphysical 'material substance' of Democritus.

At this time, his understanding of angular momentum was sketchy. As a non-mathematician, he found its directional (i.e., vector) orientations in elliptical orbits and in spinning bodies difficult to cope with. Nevertheless, he felt intuitively (to be mathematically supported later), that angular momentum was the substance he needed to settle his philosophical account. It provided that immediate interconnection between physical objects—hence between objects and observers—that his direct-action thesis required. Moreover, it applied to the movements of everything from astronomical objects to microphysical particles; and because it was quantised there was no question of stasis. Perhaps, he mused, all those motions, of stars, galaxies, atoms and so on, their orbits, diurnal rotations and internal molecular movements, would turn out to be susceptible of explanation purely in terms of different forms and convolutions of angular momentum. This, he thought, should dispense with traditional explanations of these motions in terms of the invisible and notoriously fictitious 'gravitational', 'electric', 'magnetic' and even 'nuclear' so-called 'field-forces'. It was, at any rate, an avenue of enquiry he decided to explore. Indeed, he now felt he had no alternative, in seeking his goal of a new and sensible natural philosophy, but to navigate, under his flag of Normal Realism, the narrow and generally unknown philosophical passage which Moore had sketched out, between subjective idealism (or 'Positivism') and reactionary Remote Realism.

XI The Disunited 'Forces of Nature'

In this adventurous frame of mind, on clear nights our odysseyist would look up at the heavens and see, in the motions of the stars, planets and speeding artificial satellites, not 'gravitation' but angular momentum, which he now regarded as the true substance of the observational world. This, it seemed to him, was what held the world objectively together behind our relative—and, all too often, subjective—perceptions of it. This cohesion, as he saw it, is all-embracing, despite the fact that at the microphysical level, angular momentum is quantised, that it reduces to elements that are discrete and dissociate—as it has to, he reminded himself, in order that everything is not be blocked solid.

At such times the focus of his attention would shift from that quantised, microphysical aspect of angular momentum towards those astronomical levels, where it presents itself, so to speak, as the 'wood' rather than the 'trees'. For him there was no longer any question of the 'universe' being bits of self-sufficient matter floating about in a self-sufficient void, remote from his own perceptual here-and-now. Space, as he perceived it, was the holistic distribution of these angular momentum systems in which every physical body, including his own, was paired with every other in the manner envisaged by Mach—that is, instantly and reciprocally, in accordance with the laws both of angular momentum conservation and Newton's third law of reciprocal action and reaction. That is to say, he had ceased to think in the customary way of the stars as we see them being no more than faded relics of distant events whose images are conveyed to us by space-travelling light-rays allegedly informing us of things existing relatively to us, not now but only as they were aeons ago. For how, he thought, can we possibly relate, in any truly relativistic sense, to things which are in no way directly accessible to us? This conventional ‘light-separation’ of object and observer which had been conceived by Democritus and endorsed by Einstein, had to be no more than an unfortunate accident of history—especially since, as he had ascertained (see Chapter V), in the proper-times of quantum light-interactions the atoms of the observed objects and those in the observer’s eyes or instruments are practically touching$^{[53]}$.

Moreover, looking at those stars made him wonder how Newton could possibly have got away with saying that all natural motion takes place in straight lines. Empirically speaking, nothing—but nothing—travels in that way. Even balls on billiard-tables would be seen to travel in curves if the table was long enough, as Newton must surely have known! But, of course, anyone challenging that straight-line-motion assumption would have been told that the motions of those astronomical bodies were ‘really’ straight but that they were made to curve by the universal presence, in and between matter, of ‘gravitational force’. But that was amazing! It reminded him of a joke he once heard about a man who claimed payment from the London Council for keeping down the tigers in Hyde Park. ‘But there are no tigers in Hyde Park’, he was told. ‘Which only shows what a good job I’m doing’, was the reply.

So Newton, it seemed, had given us two concepts for the price of one. The first was that all motion is fundamentally straight, and the second was that that straightness can never be seen because of the universal presence of ‘gravitation’. But of course, Newton’s theory worked. Whatever might be its logical shortcomings, it was eminently successful for the purposes of practical Physics and Mechanics. It had got astronauts to the moon and back, with complete mathematical precision, so it had to be alright. (This was akin to what the authorities had said, prior to the Tay bridge disaster, that it had to be alright because Queen Victoria had crossed the bridge without incident. The fact that it subsequent collapsed in a high wind and that this had been predicted by ordinary workmen was kept very much ‘under wraps’.) With his similar artisan’s commonsense knowledge of how disastrously wrong even the most respectable theoretical projections can go, he decided to take a good look at Newton’s theory.

XI.1 The ‘force of Gravity’

At one time, his wife had been President of her branch of the National Union of Teachers, and he had accompanied her when she attended the NUT Conference Centre at Stoke Rochford, near Grantham. While she was kept occupied with her Union business, he had paid a visit to Newton’s house at Woolsthorpe where he saw the garden in which Newton was said to have watched an apple fall, thereby ‘discovering the force of gravity’.

This set him thinking. He remembered a common near where he had lived, in Wales, where there is a long, straight stretch of road known locally as ‘The Humps’. It is so named because cars can be driven along it at high speeds which, due to steep rises and falls in the road’s surface, produce the same ‘pit-of-the-stomach’ sensations as when going up and down in a very fast lift. Indeed, so steep used to be the drop at one particular place that it had caused a car to lift off the road and crash into the opposite rise as though into wall, with the result that the occupants of the car were killed and the road had to be levelled at that spot for reasons of safety.

Now what, he wondered, might Newton have discovered if, instead of sitting in the orchard at Woolsthorpe, he had been a passenger in a modern car driven along that humpy road? Keen observer that he was, he would surely have noticed that the same visceral sensations of rising and falling can be felt on a perfectly level and straight road if one is lying prone with one’s head and feet in line with the

$^{[53]}$ As someone later pointed out to him, this proper-time touching in the case of light-quanta was a consequence of Special Relativity which had been pointed out by the chemist, Gilbert Lewis, some seventy or so years earlier. (see Lewis, G. N., ‘Light Waves and Corpuscles’, Nature, 117, 256 (1926)).

road and the driver is violently accelerating and braking. What should Newton have made of all this? Where, in that situation would have been his 'universal force of gravity'? At what speed and in what direction, up or down, might an apple fall in those circumstances?

'It all has to do with momentum,' Newton would have noticed. But in this new situation that momentum would no longer be purely linear, or 'inertial', in the way that his predecessor, Galileo, had taught. It also had to do with curvature. At places where the radius of the slope is smallest, the visceral sensation is greatest, and vice versa. The relation between the mass, velocity and radius of the motion, Newton might therefore have realised, was neither that of his purely linear momentum nor of his uniformly downward 'force of gravity' but that of angular momentum in which the real (i.e. visceral) force may be in any direction whatsoever.

As our odysseyist reasoned, then, allowing for the anachronism, if Newton had studied the apple on that road in Wales rather than in his garden at Woolsthorpe, his straight-line inertial momentum $mv$ would doubtless have been seen as a special case of angular momentum $mvr$, with $r$ at theoretical infinity. Obviously, however, with $r$ at infinity, for non-zero momentum $mv$ the angular momentum $mvr$ also has to be infinite, which is unreal. For all real cases, then, $r$ has to be finite and the motion naturally curved, or orbital, as Aristotle had argued [54], with no need to think of any 'force of gravity' being responsible for these curved trajectories. Instead, we would think of freely moving bodies as automatically paired, so that, depending on the magnitude of $J$, bodies $m$ and $M$ are balanced, by the law of moments, at a mean distance $r_0$ which is

$$ r_0 = J/mv_0 \tag{9}, $$

where $J$ signifies the angular momentum $mvr$ and the variables suffixed 'O' are to be read as 'orbital'. Newton's 'gravitational' equation, he decided, can therefore be replaced by the following angular momentum equation:

$$ J = mv_0r_0 = \mathbf{G}mM/v_0 \tag{10}, $$

where $\mathbf{G}$, which he called 'curly gee', is a variable which, for free orbital motion is equivalent to the usual 'gravitational constant' $G$ ($= 6.673 \times 10^{-11} \, \mathrm{N \, m^2 \, kg^2}$)[55].

As he perceived it, then, the way this formula works is as follows. Let $m$ be the mass of a typical British car (say, 1500 kg) and $M$ the mass of the earth ($M = 5.976 \times 10^{24} \, \mathrm{kg}$). Standing still at the earth's equator, which is at a radius $r = 6.373 \times 10^6$ metres from the planet's centre, the car has a circular motion in sidereal space of $v = 464.74$ metres/second. Its angular momentum in that instance is $J = mvr = 4.4 \times 10^{12} \, \mathrm{kg \, m^2 \, s^{-1}}$. For that amount of angular momentum, if the car were allowed to follow a natural free orbit in space around a superdense football-sized earth-mass $M$, that is, for $\mathbf{G} = G$, then its speed (assuming the orbit to be circular) would be, according to (10):

$$ v_0 = GmM/J = 1.4 \times 10^5 \, \mathrm{m/s} \tag{11}. $$

[54] Aristotle argued that free or uninterrupted motion cannot be straight because, if it were, then the motion-trajectory would have to be infinitely long, which is impossible, or else it would have to begin and end in oblivion, which is also impossible. So, by default, Aristotle concluded, free and uninterrupted motion has to take place in closed loops.

[55] See Pope, N. V. and Osborne, A.D. 'Instantaneous Gravitational and Inertial Action-at-a-Distance', Physics Essays, Vol. 8, No. 3, pp. 384-397 (1995).

This provides a value for the free-orbit radius, again according to (10):

$$r_{\mathrm{O}} = J/mv_{\mathrm{O}} = 2.18 \times 10^{4} \mathrm{m} \tag{12}$$

which is far below the earth's normal surface radius, that is, at 0.003, or 1/290th of the earth's mean surface radius — just under twelve miles from the centre, or three thousand nine-hundred an forty-seven miles down below our feet.

If this had been Newton's experience of motion, with fast cars and modern roads, he might have taken a different view of falling bodies. He might logically have concluded that the reason why the car normally presses down on the earth's surface is not because there is some fictitious 'force of gravity' pulling it towards the earth's centre but simply because with that comparatively small amount of angular momentum the car is given by the diurnal rotation of the earth, it seeks to follow its natural force-free orbit, nearly four thousand miles below the earth's surface. So, by reaction against that surface which is resisting its descent, the car presses on it with a real force depending on the difference between the value of G for the free motion at radius $r_{\mathrm{O}}$, which is $G$, and the restrained motion $v$ at the earth's surface radius $r$, for which G is

$$\mathbf{G} = Jvm/M = 2.27 \times 10^{-13} \mathrm{N} \mathrm{m}^{2} \mathrm{kg}^{2} \tag{13}$$

That real pressing-force, at the normal distance $r$ from the earth's centre, is therefore,

$$F = (mM/r^{2}) (\mathbf{G} - \mathbf{G}) \tag{14}$$

which is

$$F = -14676.4 \text{ newtons of force}$$

Divided by the BSI figure [56] for converting kilograms of force into kilograms of weight (i.e., 9.80665 N per kg) this is

$$-1496.58 \text{ kilograms} \tag{15}$$

The minus sign signifies, of course (by that chosen convention), that the force acts downwards, in the manner ordinarily understood as 'weight'. If the car followed the sun at 1000-mph (464.74 m/s) along some imagined equatorial highway, its speed $v$ due to the earth's rotation, in (11), would be cancelled and its weight would be precisely 1500 kg, as in the vehicle handbook. On the other hand, a car speeding so fast that its angular momentum radius $r_{\mathrm{O}}$ was greater at some point than the radius of the road-contour at that spot would obviously lift off the road and go into free fall, as had the car, momentarily, in the tragic 'Humps' incident. And if that lifting were prevented —in the manner, say, of a rollercoaster— then G would be bigger than $G$, in (14) and the measurable force would be in the opposite (i.e., positive, or centrifugal) direction. That is, an apple, like everything else in the car, would gravitate upwards instead of downwards. The roof of the car would be virtually a centrifuge.

[56] British Standards Institute, April 5th, 1988.

Newton might also have been interested to notice that these measured forces were of the same 'inverse square' variety as his erstwhile conceived 'gravitational force'. That is to say, with all other things being equal, the strength of this downward/upward force varies inversely as the square of the distance of the two masses from each other, as can be seen in (14). For instance, it is plain from that equation that if the radius of the earth's surface were halved, then the weight of the car (positive or negative according to the value of G in (13)) would be quadrupled, and vice versa. Also, Newton would surely have seen that the force classically called 'inertial', due to the car-driver's ordinary braking and accelerating, is the same kind of force except that on the earth's surface, with restricted r, all changes in the angular momentum mvr, have to be purely in terms of the 'linear' momentum mv, as with the sorts of bodies with which Galileo and Newton experimented on flat surfaces like those of billiard-tables.

So, our enquirer concluded, if Newton's experience, had been with, say, modern motor-racing rather than with falling apples, then our customary fourfold distinction between the forces currently known as 'centripetal', 'centrifugal', 'inertial' and 'gravitational', need never have arisen. They could all have been seen as no more than directional aspects of the same force, namely, the force preventing bodies from following their natural orbital motions with respect to one another. As things are, however, he reckoned that these forces could now be unified without having to postulate the special 'principle of equivalence' which Einstein had thought necessary to 'equate' gravitation and inertia. Also, since this unified force is an automatically balancing force between bodies, in an overall angular momentum relation involving all the planets, stars, galaxies and so on, there is no question of its having to be mediated by any kind of substance or quasi-substance, such as an 'ether' or whatever. Its influence is instantaneous, in accordance with Newton's second law of reciprocal action-and-reaction. Hence it is pure action-at-a-distance [57], and all bodies, in one way or another, are connected in terms of it, as 'universally' as in Newton's postulate of 'gravitation'. Nor does it act 'in space'. The distances between bodies in terms of r₀ are not 'in' space'; they are space, and there is no other space that it makes sense to think of—far less the Euclidean 'void' envisaged by Democritus (see previous chapter). Moreover, he reflected, that angular-momentum space provides an absolute background frame for motion, of the sort which was thought necessary by Mach. This is the frame of the 'fixed stars' with respect to which the axes of gyroscopes and spinning planets orientate themselves. Clearly, as Mach had observed, the fact that bodies such as the earth are oblate spheroids when they spin in that fixed frame and perfect spheres when they don't signifies the presence of that preferred state of non-rotational motion. It might also, he thought, have some bearing on the Sagnac effect [58].

In any event, he was now convinced that this self-sufficient explanation of 'gravity' as pure angular momentum was much simpler than Newton's mysterious 'universal force of attraction'. It was also, surely, much simpler than Einstein's doubly mysterious interpretation of 'gravity' as a 'force of acceleration', which required one to think of the earth's surface as somehow accelerating upwards, everywhere, under our feet like the floor of a lift—miraculously without increasing the earth's overall size! In any case, for himself at least, there was no traumatic upheaval in this conceptual shift from thinking of free motion as naturally rectilinear to thinking of it as naturally curved. Newton's 'straight-line' free momentum mv could be regarded as either a free angular momentum mvr with r at theoretical infinity (a straight line and a circle of infinite radius being one and the same) or as an angular momentum of fixed or constrained radius, like that of balls on a billiard table, whose directional

[57] Pope, N. V. and Osborne, A. D. Physics Essays op. cit. (1995); also Pope, N. V., 'The Complete Physics Heresy', Galilean Electrodynamics, Vol. 10, Special Issue 2, (1999), pp 23–31.

[58] S. Sagnac, in 1910, experimenting with a Michelson-Morley type of apparatus (a combined beamsplitter and interferometer) claimed to have discovered that a beam propagated in opposite directions around the circumference of a rotating disc establishes, in the way the M & M experiment failed to do for linear motion, a favoured reference-frame for rotational motion.

freedom is limited to the horizontal. The details of our ex-lineman's discovery of how to dispense with the notoriously fictitious 'gravitational force' were explained in a paper which subsequently appeared in a Physics journal [59].

XI.2 'Electric Force'

Such, at any rate, he presumed, was how it might, logically, have turned out if the circumstances of history had been different—if, for instance, Newton had been more familiar with modern fast cars and rollercoasters than with apples in orchards. And, he further surmised, if Newton had thought in that different way then so, surely, would his successors—at least it was scarcely likely that without that illustrious precedent, they would have dreamed up those other forces, of so-called 'electricity' and 'magnetism' which are conceived by analogy with 'gravitation'.

Nor, as it now seemed to him, were these the only implications of his neo-Newtonian change of conception. He found that extending the angular momentum equation (10) to include the kinetic energy expression, $K_0$ of the orbital motion of $m$ around $M$ produced:

$$J = m v_0 r_0 = 2 K_0 r_0 / v_0 = G m M / v_0 \tag{16}$$

This, of course, was for a pair of relatively inert masses $m$ and $M$ like the moon and the earth.
