What would happen, he asked himself, if in addition to their orbital kinetic energy, these particles possessed some extra kinetic energy $K_X$ in the form of, say, spin or some other kind of mechanical oscillation, so that the total angular momentum included those two different kinds of motion. Looking at this formula (16) he noticed that this additional energy should affect the value of $\mathbf{G}$ for those freely orbiting bodies [60]. Discussing this with his Maths colleague he found that he, also, for different reasons (i.e., having to do with Einstein's General Theory of Relativity), had also contemplated, in his Ph.D. thesis [61], the necessity for variations in the 'gravitational constant' $G$. This was when they together decided to replace the 'varying $G$' with $\mathbf{G}$ and call it 'curly gee'. The modified version of the Newtonian angular momentum formula thus became:

$$J = m v r = 2 (K_0 + K_X) r / v = \mathbf{G} m M / v \tag{17}$$

As a mathematician, of course, his colleague had to point out the limitations of this formula, namely, that it was valid only for circular orbits and for spins in the same plane as the orbit—i.e., with all angular momentum vectors parallel. The mathematician was nevertheless persuaded that the formula, 'as a first approximation' to the description of real motion, could be presented on the basis of that stated condition. And on that basis, an interesting consequence emerged. In equation (10), viz.:

[59] Pope, N. V. and Osborne, A. D., ibid. (1995) pp. 394-397.

[60] At this stage he neglected to consider the vector-orientations of the angular momentum, which was tantamount to assuming that all the motions, orbital and spin were circular and in the same plane. This was so that he could, for the sake of simplicity, deal with magnitudes only. The vector considerations were to become part of a later project in collaboration with his colleague at the Mathematics Department of a university, see following.

[61] Osborne, A. D., Gravitation and Dynamical Systems, The City University, London, (1980).

$$J = m v _ { \mathrm { O } } r _ { \mathrm { O } } = G m M / v _ { \mathrm { O } } \tag { 1 0 } ,$$

with G being equal to ordinary G (= 6.673 × 10⁻¹¹ N m² kg⁻²), for a pair of plain masses m and M of 9.109 × 10⁻³¹ kg and 1.673 × 10⁻²⁷ kg (the masses of the so-called 'electron' and 'proton', respectively), with J being a quantum of angular momentum (i.e., J = ħ = 1.0546 × 10⁻³⁴ kg m²/s, or Planck's constant divided by 2π), solving in turn for v and r produced

$$v = G m M / J = 9 . 5 \times 1 0 ^ { - 3 4 } \mathrm { m e t r e s / s e c o n d } \tag { 1 8 } ,$$

which is almost stationary, and

$$r = J / m v = 1 . 2 1 8 \times 1 0 ^ { 2 9 } \mathrm { m } . \tag { 1 9 } ,$$

which is prodigiously large, being twelve billion light-years, a truly extragalactic distance!

The orbital kinetic energy, K_O = ½mv² for such an extremely small velocity v would, of course, be negligibly small (in the order of 10⁻⁶⁷ joule). However, suppose that to one of those same plain masses, say m, orbiting in that way, with that same quantum of angular momentum J = ħ there is imparted a comparatively large spin-energy K_X in (17), equivalent in mechanical units of joules to a half the so-called 'ionisation energy' hcR which is the energy mc² in Equation (8), chapter VII (i.e., with n in that equation approaching infinity – see also Appendix 3). Solving for v and r in (17) then produces:

$$v = [ 2 ( K _ { \mathrm { O } } + K _ { \mathrm { X } } ) / m ] ^ { 1 / 2 } = 1 . 5 4 7 \times 1 0 ^ { 6 } \mathrm { m } / \mathrm { s } \tag { 2 0 }$$

and

$$r = J / m v = 7 . 4 8 4 \times 1 0 ^ { - 1 1 } \mathrm { m } \tag { 2 1 } ,$$

which are in the same order of magnitude as the standard textbook values of the velocity and radius in the Bohr model of the hydrogen atom [62].

In short, then, for a quantum of angular momentum J = ħ, imparting a purely mechanical spin-energy K_X = hcR to the plain masses m and M collapses the gigantic size of the purely orbital radius r, in (19), down to the size of the (ideally circular) radius of the Bohr model of the hydrogen atom, according to the latter's conventional 'electrostatic force' interpretation of those orbits. By the same token, with K_X in (21) reduced to zero (i.e., by assuming equal and opposite spin angular momentum vectors of m and M) the distance between m and M would revert to 10²⁹ metres, which is tantamount to the sort of repulsion and complete ionisation that is observed in so-called 'electrostatics'.

With these values, solving for G in (18) produces

$$G = G _ { \mathrm { C } } = J v / m M = 1 . 5 1 5 \times 1 0 ^ { 2 9 } \mathrm { N m } ^ { 2 } \mathrm { k g } ^ { 2 } \tag { 2 2 } ,$$

[62] Actually, this velocity v is 1/√2 times the Bohr velocity and r is √2 times the Bohr radius, which turned out eventually to be more consistent.

where $G_C$ may be called the 'Coulomb gee' for atomic orbits. Moreover, as our enquirer subsequently discovered, with a further suitable change in $K_X$ and consequent changes in $\mathbf{G}$, the 'magnetic force' hypothesis can also be dispensed with in the same way [63]. Indeed, it seemed to him that with appropriate step-changes in the value of $\mathbf{G}$ the parameters of all particle-orbits might be explained as due to various involutions and convolutions of angular momentum (at least in simple magnitude-terms applied to ideally circular motions). It was one method, he decided, of solving the notorious 'unified field' problem that had defeated even the great Einstein—that is, by dispensing with those mystifying 'fields' and their associated 'forces' altogether, a unified non-field theory, as he was pleased to describe it [64].

XI.3 The force of circumstance

There was one force, however, which he found could not be included in this logical unification, and that was the force of habit. For now he was in two minds. On the one hand, he could not doubt that the concepts of 'electric charge', 'coulomb force', 'capacitance', 'farads', 'field-strengths' and so on, which he had inculcated in qualifying for his job as telephone lineman, had been indispensable to him in that occupation. On the other hand, from his present standpoint as a philosopher of physics, these same very familiar concepts and associated trade-jargon now made little sense to him. Indeed, they sometimes seemed just a little bit pretentious and silly, like the rituals of some kind of secret society. At other times, however, he realised that no matter what might be the merits of his new philosophical position, there was clearly no way in which it would have helped him in his occupation of telephone lineman. Nor could he imagine what the Industrial and Education-Administrational consequences would have been if, in his later occupation of Liberal Studies lecturer, he had sought to influence his Electrical and Telecommunications students with these revolutionary ideas. Indeed, even his Maths colleague, at university, who was imbued with these same philosophical ideas, due to his professional concern for his students and their careers was not free to diverge sufficiently from the laid-down university curriculum to point them in this new philosophical direction. As the ex-lineman had come to realise, for any person or institution sensibly concerned with career, only a complete fool would seek to go down that revolutionary road. And in any standard assessment of teaching-ability, any member of that profession might as well take hemlock as aim to make a living by such 'subversive' means. As a future colleague, a Ph.D. in the Electronics industry would put it, 'If I became a millionaire I would be able to study your theory of angular momentum in depth. But as it is,' he added, 'I simply can't afford to!' Another said that he had too much invested in what he was already doing (i.e., quantum wave-dynamics) to consider anything that might so radically change his mind. Our ex lineman-cum-Liberal Studies teacher, whilst sympathetic towards these careerist concerns, was glad, for himself, that he was no longer in the 'Electrical' profession. And so far as his teaching was concerned, he blessed his wife's wisdom and devotion in encouraging him to retire early from a post from which it was now plain he would inevitably have been ousted, sooner or later.

What amazed him more than anything, though, was the lengths of irrationality to which people would descend in order to defend the conceptual status quo. Why all the fuss? said some. Why bother with all this 'logical unification' stuff when anyone knows that logic is no more than a product of the

[63] Keele University Maths-Philosophy Project, 1987 to present time.

[64] Pope, N. V., 'A Relativistic Quantum Phenomenology', Proceedings of the 5th International Conference on Problems of Space, Time and Motion, St. Petersburg, Russia, June, 1998.

human brain? Other, no less typical responses were: 'From my point of view, theories are useful for inventing (experimental) processes and mechanisms, so the more theories I have at my disposal the better it is.' In experimental physics, this commentator argued 'unification' is counter-productive, 'because it puts imagination into a strait jacket.' (The absurd implication of this, for our adventurer, was a science of the future so liberally minded as to have a different theory for every different experimental situation—but not so liberal, of course, as to tolerate any theory of logical unification!)

So far as this same commentator was concerned:

Newton started with gravitation and inertia and derived angular momentum conservation. You start with angular momentum conservation and discover gravitation and inertia. I am happy with both Newton's and your approach.

And what about the unification of electric and magnetic forces within that same concept of angular momentum conservation? That, said the commentator, was 'ingenious'. But if it didn't lead to experimental predictions which disagree with textbook electromagnetism, he said (in effect), then it would be a waste of time studying it.

In short, there were people who, in the conceptual confines of 'physics'—though, mercifully, not in the wider word of commonsense—simply could not imagine why he would want to 'buck the system' in this thankless way and run the gauntlet of its inevitable reactions. There were those who, in association with that same insular sentiment, seriously suggested that what had driven him to 'mess with science' in the way he had was some kind of 'sheer pigheadedness', 'pure ego', 'balls-over-brains' ... or whatever—as various commentators expressed it. The lesson he should have drawn from it all, one person kindly advised, was that in science, no theory was ever right or wrong, that it was all a matter of 'horses for courses', and that for different purposes and in different eras, different 'language-games' were appropriate, all properly geared to the conventions of their time. All such theories had to be taken as true in their way, and to 'attack' them was like vandalising artefacts in a Natural History museum. Another objector, at a conference, commented that such changes should not be rushed, that must be allowed to 'take place gradually'. To this he had responded, to the amusement of the audience, with the joke about the Irish Government's resolve to change to driving on the right, promising the public that the changeover would 'take place gradually'.

Such pure eclecticism, not to say sophistry, in whichever form it took, could not be squared with his purist philosophical aim of conceptual unification or synthesis. A chaotic proliferation of 'language games' or theories, as he saw it, was the Babelian affliction which ailed Modern Science. So while it seemed to be the 'going rate' among modern physicists to produce more and more 'interesting and diversified theories', his own philosophical aim was, by logical elimination (Occam's Razor [65]) to make those theories fewer and fewer. This was, insofar as it might prove possible, to reduce them all towards the evolutionary ideal of a single common theory which, if it could be achieved, would become 'commonsense' by definition.

But, try as he might, he could not convince those to whom he described this analytic or dialectical aim as anything but a kind of intellectual 'fascism'. People who, without demur, accepted that the aim of newspaper reporters, detectives, judges and the like was to 'find the truth' would rear up in high dudgeon at his suggestion that such should also be the aim of physicists and philosophers. The trouble was, of course, that in our highly structured society, when whole occupations and careers become organised and settled around theories such as those of Newton, Faraday, Einstein, etc., to suggest fundamental revisions of those educationally approved and settled ideas was just bound to

[65] William of Occam c.1285-1349, a Franciscan friar, propounded the principle of that name according to which, in explanations, one should choose the simplest and/or the least number of hypotheses that will fit the facts. Only what cannot be dispensed with he regarded as real, whence to postulate other things is arbitrary and mistaken.

produce reactions. In his continual frustration with this sort of reaction he wrote, in the form of a poem, a tongue-in-cheek swipe at the establishment. This was first published in the ANPA newsletter$^{[66]}$, of which he was, at that time, the editor, and then, to his surprise, in a sister newsletter in America$^{[67]}$, of which he was not the editor. This attempt at irony (see Appendix 2) did much to relieve his feelings and foster a sense of truly philosophical resignation, which sustained him from then on.

In this resigned and more relaxed state of mind he would console himself that there was the definite advantage that now, as a ‘committee of one member’ his decisions were unanimous. Unencumbered, as ever, by the usual careerist concerns, he was now free to think what he liked—except, of course, for the objective constraints of logical reason. However, there would always be attitudes of people to which he could in no way relate. His daughters were now grown up and married, with children of their own, so he and his wife eventually retired to their hometown in Wales where he sought out some of his old friends and colleagues (who were now, like him, retired). This was with a view to sharing his new ideas with them in the manner of something like the freethinking ethos he had enjoyed in his idyllic pre-university days at the Adult Liberal Studies college in the beautiful North Wales setting. However, for the most part, these friends now remained—even in their retirement—so occupationally conditioned that his invitations to them to help him ‘change the system’, as they put it, was perceived by them as nothing more than an imposition. Having been put thankfully ‘out to graze’, they now occupied themselves with pleasant and undemanding leisure-pursuits provided for Old Age Pensioners. Nevertheless, a group began to meet at a pub called the Blue Anchor, from which the group took its name. It held together long enough for its name to appear in publications, showing what a scourge it might have become in academic circles had its members been younger and more vigorous. Unfortunately, as the Group’s success began to increase, so did its call for commitment. This created tensions between himself and even his most loyal and well-regarded friends, which led to the Group’s eventual collapse. These people would happily poke the lion of Academia through the bars but in no way could they be coaxed into the cage with it. ‘We leave it all to the young minds,’ these retired people said—another case of ‘Catch 22’, he thought, considering that ‘the young minds’ had to toe the careerist line or else be Educationally exiled, as he had been in his own freethinking enterprise. So, in the end, having fought hard to keep the Group together he became convinced that there was no more help forthcoming from that quarter. If the friends individually remained, the Group was gone.

All that, of course, was still to come. Whilst still living in the Midlands, in his growing concern for the future of free philosophical thought he sought to pursue his campaign under the aegis of the Liberal Party, of which he and his wife had, for some time, been members. On the strength of this political association, they had been delegated, after some correspondence with the Liberal Party’s Education spokesman, the Member of Parliament, Clement Freud, to attend quarterly meetings at Westminster. There our odysseyist hoped had to alert his listeners as to how inimical to truly innovative thinking the Educational Establishment had become. This was with a view to mobilising some sort of legislation against what he perceived to be the stultification of the Educational system. However, in these shallow political waters, his philosophical frigate soon got stuck in the mud, from which he eventually freed himself by giving up politics altogether. To avoid shipwreck, he told himself, like Odysseus steering between Scylla and Charybdis he would have to steer between these mud banks of Politics and the rocks of careerist Education. But what other landfall he might then anticipate, he failed to see. Like a philosophical ‘Flying Dutchman’, his pariah of a thesis would have to remain, other than in its own so far unrefuted rational integrity, completely and continually ‘at sea’.

But at least, so far as he was concerned, this was in clear blue logical waters. To return, then, unfettered, to the issue in hand, namely, that of angular momentum, there was still the question of vectors to be dealt with. He learned that by the law of angular momentum conservation, in a system

$^{[66]}$ ANPA Newsletter No. 16, 1996, p.7. (ISSN 1358-22400)

$^{[67]}$ ANPA West Journal Vol. 6, No.1, 1996, p.13. (ISSN 1075-8887)

containing any number of angular momentum subsystems, not only is the overall magnitude of the angular momentum conserved but so also is the sum of the component vector orientations. By the same law, the affect of one mass upon another in angular momentum balancing-relations, in vector as in magnitude terms, had to be automatic and instantaneous, with no question of mediation by any intervening material or quasi-material agency. In the jargon of modern physics, angular momentum interrelations, both quantitatively and vectorially, were pure non-local action-at-a-distance.

Nor did this instantaneity present any kind of problem relativistically. In Einstein's as well as in his own version of relativity there was no proper-time simultaneity (i.e., concurrence between distance-separated clock-readings —as, for example, between A and B in the original 'Clock-Paradox' situation). There was also, in both versions, that Machian-type, proper-time instantaneity (i.e., that immediacy or directness of connection, tP = 0 between objects in different locations). Being relativistically invariant —i.e., the same in all systems of reference— this proper-time-instantaneous connection sufficed to describe the influences of masses on one another in angular momentum relations. Angular momentum, he had concluded, was therefore well and truly the missing substance with which, in his commonsense relativism following Moore, he had sought to replace the metaphysical 'matter and void' of Democritus and Descartes. It was atomised in units h, whose fundamental mass-components m and M are distanced by the moment-arm (balancing arm) r in the relation mvr = J. Quantum shifts in the orbit-radius r, with instantaneously transacted energies of the kind represented by those fractions of mc² expressed in Equation (8), Chapter VII, section 1, were then the light-quanta whose spectral characteristics (for simple hydrogen, at any rate) were as described by that and Balmer's identical equation [68]. It all fitted together, he thought, rather nicely.

Reactions to this demonstration, at Physics conferences, whilst no longer disrespectful, continued to be mostly —as by then he might have expected— dismissive. 'It is not Physics!' declared one professor. 'It is immoral', declared another. And, said yet another, if this was what he was teaching at the Blue Anchor, then he was 'corrupting those good people' —which incautious remark, in view of what had happened to Socrates, prompted the immediate and obvious reply, 'That is a classic charge if ever I heard one!' [69]

Reassuring though it was to be charged, almost verbatim, with the same crimes as Socrates, it was nevertheless frustrating to find that far from his thesis being able to compete on its merits with the accepted theories, the philosophical 'game' he was playing simply wasn't allowed. Other variations on this same theme of rejection were that his prototype theory couldn't compare, in terms of sheer practicality, with the present paradigm of Physics. These people were blatantly dissembling. It was as though they were insisting on testing the wood-strength of a sapling, against that of an ancient oak.

XII A Synthesis of Instantaneous and Time-Delayed Distant Interaction

Undoubtedly, one of the main reasons why this logically pared-down approach to physics could not be taken seriously was that regardless of what might be the logic of it, historical force of habit had

[68] See also, Appendix 3.

[69] 'Corrupting the populace' had been the charge on which Socrates had been arraigned and subsequently executed.

made it seem a complete contradiction to suggest that physical interaction between distant bodies may have both immediate and time-delayed components. As Hiley had put it, how can things apart also be together [70]. Yet, plainly, they can be, as the relativistic formulae had shown. Moreover, quantum physicists claimed to have verified the togetherness (the non-locality) and the relativists had verified the apartness (locality). Yet in the minds of physicists, the conflict persisted. In conferences, our enquirer had been amazed at the lengths to which people would go in the direction of sheer irrationality in seeking to ‘solve’ what they saw as this ‘problem’. Mostly, these ‘solutions’ consisted of each of the two factions seeking to discredit the other or else diplomatically trying to ‘reconcile’ them by simply cramming the two sets of evidence together regardless of the need for logical consistency. But if Einstein’s dictum were taken as true, that nothing can travel faster than light, then that categorically forbade instantaneity. On that basis, theories about instantaneous distant connections could never be anything but contrived and therefore completely mystifying—as, for instance, in the notion of ‘spooky superluminal’ influences out-pacing the light, mysterious ‘virtual’ particles and/or ‘pilot-waves’ getting there first and guiding the light-quanta to their destinations. The fact that, according to the relativistic formulae, the relative time of any ‘superluminal’ connection would have to run backwards was either buried in a welter of mathematics in which all commonsense logic was lost, or else it was interpreted as a positive suggestion as to the existence of ‘tachyons’ and thrown into the eclectic mix with all the other brilliant theories about ‘time-tunnelling’, ‘wormholes’, the ‘expanding universe’[71], and so on. With all these theoretical jewels, he thought, Modern Science was dazzling the public whilst starving them of understanding.

But of course, he reflected, given all those different Physics theories, anything can be made to seem possible. With no logical checks and balances, nothing whatever can be ruled out, not superluminality and not even backwards-enduring particles. To paraphrase Nietzsche, if truth is dead, then everything is possible. All that would be left would be a conceptual slum. It was just another case of the Protagonian maxim: ‘Such as appears to you is for you and such as appears for me is for me’. For these sophistic theorists, therefore, whatever commonsense might understand to be the truth of things behind all the various theories, had become not only impossible to determine but also senseless even to talk about. In this way, the meanings of words like ‘proof’, ‘fact’, ‘evidence’, etc., had become completely confused, so that what does and what does not happen in experiments becomes anyone’s guess, with experimenters nevertheless squabbling over the ‘factuality’ of their findings, like politicians. Such Babelian confusion made it possible, with equanimity, to claim ‘facts’ that are logically impossible. For instance, it had been claimed that ‘photons’ have been photographed ‘in flight’. But how could that be, when, as already noted, a ‘photon’ is by definition irreducible, so that it has no energy to spare for energising a photographic plate or for being detected in any other way ‘en route’ between where it is emitted to where it is totally consumed? Also there were claims to have sent signals at many times faster than the speed of light, yet how might that be possible when the speed of light is proper-time-instantaneous! By that token, the proper time of those ‘faster-than-light’ influences would be more instantaneous than instantaneous, which is absurd! There were also theories happily proclaiming that cats shut away in boxes may be regarded as neither live nor dead until the box is opened by an observer [72]. Others claimed that the very operation of observing an elementary particle

[70] Phil. Glim., Letter, 5039 – 5041.

[71] See Appendix 4.

[72] Erwin Schrödinger imagined a bizarre experiment in which a cat was placed in a box with a flask of poison gas under a drop-hammer triggered, via a Geiger counter, by a radioactive source, so that the cat may or may not be killed, with 50% probability either way. By this, Schrödinger had hoped to demonstrate the absurdity of the misuse of his probabilistic wave-function by those who assumed that behind the actual appearances the wave-function contained, in itself, opposite probabilities which were ‘collapsed’ into reality by the operation of observing it. The absurdity determines not only the spin of that particle at the point of observation but also that of its angular-momentum twin, which may, at that same moment, be parsecs distant. This predicted effect is known as 'EPR' from a paper in which Einstein dismissed it as absurdly implying 'spooky superluminality'[73].

These, of course, were consequences of the popular view, among theoretical physicists, that the substratum of all physical existence is an entirely abstract or super-real, mathematical 'wave-function' which continues to exist in some immaterial and 'behind-the-scenes', God's-eye-view way, until it is 'collapsed' into materiality by the observer—a new and glorified, altogether extreme version of Berkeleyan idealism or Positivism. But since Positivism had been shown by Moore et al. to be defunct, nothing, it seemed, was more symptomatic of the academic estrangement of modern Physics and Philosophy than this weed-like proliferation, in Physics, of theories of this 'Positivistic' kind. These theories took no account whatever of what had happened to that abortive philosophy in decades past—such as having been abandoned by its very founders, Ayer, Wittgenstein, et al. In this 'wave-function', as some theorists had presented it, hovering metaphysically between potentiality and actuality there are many worlds of the kind we know but accessed by other observers whose lives are just like ours but completely separate, being no more known or knowable to us than the lives of saints and angels in heaven. It seemed to him that by skipping blithely from theory to sophistic theory, these theoreticians, had awarded themselves the facility of being able to claim just about anything—that black is white, that A is non-A and that A is both A and non-A, for all he knew [74].

What he found most disconcerting about all this, from his commonsense-philosophy point of view, was that instead of being taken as a warning sign, this new-found facility of classifying and dividing observational experience in ways which confound all the laws of logic had been hailed, by teachers of physics, as a 'new liberalism' in science, regarding it as a peculiar virtue of the profession. As it seemed to him, however, the fuel-gauges of that kind of physics, which were plainly empty of understanding, were claimed as reading full. In short, the impression he received from his increasing contacts with these physicists—mostly through the growing number of conferences he was now attending—was that Theoretical Physics was spiralling out of control and, on its way down, falling to pieces. Its once characteristic logical integrity, he felt, was completely shot. From its original height of natural philosophy it had descended to a level of professional self-preservation where scarcely anyone either knew or cared what another was doing. As he remarked to someone at a conference, there was now about as much philosophical coordination among these scientists as between woodworm in a beam.

Schrödinger intended to demonstrate was that until the box was opened by the observer and the wave-function thereby collapsed, the cat had to be considered both dead and alive. In spite of this, the assumption of behind-the-scenes (i.e., 'realistic') superposition of quantum states was actually embraced into the teaching of quantum mechanics.

[73] See description in Chapter VI.

[74] In Logic, as he had studied it at university, if anything is A it is A; nothing is both A and not-A; and anything must be either A or not-A. These were known as the 'laws of thought' (See Cohen, M. and Nagel, E., An Introduction to Logic, Routledge, London (1963) p. 181.

But why, he wondered, was all this mystery mongering regarded as necessary when the instantaneity required by quantum physics and the time-delay of relativity plainly co-existed in the relativistic equations? As his Maths colleague had once remarked, just about every first-year student of Relativity knew that when the proper-time of a transition from A to B is zero, the relative time of that same transition is the non-zero time s/c. Why, then, were we being so stubbornly starved of understanding in the midst of plenty? The answer, he decided, had to be the sheer social momentum of modern physical science in the direction fixed by standardised and standardising Education, which he was now thanking his lucky stars for having missed-out on during his mentally formative days of wandering around in scrap-yards and experimenting with the bits he found, when he should have been at school. His natural, free-range curiosity had enabled him now to take these 'diabolical liberties' with The System which were unthinkable to the Educationally robed and endowed. And what was perfectly plain to him from his now matured, albeit Educationally unclassifiable viewpoint was that quantum instantaneity and relativistic time-delay were completely and naturally reconciled in themselves, without any need for those academic cavortions which he could not regard as anything but silly. It was not as though he was saying to these people —as he had heard so many theorists say, in effect— 'My theory explains everything so simply that if you don't accept it you must be a fool!' So far as his own default theory was concerned, it was not so much a theory as a thesis, a logical reduction of the mash of opaque theory to a clear wine of empirical fact. Yet it remained, at every stage, as open to question as any thesis should be by inviting the dialectical presentation of fair and challenging antitheses (the scarcity of which, in his case, the reader will no doubt have noted).

However, regardless of the fortunes or misfortunes of this thesis at the social level, the fact remained, incontestably, that the solution which so many theorists were seeking to the problem of the alleged disparity between Relativity and Quantum Theory had been there all along in the currently accepted time-equation of Special Relativity! Surely, he thought, some one or another of those students his Maths colleague had mentioned (unless, of course, tongue-tied at the temerity of it) should have noticed this! For instance, such a student, looking at Einstein's relativistic time-equation, viz.:

$$t_R = t_P / \sqrt{(1 - v^2/c^2)}.$$

would see that substituting for v in this equation the equivalent expression s/tR produced

$$t_R = \sqrt{[(s/c)^2 + t_P^2]}$$

which was the Pythagorean equation with which he himself had started (see equation (1) Ch. 3). Someone other than himself must surely have realised that according to these equations, for a quantum of light-interaction between two atoms a distance s apart, the speed v in the first of these equations is c, whence tP in that same equation is zero. That person should then have seen that in the second equation, with tP being zero, tR is s/c. These equations would be telling him that there are two speeds for the same light-interaction. The one, following the nomenclature of Cynthia Whitney [75], is the 'Galilean' speed V = s/tP, the upper limit of which is infinite or instantaneous, and the other is the 'Einstein' speed v = s/tR, with its finite upper limit c (compare Figures 4 and 5, Chapter VIII). So why had no-one recognised what these equations were so plainly telling us, that instantaneous and time-delayed are components of the same light-interaction?

[75] Whitney, C. K., 'The Mass-Connected Photon', in Causality and Locality in Modern Physics, eds. Hunter, G., Jeffers, S. Vigier, J.P., Kluwer, Dordrecht (1998), pp. 219 - 226.

The answer to that question was the difficulty of visualising that situation without some acceptable intuitive model for explaining how a light-signal can be both intrinsically instantaneous and relativistically time-delayed.

XII.1 'Together while apart': a 'movie' model

However, he realised, there was a model, a very natural and familiar one, which provided the essential clue as to how that instantaneity and time-delay may be combined. This was the movie in which what the film-director describes as the action of the movie, instantaneity and time-delay exist together in the time sequencing of the purely distance-extended frames or 'stills'. In other words, the measures s in the second of the above pair of equations, may be taken as pure distance extensions or 'stills' (i.e., when tP in that equation is zero). This is analogous to the way in which objects in a single photograph are extended and separated, all in a single instant in time. Any non-zero time tP (i.e., for any speed s/tP = V of a body which is finite) can then be conceived as the sequence-time of those instantaneous extensions s in the running of the film, with s/tR being the speed v of that same body relative to the viewer (observer) of the motion, up to the limit c. With that natural analogy in mind, he felt, there should be no difficulty in conceiving the situation described by the equations, which was that at every stage in their delayed-time interactions, as observed by Römer, et al., physical objects are also instantly connected at the quantum level in the manner envisaged by Mach. At a conference in Cambridge our proponent of this movie model concluded his paper by writing on the blackboard the following piece of verse [76]:

I feel that I must be emphatic

That quantum extensions are static

Between A and B,

While at the rate c,

Their sequences are cinematic.

This 'cinematic' analogy, as a model for the delayed-time sequences of instantaneous quantum connection should therefore, he thought, logically remove any need for theories of 'spooky superluminal' pilot waves and/or virtual particles which shoot ahead of the light and show the quanta (the so-called 'photons') where to go. The quanta would 'know where to go' because, being instantaneous, they would be there as soon as they 'started out'. It would also explain why (other than in the minds of highly imaginative metaphysicians) in experiments with these so-called 'photons' there is no accounting for what happens to those 'photons' and other quantum entities in their quantum jumps between emission and absorption. Any such description would be completely indeterminate and therefore pointless to pursue — like trying account for what happens to the mouse and the cat or their atomic parts between the stills in a 'Tom and Jerry' film-cartoon [77].

[76] ANPA, Cambs., England, 1996.

[77] 'Why a cartoon film?' someone asked. His answer was that this was in order to field the objection which others had raised that in the shooting of an ordinary film there are always intermediate events in the real world between the frames, as distinct from the absolute discreteness of the frames (drawings) in the making of a cartoon film. In the quantum-relativistic account, if carried through consistently, there is no such behind-the-scenes 'real world' which is not entirely metaphysical.

XII.2 The continuous discontinuum

Of course, this conceptual readjustment, from mechanism to cinematography as a model of quantum physics would have been, to say the least, unsettling to any bona fide Physics student, because it would not have been possible without his having to consider a wholesale paradigm shift in his standard ideas of perception. For instance, it would entail abandoning the classical 'analog' way of thinking about space as the pre-existing conveyor of photons between distance-separated bodies and adopting in its place a comparatively digital interpretation of those photons as intrinsically distanceless quanta out of which that space is optically projected [78]. This would be to adopt a view of optical space as being like that of a video-scenario —which, from the standpoint of our mechanistic tradition, carries overtones of subjectivism. That is to say, it offends our reflexively materialistic (so-called 'realist') ways of thinking by suggesting that the physicality of objects consists of nothing more than informational patterns and sequences of observational pixels [79].

Well, at least, by combining quantum instantaneity and relativistic time-delay in this 'cinematic' way, the student might well have claimed to have settled the dispute between Einstein and Bohr over their respective interpretations of distant interaction. Unfortunately for his career prospects, this would have landed him in the mire of Berkeleyan idealism from which, like our telephone lineman, he might not have emerged for many long years.

Undoubtedly, then, this now Educationally bemused and disadvantaged Physics student would have been pressed, as our ex-lineman had, to provide a more substantial, more physical explanation of the instantaneous pure-lengths $s$ separating bodies in angular momentum. What, his tutors might have asked him, was the physical nature of that instantaneous quantum connection in the case of light, since there was nothing like it in the standard electrodynamics of Maxwell and Einstein. The student might, of course, have discovered that, as some of our ex-lineman's latter-day colleagues had pointed out, there had been something like that instantaneous longitudinal connection in the earlier electrodynamics of Helmholtz (1821-1894), whose theories had been discarded (unjustly, so these colleagues judged) in favour of Faraday and Maxwell [80]. As far as he could gather, Helmholtz's equations had included the description of an instantaneous electrodynamical connection whose nature was assumed to be that of an incompressible 'ether' in which electrical disturbances were conducted at an instantaneous speed in accordance with the assumed infinite density of the medium [81]. To postulate an infinitely dense ether, however, would have been a retrograde step for the student to take in answer to the question of the nature of the instantaneous pure-distance connection $s$. As his tutors would surely have reminded him, it would have been a theoretical throwback to the pre-relativity days of the ether and how to detect it, raising the necessity of having to wrestle all over again with all the old questions regarding the notorious failure of Michelson and Morley [82] to detect the existence of any kind of 'ether' —far less one of infinite density.

There would also be the problem of stasis with the idea of such an unbroken underlying continuum —that is, the problem of 'cosmical gridlock' encountered by the Eleatic monists. So far as

[78] This difference between 'analog' and 'digital' will be explained in more detail later, see Chapter XIII.

[79] See Pope, N. V., 'The New World Synthesis', Philosophia Mathematica II. 4, 1, 23-28 (1987); also Pope, N. V., 'The Overdue Revolution' MENSA, 23-24, (April 1987).

[80] Smirnov-Rueda, R. and Chubykalo, A. E., 'Incompleteness of Classical Electrodynamics and Longitudinal Non-local Electromagnetic Action as one of the Missing Elements', Causality and Locality in Modern Physics, eds. G. Hunter, et. al., Kluwer Dordrecht (1998), pp. 119–126.

[81] As is well known, the speed of a compression-wave in a medium is proportional to the density of the medium; so in an infinitely dense medium the speed of the wave-conduction would also be infinite.

[82] See chapter II.

our odysseyist was concerned there were no such problems because, for him, this question of the nature of $s$ was already answered. It was the distance in metres we observe and measure between one body and another in angular momentum relations of the sort already described. In angular momentum there could be no problem of gridlock because in that measure, by definition, motion is a built-in conception—that is, it is defined as the motion, or momentum, of masses with respect to a common centre. Also, unlike Helmholtz’s continuous and incompressible ether, his angular momentum field is a discontinuum, or discretum, which analyses out ultimately into those dimensional irreducibles, the quantum stills of angular momentum in units of $h$. So for him there was no question of ‘gridlock’, or Eleatic stasis.

XII.3 ‘The Intellectual Problem’

As our adventurer saw it, then, the only way to make a clean break with these past theoretical cavortions was to tackle what someone dubbed ‘The Intellectual Problem’. This was to consider a wholesale revision of our customary notions of space, time and matter. Such, at any rate, was the philosophical transition he was urging in seminars at his Midlands house during the 1970s and 80s. In these seminars his aim, as he presented it, had been to explore the possibility of extending into physics what he regarded as the radical shift that had taken place in philosophy from the Remote Realism of Democritus and Descartes, in which things and observers are separated by ‘void’, to the direct realism of Aristotle and Moore in which things and observers occupy the same world—the world of commonsense as opposed to that of Berkeleyan idealism. The name of this new philosophy, as those imbued with the idea agreed to call it, was Normal Realism, as described in Chapter VIII. In these seminars much use had been made of a term coined by a member of the group who had been a fellow student at university. This was used to describe the conceptual transition from the metaphysical Remote Realism to empirical Normal Realism. The term was ‘conceptual flipover’, and it stuck. It was used to denote the same sort of mental process that occurs in what Gestalt Psychologists describe as the radical switch in perceptual organisation which can arise from seeing a set of dots or lines as forming one configuration to seeing it as forming another, as in ambiguous pictures of the sort associated with Escher (see Figs. 1 and 2, chapter VII.2). The conceptual ‘flipover’ in the context of these discussions was the radical shift in thinking which, if carried through consistently into physics, would mean that what we customarily think of as the ‘void’, or space, would become a substance, and what Democritus had thought of as the substance (i.e., the ultimate quanta which he conceived as ‘atoms’) would become the void. In other words, in Normal Realism, following Aristotle and Moore, the substance of physical reality becomes angular momentum, as manifest in our commonsense perceptions of the separations of satellites, planets, stars and so on, which we commonly call space; and the void (voids) becomes the quantum jumps or cinematic discontinuities, in action-units $h$, that take place on the ultimate microphysical level between quantum angular momentum frames or stills in static units $h$.

To carry this ‘flipover’ through consistently proved mentally difficult, mostly from having to think of space—the traditional ‘void’—as substantial. Yet that was the inevitable consequence for physics of the proposed philosophical transition. However, as he learned from the sorts of questions with which he was continually assailed, it was all a very difficult conceptual pill for his readers and listeners to swallow. For instance, one of the parties to the discussion at the time—a MENSA member, no less—described the philosophical ‘flipover’ as being, intellectually, ‘like trying to flip over a mile-long wet mattress’. But then, it would be, wouldn’t it, our odysseyist replied, since it meant having to invert just about everything that we had been traditionally taught in science. Steeped in it though he was, he even found himself, on occasions, failing to understand his own thesis due, as he later discovered, to the mental interference set up by conceptions he had imbibed, so to speak, with his mother’s milk.

A particular difficulty which kept cropping up in these discussions was that of how, exactly, to picture in one's mind the ways in which bodies were supposed to interact in an angular momentum field. As he explained to those who were puzzled about it, at the ultimate microphysical level these angular momentum connections were discrete, involving no more than two entities at one time, such as the 'electron' and 'proton' discussed in Chapter XI.2. This quantum bolas with just those two masses, forming the simplest atom (of hydrogen), was the fundamental unit of angular momentum $h$ —or some multiple $n$ of such units according to the atom's level of excitation. The way in which those atoms interact could then be likened to the familiar children's game of 'touch' ('tick' or 'tag', as it is sometimes called). In that game, when A touches B, C, D ... or whoever, and calls 'touch', that person —say, D— has to go on to touch someone else. That may be any one of the others in the game but never more than just one at a time, and not in any particular order of sequence. This touching is continuous in the sense that for the game to continue, whoever is touched must sooner or later touch some other player. These touchings, as between atoms, are not only continuous in the above sense but are also instantaneous, insofar as A touching B and B being touched by A take place in the same instant —that is to say, as described in Newton's law of instantaneous, equal and opposite action and reaction. Moreover, in this continuous sequence of instantaneously reciprocal atomic touchings, the touchings, like those in the game, do not take place all at once but are sporadic. This is why the touching ensues without the consequence of an Eleatic stasis —the situation imagined by the Eleatic philosophers in which all the touchings are simultaneous, so that nothing can move without moving everything else.

As he presented it, then, angular momentum is instantaneous, continuous, discontinuous and conserved. That is, it is i.) instantaneous in the sense that the interactions between atoms, no matter how many we may think of as taking place concurrently in statistical terms, all take place on a strictly one-to-one basis of proper-time-instantaneous quantum exchange —what, following Gilbert Lewis, have been described as 'quantum touchings' [83]; ii.) continuous in the sense that an atom which is touched in this way is energised and so, in that state may, like the children 'touched' in the game, go on to touch, or energise some other atom which is initially less energised than itself; iii.) discontinuous in the sense that these touchings do not take place all together, i.e., simultaneously, in the way that would create an Eleatic gridlock, and, iv.) conserved in the sense that in all these myriads of different exchanges, no touch ever appears 'out of the blue'. That is to say, no angular momentum comes from or disappears into nowhere but remains the same, throughout all those atomic exchanges in accordance with the law of overall conservation.

However, many with whom he discussed these revolutionary ideas found his analogies difficult to swallow. For instance, some of his fellow members of the Cambridge group, ANPA (q.v.) continued to protest that these quantum units of angular momentum could not possibly form anything like a continuum at the macroscopic level. There is no way, they said, in which things that are discrete and dissociate can combine to create the order and continuity we see all around us. The only way in which that order could be constructed, so far as they were concerned, was by combinations of pure numbers according to strict rules of mathematics (combinatorics). To these he pointed out that their objection, based as it was upon traditional presuppositions of 'bottom-up' constructivism (reductionism) did not signify in the contrastingly 'top-down', holistic approach that he was exploring, namely, that of Normal

[83] See Pope, N.V., 'A Quantum Digital Theory of Light', in The Present Status of the Quantum Theory of Light, eds. Jeffers et. al. Kluwer, Dordrecht, 1997, pp. 495-502.

Realism. Time and again, he would have to reiterate that in this different way of thinking the substance—hence the reality—of things resides essentially in the macro level of experience. This is the level of commonsense observation as described by people like Moore and Austin. It was not to be compared with—far less squared with—the metaphysical, God’s-eye-view projections of atomic self-sufficiency by Democritus and Descartes or with the ‘pure numbers’ projections of the likes of Pythagoras and the advocates of the ‘combinatorial hierarchies’. The ultimate ‘elements’ of nature, so far as Normal Realism was concerned, were neither the ultimately hard and dense cosmic ‘bricks’ of Democritan physics nor the ‘pure numbers’ of these Pythagoreans. They were the ultimate terminals of reduction to which, empirically and in relation to one another, all substances on the observationally real level—even the ‘hardest’ of these, such as mass—reduced, without remainder.

Difficult though it was, undoubtedly, he could see no logical contradiction in this revolutionary, flip-side way of thinking about atoms and their light-interactions, just so long as it was followed-through consistently without adulteration with incongruous precepts and foreign theoretical agendas. On this consistently-adhered-to Normal Realist understanding, the way in which he would summarise his understanding of light-interaction was that when an electron in one atom loses a quantum of angular momentum the electron jumps inwards towards the nucleus while at the same instant, due to the conservation of angular momentum, another electron in another atom somewhere else, immediately jumps outwards in resonance with that transfer of energy. (See equation (8), Ch. VII). The transfer of that angular momentum in the form of action (energy multiplied by time), in units $h$ has to be immediate because the law of angular momentum conservation permits of no time-delay—‘no holding pattern’, as one of his contacts vividly described it [84]. In the relative macro-time (ordinary time) of those bodies of which the atoms (the angular momentum boluses $nh$) are the analysed parts there might, of course, be any number of similar atomic angular-momentum couplings and decouplings taking place in other places, both inside those bodies and between them. There is thus, macroscopically, no discernible instant during which those bodies (e.g., the earth, moon and sun or the ordinary-sized parts of those bodies) are disconnected. The same could not be said, as he was continually forced to emphasise, of the individual atoms, because being irreducible, those quantum interactions cannot be shared with more than just those two atoms in the one instance, which makes those paired atomic interactions absolutely discrete and dissociate. (In the terms used by logicians, those interactions are intransitive.)

He had also to remind his adversaries, on many occasions, that these angular momentum couplings, both macro- and micro-physical were the moment-arms $s$ by which the masses are separated and on the ends of which they are instantly (i.e., reciprocally) balanced, holistically on the astronomical scale and in discrete pairs at the quantum level. The cinematic jumps from one of these quantum links (or stills) to another in the case of ordinary-sized and astronomical bodies are practically invisible, so that the changes in their angular momentum appear to all intents and purposes seamless. This is in the same way that the Brownian (i.e., thermal molecular) motion which is visible in particles like pollen grains cannot be detected in such things as billiard-balls and planets. However, at the quantum level where this seamlessness disappears, all the usual mechanical laws also disappear and randomness reigns supreme. As he continually had to stress, for all their discreteness, there was no logical way of dealing with those quantum events as isolated autonomous entities. Nor was there any way of even thinking of them as existing or being capable of doing anything in their own right—far less in accordance with the classical rules of mechanics as applied to ordinary objects.

This did not mean that the occurrences of those fundamental events were ungoverned. They were governed by rules applied to the wholes of which they are the analysed parts—statistical rules like

[84] Thomas E. Phipps (Jr.) in a private discussion at the PIRT (Physical Interpretations of Relativity) conference in London, September, 1990.

that of entropy, from which all macrophysical rules stem and which cannot be applied to the individual events but only to very large numbers of them. At the ultimate microphysical level, as his thesis described it, in the angular momentum quantum stills the connection between the balanced masses (the electron and proton) was instantaneous, whereas the period of each angular momentum cycle in itself was not instantaneous but irreducible —like the small but non-zero time during which a cinematographic still stands in the gate of the projector before being replaced by the next in sequence. Nor was the time-interval separating a pair of angular momentum stills instantaneous. Like the angular momentum period it, too, had a definite period, one which was, again, non-zero but irreducible. What is not covered by the cinematic analogy, he stressed in his explanation, was the transfer of that difference in the internal angular momentum states within the first atom to another atom in accordance with the conservation of angular momentum. When one photographic still replaces another in the gate of a cine-projector, the difference in the two frames doesn't have to appear immediately somewhere else. In the angular momentum field, however, that is precisely what has to happen. What is transferred, is the quantum jump in the form of action. The period of that transferred action, he reminded his listeners, was that of the quantum of mass-energy which is manifest in the spectroscope in those series of fractions shown in equation. (8), Chapter VII. He also reminded them that as distinct from its intrinsic quantum period (its irreducible 'internal time', so to speak) the transfer-time of that same quantum from the atom in the light-source to the one in the spectrograph was zero, regardless of whatever might be the macroscopically measured distance between those two objects.

In this way, he argued, with all these instantaneous linkages between atoms in terms, alternately, of angular momentum stills and action-jumps between those stills, the overall angular momentum, on the macroscopic (i.e. datum-of-reality) scale, was constantly maintained. This provided the holistic cosmical background envisaged by Mach, of which the poet Francis Thompson had written: 'Thou canst not stir a flower without the troubling of a star' [85]. However, like Heraclitus' candle-flame [86] burning serenely in still air, all permanence and continuity of the system at these macroscopic levels was a phenomenon which, although strictly governed by statistical laws like those of thermodynamics, analysed-out ultimately to a complete chaos of violent underlying change. At that level, in the words of the late Richard Feynman, 'everything is jiggling' —jiggling, our freethinker would add, unpredictably in and out of existence like the spaces between the dots on the screen in a video drama. Little wonder, he reflected, that these so-called 'elementary particles' defied all the rules of logic as applied to ordinary objects, in experiments where they were reported as switching miraculously between being 'particles' and 'waves'. However, as he perceived it, in an angular momentum field, where all particle-interactions are resonances involving distance-times $s/c$ and associated relative times $t_R$, it was scarcely surprising that so many experimenters report that particles act like waves without necessarily being waves —or, indeed, even particles as ordinarily understood.

XII.4 The mysterious two-slit experiment

[85] Francis Thompson, 'The Mistress of Vision', line 22. This macroscopic continuity envisaged by Thompson and Mach is, in angular momentum terms, not absolute but is conditional upon the extent to which the number of quanta involved in the causal event is sharable as an effect —in the way that a prize consisting of a hundred of the smallest coins can be distributed equally among no more than a hundred winners.

[86] Heraclitus of Ephesus: a Greek philosopher who lived around 400 B.C. A disciple of Plato's academy, he was also an atomist and much influenced by Pythagoreanism, hence very much a thinker in his own right. The emphasis on Heraclitus' philosophy was the recognition of strife in nature, or 'the conflict of opposites' as a proactive natural principle.

This seemed to have some bearing on the famous and mysterious two-slit experiment, first carried out by Thomas Young in 1801. In that experiment, a beam of monochromatic light (light which is all of the same frequency) is shone on a screen having passed through a pair of slits in an intermediate screen so that on the final screen there is an area where the illumination from the two slits overlaps. In that overlapping area, Young observed a pattern of dark and light bands, demonstrating what is classically interpreted as 'interference fringes' due to the assumed 'wave' nature of light.

What makes this phenomenon, as Richard Feynman described it, the 'biggest problem in physics' is that since Planck and Einstein proved that light is quantised in little particles which Einstein called 'photons', there is no commonsense way of explaining how these little bullet-like objects can 'interfere' in that wave-like way. If we think of those 'photons' striking the screen like a hail of shot, then in the overlapping area we should expect to find a more or less even distribution of strikes with a density greater than in the remaining areas. The fact that these shot-like photon-strikes form patterns of 'interference' is therefore generally regarded as a complete and utter mystery.

Nor is that mystery alleviated by the fact that even when the intensity of the light is reduced to one photon at a time the accumulated pattern of strikes, over a sufficiently long period, displays that same pattern of interference. This makes it seem as though each photon goes through both slits at once to interfere with itself in the manner of a wave. Moreover, any attempt to detect the passage of a photon through either one of the slits destroys the interference pattern on the final screen. The effect is as though the photon anticipates that attempt at detection in the one slit and, perversely, decides to go entirely through the other. Nor was that the end of it. Further experiments had confirmed than not only do 'photons' behave in that 'clairvoyant' manner, at their limit-speed c but so do particles such as electrons and protons, and even whole atoms, at their slower speeds v.

The 'official' explanation of all this was that since it defeats commonsense, then commonsense must be ruled inadequate. (If the news is bad, then shoot the messenger.) As Bohr interpreted it, these difficulties with logic and commonsense showed that nature, as it presents itself to scientists, is no business of either logic or commonsense. To be a 'proper scientist', Bohr's pupils were told, one simply had to accept the facts as they stand (i.e., 'positivistically'), no matter how illogical and unintuitive they might seem, and that for the purposes of science from thenceforward the usual urge to seek 'explanations' was naïve and should be suppressed by the true intelligentsia.

So, once again, with modern Science as with classical Religion, there were priests claiming that the cosmos was confiding in them, to the exclusion of the common man. This, he thought, might satisfy physicists, but he himself, for obvious reasons, could not dismiss commonsense in such a cavalier fashion. So far as he was concerned, if an idea could not be explained in commonsense terms, then something was wrong, not with commonsense but with that idea. In this frame of mind, he considered the two-slit problem.

From any commonsense point of view, he reflected, it was plain that the only thing that could 'go through' the two slits and 'interfere' in that way were not particles in their tracks but the tracks themselves. In his angular momentum discretum, all lengths are resonances in terms, not of space-travelling waves but of pure and direct quantum touchings. In accordance with the well-confirmed de Broglie hypothesis, if mv is the momentum with which a particle is ejected from a source, then dividing Planck's constant h by that momentum expresses a quantum length λ (lambda) which physicists call the de Broglie wavelength [87]. Evidently, then, in the two-slit experiment, where the only routes from the source to the final screen are those projected through the slits, we should expect, as a matter of pure geometry, that where those routes (measured in units of the quantum cycle-lengths for whatever particle-energies are involved) converge at the final screen they will meet either constructively or

[87] See, e.g., Blanchard, C. H.; Burnett, C. R; Stoner, R. G.; and Weber, R.L., Introduction to Modern Physics, Pitman & Sons, London, 2nd ed. (1969) p. 175.

destructively in terms of the positive and negative halves of that cycle. Such in-phase and out-of-phase conditions in the geometrical lengths of the paths between the source and screen through the slits are thus sufficient in themselves to define the probabilities of direct-action resonances (quantum touchings) between the source-atoms and the absorber atoms at the various points on the screen. The pattern of these probabilities of distant direct-action between the source and screen, when traced out geometrically, is precisely that of the so-called 'wave-interference'.

That, of course, is without any particles actually travelling along those paths, so the pattern of interference actually observed when particles are involved depends on the actual length $\lambda$ of their emission-energy at the time. When those emissions are 'photons', their arrival at the screen is, of course, proper-time-instantaneous. In the case of the 'slower-than $c$' particles, the time between emission and arriving is the non-zero proper-time $t = s/V$, where $s$ is the distance in ordinary metres from the point of their emission to their point of arrival, and $V$ is the distance they travel in their own (i.e., proper) time $t$.

What, it might be asked, are those particles doing during that time, which might be of any length whatsoever? The short answer is: whatever is most probable as defined by the resonance-metric of the discretum.; for it had to be remembered that at this level of 'elementary particles' there are no well-defined, determinate tracks to be considered, not even in the case of a particle received from the farthest galaxy. According, then, to the built-in uncertainty of events on that quantum-digital or 'cinematic' level, the classically conceived question of what is happening to a particle when it is not interacting with anything is, again, no more significant than asking what is happening to the characters between the stills of a movie cartoon.

The time, therefore, 'during' which a particle travels from the source to the target-area in a two-slit type of experiment cannot be the sort of time we usually think of in which we might interrupt its flight, like hitting a ball with a cricket bat. In that two-slit apparatus, as in the world at large, the trajectories of the elementary mass-quanta, no matter how fast or slow their 'speed', are determined not by their own localised momentum in the way Galileo and Newton envisaged but by the non-localised or holistic resonance-metric of the angular momentum discretum—and even there, not determinately but probabilistically.

Plainly, then, anything placed in either one of the slits in order to detect the flight of the particle would be ineffectual because all it could do would be to cancel one set of geometrical options for source-screen resonance along that particular track. Unlike placing a road-block to be encountered by a motorist in the midst of a journey from A to B, the effect of the detector would be more like obliterating a whole road, so that any journey along it cannot be even contemplated.

So, he concluded, there is no need to think of any particle in the two-slit experiment as mysteriously having to navigate the two slits at once in order to create the typical fringe-pattern of interference. On pure commonsense grounds, with the angular momentum resonance-metric in mind, it is perfectly predictable that when one or the other of those resonance-routes is made unavailable, by the insertion of a detector anywhere in that length, no matter whether it be before or after the slits, only one path remains open and there is no interference. There is no mysterious 'clairvoyance' or 'telepathy' on the part of the travelling entity, be it an electron, photon or whatever, nor of any mysterious 'superluminal guiding-wave' preceding its passage, like cockpit radar homing an aircraft. Nothing 'goes through the two slits at once' but only the routes, or paths themselves, which presents no mystery whatsoever. The particles/waves in the experiment are directed by those paths as instantly extended wholes whose lengths are either congruent or incongruent in terms of the wavelength $\lambda = h/mv$ of the emission energy. Furthermore, he reflected, wherever the resonance-probabilities may be reduced to zero—in exactly ninety-degrees out-of-phase geometrical conditions—the effect, so far as the possibility of that specific interaction is concerned, is to erase that particular route from the face of the earth.

In short, then, those elementary particles may go only where the routes for them are geometrically congruent, with probabilities as defined in what quantum physicists call the 'wave-function'. The difference, however, was that in his own probability metric there is no underlying wave-continuum. His contention, therefore, was that the universe, such as it is —pluriverse' or 'polyverse' it had been suggested was a more suitable description— is not, on its ultimate analytical level, an Eleatic continuum. It is a Heraclitan discretum, with a metric defined in terms of purely transient interaction opportunities, with there being nothing in between —not even empty space. Also, in complete opposition to the Democritan tradition and more in the Aristotelian way of thinking, for him the substance of that discretum lay in what we perceive (directly and/or instrumentally), not in realms of pure speculation underlying and forever inaccessible to actual perception, such as the absurd assumption of a wave-continuum, or 'wave-function', that exists behind our perceptions of things and in which equal and opposite quantum states (probabilities) exist superimposed.

XII.5 The problem of the Doppler effect

At this stage, our odysseyist was reminded of the problem which had troubled him earlier and which he had not been able to solve at that time (see Ch. VII.1). This was the problem of how to explain, in these instantaneous-connection terms, the well-known phenomenon of the Doppler effect in which the frequency of light is increased or decreased as its source travels away from or towards the observer. It was the same problem as was encountered by the Helmholtzian theory, in which the infinitely dense 'ether' performed the same function between emitting and absorbing atoms as do the massless moment-arm in angular momentum. If it were allowed that bodies could move in such an infinitely dense medium (which, logically, they could not), there was no way in which that motion could create a Doppler effect. This, of course, is because where every position of a body is signalled to the observer instantly there can be no time-difference incurred by any change in that position, hence no Doppler increase or decrease in observational time due to that change in distance. This left the Helmholtzian theory bereft of any explanation of that familiar Doppler phenomenon.

However, the same applied to his own instantaneous angular-momentum connection, which was why he had not been able to solve that problem earlier. But now, in the context of the cinematic conception, the solution was clear. That instantaneous, Doppler-immune connection [88] was just one component of the light interaction, the direct longitudinal angular-momentum equivalent, in atomic terms, of the celluloid-connection between objects in cinematographic 'stills'. In the quantum jumps between these stills, there might take place, in addition to the angular momentum change within the atoms, a change in the distance s between the atoms themselves. The overall effect of this second change would be to extend or reduce the intrinsic time of that exchanged quantum from ntO to

$$t _ { \mathrm { Q } } = ( n t _ { \mathrm { O } } \pm \Delta s / c ) / \sqrt { ( 1 - v ^ { 2 } / c ^ { 2 } ) }$$

This was equivalent to the relativistic Doppler effect, usually conceived as the effect on waves propagated in space due to relative motion between their sources and sinks [89]. In his cinematic

[88] N.B. Thomas Van Flandern reports discovering an observational distinction between the 'present' (instantaneous) position of distant objects such as the sun and planets, in what he classically conceives as a 'gravitational' connection, and their light-aberrated (e.g., Doppler-affected) position which he conceives as 'electromagnetic'. (See T. Van Flandern, 'The Speed of Gravity —What the Experiments Say', paper presented at the Vigier II Symposium, York University, North York, Ontario, Canada, Aug. 25-29, 1997.)

conception, of course, the effect takes place purely in terms of distance and time without any need of the usual talk about waves and how they are mediated by any medium, be it 'ether', 'field' or whatever.

On balance, then, he decided, it was easier (albeit less familiar) to think of the nature of the instantaneous connection which quantum physics required between atoms as being that of angular momentum. This was a measure known to just about anyone in the commonsense field of mechanical engineering. It was therefore easier to think of that connection in terms of angular momentum than in terms of the completely ad hoc and esoteric 'fields' and 'ethers' of classical gravitational and electrodynamical theory. Moreover, to switch from these various 'field' conceptions to the single conception of a unified angular momentum nexus required no great and traumatic upheaval of language. The traditional word 'field' could be used quite suitably to describe the overall angular momentum connection without raising ontological and epistemological problems as to its nature. Indeed, as he reassured his Electrodynamics colleagues, even their familiar word 'charge' could still be employed to denote the $2 \times 10^{-18}$ joule of spin kinetic energy which (with apologies to Goudsmit and Ühlenbeck) he had ascribed, in chapter XI.2, to the mass which G. J. Stoney, in 1881, had dubbed the 'electron'.

Nevertheless, this assessment of the advantages of angular momentum over the usual 'fields' and 'ethers' was not shared by his Electrodynamics colleagues, who chose to stick with the existing esotery. Their reasons, as they explained, were that 'Now is not the time for such radical changes'. In 'fifty-or-so year's time,' they conceded, such radical changes might be considered, 'but not now'. It was not a matter of whether his thesis could or could not be refuted. It was a question of pure politics because their careers in the Electrodynamics industry would undoubtedly suffer if they sought, from within it, to accommodate his ideas

What they asked of him, therefore, was his help in making some comparatively small but significant adjustments to the existing electrodynamics of Faraday, Maxwell and Einstein towards reinstating the 'instantaneous-connection' theory of Helmholtz. This, they estimated, would be difficult enough to get accepted by the main-line journals without going to such lengths as to challenge the whole establishment. Now approaching his seventies, he was much amused by this advice, from colleagues who were considerably his juniors, to keep these radical ideas under his hat 'for another fifty years'. Even assuming that he would live so long, what did they propose he should do in the meantime, whittle wood or play cribbage?

Sir Karl Popper, predicting this kind of reception his radical ideas could expect, had advised him, early on, that in order to get them accepted he should consider how they might be tested experimentally, without making too much fuss of the philosophy behind them. If that should lead to any dramatic discovery, said Popper, then, as a result, the philosophy might be seriously considered and perhaps even accepted. Acting on Popper's good (and eminently pragmatic) advice, he had formulated a proposition which he subsequently peddled to various scientists at different universities.

XII.6 Prospects of an action-at-a-distance experiment

The gist of this experimental proposal was that if quantum exchanges are truly instantaneous and conserved, then an emitter cannot release a quantum without that quantum being immediately assured of a billet at some perhaps far distant absorber. This prediction, he thought, should in principle be experimentally testable. People he approached with this proposition showed much interest, and in many

[89] 'Doppler 'wavelength' formula: $\lambda = \lambda_0 (1 + \nu_c/c)/(1 - \nu^2/c^2)$', where $\lambda_0$ is the wavelength measured by the observer at rest, $\nu$ is the line-of-sight velocity and $\nu$ is the relative velocity of the observer (see Dictionary of Physics, Penguin Books, 2nd ed. London (1991) p. 125).

cases followed up their interest with correspondence and discussion [90]. Indeed, some perfunctory experiments were actually performed. These were along the lines that if, in a steady flow of instantaneous quanta, the flow could be blocked at some distant point (by the creation of out-of-phase conditions, say), then if it could be shown that as a result, those quanta would be prevented from leaving their source or were diverted elsewhere, then that would be sufficient proof of their instantaneity.

Now it had occurred to him, of course, that such an experiment had already been done and that it had confirmed this hypothesis. This was the Thomas Young experiment (q.v. XII.3) in which precisely that kind of flow-diversion took place from those parts of the screen which were non-receptive towards those parts which were. That is to say, where the intensity of the illumination was reduced at one place it was increased at another, so that the overall intensity remained unaffected—which was plain and sufficient proof of the predicted quantum blocking.

However, in the two centuries since those experimental results were published, so dense had become the fog of sheer mystification as to how ‘photons’ could behave in that seemingly ‘telepathic’ way, that this obvious, commonsense demonstration could hardly be expected to be recognised. Some other, much more novel and exciting way would have to be devised of demonstrating that same principle, based on grounds of commonsense logic into which, hopefully, the mystification had so far not reached. As he described it to some interested parties, the aim was to devise, with ‘flows of photons’, an experiment analogous to an ordinary engineering one in which water flows along a steel pipe. In that analogous water-experiment, between the tap and the pipe a rubber bulb would be inserted. This would show, by its swelling, any backpressure in the pipe caused by blocking of the flow at the far end.

In this analogous water-pipe experiment, there would be two ways of passing messages between observers at opposite ends of the pipe. One would be for the tap-end observer to inject into the flow something like the yellow dye that water engineers sometimes use to show leaks. That dye, emerging from the far end of the pipe would be like the ordinary sort of light-signal that Galileo flashed to his manservant from their respective mountain tops in their rudimentary attempt to measure the ‘speed of light’. The other sort of message, analogous to demonstrating the instantaneous quantum-blocking connection, would be for the observer at the far end of the pipe to resist the flow of the water at that point by stamping, in Morse code, say, on his end of the pipe and for the observer at the tap-end to read the message in the resulting swelling of the bulb (water being taken, of course, as incompressible for the purpose of the experiment).

In a similar way, he reasoned, light or microwave radiation might be channelled in such a way that a wave-guide or whatever would confine the light just like the water in the tube. By inhibiting the reception of the light at the far end (by introducing out-of-phase conditions or something of that sort) the instantaneous quantum transition would be blocked in that channel, causing a glitch at the emitter analogous to the swelling of the bulb in the water analogy.

Simple though this concept appeared in principle, it soon encountered practical difficulties which seemed insuperable. Some people dismissed the analogy outright on the grounds (fallacious, of course) that ‘water is not incompressible’. Others, with far more logical justification, judged that in purely practical terms the effect to be observed at the emitter-end of the light-flow would be, according to the water-analogy, like stamping on the end of a miles-long fisherman’s sock.
