(Their meaning, of course, was that instead of swelling the bulb at the source, the blocking at the far end of the conduit would cause the water to squirt out just about everywhere else, so that any glitch at the source would be virtually undetectable.) Moreover, others queried, even if the anticipated result were positively

[90] Philosophical Glimpses, Vol. 4, pp 1167-70f; Vol. 6, 2083-4, 2090-2092; Vol. 11, 3960-60f, 3965.

obtained, how would that effect be distinguishable from that of standing waves set up by the to-ing and fro-ing of light in the conventionally conceived way at its propagational speed $c$?

In that commonly conceived way, light emitted from a spherical source such as the sun, say, propagates in vacuo in all directions, regardless of whether or not there is in that volume anything to receive it. The trouble was that since quantum light-interactions are distance-independent, the same omnidirectional propagation of light could be taken as evidence of the existence, on average, of a sufficient number of evenly distributed absorbers, all over, to make it seem as though space itself was the absorber of light travelling in the conventionally conceived 'space-travelling' way.

This seeming 'conspiracy of nature' against being able to distinguish observationally between the two interpretations led some of his contacts to suggest a method based on the detection of anisotropies (unevennesses) of distribution in the astronomical masses. One such suggestion appealed to the anisotropy recorded by the COBE satellite which is interpreted as a left-over of the universal 'Big Bang' [91]. Another was that some kind of laser-device, sending out signals in a narrowly confined frequency-band might be used to scan the galaxy. In that way, the naturally expected differences in the distributions of billets of resonance in the plane of the galaxy and in directions at right angles to that plane might cause the anticipated pattern of glitches at the source. If so, it was proposed, then this might be presented as evidence of the predicted blocking and accommodating effect of distant quantum receivers.

Unfortunately, the ability to arrange the distributions of astronomical objects to suit our purposes does not fall to present experimental science. So, he thought, if there really is a 'conspiracy of nature' against distinguishing the two views, then any verification of the anticipated effect cannot be attempted by that method. On the other hand, he reflected, that same 'conspiracy of nature' cannot be complete, because the two-slit experiment quite clearly displays the effect. So the prospect of carrying out some modified version of the two-slit experiment could not be discounted, and on that understanding all sorts of plans were discussed.

Almost invariably, in these discussions much interest was generated (virtually to the exclusion of any philosophical concern) in the 'military' significance of these theoretical suggestions. This was on the grounds that they offered the possibility of a form of communication 'faster than radar'. In other words, it was felt that any prospect of being able to 'feel' objects at a distance by means of these direct (probing-stick) sorts of quantum connections promised obvious advantages over techniques involving the usual two-way electromagnetic signalling. Discussions as to the practical (and economic) feasibility of conducting decisive experiments along these action-at-a-distance lines continue to the present time of writing.

XIII From Analog to Digital in Fundamental Physics

Looking back on it all, he reflected how easy it should have been. The current problems concerning the instantaneous —or, in Physics jargon, 'non-local'— connection between particles that Quantum Theory required and the delayed-time ('local-process') connection required by Relativity, over which so many theoreticians were embattled, need never have arisen. Such problems were due, he now plainly perceived, to nothing more than stubbornly held precepts about space and time which made it seem

[91] See Appendix 4.

somehow paradoxical that there can be togetherness among things that are apart. These problems, he saw, were of our own making. As Berkeley had put it, 'We kick up a dust and complain we cannot see.' In theoretical physics, instead of encouraging simplicity, we had rewarded complexity. Too many Educational premiums were placed on highly abstruse, so-called 'æsthetic' theories rather than on commonsense-logical explanations of things —as witnessed by the fact that the partisan esoteries, Relativity and Quantum Theory, remained jealously at odds, in a state of 'Mexican stand-off' dignified by the expedient name of 'complementarity'.

By that same policy of 'complementarity', all sorts of other logically incompatible theories had been allowed to proliferate. Surely, it should have been plain to anyone whose mind had not been academically brainwashed, that what was required was not more theories but fewer, towards the theoretical ideal of ending up (by continuous application of the principle called 'Occam's razor') with just one. So sophistic, however, had some members of the Science community become that they were professionally appalled by this suggestion of rational selection, seeing it as some kind of intellectual 'fascism'. But how, he wondered, could these people possibly be concerned with understanding if all they wanted were more and more clever theories? What else could they possibly be doing but just enjoying the game? They were no more easy to understand than the retired vicar his wife had asked to conduct prayers for the children in a village-school during the harvest festival. 'Sorry,' he replied, 'I don't pray any more; I'm retired!' Whatever could this man possibly have been doing all his working life? It must have been the sort of vocation a fellow student at university had facetiously described when asked what he did prior to entering university. 'Curator of a mock museum,' he said.

And what else, he wondered, but a 'mock museum' would natural philosophy become (assuming it already hadn't) if its natural truths were replaced by more and more 'interesting theories'? And why did these theories have to be so confoundedly mathematical? Mathematics was surely the servant of reason, not its master. Yet even among the more dedicated researchers he had found that in many cases, mathematics was taken, not as a tool to be applied in science but as a kind of game to be played as an end in itself, a system set apart from nature and elevated to a Platonic level where, in some cases, it had become sacred. His thesis had been unpopular, he now saw, because it had 'cut the crap', as the Americans say. Hadn't it shown that Relativity, which, as a 'menial lineman' he had been told could not be understood other than by becoming a card-carrying Mathematician could be derived so much more simply and less mysteriously by ordinary engineering-drawing type methods? And hadn't it also shown that the essence of the even more mathematical Quantum Theory could be deduced from relativity in that same commonsense way, without pages and blackboards full of differential equations?

It seemed to him therefore, that there was simply no excuse for the continuing polarisation of quantum togetherness and relativistic apartness —between 'non-locality' and 'locality' in the jargon beloved of the Physics writers. How could the together-yet-apart-ness of atoms be so stubbornly regarded as logically contradictory when it so obviously was not? For instance, not only were things in movies both connected (in the celluloid matrices of the single frames) and separated in the sequences of those frames; they were also connected-whilst-separate in instances of ordinary perspective. As he explained it in answer to another critic on the issue of the cone-model (see Figure 6, chapter IX) [92]:

It is difficult, you say, to think of everything being both "immediately in contact with everything else" and "distance-separated" in the way my cone model requires. But is that really a problem? The characters in the line I am now typing all collapse together when seen edge-on from the left of the page, and so do all the line-spaces when seen from the bottom edge. There is no paradox, then, surely, in having to think of things that are separate in one dimension being together in another. In the same way, there is no

[92] Philosophical Glimpses, Vol. 13, p. 5041d.

paradox in thinking of the two ends of a photon interaction as separated by a length $s$ in one dimension and by zero length in some other dimension or dimensions. In this way, I see 'instantaneous action-at-a-distance' as living quite naturally and unparadoxically alongside the 'finite interaction-limit $c$' in the respective end- and side-elevational views of the cone-model.

These instances, he felt, should have been sufficient to offset any idea that togetherness and apartness are necessarily contradictory. Nevertheless, one of his regular 'sparring partners', a well-known Mathematical Physicist, remained highly critical. He complained that in spite of everything that had been argued: 'Your thesis gives no hint as to how its description of the angular momentum pairing of particles A, B, C, and so on, can avoid the consequence of complete overall rigidity.' So our protagonist tried yet another analogy:

Yes, in my view, all the bodies in the universe are paired. And, yes, angular momentum is discussed separately for each pair. But this intransitive pairing relation is only at the ultimate microphysical level where, in atoms, the paired masses we call the "electron" and the "proton" either absorb or shed angular momentum in irreducible (and therefore unsharable) units of $\hbar$. And because the overall angular momentum has to be conserved, this can happen if and only if there is, at that same instant, some compensatory change, of a resonant character, in the angular momentum of a similar pair of bodies in an atom somewhere else.

Think of it this way. At the atomic level, the transactions between bodies are like those of a coinage where, although the values of the coins are continually conserved (barring inflation due to counterfeiting, et cetera), business people scarcely ever see these coins in their banking transactions. Such transactions, obviously, may also be carried on concurrently between more than just two people without any one transaction having to wait for others to be made; i.e. there is no strict sequence of any mathematical or mechanical sort involved with these transactions. So according to that monetary analogy, the quantum 'coinage' in physical processes is such that any quantum of angular momentum 'spent' implies that that same amount is, somewhere or other, immediately 'received' without that transaction being in any way mechanically or mathematically determined. This, of course, does not rule out statistical prediction according to laws like those of economics in finance or, in physics, the second law of thermodynamics.

And, of course, not only are the parts of atoms connected by angular momentum internally but so, externally, are the atoms themselves, no less immediately. And this is not only with atoms within the same body but also with atoms of different bodies—not monolithically but statistically, in the same way that in a properly managed economy, all the various financial transactions are never completely unconnected. You could say that this replicates, in angular momentum terms the sort of overall non-local connection conceived by Mach, but in quantum (i.e., discretist) terms, of course, not continuist ones. That (continuist connection) would be like the cosmic treasury having minted nothing but a huge single note, with which, as illustrated by the film 'The Million Dollar Note', no transactions could be made because there was no small change.

So, in the same way that every coin or ultimate token in all the multitude of ongoing financial transactions, with bankers, shopkeepers, mortgage managers or whatever, is, in the context of the overall economy, immediately connected with every other, so, according to my thesis, every object is connected with every other at every distinguishable instant. This is by hosts of different and overlapping angular momentum balances and transactions, with nothing ever falling out of account.

In this response to his critic he was stressing, of course, that the way he had dealt with the problem of rigidity which other theorists such as Feynman and Wheeler$^{[93]}$ had encountered with their theories of direct action, was similar to that in which Democritus had dealt with the monolithic 'One' of the Eleatics. That method was to break it up into discrete units, like pulverising a rigid block of salt to make it mobile enough for sprinkling. The essential difference, of course, was that whereas for Democritus those ultimate discrete units were measures of pure self-sufficient mass, these newly proposed ones were inseparable combinations of the interdependent measures, mass, length and time, called angular momentum. These units were irreducible not because they were infinitely hard and unbreakable, like the 'atoms' of Democritus, but because at that ultimate microphenomenal level, to reduce any one of the component measures increases another, so that no matter what you do with them, their product remains the same. As Victor Weisskopf, the director of CERN admitted to our researcher, at that establishment in 1975$^{[94]}$, 'Nothing we have done here has ever succeeded in splitting $h$'.

The 'problem' which Mathematicians like his critic continued to see in this breaking-up and separating-out of the ultimate quanta of action and angular momentum was, again, that of how these separate bits and pieces could associate, in themselves and by their own volition, to make a universe. As the critic put it, 'Physicists are people who demand to know all the rules and all the pushes and pulls so that they can know that the scheme works.' That, he concluded, 'is what Physics is like.'

This took our lineman right back to what he had studied for his University Dissertation, concerning Plato's 'Gods and Giants' (see Ch. IV), the aptness of which he himself had confirmed, time and again, in his dealings with people in general. On the one hand there were the sorts of reasoners Plato had named the 'Gods' who, characteristically, would accept a purely 'abstract' explanation of some phenomenon, so long as it was couched in terms of a disciplined use of logical reason. They would agree, for instance, that if one accepts that a system of angular momentum is conserved, then one also has to accept that bodies must move in response to one another simply because for them not to do so would disconserve the overall relation. On the other hand, there were those of the sort Plato had named the 'Giants' for whom such purely logical explanations, no matter how formally incontestable, were 'too vague and abstract'. These would settle for nothing less than a concrete, stage-by-stage, explanation in terms of unbroken sequences of mechanical connections such as are provided by the theories of 'fields' and 'ethers'. But what sort of compulsion was it, he wondered, that made these people postulate the presence of such 'mechanisms' where there was no logical necessity for them nor any possibility of obtaining factual evidence for their existence? Nature was plainly informational, and for information to be information it had to based on sequences of events that were truly indeterminate, that is to say, absolutely discrete and unconnected—save, of course, in the context of message as a whole. Yet there were people, calling themselves 'realists', for whom this fundamental unconnectedness of things was unacceptable. It was, as he once put it at a conference, as though these people who were typical of Plato's 'Giants', dissatisfied with what they saw as the 'vagueness' of ordinary newspaper reports, sought to 'rationalise' the choice of characters in the printer's font by gluing or stringing them together in some imagined 'correct' mathematical or mechanical order, so that all news, as such, would disappear and be replaced by pure and orderly predictability. Nature was plainly nothing like that. Where had these people been, he wondered, that they had not noticed this? If modern Theoretical Physics could cease being a mathematical game of 'Trivial Pursuit'; if it could give up its vain search for theoretical hidden mechanisms and seek, instead, truly to represent nature

$^{[93]}$ Ref. Feynman, R. P., 'The development of the space-time view of quantum electrodynamics', Nobel Lecture, December 11, 1965. Nobel lectures, Physics 1963-1970, Elsevier Amsterdam (1972).

$^{[94]}$ This was during a visit on behalf of Phi the Philosophical Viewspaper, see Chapter VII.

as we experience it, then it would clearly have to come to terms with this fact of the fundamental stochasticity of physical events that were informational rather than mechanical.

In this way, in contrast to classical Mechanistic Physics, which may be called ‘analog’, any truly representative modern approach to fundamental physics had to be digital —that is to say, information-theoretical as opposed to mechanistic [95]. As he understood it, an analog explanation of physical phenomena, in this context, would be of the sort demanded by Plato’s ‘Giants’, in terms of concrete events like the clatterings-together of material particles. These ‘Giants’ would say that nothing existed that you couldn’t poke with your finger or, in principle at least, with a long stick. The ‘Gods’, at the opposite extreme, would say that anything existed whether or not it could be seen or poked at, so long as it could be rationally conceived and named. In other words, the ‘Gods’, as represented in modern times by the Logical Positivists, would be satisfied with explanations of a purely formal —not to say idealised or ‘idealist’— kind, in terms of pure logic, or even pure mathematics, all of which the ‘Giants’ (as represented by the Reactionary Realists) would dismiss as ‘abstract’ or ‘immaterial’. Informational systems whose elements are pure ciphers, ‘logical atoms’ or sequences of pure quantum digits, would therefore as predictively be rejected by the typical ‘Giant’ as accepted by the typical ‘God’.

XIII.1 The inside-out ontology and causality of digital physics

True empiricism, as represented by our odysseyist’s thesis of Normal Realism, was the stance neither of the ‘Gods’ nor ‘Giants’ of Plato but rather of what Plato, had described as his ‘God-Giants’ (see Chapter IV). To be truly empirical, therefore, as well as truly representative of the informational revolution in modern technology, modern physics should as studiously avoid Logical Positivism as it should eschew the reactionary Remote Realism. That is, it should not only cease depending on theories and equations couched in terms of variables of a purely disembodied, ‘positivist’ sort, but should also, with its feet planted firmly in the world that we observe, reject those presumptuously ‘realist’ theories of ‘hidden variables’ that are so hidden as to be forever beyond our ken. As he conceived it, in the same way that a metre rule is what it is due to measuring operations on ordinary levels of experience and not a construct of imagined primordial millimetres (micrometers, nanometres or whatever), so the fundamental microphysical measures $h$ and $h$, of action and angular momentum, respectively, were dependent on what existed at the ordinary level of experience, not vice versa. These fundamental quantum measures were thus the terminal irreducibles of physical phenomena, not self-sufficient ‘elements’ of the same. Starting on the ordinary informational level where all measurements are made, they were discovered by extrapolated down instead of starting with imaginary pure numbers or mechanical atoms and extrapolating up, as if the universe were some mathematical game, played by God, of ‘painting by numbers’ or Cosmic Lego.

The difference was thus, basically, between a holistic and a reductionist approach. But there were two kinds of holism to be distinguished. There was the ordinary empirical holism (small aitch) in which what we actually observe, in all commonsense and scientific experience, are the forms of things, not their ultimate atomic particulars. Then there is the metaphysical Holism (capital aitch) of those people, such as the Eleatics, for whom all things were no more than arbitrarily delineated parts of a single, unseen, Universal or Cosmical Whole. So, whereas the small-aitch holist (empiricist) looking at the world through his microscopes, telescopes and other instruments said, in effect: ‘Where do we go from here?’, these extremists, of opposite Holist and Reductionist persuasion, were both saying in effect what the yokel said to the motorist asking for directions: ‘If I was you, Sir, I wouldn’t start from here!’.

[95] This usage is as applied to computers and compact disk systems, where ‘digital’ signifies a process that is discrete, as opposed to one that is —or is assumed to be— continuous. (See Pope, N. V., ‘The Complete Physics Heresy’, art. cit.)

This small-witch-holistic approach to natural philosophy was, of course, the one our adventurer had named Normal Realism and which he had sought to promulgate through the organ of Phi, the Philosophical Viewspaper — ‘God rest its soul!’, he sighingly reflected. The philosophical ‘flipover’ that Normal Realism advocated, so far as physics was concerned, was to think of the substance of physical reality as angular momentum, with its inseparable components of mass, length and time. The void —the true void, or null-and-void — is what lies beyond the limits of reducibility of those measures, such as the purported ‘insides’ of electrons and protons or the metaphysically imagined positions and periods within their orbits or trajectories. (The fact that some physicists such as Bohr and Heisenberg had also regarded these ‘inner’ descriptions of the trajectories of electrons and so on as ‘indeterminate’ gave encouragement and support for this ‘flipside’ way of thinking.)

This meant that beyond the process of empirical reduction (provided it was carried out exhaustively) there was no substance whatsoever. In other words, the ultimate ‘elementary particles’, such as ‘electrons’ and ‘protons’, far from being the traditional infinitely hard and filled-in, self-sufficient bits of matter that Democritus had envisaged, were existentially void. In other words (to use philosophical language), they were both ontologically and epistemologically ‘hollow’). That is to say, on the premise that those ‘elementary masses’ in angular momentum relations are truly irreducible there could be nothing on their ‘insides’ that it made sense to speak of —no mass, no duration, nor even empty space. They were, in fact, the complete opposite of what Democritus had described as ‘atoms’.

Nor, he had to stress, are these entities (non-entities or however we choose to think of them) which we call electrons and protons ever isolated. Regardless of the distances by which they are separated (in what physicists call ionisation), in angular momentum terms they as paired to form atoms as when they are closest together. In that sense, instead of being localised in the way Democritus envisaged them, those ‘atoms’ may be indefinitely (i.e., non-locally) extended. The problem, of course, with thinking of those entities we call the ‘electron’ and ‘proton’ forming an atom when separated by vast distances is that it prompts us to think of them in the traditional way as self-sufficient material particles. Difficult though it might be to switch to thinking of them as ‘voids’ (bubbles, gaps, bubble-like or sponge-like holes or whatever) there had to be these discontinuities in the substance of things for that substance to be mobile. Substance, in the Normal Realist way of thinking, was thus not ‘atomic substance’. It was the relatedness or connectedness, in terms of angular momentum, of the particle-like voids which the Giants of physics have reified with names such as ‘electrons’ and ‘protons’ [96].

He realised, of course, that in any ordinary way of looking at things, it seems like nonsense to think of these ‘holes-in-the-substance’, or voids, as possessing mass and moving like particles on the ends of moment-arms. However, if the philosophical flipover from the Democritan conception of substance to that of Aristotle and Moore was to be accomplished, then that became an inescapable logical consequence for physics, namely, that as Mach had taught, the mass of a particle is not a property it possesses absolutely, in itself, but a property it possessed only in relation to other particles. From this consistently empiricist (Normal Realist) point of view it was therefore altogether too facile to think of elementary particles as orbiting inside atoms like the planets of our solar system or winging their way through deep space, existing self-sufficiently for perhaps aeons to end-up making vapour-trails in observatory cloud-chambers (see following). Such particles were never truly isolated and self-sufficient, no matter how far apart they became in macroscopic space. In the same way that in monetary system the value of the smallest coin or money-token takes its value not from anything inside itself but only from its relation with other coins within the currency as a whole, the masses of these ‘elementary particles’ consisted of nothing but their interrelations with one another as measure-components (dimensions) of the substance that is angular momentum. That is to say, while ordinary bodies such

[96] In terms of the ‘cinematic’ analogy described in previous chapters, the ‘photographic’ process is reversed, with the ‘stills’ being thought of a photo-negatives in which the substance of the presentation is the space (the black bits) and the voids (the transparent blobs) the particles.

as billiard-balls and planets can be conceived as being filled with matter in the form of smaller and smaller bits of mass, when that 'smaller and smaller' reaches its absolute limit of reduction, the mass which is measured at that limit is the ultimate mass, of which there can be no 'inside' or set of internal parts that it makes sense to speak of. In other words, whereas most masses are physical particles with parts and therefore interiors which signify as masses, the 'interiors' of these 'elementary masses' have to be regarded as no more than ciphers. That is to say, they are empty bundles of dimensions—dimensions which are relative to other such bundles in the relational system as a whole.

Among the things of which he was thus forced continually to remind his critics was that these ultimate 'hollows' (discontinuities or 'voids') at the terminal levels of reduction were necessary to ensure the complete fluidity of physical processes. Of course, with his customary temerity, our philosophical freebooter had inverted classical atomism by putting the 'void' (the original null-and-void) inside the atoms, not around and between them as Democritus had done. The effect, nevertheless, was the same, which was to resolve those paradoxes of motion which are associated with the names of the Eleatic philosophers, Zeno and Parmenides.

Something else our Normal Realist was forced to reiterate, on practically every occasion, was that the ultimate substance of reality, which he had decided had to be angular momentum, was essentially a macroscopic phenomenon. That is, it was a property of things defined in observation on ordinary levels of experience and then analysed down to quanta. It was not something projected upward by man out of entities residing on some remote sub-phenomenal level known only to God. This was the aforementioned fallacious way of thinking called reductionism (Remote Realism) in which wholes are thought to have no existence in their own right other than as the sums of ultimately real but observationally inscrutable (i.e., atomic) parts. Angular momentum, accordingly, was essentially a characteristic, not so much of atomic elements but of the observed motions of objects such as stars in galaxies and planets in our solar system. Every free object in the system quite obviously orbits each and every other, in such a way that the motion of every one of them is connected with the motions of the others and therefore automatically influences them.

However, it had to be remembered that these angular momentum linkages are continuous only on the macrophysical (i.e., information-statistical) level—which was the datum-level, or 'bottom line' of all empirical enquiry so far as Normal Realism was concerned. At the ultimate microphysical level of analysis these linkages are quantised in irreducible amounts of angular momentum $h$. On that quantum level, he reminded himself, an electron which is connected (proper-time-instantaneously) with a proton in one atom cannot, in that same instant (i.e., simultaneously) be connected with any other particle in the way that, at the ordinary level, the earth interacts simultaneously (in angular momentum terms) with the moon, the sun and other objects both in and beyond the solar system. This, of course, is because, being irreducible and therefore unsharable, these quantum connections can occur only between one atom and another in a single instance. In other words, the connecting relation is what logicians call intransitive.

XIII. 2 Conceptual readjustments

He tried, then, to envisage the angular momentum field as a discretum, somewhat like Einstein's space-time substratum, with the same sorts of geometrical features such as 'warps' and so on but whose structure was ultimately sponge-like, in which the 'holes' (the 'electrons', 'protons' and so on) were hollow angular-momentum vortices, somewhat like cyclones and tornadoes moving and tracking about in the earth's atmosphere. This, of course, contrasted radically with our usual Democritan-conditioned way of thinking, in which the 'substance' of physical phenomena lay in the hard and self-sufficient material particulars which Democritus had envisaged—and which many physicists still envisage—as 'atoms'.

However, this raised the question of how it was possible, if these elementary particles were simply ‘ciphers’, that they could move and balance one another on the ends of moment-arms and make the sorts of tracks they do in bubble-chambers. Sitting in the garden one day, he spotted, appearing in the clear blue sky, the vapour trail of an aircraft. This set him thinking. There are, he reflected, singularities of all kinds. If, on a beach, on the flat sand left by the receding tide, some sort of track was seen, we may well suppose that it hadn’t appeared spontaneously but that it was traced by something—someone’s shoes, an animal, a bicycle tyre or whatever—the nature of which might be guessed from the character of the track. Similarly, if in a clear blue sky, a white trail appears, we may suppose that it is made by an aircraft flying too high to be visible. On the other hand, there are singularities (things that noticeably stand out from their surroundings) which need not necessarily be caused by such ‘foreign objects’. An ocean current or a tide-rip, for instance, a whirlwind or whatever, may appear as a singularity of the medium itself. The path of a tornado, for example, may be well defined by the trail of devastation it leaves, with nothing but a vacuum inside it. Logically, then, he told himself, the appearance of a singularity such as a bubble track does not necessarily signify the existence of anything foreign to the medium in which it occurs. But of course, no one in his senses would look at a vapour trail and claim that it had appeared spontaneously in the way clouds do, without some sort of aircraft being the cause. And even in the case of clouds, we commonly take their appearance as an indication of dust-particles causing precipitation of water-molecules in the atmosphere. This made it difficult, to say the least, to think of the trails he had seen in bubble-chambers, cloud-chambers and so on, as anything other than indications of particles zipping through the medium like aeroplanes through supersaturated air.

But, he reminded himself, in the case of the high-energy ‘particles’ and the scatter products of their collisions with other particles that were studied in these ‘atom-smashing’ laboratories, we can never see or in any way detect—not even in principle—the entities that are supposed to be causing those trails. What we see are what we suppose are their ‘effects’. In other words, what we actually perceive are the bubbles, droplets and so on forming the trails. Besides, there was always the purely logical possibility—which could not be excluded—that these trails and so on are evidences, not of particles but of singularities of the surroundings themselves, like the tracks of tornadoes. That, he realised, would be a very unfamiliar concept. But then, he thought, this was the way it would have to be at the ultimate limit of microphenomenal reducibility if his philosophical flipover was to be carried through consistently.

There were also, as he recalled, the reports by experimental physicists that in two-slit experiments (q.v.) using particle beams instead of light-rays, those same sorts of ‘particles’ which cause tracks in bubble chambers behave more like waves. That is, instead of keeping to those well-defined paths we see in bubble chambers, in the two-slit experiments they create interference-patterns at the detection end. They could do this, the two-slit experimenters had reasoned, only if, like waves, they passed (miraculously for particles) through the two adjacent slits at once. These reports therefore seemed to contradict the evidences of the bubble-chambers, which suggest that in the space between the source and the target these ‘particles’ follow well-defined trajectories. Clearly, however, something had to be conveyed from the one place to the other, even in two-slit experiments, because closing either one of the slits cancelled the interference and made that ‘something’ act like a particle, whereas closing both slits blocked the passage altogether (see Chapter XII.3).

One day, he was grumbling to an old school friend about the difficulty he was having in these philosophical attempts to put all the various reported facts of experiment logically together without resorting to theoretical contrivances like that of ‘complementarity’—a sophistic ploy if ever there was, he told his friend, scornfully. This friend, a retired engineering draughtsman, listened carefully to his argument that a one-quantum interaction between a source and sink cannot be said to exist or to travel in the space separating the two because, since that quantum event is irreducible, there can be no question of its expending parts of itself in order to manifest itself in that distance. 'So,' enquired his friend, 'what's the problem?' The problem, explained the puzzled philosopher, was how a quantum particle consisting of a single quantum can manifest itself in the way it does in bubble-chambers and cloud-chambers when the only manifestation of that interaction would be the loss of that single, whole quantum by the source and its immediate gain by the sink, with nothing that can sensibly be said to be happening in between'. The engineer's response was impeccably logical, and should have been obvious to the philosopher. It was that an interaction-event which does manifest itself over a distance in space (as in forming a track in a bubble chamber), cannot therefore be a one-quantum event but must consist of some large number of quanta sufficient to be expended in making the track. It occurred to our odysseyist that this was perfectly consistent with the fact that those events that were manifest with proton-beams in bubble chambers and cosmic-ray particles in cloud-chambers were eminently energetic, that they invariably involved huge numbers of quanta. The greater the payload of quanta, the greater the spending-power in defining the track. In that case, far from being irreducible, these high-energy events were, like all true particles, reducible. So the tracks that are observed in bubble chambers and so on had to be of this high-energy, particulate sort.

But how, he was asked, can this payload of energy be imparted to and carried along by a nothing, a hole? His answer was that in this new way of thinking, it can't. The energy that is given to what we think of as 'the particle' is really given to the environment at that concentrated place where hole (the vorticle) occurs. This, he added, is in the same way that a concentration of energy in the atmosphere can turn the merest dust-devil into a tornado. And when that concentration of energy dissipates itself in creating its track of devastation, in the end it disappears altogether, leaving no remainder, or solid core that can be put in a case, like a meteorite, and labelled 'tornado'.

Moreover, he argued, unlike particles, these existential hollows, or vorticles can cancel one another out. They can disappear in one place and appear in another, literally 'out of the blue', without this being in the least bit mysterious to anyone who knows anything about the dynamics of atmospheres and fluids. But by that same token, any notion we might have of protons whizzing through vacuum tubes and of cosmic-ray particles winging their way across megaparsecs of space for aeons of time, would have to be cancelled. In his different way of thinking, that would be as absurd as to imagine that whirlwinds, whirlpools and tornadoes could exist and travel great distances in a pure vacuum.

So, regardless of how convincingly 'particulate' the events observed in bubble-chambers, cloud-chambers and so on might seem, logically (on the premises provided) they could not be anything but local responses to tensions at distant places and conveyed non-locally to that location in the way that, say a volcanic upheaval on Jupiter might shake a seismograph here on earth.

In this way, then, our odysseyist had arrived at an extreme version of Machian relationism which rejected atomism in favour of a purely interrelational conception of matter, not only on those astronomical levels envisaged by Mach but also on the level of 'atomic' physics. So now, from his own purely naïve, commonsense direction, he had arrived, like all the physicists and philosophers of his generation, right at the same limits in our understanding of 'fundamental particles' where there was no place for dogmatism or authoritarianism of any kind. The only true authority, in this game, he told himself, was logic. And now, dutiful to that authority, he had carried through his philosophical 'flipside' way of thinking, as best he could, to its logical conclusion. This was that ultimate 'particles' are really vorticles, whose substance is the space surrounding them and the void the complete 'nothing' inside them that was every bit as impenetrable and inscrutable as the inside of a solid Democritan 'atom'.

There was still, however, the question of fundamental indeterminacy of those particle-like entities, or vorticles. In a discretum, where time is quantised and different for all relatively moving objects, what meaning can be given to commonsense words like 'exist', 'permanent', 'conserved', etc.?

In the angular momentum discretum, as he conceived it, atoms (vorticles, or however they may be conceived) interact by swapping bits of themselves, which are transferred in the form of light-energy in the manner described in Equation (8), chapter VII.1. So at that ultimately fine-grained level of physical observation, mass as such is not conserved and neither is energy. Angular momentum is conserved and so is action, but only on the holistic, overall level. Are there, then, he wondered, 'Brownian-type' fluctuations in the masses of objects? If the positions and momenta of fundamental particles (vorticles or whatever) are indeterminate, as according to the indeterminacy principle of Heisenberg$^{[97]}$, then so had to be their centres of mass. (Had anyone, by the way, thought of passing cosmic rays and high-energy cyclotron beams through pairs of slits; and if they did, he wondered, would they observe the typical 'interference' patterns of distribution in the strikes at the target end?)

For questions like these it seemed that there was little purely practical difference for experimental physics as to whether these resonant features or singularities of the environment were treated as particles in a void or as vorticles in an angular momentum discretum. Whichever way one chose to describe them, the positions and momenta of these entities cannot be completely indeterminate because they do not pop up just anywhere, like complete will o' the wisps. As modern physicists report, they manifest themselves according to a well-known statistical rule of chance, a probability-function known as the Schrödinger 'wave-equation'$^{[98]}$. As our adventurer saw it, in its space-time evolution, his angular momentum discretum, with its in-phase and out-of-phase lengths was just such a probability-function whose metrodynamical features defined the probabilities of where a particle will or will not appear.

This reminded him of what he had learned about Quantum Wave Mechanics which, he remembered, had been heavy stuff indeed! He recalled having read that in the early 1920s, the French physicist, Louis de Broglie, faced with the fact that light-energy was quantised, had interpreted the intensity of a conventionally conceived 'light-wave' as being, not the energy of a real wave but the pure probability of finding a quantum of energy at a particular location. Strangely, following Einstein in thinking of light-quanta as 'particles', de Broglie had extended this notion of the wave-like probability of their appearance to all fundamental particles, assuming that particles, such as the electron and proton existed autonomously and were accompanied and guided by this wave-like probability-function. Although this wave-function was purely formal (i.e., there was no real physical quantity with which it could be identified) it was taken by physicists from then on as the amplitude of some sort of underlying wave-continuum. That is, the wave-continuum had 'in itself', so to speak, an infinite number of functions satisfying the equation but only some of which satisfied the real-world (observer-world) condition that it had to be finite and single-valued at a point, thus 'collapsing' the wave-function to a probability of one (a certainty) that the particle exists at that location. The time-dependent or progressive form of this wave-function described the motion of a free particle, whereas a particle bound in a system, such as an electron in an atom, was described by a form of the equation resembling a standing wave (stationary wave-state). This wave-like behaviour of elementary particles was held by physicists to have been confirmed in experiments by C. Davisson and L.H. Germer, at the Bell Telephone Laboratories in 1927, which revealed that electrons emitted from crystalline substances are diffracted like waves.

As already stated, he found this heavy stuff. But actually, of course, it was never these strange 'waves' that had been detected in these experiments. What were actually observed were what seemed to be the strikes of electrons. Nevertheless, the notion that it had been proved that these particles were

$^{[97]}$ This is sometimes called the 'uncertainty principle' or the Heisenberg uncertainty relation. It is that the product of the uncertainty in the measured value of a component of momentum (mv) and the uncertainty in the corresponding coordinate of position (x) is of the same order of magnitude as the Planck constant h. (See The Penguin Dictionary of Physics ed. Valerie Illingworth, (2nd ed.) 1977.)

$^{[98]}$ See, e.g., Blanchard et al. op. cit., p. 178.

waves had caught on. And so, by throwing commonsense logic to the winds, Modern Physics had launched itself on its catastrophic descent into complete mystification. This, our adventurer further recalled, was especially due to another physicist, Erwin Schrödinger (1887-1961) who, in 1926, provided, on the basis of the wave-dynamics of de Broglie, a complete mathematical explanation of Bohr’s arbitrary quantisation of the atomic orbits of electrons as regions where the wave-function, around that centre, returns in phase with itself, creating an orbital (but, of course, abstract) ‘standing-wave’. Following the work of these two eminent physicists, it seemed that but for its complete and altogether cavalier contempt for commonsense logic with its mystifying talk about impossible ‘wave-particles’, the purely ‘probability-functional’ explanation of all particle-phenomena was, for purely pragmatic, mathematical purposes, more or less entirely satisfactory.

All well and good, then, he decided, and ‘bully for the mathematicians!’. But the essence of all these so-called ‘wave-dynamical’ explanations, was still no more, in the last analysis, than purely geometrical phase-relations between these particulate emptinesses and the distance-times $s/c$ separating them. The time-dependent or progressive form of this phase-relation might well look like a wave. But, as his own cinematic model had logically demonstrated, it was logically feasible to think of those phase-relations in terms of pure time-sequences of instantaneous connections without having to think of this as being due to ‘waves’ of any kind, far less those mysterious ones of ‘pure probability’. Further, as he had read it, Schrödinger’s ‘wave-function’ was like his own angular momentum discretum (in its ‘Newtonian’ aspect, see Figure 4, Chapter VIII), insofar as it was basically non-relativistic. Also, the energy of the ‘wave-function’ was like his own angular momentum, in that it involved the whole system (that is, in Schrödinger’s sense, ‘particle plus field’). The only difference he could see, then, between this ‘wave-function’ and his own no less probabilistic angular momentum resonance-metric was that whereas the former was purely abstract and metaphysical, the latter was physical and empirical.

There was, then, he felt, no reason why this mathematical ‘probability function’ of de Broglie and Schrödinger, with those philosophical adjustments he had suggested (that is, dispensing with the mystifying modern physics-speak about ‘waves’ and whatnot) could not be fitted neatly to his angular momentum discretum. This, he felt, should complete a three-way logical merger, between modern philosophy and the two factions of modern physics, along the lines he had projected, providing a reasonable logical and philosophical underpinning for all the various bits of mathematical eclecticism that had increased our knowledge of nature at the cost of common understanding.

XIII.3 Form and Content

For our explorer, then, the proper subject of physical research was, needless to say, not ‘atoms’ and their mechanical interactions. It was an angular momentum discretum in which physical explanations had to be couched in terms of something like a combination of fluid dynamics and thermodynamics with the probability-functions of de Broglie and Schrödinger thrown in. However, at this stage in his odyssey, some health problems put paid to his intentions of following this particular course of study in the way a physicists might. Now approaching his seventies, he decided to leave the detailed working-out of these suggestions to others, younger and more practically and mathematically disposed who, now that the implications for physics were plain, might wish to pursue them. There would surely be enough of these who, if they wished, could continue in that course with no further help from him. So far as he was concerned, with a new lease of life which some surgery secured, his own aim should be to concentrate his attention on what, it seemed, no-one else was doing in quite the same way, which was to study the broader, more philosophical issues concerning modern science and diagnose the reasons for its disaffection from commonsense.

In the first place, then, there were the philosophical consequences to be worked-out, from his reasoning so far, that those ultimately irreducible elements of matter, the ‘electrons’ and ‘protons’ (assuming that these really were the ultimate irreducibles$^{[99]}$) were existentially hollow. This, he clearly discerned, should dispose of any reductionist or ‘realist’ interpretation of the world which sought to reduce the irreducible —to lift the imagined ‘veil of perception’ and peep into an underworld of completely inaccessible metaphysical ‘reality’. He remembered his 1970s visit to the ‘atom-smashing’ laboratory, CERN, in Geneva, where the object of that kind of research had been described, by the Director, V. F. Weisskopf, during the ceremony marking the 25th anniversary of that institution, as being ‘to study the ultimate constituents of matter and the ultimate forces of nature, the driving forces of all natural processes’$^{[100]}$. To this our lineman —by then the editor of the Philosophical Viewspaper— had responded that what was going on at that establishment was, to him, like ‘smashing chessmen to discover the rules of chess’.

He remembered also that even at the time of his visit there had been rumblings in the media about the sheer waste of such large chunks of the Gross National Products of the various subscribing countries on an enterprise whose aim was completely obscure to the populace in general. He recalled the buzz of consternation that ran around the hall when, at the end of a lecture he attended at that establishment, he had raised the question: ‘What are you people here looking for and how will you know when you’ve found it?’ After the buzz had subsided, the answer he had received was as high-sounding as everything else he had heard in that lecture. But it actually said nothing that he could recall, either then or since.

So it had not surprised him that over the following years, that enthusiastically projected reductionist aim, to discover the ‘ultimate mechanical constituents of nature’ (or ‘Key to the Universe’, as one TV programme presented it) had fallen far short of its original intention to produce the required common understanding of how nature works. On those theoretically projected ‘fundamental’ levels, instead of finding fewer and fewer of these imagined constituents the ‘atom-smashers’ had unearthed more and more strange ‘particles’ with even stranger properties. This was, as many people judged, a standing indictment of that reductionist form of research. As someone expressed it, the number of ‘elementary’ particles and properties that were appearing seemed to be more or less directly related to the size of the laboratory’s fuel-bill. And indeed, he mused, there might be some truth in that jest. For it seemed to follow from the limit of reducibility of action and the well-known relation $e = mc^2$, that in all the experiments at that establishment so far, pumping more and more energy into the ‘smashing’ process would naturally create more and more energetic ‘fundamental’ particles —with the emphasis on ‘create’, he mused, as opposed to ‘find’.

But of course, he cautioned himself, he could be wrong about that. It was not his kind of expertise, anyway; and those who wished to follow that Democritan line of enquiry were obviously free to do so. It was up to them to justify to the public why, with all the modern equipment and computer-power at their disposal, that high-budget operation had so chronically failed to produce anything like the expected common understanding. In discussion with T. D. Lee (the discoverer of the ‘meson’) and John Bell (of ‘EPR’ fame, see Chapter VII) he had discovered that they, too, were puzzled about what the experiments at CERN were revealing. As Bell recounted to him, despite the fact that no expense had been spared on those sorts of experiments there was, so far as he could see, no immediate prospect of a clear understanding. ‘So why,’ Bell had asked him, ‘aren’t you philosophers here in droves helping us?’ Our enquirer, knowing that Bell was a philosopher-turned-physicist, thought this was a

$^{[99]}$ The even more fundamental particles called ‘quarks’ are not particles in the sense of being, like electrons and protons, manifest as such. They are entities extrapolated from mathematical regularities (symmetries) involving particles involved in nuclear (e.g., ‘strong’) interactions.

$^{[100]}$ CERN Courier, No. 6, Vol. 19, 1979, p. 233.

somewhat strange response, and with his doubts about the tack these 'atom-smashers' were taking, he asked Bell, 'If it's all so confusing, then why are you here at CERN instead of doing philosophy? 'Well,' Bell replied, smiling, 'Its better than working down a mine!' And indeed, looking around at the sumptuous working conditions, who could blame anyone from wanting to keep the place going.

But regardless of this, it was surely, he thought, by now perfectly plain that it was high time someone tried another tack —which, of course, was what he had been doing in his extramural and decidedly low-budget way.

The natural logical alternative, as he saw it, then, was to switch systematically from the Democritan tack to the one that had been taken by Aristotle and followed through, after Moore, by modern philosophers. In that way of thinking, what exists is what can be directly perceived and described by the careful and unambiguous, logical use of language, which was the 'more moderate' way of thinking on the part of Plato's 'God-Giants'. To extend an argument used by Plato, in the far distance we see the wood rather than the trees; in the middle distance we see the trees rather than the leaves. In the foreground we see the leaves but not their cells; in the microscope we see the cells rather than the molecules ... and so on, right down to those irreducible quantum limits of sensory and instrumental perception established by Planck and Heisenberg. What we perceive in any of these sense/instrument categories, therefore, is always a Gestalt [101], which is never an isolated sense-datum but always a form, that is, a character-complex of some kind, what pre-Modern scientists used to call a species [102]. Nor are these forms (universals, species, Gestalten, or whatever we might choose to call them) necessarily subjective projections of human perception. The form, or shape of an object may exercise, in its own right, very objective physical effects. For instance, a snooker-game would be very different if the balls were replaced by cubes, and a machine would not work in the way it does if its properly formed parts were replaced by an equivalent weight of raw materials. From this neo-Aristotelian point of view, therefore, what science should be seeking in order to satisfy normal human curiosity is not a continual reduction of particulars into further and further particulars —although the usefulness of that kind of research for industrial and other purposes could not be discounted. What it should be seeking, in the way of understanding, are the principles governing the various ways in which forms fit or do not fit with one another, like the meshing of gears, to borrow a mechanical analogy. As the perception theorist, Jerome Bruner expressed it:

In learning to perceive, we are learning the relations that exist between the properties of objects and events that we encounter, learning appropriate categories and category systems, learning to predict and to check what goes with what [103] (that author's italics).

[101] Gestalt, in this context means 'configuration'. The Gestalt School of psychology was initiated by M. Wertheimer in Germany around 1912, moving to the US after Hitler came to power. The word 'Gestalt', as originally applied to visual shapes, was extended by the perception psychologists to signify forms (configurations) of sound, as in music; letters, as in writing and reading —in fact to anything in perception that has informational significance. The movement was based on an observation, in 1890, by the Austrian psychologist Christian von Ehrenfels, that for the occurrence of a visual field, neither single stimuli nor mere sums of such stimuli can be made responsible —which is particularly obvious in cases where all stimuli are changed in the same proportion.

[102] What the German Perception-psychologists called a Gestalt the Aristotelians called a species. In light, as these Peripatetic philosophers conceived it, in what J. L. Austin described as 'paradigm' cases of seeing (that is, when not distorted by mirrors, mists, prisms or whatever) we see or perceive the very essence of things —that is, their reality as opposed to their mere appearance. (see letter to David Bohm, Feb. 2, 1981, Phil. Glim. Vol. 5, p. 1487).

[103] Bruner, J. S., 'Perceptual Readiness', Readings in Perception, eds. Beardsley, D. C. and Wertheimer, M., Van Nostrand Co. Inc., London (1958), p. 691.

That meant, of course, learning to predict and check not only what goes with what in perception itself but also, mainly—and in the interests of our very survival—with what goes with what in Nature, in the round.

In Nature, then, our adventurer decided, things interact with one another and communicate themselves to us, observationally, essentially in the language of forms. From a commonsense point of view, there is nothing magical or mystical about ‘form’. Indeed, in this text so far, the word ‘form’ has been used more than twenty times in an entirely unexceptional, commonsense way. For the atomists, following Democritus, there were no such things as forms in nature. Forms, these people supposed, were what we, as human percipients, subjectively project into what, if we could only see beyond our finite perceptions, were no more than just collections of atoms. All the various ways in which naturalists observe how birds, bees and plants interact, the way a well-formed woman appeals to a man, or a peacock displays itself to a peahen—that is to say, in terms of their recognitions of forms of one kind or another—were thus declared by Democritus to be mere ‘conventions’ or projections of human imagination. For Aristotle, as for any scientist who is untainted with that restricted ‘atomic’ metaphysic, forms may be as real in nature as in our perceptions of them. And, indeed, from an Aristotelian, commonsense point of view, it is those forms are the primary realities in any truly realistic physical analysis.

As our enquirer had discovered, the first systematic attempt to sort out these natural forms—that is to say, to classify and divide them within their separate categories—had been undertaken by Aristotle himself on the basis of the language-analysing methods he had learned from Socrates. These categories were richly divided to the extent that this comprehensive labelling system became the basis of our modern language. However, as far as the atomists were concerned, there were no more than just the two categories, matter and void, with all else being considered illusion (or ‘convention’, as Democritus called it). Similar attempts by modern materialists to produce a ‘truer’ language of reality by reducing descriptions of everything to that of mechanical interactions between atoms would not merely rob us of our rich linguistic diversity; it would strike us dumb.

Those commonsense categories of Aristotle were, of course, revised in the eighteenth century by Immanuel Kant (1724-1804). But unfortunately, due to the influx of what were then the new and subjective idealist (or ‘mentalist’) views of Berkeley and Hume, Kant’s revision led to the fixing, in modern language, of the mind-matter dualism of his predecessor, Descartes, in which the concerns of philosophers and physicists became alienated in the way that has been described. Far from serving the cause of reason, therefore, Kant, in our freethinker’s opinion, had instituted a kind of split-minded philosophical barbarism which had been intellectually and morally devastating to our Western society.

To add to this confusion, in the evolution of modern language there was also, tangled-up with Democritan ‘materialism’ and Berkeleyan ‘idealism’, the earlier ‘Idealism’ (note the capital I) of Plato and Pythagoras. Whereas Aristotle’s forms could not exist without their particulars, Plato had abstracted those forms so that they became ‘spiritualised’ into Forms, Ideas or Universals (again note the use of capitals) which the Platonists maintained could exist apart from and independently of any particulars. When the Christian Church adopted these Platonic notions they led to the belief (to be discussed later) that there are regions beyond ordinary space and time to which our disembodied forms, or ‘souls’ depart when we die.

So, wondered our odysseyist, what could be done about this mess? Here we were, in a state of complete linguistic confusion, caught between Democritan materialism on the one hand and, on the other hand, not just one but two forms of idealism, those of Plato and Berkeley. What hope was there of restoring the lost relationist or relativist connection with nature which Aristotle had initiated but which had now been all but destroyed? True, Einstein had done much to resurrect that relationism so far as physics was concerned. However, in compromising his efforts to accommodate the ‘separability’ which is entailed by the mechanistic interpretation of light, far from removing from our language the bogus dualism of Descartes, he had established it even more firmly. Such, then, was the situation, that any attempt to repair the language on behalf of humanity, even from an approved standpoint of linguistic philosophy, would be madness to contemplate. However, to attempt it on his own behalf, in order to liberate his own thoughts and ideas was, our odysseyist felt, no more than a duty.

PART 4

PROJECTION

The wider, more philosophical implications of relativistic time-staggering, from the original correspondence with Einstein, are here followed towards their logical conclusion. This removes the stumbling block placed by our traditionally mechanistic (analog) physics in the way towards a full philosophy of relationism,

XIV 'In From the Cold' — Into the Heat

When our telephone lineman began what was to become his Intellectual Odyssey following his 'Lines from Einstein', he could not have guessed what that would entail. Naively, he had expected that his discovery of a much simpler, perfectly valid replication of Einstein's notoriously abstruse theory would have been welcomed by all and sundry. He could not have imagined the acrimony that it would generate. Cynics might have told him about 'vested interest', about how difficult it would be for people highly trained and qualified in the Educational norms and standards and who fully expected on that basis to become in their turn established custodians of those norms, to accept being virtually 'sent back to school'. They might also have warned him how difficult it would be for others, not so highly trained and qualified, to accept that someone from among their own comparatively uneducated ranks should 'buck the system' in that way. The whole thing, the cynic might say, was too radical. It was as if, in an economy based on the political and financial control of agriculture, someone had discovered how to obtain sustenance from thin air.

Such cynicism our extramural 'innocent abroad' would never have believed at the start of that spontaneous interest which, as he had subsequently learned, was called Philosophical. Even now, having witnessed the extent to which such irrationality existed, he could not accept that it was completely decisive in determining the course of our philosophical history. Surely, by now, he thought, someone would have wised-up to what was happening! But 'We live in hopes if we die in despair', his Grandmother used to say; and to that homespun principle of optimism he had adhered with a faith akin to religion. Certainly he had learned that formal Education was nothing like the liberating influence that its advocates claimed, not even in our fancied Western 'democracy'. The counsel his teachers had given him to 'think for himself' had been hollow advice, like offering him the freedom of a prison yard.

But now, in spite of everything, somehow he had ended up in the thick of it, where just about everyone in fundamental physics was seeking to solve the problem which, all those years ago he seemed to have homed-in on unwittingly. This was the problem of how to reconcile those sundered sections of science, the quantum physics of Niels Bohr and the relativistic physics of Albert Einstein. He was now a regular attender at Physics conferences, on whose committees he was invited to serve. He was honoured to be asked to issue invitations to foreign Professors for them to give university seminars, and was even persuaded to become an officer of one Physics organisation. In all of this, he had never made any secret of the fact —indeed, on many occasions he had been compelled to stress it—that he was not formally qualified in either Physics or Mathematics. Yet, on discovering that he possessed no such qualifications, one highly qualified academic had thought fit to castigate him internationally, on INTERNET to just about every colleague in the profession. The gist of this detractor's argument was that ideas in Physics which were presented other than by properly qualified people like himself were necessarily false [104].

However, this reaction, it has to be said, was rare. In the main it was as Popper had told him: 'Once inside the magic circle no-one will question your qualifications'. So now, both with his Maths colleague and on his own, he was writing more and more papers on the subject of Physics, which were published in all sorts of Physics Proceedings and journals. But although the presentations of these papers were welcomed and, in many cases, much complimented, citations of them were conspicuously scarce. As a friend had told him, many years previously, he was answering questions which were not even being asked. But now they were being asked. The question he had been addressing was that of how, logically and philosophically, to reconcile quantum immediacy with relativistic delayed-action.

[104] Apeiron Vol. 3, Nos. 3 – 4, (1996), pp. 123 – 125.

The question these Physicists were now addressing with greater and greater urgency was that same question but posed in typical jargon terms of how, in differential equations involving customary conceptions of field-electrodynamics, to account for experimental evidences of non-locality or 'the violation of Bell's inequalities'—which meant next to nothing in ordinary language. There were, of course, variations on that same theme, such as, for instance, the question of how to explain why, in astronomical observations and experiments, 'gravitational' influences were found to be instantaneous, and 'electrodynamical' ones time-delayed [105]. But whatever the differences in the papers presented, in order to qualify as a valid contribution to the professional debate, a paper about light-interaction had to be in one way or another about 'electrodynamics' and (again) couched in terms of 'differential equations'. To show a familiarity with these equations and with the names of Faraday, Maxwell, Weber, Helmholtz, Hertz, Lorentz ... and so on was as obligatory as it was to involve what these luminaries had conceived as 'charges', 'field-force vectors', etc. To omit any mention of these names and the associated conceptions immediately labelled a paper 'Philosophical' and therefore as having nothing to do with 'the real business of Physics'. As a venerated professor put it, at one conference, in response to our ex-lineman's contribution [106]:

It's too soon to say we know what light is. The thing has to be settled by the experimental physicists. No philosophical a priori theory drawn from some philosophical heaven is going to tell us any hard facts about photons.

In that same conference another delegate reported, over coffee, what another member had proudly proclaimed about our ex-lineman's talk: 'As soon as he said he was a philosopher I got up and walked out!'

Pointing out that angular momentum is sufficient in itself to explain the magnitudes of the various electromagnetic and electrostatic forces obviously made no impression whatsoever on these Physicists. Nor did it interest them in the least to be reminded that the rules which the likes of Maxwell and Fleming had applied to vector-orientations in 'electrodynamics', were precisely those which were found in systems of pure angular momentum. Any suggestion that all their 'electrostatic', 'magnetic', 'gravitational', etc. field-forces might be comprehensively replaced by angular momentum was unthinkable—as was any suggestion that the question this 'philosopher' in their midst was addressing could be even remotely connected with the one they were addressing.

These physicists, of course, were presenting their theories on their dogged assumption basis that the concept of 'Einstein separation' was philosophically non-negotiable. They accepted without question that it was absolutely and precisely what Einstein had thought it was, an inexorable Cartesian division between the physical world of physical light-sources and the psychological world of the observer. They imagined that some highly ingenious, purely mathematical and/or experimental means was required of demonstrating that there were physical influences that could cross that divide, by breaking that 'light-barrier' in the same manner that aircraft had managed to exceed the speed of sound. It was pointless to suggest that this was what philosophers called a category mistake. That Einstein might have been mistaken and that, really, there was no such thing as that kind of separation, that the instantaneity quantum physics required was already spoken for in Einstein's own relativistic equations, was as incomprehensible to them as though they had been told, after decades spent filing through the bars of their prison window, that the door had never been locked. The fact remained, nevertheless, that in the relativistic time-equation there was simply no problem of 'Einstein separation' to be solved. In its own proper-time—as, amazingly, Einstein well knew—light itself is instantaneous. And since there

[105] T. Van Flandern, 'The Speed of Gravity—What the Experiments Say', paper at the Vigier II Symposium, York University, North York, Ontario, Canada, Aug. 25-29, 1997.

[106] This remark was made at the first Symposium in honour of Jean-Pierre Vigier at York University in Ontario (August 1995), by that guest of honour, during question time at the end of our author's talk: 'A Quantum Digital Theory of Light'. The talk and its follow-up are recorded on the official video-tape of the meeting.

can be no question of there being anything more instantaneous than instantaneity, then given that light is intrinsically instantaneous, any theory seeking to 'break that proper-time-instantaneous light-barrier' just had to be pure nonsense.

XIV.1 The Incipient Deism of Classical Physics

But why, he wondered, was this nonsense so continually and doggedly adhered to in the face of so many obvious logical objections? Even in doorstep arguments with the missionaries of the various religious sects he had scarcely ever encountered more intransigence. Recalcitrant attitudes, he had discovered, were clearly not confined to the zealots of politics and religion. For instance, the conclusion at which he had arrived as to the logical necessity of the fundamental indeterminacy of physical processes was, surely, a perfectly reasonable one for anyone to present. However, it could make no headway against those who were sold on Einstein's deterministic view of an underlying geometrical (or, rather, geometro-dynamical) continuum which he, Einstein, had called the 'space-time substratum'. 'God does not play dice', had been Einstein's unthinking and completely dismissive answer to this indeterminacy principle as presented by Werner Heisenberg in 1927. Yet, plainly, what Einstein was rejecting, in this incipiently religious response of his, was no more than a logical tautology or truism of empiricist philosophy. In order to be detected, an object has physically to interrelate with some other object or objects. So, as any commonsense person must surely perceive, whatever might be assumed to be that object's completely self-sufficient, undetected state—that is to say, when it is not interrelating physically with anything—is not only impossible to determine but pointless even to talk about. And what else can that non-state be but, by definition, one of indeterminacy? To reject fundamental indeterminacy is therefore like denying that a bachelor is unmarried or that a circle is round.

That, of course, did not prevent scientists from projecting, in their metaphysical imaginations, the existence of the 'underlying realities' as they presumed 'God' perceives them behind all possibility of human perceptions. For a short while he had become interested in the ideas of David Bohm, particularly on action-at-a-distance and what Bohm called 'quantum potential', which seemed in some ways similar to his own thesis. After some correspondence (cut short, unfortunately, by Bohm's early demise) he met with a group of Bohm's followers at Oxford and also at a conference at Cesena in Italy. However, their 'realist' approach was so philosophically foreign to his own that he and they found they had little in common.

As our adventurer saw it, the Realists' implicit involvement with 'God', to which so many physicists like Einstein coyly admitted when pressed, made a complete farce of the customary idea that Science and Religion are antithetical. Science, as Einstein had presented it, was plainly a belief in some superhuman influence designing and determining physical processes. It was a pure precept transcending both logical reason and empirical experience. To present such science as an objective alternative to religion was a travesty.

Yet, what else but such a pure belief could possibly sustain us in exploring a world which our sensory and instrumental knowledge could never hope completely to encompass? Our finite experience of nature could never be treated as a logically closed system, or algorithm. For science to work, there just had to be a basic assumption, an article of pure faith, that what it was dealing with was, in some superhuman way, rational; that is to say, an ordered system, not a complete chaos. Science therefore had to remain, or at any rate had to return, to being essentially heuristic or 'open-ended', in a word, empirical in its approach to nature. This was not in the purely pragmatical or expedience sense of empirical but on the clear understanding that science is basically and unavoidably a belief, a belief that somehow, in a way we could never know a priori, it all makes logical sense. In other words, the projected end-aim of science, as of any human enterprise, regardless of what its exponents might claim to the contrary, was inescapably that of a belief-system, indeed a religion, as its most representative exponent, Einstein, had let slip.

So, since a truly empirical science cannot avoid that incipient religion he felt it ought to declare it, to bring it right out into the open for inspection, not keep it so insidiously 'under wraps'. This he regarded as essential for the continuance of true natural philosophy. In his contacts with two people in particular, one a fellow student at university, specialising in Philosophy of Religion, and another, a long-time friend who had been a pupil at Bible College, our ex-lineman had learned that there is an important distinction to be observed between two radically opposed, or antithetical, kinds of religious belief. One is deism, as expressed in Einstein's statedly determinist belief in a 'God who does not play dice' and the other is theism, as in the belief in a 'God' with whom it makes sense to have informational interaction in the form of prayer. As he had come to understand it, deism, in the sense relating to Einstein, is what had been called the (hidden) 'religion of the scientists'. This is the typical, mostly unstated belief that God created the world and set it in motion subject to natural laws (e.g., those of classical mechanics) but takes no further interest in it [107]. In contrast, theism is the belief in a personal God, albeit the creator and supreme ruler of the universe, who transcends his creation but works in and through it in revealing himself, interactively, to man [108]. Clearly, he thought, without plumping for either the one or the other of these beliefs, and from a point of view of pure agnosticism, anyone could see that whereas Einstein's scientific deism was logically opposed to any religion akin to Christianity, the same could not truly be said of the alternative, non-deterministic, information-theoretical approach to physics which his own thesis was advocating. In a paper he presented for the Proceedings of one conference [109] his colleagues and the journal's editor noticed that this point about the logical bifurcation of scientific belief, which he had mentioned briefly in his talk, had been omitted in his Proceedings paper. They concurred that it should appear in the final version. Accordingly, he included a piece to the effect that any theological implications of the new paradigm switch he was proposing had to be theistic as opposed to deistic. On that basis, science and theology—and even religion—should be studied as a rational whole (i.e., holistically, as in 'holy') instead of as separate and unrelated disciplines in the fashion of our compartmentalised (or, rather, fractured) Educational tradition.

XIV.2 The Need for Scientific Scepticism

As he saw it, then, scientific truth was not something to be discovered by covertly 'playing God'; that is, by feigning 'objectivity' whilst leaping in one's metaphysical imagination to encompass the Whole and from then on biasing all reasoning towards justifying that precept—seeking to 'know the Mind of God', as Steven Hawking had put it [110]. To our odysseyist, truth, in science, was something to be unearthed by the continual and systematic, essentially human process of eliminating error. When someone asked him what he thought 'truth' was, he replied 'Who can say?', and then added 'But we sure as hell know what it is not!'

[107] Britannica World Language Dictionary, Encyclopaedia Britannica, Inc. Funk & Wagnalls, New York, p. 338.

[108] Ibid. p. 1301.

[109] Pope, N. V. and Osborne, A.D. Osborne, 'The Holistic Simultaneity of Distant Interaction' Proc. of PIRT, (1992) p. 273.

[110] The 'ultimate triumph of human reason', as Hawking seemed to see it, was to 'know the mind of God'. (See Hawking, S., A Brief History of Time Bantam Press, London (1988), p. 175.)

What he was saying, in effect, was that in science, any notion we might have of ‘ultimate truth’ or ‘ultimate knowledge’, of ‘cosmos’ or whatever, had to be of something so far beyond our comprehension as to make nonsense of any claim to comprehend it. Not that this ruled out the possibility or even the feasibility of a pure and perhaps even dogged belief, of the kind that was basic, not only to all natural religions but also to science.
