He saw no logical reason to oppose such beliefs to the pursuit of natural philosophy. This, of course, was so long as they did not rule out the logical alternatives that there always are relating to systems of belief and thus fall foul of the sort of dogmatism that prevented Einstein from even considering the arguments for fundamental uncertainty.

As our philosophical voyager saw it, then, the system of belief or the incipient religion that science inevitably is can never claim, far less by maintaining a haughty silence, that it is, somehow, ‘the’ uniquely objective undertaking. History attests the extent to which undeclared and subliminal belief-systems superstitiously inhibit scientific progress in the way the Church, with its dogmatic attachment to the science of the ancients, had once sought to prohibit the findings of natural philosophers, as attested by the cases of, for instance, Copernicus, Giordano Bruno and Galileo [111]. But it seemed to him that scientists nowadays are equally inhibited by their no less dogmatic veneration of their own ‘saints’, such as, for instance, Faraday, Maxwell and Einstein. Barriers to progress, he concluded, were therefore erected not only by the people of Religion but also those of Science. Impatient with the plodding methods of empiricism, these metaphysicians of both camps preferred to start by grasping what they presumed to be the Whole. They then persuaded themselves that in some special way they were absolutely and intimately familiar with this Whole (call it ‘God’, ‘Universe’, ‘Cosmos’ or whatever), whilst blithely disregarding obvious subjectivity revealed by their conflicting descriptions of it [112].

Yet atheism, he found, could be every bit as subjective, based as it so often is on a completely naïve and unscientific belief that modern science explains everything, which it manifestly does not.. He once asked a class of students: ‘How many atheists are there in this room?’ They all raised their hands. ‘Here, then,’ he told them, ‘is your written exercise for this lesson: “Describe the God you don’t believe in”.’ The answers he received confirmed to him that all talk of ‘God’, whether in affirming or denying the existence of that entity, does no more than generate nonsense.

Nothing, however, was quite as nonsensical as what he once heard, that a scientist had reported: ‘I have looked through my telescope and through my microscope and can confirm, absolutely and unequivocally, that God does not exist’. This, he reflected, expressed an extreme case of the approach to natural philosophy which Plato had described as that of his ‘Giants’. The opposite extreme, he judged, would have been that of Plato’s ‘Gods’, who would readily put their faith in some purely formal system such as the Bible or a theory like that of General Relativity, keeping their eyes tight shut against any facts or arguments that did not fit. A memorable encounter he once had with this attitude was with an American priest, a qualified aeroplane pilot in whose company he had spent some pleasant and interesting hours of flying and discussion. This companion finally parted with the kind and friendly advice to ‘give up all that silly philosophy and come to God’. Another no less genial adviser, whom he had known at school, dismissed all prospect of philosophical discussion with the following quote

[111] Giordano Bruno (c. 1648-1500) was an Italian natural philosopher who was burned as a heretic for publishing his belief that the ‘heavenly bodies’ were as modern astronomers understand them. Like Galileo (1564-1642), who was forced, on pain of torture, to recant this same view, he accepted the Copernican view that the planets, including our earth, orbited the sun.

[112] It is reported that in response to these incipient religious expressions of Einstein’s, one of his friends responded by saying, ‘Albert, you really should desist from telling God what he should and shouldn’t do!’

from the Bible: ‘Beware lest any man spoil you through philosophy and vain deceit’ $^{[113]}$. Nor were these by any means the only examples of conceptual entrenchment. In yet another case, a friend he had known and argued with vigorously for many years finally terminated all further argument with the summary advice not to go around thinking for himself and upsetting people but simply to ‘read the books’ —no, not of Religion, this time, but Physics.

All sorts of contacts with this kind of entrenched attitude had warned our pilgrim of the extent to which tacitly held and often subconscious —in any event, unexamined— beliefs made true freedom of thought a rarity. Any truly unbiased and uncommitted thinker (the sort Plato had described as the ‘God-Giants’) would surely realise this and would have to lay bare his beliefs, in the way Socrates had urged, so as to present them for proper rational inspection. This was on the principle that true beliefs may well be beyond reason but can never be contrary to the same.

XIV.3 The Natural Phenomenon of Purposive Behaviour

In view, then, of the fact that knowledge and belief are inseparable, the only formal systems we can ever know, of science, religion or whatever, are those which have been created or discovered either by ourselves or by someone else, on the basis of some belief or other. Those systems, therefore, regardless of how venerated they may be, are forever ‘on lease’, pending further rational and empirical investigation. Consequently, no systems, regardless of how authoritative they may seem, are immune to revision and even, sometimes, to the need for complete recall in the light of expanding experience. For instance, those who believe, on the basis of the Democritan tradition, that all nature is basically mechanical are committed to maintaining that there is, in reality, no such thing as ‘purpose’ in nature. To these, the Normal Realist should point out that for anyone who has his eyes open —as any naturalist should have— there is, everywhere, abundant evidence of purpose. This is not only in our own conscious intentions and the capacity to put them into practical effect but also, and essentially, in nature as a whole. To deny this plain fact signified some strange and distorted system of belief. For instance, there can be little doubt that one’s own thoughts and ideas do direct, via one’s nerve circuits, muscles and so on, where one’s car goes on the road, where the balls go on a snooker table or where one’s pen goes on a piece of paper. And there is clear evidence on all hands that organisms other than man are capable of exercising similar volitions. If these evidences are declared illusory, then that can be only on the basis of a pure belief. This strange and unsupported belief is that despite there having been, over the centuries so far, no prospect whatever of any satisfactory mechanistic explanation of this natural goal-directed behaviour in living organisms, ‘it will be only a matter of time’ before these sorts of explanations become available.

As our ex-lineman discerned it, then, to stick to that mechanistic way of thinking would leave us no way of explaining the life we see around us, other than by either some miraculous breach of the laws of randomness, in the clattering together of blind, mechanical atoms, or by some equally miraculous action of a deity who had fashioned those atoms with life and consciousness in mind. In either case, the argument was a self-deluding con, since it sought to explain miracles only by postulating miracles. It reminded him of a demonstration he once saw on TV, of how life could have come about ‘by pure chance’. The demonstrator filled a bag with some peculiarly shaped blocks and then, after shaking the bag, pulled out a whole chain of these objects which, due to the shaking, had interlocked, end to end. ‘There, you see,’ said the demonstrator, ‘how the building-blocks of life (DNA molecules and amino acids) could have come about by pure chance!’ That demonstrator had neglected to mention how long and hard he must have worked designing those blocks towards the end of producing precisely that sort of ‘accidental’ combination!

$^{[113]}$ The New Testament, Colossians, 2 verse 1.

So, he wondered, what was the point of denying volition in organisms and putting it into the mind of a mystical ‘Creator’? Why not settle for that creative ability abiding right where we find it, as observed in organisms and experienced in oneself? It might confound mechanical explanation, but it could not confound explanation in plain informational or relationist terms. By the very nature and definition of stochasticity (educated guesswork) the probabilities of future physical events are presently calculable. We can calculate very precisely, for instance, how long it will take for a kettle of water to boil on a stove or how long it will take to cool in a refrigerator, without having to be clairvoyant about these things. And there are evidently organisms which, like ourselves, are capable, to some greater or lesser extent, of making similar calculations, or judgements, and acting accordingly—as, for instance, in the way predators anticipate the movements of their prey, make estimates about distances in leaping gaps, and so on.

But that seemed to present a snag. He recalled the fact, well known to naturalists, that some organic behaviour is designed to preserve a whole species, even at the expense of the individuals themselves. Bees, for instance, sacrifice themselves for the sake of the hive; and he well remembered seeing a ewe who, although defenceless and weak from just having given birth, was prepared to attack him in defence of her lambs when she could easily have run away. What prompted individuals to act in that way? How, especially among the lower organisms, were those individuals supposed to estimate that ‘greater good’ of the species, the hive, the anthill or whatever? It would require a collective intelligence far beyond anything possessed by any single individual. Nor could it be explained away in terms of the ‘selfish gene’ theory without (again) projecting that far-sighted intelligence into the mind of some mystical Gene-Designer. But then that same Gene Designer would bear the responsibility for all the nastiness of a selective nature that was ‘red in tooth and claw’. Such an entity, if it was truly intelligent, was scarcely an inspiring object of religious worship!

But, be that as it may, there had to be something in nature that was exercising that far-sighted intelligence, since it could not be the individuals themselves? Clearly, in mechanistic terms there can be no imperative towards the survival of a species, because all purely mechanical processes tend towards the dissipation of order in the process called entropy. The fact, then, of the increasing order in the growth, evolution and propagation of life-forms, in informational communication and so on, plainly contradicts the monopoly of entropy and hence puts paid to any theory of universal mechanism. For if the law of entropy rules that chaos must prevail, then, without introducing improbabilities that are virtually miracles, how could that order or negentropy possibly have come about in the first place?

XIV.4 The Logic of Nature

What needed to be considered, then, our thinker decided, was some logical rather than mechanical imperative, something like the rule of entropy but the very opposite, or correlative of that rule. But what could that logical imperative be, he wondered. The only thing he could be sure of was that whatever it was, it could have nothing to do with the heat-motions of atoms, as in the case of entropy, but with informational processes involving forms of the sort described in the previous chapter. In other words, the way in which a biological species evolved had more to do with the way, for example, in which an animal selects a mate by its pure appreciation of form (visual, aural, olfactory, aesthetic or whatever) than with the way their atoms mechanically interact. Evidently, he mused, some sort of imperative was served by the premium placed by nature on the selection of better and better (i.e., more and more well-developed) forms adapted to future survival, which confounded the notion of ‘universal entropy’. Aristotle, the inveterate empiricist, had named these future-directed functions ‘entelechies’, and the process of their fulfilment he called ‘becoming’. Darwin had called that same process ‘natural selection’, and someone else had named it ‘negentropy’, all of which was plain empirical recognition of the limitations of mechanistic (i.e., reductionist or atomistic) modes of explanation.

Something else which had to be recognised, reflected our researcher, was that if entropy does not rule decisively, then neither does negentropy. In nature, the two principles are, to all intents and purposes, in continual and unrelenting conflict, as Heraclitus had emphasised [114]. But of course, mechanists would say that the final prevalence of entropy was inevitable, that all organic forms must eventually disintegrate towards the situation dramatically described as 'the heat death of the universe'. In this situation, all order and formation would end in a permanent state of pure randomness or chaos—which, as already stated, would require postulating yet another miracle as to how so much order (negentropy) had ever come about. But that 'heat-death' argument, as he clearly discerned, was just another 'con', because such a catastrophic inevitability could be projected only by arbitrarily ruling-out the empirical fact of negentropy. This was just another example of the fallacy which Democritus had committed, and Descartes and Berkeley had compounded, of splitting X into Y and Z, arbitrarily ruling out Y (or Z) and concluding that X was therefore all Z (or Y). In Berkeley's case, the world (X) was all mind (Y), whereas for Democritus it was all matter (Z). And here, once again, was that same Procrustean fallacy of arbitrarily ruling out everything to do with life, purpose and conscious communication and concluding that it was all pure chance, or entropy.

So, of one thing, at least, he was convinced, which was that in order to avoid that recurrent sort of fallacy, one had to accept the obvious, which was that there are processes in nature, which are as purposive or intentional as others are purely mechanical, and that without theoretical jiggery-pokery neither one can be reduced to the other. No longer, then, was he captivated, as he once had been, by the fatalistic view expressed by the Romantic poet, Omar Khayyám [115]:

Yea, the First morning of Creation wrote What the Last Dawn of Reckoning shall read.

There was, so far as he was concerned, no mystical 'Future' towards which life is dragged, nor any mechanical or mathematical precondition ('creation' [116]) from which it is pushed. Far less was there any need to assume that life is a continual choice between many complete, pre-extended futures, as in the mystifying 'many worlds' interpretation of quantum wave-mechanics. (For people to present such theories in the name of objective Science was, he felt, a travesty.) The way the world works, as he saw it, is more or less as commonsense everywhere acknowledges, with all sorts of activities, interests and principles in conflict. Within the limits imposed by nature we undeniably forge our futures according to our intentions and calculations; and whether or not that future turns out as we would wish largely depends on the wisdom or otherwise of those intentions and the effectiveness or otherwise of their physical execution.

As he judged it, then, if our science were to jettison its traditional and completely inhibiting belief in 'universal mechanism' it would be free to recognise, as commonsense recognises, the existence in nature of self-determination and responsibility. It would include, in its studies of natural phenomena, alongside those mathematical balances we call equations, other balances in terms of justice, wisdom, obligation, morality, virtue and so on, evaluations which the custodians of prescientific Western culture called 'spiritual' and which the Church once feared would be destroyed

[114] Heraclitus, 'conflict of opposites', see Chapter X11.2.

[115] The Rubaiyat Of Omar Khayyám, Transl. by Edward Fitzgerald, Collins, London, 1st ed. 1859, p. 53.

[116] This issue of 'creation', in the context of the 'Big Bang' cosmogony, is discussed in Appendix 4.

by the rise of ‘materialistic’ science. And who might say, nowadays, he mused, that the moral foundations of our science-based society have not been undermined in precisely that way!

XIV.5 The Restoration of Natural Philosophy

What needed to be done, then, he felt, in the interests of man’s survival on this planet, was to dissolve the now defunct, altogether pretentious and purely arbitrary division between the mutually suppressive, dogmatic extremes of religious belief and mechanistic science (the ‘Gods’ and the ‘Giants’ of Plato’s analysis) and settle for a more open and amicable debate combining the best interests of both. The knowledge that Relativity and Quantum Theory has given us, if we refuse to accept the allegation that those two idea systems are antithetical, makes it possible to re-embark unselfconsciously and undogmatically, on the free dialectical course of discovery which was once called natural philosophy. In short, what else could true Natural Religion and true Natural Philosophy be but parts of the same spectrum of philosophical enquiry, which could never have been isolated other than by mutual intolerance on the part of classical religion and science.

His own attitude towards the polarised overt religion of the Churches and the covert religion of classical Science was, in the words, of Shakespeare [117], ‘A plague on both your houses!’ So far as he was concerned, empiricism went hand-in-hand with agnosticism. For how could anyone declare himself dogmatically to be either a theist or an atheist, a cosmist or an acosmist and expect not to be challenged to produce good reasons for his stance? Such powers as might be responsible for our existence are, quite obviously, so far beyond our comprehension that any attempt to name them or otherwise encapsulate them in language could never be more than a pathetic presumption. The nearest he himself got to a ‘religious’ view, as he recalled, was to choose optimism over pessimism and what he perceived as beauty over ugliness. He could not claim that these evaluations were absolute and he was well aware that other people and other creatures reckoned things differently. Nevertheless, he was as free as anyone might be, who was not enslaved by dogma, to believe in some sort of superhuman presence in nature. However, he saw no point in personifying it, far less degrading it with a name over which factions could quarrel, dividing people and institutions and ending in stupid wars.

But no matter how subjective he would have to confess to be his own evaluations of beauty, goodness and so in the eyes of others, he could not concede that same subjectivity in the matter of truth. There had to be truth because to claim that there is not and hold that claim to be true was a contradiction in terms. Besides, if there were no such thing as truth, then neither could there be such a thing as error—which, he decided, there definitely is, not only for man but for life-forms in general, in making false judgements and hence choosing actions which negate their own best interests and intentions. We know that things can go wrong, and wherever there is a wrong there has to be, by comparison, a right, in the same way that where there is an up there has also to be a down. But, without the experience of divine revelation—which is so often indistinguishable from plain madness—the only truth he could contemplate was that which could be gained by the gradual elimination of error. Where that process might lead eventually he, personally, had no more clue than it would be right and proper to have if his life were some kind of exam, in which there could be no cheating and in which the results were decided, if ever, at the end of the process, not at the beginning or any particular stage in between.

So, while plainly there could be no sure way of comprehending the universe in its history and futurity as a Whole—if indeed it was a Whole—there was, no less plainly, an undeniable logic to be discerned in the system of things, at least in the finite region of human perception and experience. For instance, a properly formulated mathematical equation was a balance, like that of the scales of justice. You could never hope to solve an equation by the exercise of any form of charisma or chicanery.

[117] Shakespeare, W., Romeo and Juliet, Act 3, Scene 1.

(Indeed, he thought, if there was anything like a universal form of justice it might well be conceived as being something along those equation-like lines.) Nor could any logically minded person be persuaded that if the biscuits were in the tin and that the tin was in the cupboard, that the biscuits were anywhere but in the cupboard. There was therefore a very close correspondence between the rules governing our correct uses of language (which Aristotle had established as the rules of logic) and those governing the courses of events in nature. Indeed, were it not for the fact that human ‘logic’ can be mistaken, whereas the logic of nature (what the Greeks called logos) can not, one might sensibly claim that they were the same rules governing both.

So far as he could gather from all sorts of different sources, logos, in the original Greek, simply meant something like what we would nowadays describe as ‘language,’ ‘reason,’ ‘ratio’. It was the ‘something’ that for any normal thinking person obviously has to be there and acts as the ordering principle of the universe. As Robert Brumbaugh described it$^{[118]}$, the 6th-century BC Greek philosopher Heraclitus was the first to use the term in a metaphysical sense. He asserted, says Brumbaugh, that the world is governed by a firelike Logos, a divine force that produces the order and pattern discernible in the flux of nature. Heraclitus also regarded this force as similar to human reason and believed that his own thought partook of that divine logos. Brumbaugh further explains that in Stoicism, as it developed after the 4th century BC, the Logos was conceived as a rational power that orders and directs the universe and was identified with God, nature and fate. The logos is thus ‘present everywhere’ and seems to be understood as both a divine mind and as, at least, a semiphysical force, acting through space and time. Within the cosmic order determined by the Logos, says Brumbaugh, are individual centres of potentiality, vitality, and growth. These are ‘seeds’ of the Logos (logoi spermatikoi). Through the faculty of reason, all human beings (but not any other animals, the Stoics believed) share in the divine reason. Stoic ethics stresses the rule ‘Follow where Reason (Logos) leads’, so that one must therefore resist the influence of the passions—love, hate, fear, pain and pleasure. Early Christian theologians developed identified logos with the will of God, or with the Ideas (or Platonic Forms) that are ‘in the mind of God’. Christ’s incarnation was accordingly understood as the incarnation of these divine attributes.

XIV.6 The Relationism of Heraclitus and Coleridge

Now where his own ideas of logos had come from our odysseyist could not rightly recall. What he did know was that they stemmed, not from any of these religious sources but from ordinary practical considerations, of the sort he imagined to have been no more than pure commonsense among the secular Greeks. Most likely, he thought, these ideas of his had been imbibed from his studies in logic and in philosophical discussions at college, the word ‘logos’ being connected with these circumstantially, as a serendipitous afterthought. In any event, the significance of these ideas had been growing in his mind over the years. One day, his wife said to him, ‘I’ve brought you a present’, and she handed him a book called ‘Coleridge’s Philosophy’, subtitled ‘The Logos as Unifying Principle’$^{[119]}$. On seeing the book advertised she had been reminded of their many discussions on logos and she thought that perhaps Coleridge’s ideas would have some relevance to her husband’s philosophy. And indeed, it had. From the book he learned that Samuel Taylor Coleridge (1772-1834)—known to our odysseyist only, from his ‘blood-and-guts’ interests as a schoolboy, as the writer of the famous poem ‘The Ancient Mariner’—had, in the words of the book’s author, Mary Anne Perkins, offered a ground for philosophy which was relational rather than mechanistic’. This obvious similarity with his own

$^{[118]}$ See Robert S. Brumbaugh, “Logos,” Microsoft (r) Encarta. Copyright (c) 1994, Microsoft Corporation, (c) 1994 Funk & Wagnalls Corporation.

$^{[119]}$ Perkins, M. A., Coleridge’s Philosophy, the Logos as Unifying Principle, Clarendon, Oxford, 1994.

work set him bolt upright. It was as good a description of his own philosophy as he himself might ever have conceived! For Coleridge, as for Heraclitus, the logos was ‘at the same time the word he uttered, the truth which it contains and the external reality which he conceives himself as describing’ [120]. But this could as suitably apply to the ‘language’ of modern philosophers such as Moore, Austin and (the later) Wittgenstein. It was also, he read, an attempted reconciliation of philosophy, religion and the natural sciences, in precisely the way he himself had envisaged.

For Coleridge, however, Logos (Coleridge always capitalised this word) was as much a focus of religious faith as a philosophical principle, and Coleridge’s interpretation of it had followed the usage set by the Fourth Gospel of St. John in which logos was rendered as ‘Word’. (‘In the beginning was the Word’.) Coleridge’s method was, however, generally regarded as incomplete, as it surely would have been since his emphasis in the use of the term was on religion. This was as opposed to our odysseyist’s own emphasis, which was on science —or, rather, natural philosophy— which is why he had taken the meaning of logos, not from the Christian Gospels but from that of the earlier, Greek philosopher-scientist Heraclitus.

In any event, the description ‘communicational intelligence’ given to the word by Perkins was, our thinker felt, particularly apt, and was precisely how he himself would have interpreted it. It was much more in line with the informational (or quantum digital) approach to science which he was advocating. Nor could he fault the description of logos as ‘the ground of all human discourse and communication’, except that he himself would not have drawn the line at ‘human’. Otherwise, he could fully concur that logos, the system of logical relations that was nature, was the ‘source and living power of language’ —so long, that is, as ‘language’ was not restricted to human language but was extended to communication in general, animal, vegetable and even, perhaps, mineral.

So unless his thesis was completely mistaken —and despite all the objections that had been thrown at it, he could not see how it could be— nature was more logic than mechanics, more logos, as the Greeks would have said, than physis. For some philosophers, notably Heraclitus (from whom, it seemed, Coleridge had taken his cue) the experience of being able to wield this power of logic in ourselves was evidence that as humans we partake directly of the essence of something which is altogether superhuman and perhaps even divine. However, the fact that there is also illogicality, as well-attested in human affairs, put paid to any claim that logos and nature are completely coextensive. As our philosophical pilgrim had experienced it, in the bit of nature that is human nature there is definitely as much illogicality as logicality —perhaps even more. That illogicality, he perceived, can certainly put us out of joint with the logos to the extent that our own intentions are frustrated, not only for our own and our society’s well-being but for the survival of our race. And the fact that this abandonment of logical reason is usually due to the uncontrolled pursuit of purely sensual gratification is well testified by the history of literature in comedy and tragedy, as the Greek dramatists well knew.

But whether or not this tragic tendency in man signified the existence of some anti-logos of the sort that Christians called ‘sin’ and which they had personified as Satan (The Tempter in the Garden of Eden), our critical freethinker would not like to say. This was for the same reason that knowledge of such an entity was as far beyond us as was that of ‘God’. However, a forensic psychologist he chatted to one day in a bar told him that from his clinical experience, and despite his dispassionate professional training, while he had extreme difficulty in believing in a Power of Good he had no difficulty at all in believing in a Power of Evil. But be that as it may, it was undeniable, our odysseyist felt, that even on the finite, most mundane level of human experience, these two natural promptings, of reason and passion, were largely in conflict. As the philosopher Epictetus (1st century, A.D.)

[120] Ibid. Introduction, p. 11.

observed, on the basis of sheer secular wisdom: 'He who follows his passions without constraint of reason is the biggest slave of all'.

But, of course, he reflected, not everything can, or perhaps eve should, be dealt with logically and dispassionately. There is plainly no reason why people should even attempt to live like computers. There is plenty of scope in human affairs for that non-logical activity called art—as, for instance, in music, painting, literature, even in the humorous appreciation of the ridiculous. Nor was the passion Epictetus mentioned necessarily in conflict with these activities. From his own personal experience, if reason was the wheel of a ship, then passion was its engine. And what use, he asked himself, would a ship be which was all engine and no steering? Without passion (or, as his brother-in law called it, 'bottle'), reason was about as much use as an engine-less wheelhouse in a rough sea.

So how far, he wondered, might the probing scientific mind legitimately proceed in this newly projected philosophical direction of relationism or relativism? In his studies at university he had learned that prior to its mechanistic or 'physicistic' crimping, natural philosophy had made much progress of that more liberal and humane kind. The fact, for instance, that it regarded nature as more logos than physis is encapsulated in the tradition followed to this day, of classifying departments of science as divisions of logos. This is signified by the appendage '-logy' as in 'geology' (the logos of the earth), 'biology' (the logos of life-processes), 'vulcanology' (the logos of volcanoes), 'psychology' (the logos of the psyche ... etc., etc., with what we now call 'physics' as a significant exception.

Characteristic, also of natural philosophy, he reminded himself, had been its recognition of the role played in natural processes by what Plato and Aristotle had named the forms (or 'universals', as later philosophers had dubbed them). In the mechanistic physics which had taken its departure from Democritus, the forms of things had no real existence. They were regarded as no more than 'names' applied to groups of particulars (ultimately, atoms) by human convention, where our perceptions were regarded as incapable of discerning the truly fine-grained 'atomic' reality of things. This was especially so where objects were separated from us by light, which physicists still conceive (as, of course, did Einstein) as the mediatory 'streaming substance' of Empedocles$^{[121]}$. By contrast, for those natural philosophers who took their cue from Aristotle, as already mentioned, forms—at least, those which were not illusory or hallucinatory—existed in nature, every bit as objectively as the particulars. For those philosophers it would be as absurd to think of the particulars without the forms as to think of the forms without the particulars (since speaking of their very plurality gives them form anyway, if no more than the minimal form of a pair). Indeed, as already indicated in the previous chapters, for the Aristotelians it was the forms rather than the particulars that were the active and directive principles of all physical processes, even those we regard as the most automatic or mechanical. Hence it was the study of these forms, the classifying and dividing of them in language, that these Aristotelian philosophers regarded as the true purpose of science.

This purpose, accordingly, was to discover the order and arrangements, antecedents, consequents and so on, of the natural forms, to distinguish them one from another and ascertain how they are interrelated. As Aristotle discerned it, the form 'man' included the form 'Socrates' but excluded that of his horse, whereas the form 'animal' included the form 'man'—and therefore that of both 'Socrates' and 'horse'—but excluded the general form 'plant'. Then there were the 'becoming' connections between forms signified by statements such as 'If this is an acorn, then it will become an oak'. Also there were the higher forms, types or categories, as they came to be called, which distinguish whether the objects referred to are or are not in space, whether they are material or abstract or

$^{[121]}$ A poet and philosopher of the fifth century B.C.

whatever. The logic, or logos, of these different arrangements, as we finite logicians understand it is, of course, developmental. As one source described it$^{[122]}$:

With the development of objective reality and the progress of scientific knowledge, the number and content of scientific categories are constantly enriched and the system of categories is drawing increasingly closer to complete and all-round reflection of the objective world. Expressing essential connections of developing reality, categories must also be as mobile and flexible as the phenomena they reflect.

How different from this completely empirical approach to science was that rationalised approach which our researcher had heard described in his visit to the ‘atom-smashing’ laboratory, CERN (See above, section XII.1), which was ‘to study the ultimate constituents of matter and the ultimate forces of nature, the driving forces of all natural processes’. That there are forms in nature was undeniable. That the truly directive, or proactive principles in nature are a logos of forms, not mechanisms of atoms, was also, he felt, scarcely to be doubted. This was especially in view of the facts demonstrated in TV nature programs, in studies of biology, zoology and so on, in which natural selection among all the different life-forms was an undeniable phenomenon. These facts plainly indicated that the natural processes these scientists were studying were forms calling to forms and were by no means the blind mechanisms of Democritan physis. Evidence was abundant on all hands, in the behaviour of animals from virus particles to humans, that these forms are active —indeed, proactive, since they are, quite obviously, anticipatory, goal-seeking functions that are in no way reducible to purely mechanical processes. It seemed to him, also, that with the burgeoning of modern information-technological, so-called ‘digital’ developments in science, following the work of Claude Shannon$^{[123]}$, there had been a definite resurgence of recognition of these future-directed or functional features of natural processes which Aristotle had named ‘entelechies’$^{[124]}$. In the face of these developments, the old reductionist physics of the likes of Democritus and Empedocles now definitely seemed to be losing ground, especially as modern computers and cybernetic systems in general became more familiar than the steam engines of the Industrial Revolution.

XV A New Approach to the Questions of Porphyry

Ruminating along these lines he recapitulated what he had learned about the origins of our modern ideas as to how, as observers, we are related to nature, and how our language has evolved around these, often intrinsically strange, ideas. He recalled that whereas for Democritan physics, forms as such had no existence, for Aristotelian scientists, forms and particulars went inseparably together. In contrast to both these opposing views, for Plato, as for his predecessor, Pythagoras, the forms of things, in logic, music, mathematics and so on, existed apart from and independently of the particulars comprising a Logos (capital el) of pure forms. These included the line, the square, the tetrahedron, the ‘music of the spheres’, human ‘souls’ and so on. Even animal souls were included according to the Pythagorean

$^{[122]}$ A Dictionary of Philosophy, Translated from the Russian by Dixon, R. R. and Saifulin, M, USSR, 1967, p. 68.

$^{[123]}$ Shannon, C. E, op. cit.

$^{[124]}$ The non-physical, non-chemical principle, or vital force, assumed to be responsible for life and growth. (See, e.g., Enc. Brit. Dict.)

theory of ‘transmigration’ (metempsychosis). In that theory it was believed that our souls transmigrate in different lives from one animal form to another in a hierarchy where ‘upward’ or ‘downward’ depended on how virtuously or otherwise, in each life, one had conducted oneself$^{[125]}$.

Moreover, for Pythagoras and Plato, forms were synonymous with ‘Ideas’; and the true forms (as opposed to those which are obviously subjective or illusory) were supposed to constitute a realm of supra-reality which was as remote from the ordinary logos, at the one end of the scale, as the infra-reality of Democritus was at the other. This entirely abstracted Logos of Forms, or Ideas, constituted for those Idealists a static and impersonal world of divine revelation and contemplation. In order to qualify for the Pythagorean priesthood, a student was required to achieve a supra-sensory mental state approaching what might be called ‘holy coma’. Following this same ‘other-worldly’ and therefore priestly line of thinking, the organisers of canonical Christianity had conceived human consciousness as a form capable of existing as a disembodied ‘soul’, so that at bodily death it was transported into a ‘heaven’ (or ‘hell’) conceived along those same Platonic/Pythagorean lines. Indeed, in the Canon Law synthesis of the Christian religion, by Thomas Aquinas (1225-74), around which so much of our Western language has evolved, elements of these Pythagorean, Platonic and Aristotelian influences became entangled to form the veritable ‘Gordian Knot’ in which subsequent natural philosophy had ended.

Our adventurer’s studies had thus convinced him that in the subsequent evolution of our language, through such a tortured intellectual process all talk of ‘forms’ had become wrapped in mystery. There are, obviously, forms of things which it makes perfect sense to speak of, nowadays as of yore, but due to that historical process of mystification, torn between Materialism and Idealism the original meanings of words like ‘form’, ‘idea’, ‘consciousness’, ‘soul’, etc., had been all but completely shredded.

It seemed, then, that the conceptual clutter left by all these various and conflicting attempts at improving on —or, indeed, replacing— normal realism had inhibited the rational evolution of our language. Ordinary and perfectly useful words had been polluted and corrupted to such an extent that, in philosophical contexts, scarcely anyone could agree on what they mean. Obviously, then, there was much tidying-up to be done along the ‘linguistic’ lines suggested by the likes of Austin and Wittgenstein before any further progress in natural philosophy could be contemplated. He would have to uncover the original meanings of these words ‘form’, ‘idea’, ‘soul’, ‘consciousness’, etc., to clean them up and reassess their relevance for the continuance of natural philosophy.

There was no recourse for him, then, he concluded, other than to review for himself, in the light of contemporary knowledge, the sorting-out of the forms which Aristotle had begun and which had ended nowadays in such confusion, not least due to the works of the more recent philosophers such as, in ascending order of esoteric, Descartes, Berkeley, Hume and Kant. In this frame of mind, in order to allow his relationist arguments to point their own logical conclusion, he decided to extend his sceptical analysis in this new direction of the natural logical arrangements of forms.

The fist thing he discovered was that he was not the first to think in this critical way. In response to the confusions bequeathed by the Greeks in their probing attempts to map-out the logos

$^{[125]}$ For the Pythagoreans, the religion that went with this Idealism was an extremely strict and studious one called Orphism. This was the worship and study of the esoteric doctrines and rites of a group of ancient Greeks claiming Orpheus, the mythical musician, as their founder. Orpheus was reputed to be able to charm beasts, rocks and trees with his music. These were known as the Orphic mysteries.

of forms, a third century philosopher, Porphyry [126], had posed the tripartite question which, paraphrased in modern language, was:

i) Do forms really exist or are they 'bare notions only'? ii) If they do exist, are they embodied in things or can they exist disembodied? iii) Do they exist in things independently of our observations or only in relation to us as observers?

These, he thought, were very fair questions, much along the lines he himself would like to have asked, So he decided to tackle them in order. He considered the first question:

i) Do forms really exist or are they 'bare notions only'?

Evidently, he told himself, there are forms that are pure illusion, such as faces in the embers, pictures in clouds, mirages, hallucinations, psychological projections in Rorschach tests, Müller-Lyer illusions and so on. These fitted Porphyry's classification of 'bare notions only'. Not so illusory, however, are forms in biology, such as the patterns of genes in the cells of animals and plants, which not only determine what form that organism may take but also are determined themselves by the way in which males and females had appealed to one another, selectively in terms of forms evolved over the ages. Then, to take a more physical example, there is the inorganic form of a crystal which has polarising and other effects on physical processes, while the same atoms, disarranged, exhibit no such effect. There is also the wetness of the form we know as water, in which we can swim and which we can use to put out fires, as contrasted with the airy dryness of its constituent gases, oxygen and hydrogen, in which it would be disastrous to attempt to swim or use to put out fires. And, of course, one should not forget the molecular form of a gene as handed on from generation to generation to perpetuate a species. The fact that these forms, or formations of things have such definite physical characters and effects, of which their particulars exhibit no trace should forestall any idea that all forms, indiscriminately, are 'bare notions only', as Porphyry had put it.

The plain answer to Porphyry's first question, then, had to be that not all forms were illusory but that one had to be discerning and systematic, in the way Aristotle had sought to be, in deciding, on unbiased empirical grounds, which forms are natural and real and which are no more than apparent. But wasn't that precisely the point which Moore had made in his refutation of Berkeleyan idealism, that there is no way in which the essences of all the things we describe in commonsense language can be confined to perception—or, in Porphyry's language, that they are 'bare notions only'?

So, to the next question:

ii) If forms do exist, are they embodied in things or can they exist disembodied?

The first thing that occurred to him in this regard was that forms are not necessarily temporal in the way that all physical processes are. That is to say, a form Gestalt, species, character complex or whatever, may not be subject to entropy, or decay, in the way its material embodiment always is. The physical contents of forms may come and go, while their formal essence remains. Documents such as Egyptian parchments and the Dead Sea Scrolls may be found in badly deteriorated condition, yet when

[126]Porphyry, A.D. .233-304. See Aaron, R. I., The Theory of Universals, Clarendon, Oxford, (1967) 2nd ed., Part 1.

their bits are collated and correctly interpreted the messages (forms of information) they convey, even with bits missing, may be resurrected complete and immaculate, transcribed onto paper, onto computer disks or some other medium.

There is an obvious sense, then, he decided, in which some forms may exist disembodied and may be even, in an archaic terminology, ‘immortal’. For instance, it is difficult to imagine how a Gestalt such as the relation of the circumference to the diameter of a circle has to be tied to any particular circle, or can be corrupted or eroded, or be different for different people and different cultures—even among extra-terrestrials, supposing these exist. The fact that commonsense recognises this atemporal and disembodied character of forms is witnessed by the fact that one may sensibly describe the same play, tune, idea, TV programme or whatever, as someone else describes, though the entities so identified may be rendered in different media and in places perhaps miles apart in space and years apart in time. And if an informational package of that sort may survive in that way, then, someone was bound to speculate, why not a human consciousness? If that can survive, intact, a lifelong relaying by comparatively short-lived body cells, then may it not, as Plato argued, survive the final destruction of those body-cells altogether and continue on into some transcendental realm of Platonic projection? In any event, he reflected, is it any more suitable to think of a conscious identity as pegged to the limitations of space, time and physical process, than the Pythagoras’ theorem, the National Anthem or the circular relation pi?

To the question, then, of whether forms exist embodied in things or can they exist disembodied, the answer had to be that, quite obviously, they may exist disembodied—that is to say, independently of their particulars, which may change entirely while the form remains the same. He recalled, once again the still-burning candle-flame whose particulars are rapidly changing, or the river into whose particular waters, as Heraclitus had remarked, no one can step twice.

It might be interesting, he thought, to make a short digression at this stage to dwell on the question of how this ‘disembodiment’ facility of the forms might apply to the form of human consciousness. Can a human identity, or soul, replicate itself at different places and times, like a TV play or a tune? But what empirical evidence was there for this? What seemed to militate against it was that psychological studies of identical twins had indicated that although their physical constitutions and psychological dispositions were practically identical, the twins reported completely separate and independent conscious identities. Nor, it seemed, had there been any reliable evidences of ‘telepathy’ between them. In that particular case, sameness of form did not guarantee identity. If it did, then we might expect that the cloning of animals would produce, among the clones, a single, non-localised awareness like that which some naturalists claimed to have discovered among the social insects, plants, schools of fish, flocks of birds and so on.

It would be very interesting, then, to say the least, if any such effect of human cloning were ever established—though on the evidence provided by the studies of identical twins, he wouldn’t bet too much on it! But supposing such experiments were ever allowed, even if they failed to establish that human consciousness can be de-localised in that way, that in itself would not logically terminate the possibility of what many people think of as the ‘immortality of the soul’. For, after all, there was also the evidence to be considered that in some cases of suspended animation due to profound hypothermia, where consciousness and all the associated physiological processes had ceased altogether, hours later the consciousness had continued more or less where it left off (In fact, he had had some direct experience of this when, as a boy of thirteen, he woke in hospital after three days in a state of concussion following a cycling accident, with no recall whatsoever of any time-lapse between whizzing down a hill and waking up in bed.)

For the moment, however, he would have to let all that lie, to be taken up later. Meanwhile, he had to address the third of Porphyry’s questions:

iii) Do forms exist in things independently of our observations or only in relation to us as observers?

This was easy!. The question had obviously been answered decisively by the modern theory of relativity, in which it is plain that the forms of things in nature, as described in terms of their length, time, mass, momentum, energy, colour ... in fact, just about every measure, quality, quantity, property or whatever, exist essentially, not only in relation to one another but also 'in relation to us as observers'. In short, then, so far as he could see, the answer to Porphyry's tripartite question was that forms really do exist, both embodied in things and disembodied in the sense that they exist not only in relation to our own senses and instrument systems but also to those of other observers. But so far as he could see, they are never entirely disembodied in the sense of existing relatively to no-one and nothing but themselves —that is, absolutely self-sufficiently, as in the Democritan, so-called 'realist' sense in which a thing (ultimately, an atom) is what it is absolutely, in and of itself, independently of any relation with other things.

XV.1 The Philosophical Implications of Relativistic Time-Staggering

At this stage in his deliberations our enquirer remembered that there was something else to be considered. This concerned the 'clock-paradox' situation which had bothered him right from the start of his odyssey but which he had shelved for future attention (see Chapter II). At that early stage he had wondered how it could be that since our consciousness consists of only a tiny moment in time we can be sure that the consciousness of a person we are communicating with on one occasion will be 'in there' on the next occasion if, in the meantime, our living-times are staggered like those of A and B in the situation discussed in the correspondence with Einstein.

At that embryonic stage in his thinking, he had assumed, as most people seemed to do, that everything had to exist in a single, all-advancing 'now'. In that case, future states of things —and our consciousness of them— do not yet exist and their past ones no longer exist. This would mean that all physical existence is confined within an indefinitely small time-band called the 'present moment'. What had struck him as so absurd about the idea of relative time, therefore, was that if the times of things were changed in the way Einstein had argued, then the proper times of those things would no longer tally. And if their proper-times varied in that way relatively to one another, then so, it seemed, would their very existence. As he reasoned it, if Einstein's theory and that conventional precept of the time-confined 'now' were both true, then when, after his journey, B returned to A he would arrive in A's past, when A no longer existed (A's time having gone faster than B's in the interim), whereas so far as A was concerned, with B's time lagging behind his own, that return-event would be in B's future, when B was yet to exist. Everyone's and everything's now of physical and conscious existence would be temporally 'out of synch'. Something was definitely wrong somewhere!

One logical answer he considered at that time was that even if Einstein's theory was correct and this existential 'out of synch.' state of affairs really does take place, then it could never be noticed. For if, as according to Einstein, the time-tracks (geodesics) of objects were pre-extended like the groove in a gramophone record, then no matter where in that 'groove' you placed the needle, you would always find the tune. For a while he had been satisfied with this explanation of physical existence as some kind of pre-existing wavy or 'warped' underlying continuum like the record-groove, so that no matter where you placed your 'needle' of observation you would always find something there.

However, the more his ideas had developed, the less happy he had become with that deterministic 'time-track' solution. For instance, to assume that things existed in that time-extended way made it seem feasible, in the way many science-fiction writers had exploited, to think of a time-traveller being able to move about in that continuum and 'land' anywhere (anywhen) to make that point in history the 'now' of his observation. However, in view of the fact emphasised by Heisenberg that all observation is physical interaction, that sort of time-travel would have to be an intervention; and an intervention, by definition, changes things. So if one were able to go back in time, the merest glance at what was going on would change everything from then on. For instance, in transporting back to the time of one's childhood, one might materialise in a roadway of that period, causing a car to swerve and kill a child which might be one's younger self, making nonsense of the whole operation.

This, with his apologies to Einstein, disposed of any notion that there can be existential continuity in the form of 'time-tracks' in which all the various times exist at the same time—which only had to be stated to show how nonsensical it was [127]. To comply with the findings of Heisenberg, et al., one had to accept that indeterminacy really is indeterminacy and that information really is information, not an illusion. This meant that one had to abandon any notion of physical existence being the sort of continuum that Einstein had envisaged, of time or space-time or whatever. The only 'time-travel' one could sensibly talk about, therefore, he decided, is in the well-authenticated, relativistic sense that since all distances are times in the constant ratio of units c, all travel is time-travel. Going forward and backwards in time was therefore commonplace insofar as it is simply what everyone does with respect to everyone else when they travel relatively to one another, like A and B in the clock (non)-paradox example.

This brought him back to the paradoxical consequence of the staggered existential 'nows'. It was a dilemma to challenge his whole thesis, a test of the logical consistency of its premises and conclusions about space and time. It had to be confronted, he told himself, fairly and squarely, in commonsense terms consistent with his Normal Realist stance, without any tortured theoretical contrivance involving hypotheses designed for no purpose other than to save the theory. For who on earth, other than for reasons of academic kudos—which was an irrelevance in his case—would want to be landed with an illusion? Better to end, even on one's deathbed, he thought, with the knowledge that one had been wrong all one's life than having succeeded, to the last, in fooling oneself completely.

Our odysseyist, now that he was older and with waning physical energy, often found himself lying awake in the small hours, reflecting on the events of his unfinished odyssey. Sometimes, his wife would wake too and they would sit with a comforting cup of tea and chat about life. Not all their reminiscences were happy ones, not even of the recent past, when problems with their children and grandchildren had had to take precedence over scientific interest. It was also now a trying time when old friends and relatives died or succumbed to the frailties of advancing age. Funerals became frequent social occasions, emphasising their own mortality and awareness of their own deteriorating health.

Nevertheless, our adventurer felt that they had little to be sorry about, and took it all as part and parcel of the same philosophical adventure. Although not wealthy, they lived quite comfortably and were grateful for what they had. They could afford to travel, and with their family scattered to different parts of the globe, they were able to visit places which, when they were young, they could only have dreamed of.

[127] There had been, however, a theory, by John William Dunne (Experiment with Time, Faber and Faber, London, (1952)) to the effect that all the time-tracks in the one time exist as an instant in another, higher-order time, which, again, forms an 'instant' in yet another time ... and so on, ad infinitum—the theory which Dunne called 'serial time'. Indeed, our odysseyist had conceived such an idea himself (which was why Dunne's book had been recommended to him) and was, for a while much taken up with Dunne's theory before abandoning it as having committed the logical fallacy of infinite regress.

One area they visited regularly was British Columbia, where one of their daughters lived on a remote farm. Feeling like a backwoodsman, our retired lineman and his wife happily canoed the lakes and walked the trails of this beautiful country. Long rambling discussions led, on returning home, to their writing a book which they entitled 'Wondering in the Wilderness' (as yet unpublished) in which our intellectual adventurer's scientific and philosophical insights were combined with their real-life adventures, some of which were quite hair-raising.

His scientific theory now being well-documented, if not wholly welcomed within the mainstream scientific community, the time had come to turn his attention to the deeper implications of relationism. So thinking, he girded himself, yet again, with his best use of logic, to make or break his own thesis. This would be by following its implications, for better or worse, to wherever they might lead, regardless of the academic boundaries. Plainly, since relativistic time-staggering does not destroy the existence of things relatively to one another, the premises leading to his dilemma would have to be reviewed.

He spent much time discussing these problems with his wife and friends, but without reaching any satisfactory conclusion. Then one day his wife said, 'Remember how, when you've had difficulties like this in the past, you've eventually realised that it was because you were not applying your own theory properly? Well,' she said, 'perhaps it's like that now.' And she was right. Where he had gone wrong was that he had strayed back into standard traditional 'God's-eye-view' metaphysics. He reminded himself that in his own relationist thesis, as in Mach's, objects do not exist in themselves, absolutely or self-sufficiently, but only relatively to one another, in whole systems of interrelation, or forms, from which relation each form—each thing, in effect—took its individual character. In that case, there was no question of what objects might be doing or at what rate their times might be advancing in complete isolation, as God might be presumed to see them.

And the same applied to consciousness. Where he had gone wrong was in thinking of all things and all consciousnesses everywhere having somehow to exist absolutely in parallel, all at the same time-rate, so that the slightest deviation in their imagined 'line-abreast' temporal advance would put them existentially out of step. In his own thesis, as in Mach's, people are not just 'conscious' in that metaphysical, 'God's-eye-view' way. Their consciousness are essentially of other people and other things, and of themselves only in those interrelations, albeit each in his own time-frame. So in that consistently relationist way of thinking there can be no question of those consciousnesses getting temporally 'out of step'.

This meant that objective history is a communicational/interactional whole in which it makes no sense to speculate as to what, at any stage in the process, either physical objects or consciousnesses are doing metaphysically; that is, as we may imagine them to exist and endure completely 'in themselves' in some manner known only to God. In a relational system, even when they are at their remotest from one another, things never exist completely and isolatedly 'in themselves', far less all at the same time.

The problem, then, of the relativistic time-staggering of consciousnesses was, from a consistently relationist point of view, a complete non-starter. There was no sensible way in which consciousnesses could be said to get 'out of joint' with one another in the way he had supposed because, despite their relativistically staggered time-rates, as the interagents move about, no matter how far or how fast they move relatively to one another, they remain, throughout, in physical contact in hosts of interatomic angular momentum contacts, or quantum stills (see Chapter XII.2).

However, while these quantum-still interconnections are instantaneous, their time-sequences (as, e.g., of A seen in B's telescope and of B seen in A's) are relativistically Doppler-shifted. So the nows that all these different observers share when they are close together are time-separated when they are apart by their instantaneous distance-separation s divided by c. And when they all return together, those which have undertaken the returning action relative to some one or some group of them which may have remained passive (unaccelerated) throughout, will be those whose times have been, by common consensus, dilated to an extent depending on their speed and acceleration.

Given, then, that there are as many 'observers' as there are stars and other astronomical bodies, there has to be some one or some group of these observers which is more passive (unaccelerated) than all the others. That would be the uniquely 'passive' frame with respect to which the activities of those others would be judged. And what else, he asked himself, would that datum-frame be but that of the overall angular momentum connection which Newton called 'gravitational' and Mach called 'inertial'? In any case, the interconnections would be of the sort which Mach described, in which the presence of distant objects is immediately felt. As Phipps had put it [128]:

If Mach was right, the seen universe and the felt universe are two quite different places. Even within the solar system the seen sun is measurably displaced from the gravitationally or inertially "felt" sun.

In other words, with respect to that 'felt' angular momentum frame, the sort of communication called 'electromagnetic' would be like the delayed-time sound of thunder with respect to the comparative immediacy of the lightning flash [129].

This difference, he realised, between the 'felt' now and the 'seen' now of distant bodies becomes hugely magnified on a cosmic scale. The consensus-existential now, therefore, it seemed, had to be the one on which all distance-separated observers can agree only when congregated at some common location —because, of course, being apart entails moving apart and therefore involves relativistic time-dilation. But that could not mean that the existential now is confined to some unique point in the universe, because that 'point' cannot be specified. It might be placed anywhere in space, between which different locations there would be the normal communicational time-delay s/c. Moreover, it had to be remembered that due to relative motion, the proper-times registered between one shared (on the spot) now and another was different for different observers and different groups of observers. Where, then, in all of this, he wondered, was the true datum now of the universe; and what was the truly cosmical time-measure separating those now, since it was neither the proper-time nor the relative-time measured by any particular observer.

The answer to the last part of that question, he decided, was that this datum time was that of the preferred state of rest implicit in the relativistic equations. He had long since ascertained (see Appendix 1) that these equations enable us to decide, quite unambiguously, which of however many relatively moving objects/observers are time-dilated and which are time-contracted relatively to which. It followed, then, that according to these equations, in a universe consisting of bodies all moving in different ways relatively to one another, those travelling fastest and farthest with respect to the statistical zero of the collection will record the least time, whereas those travelling slowest relative to that same datum will record the most time. The proper-time of that statistical zero of motion and acceleration, he concluded, is the standard time —the universal 'GMT', as it were— of the whole relational system, or universe, the time relative to which all other times are either equivalent or dilated. In real terms it would be the time of the frame which is uniquely unmoving relative to the 'fixed stars' —the frame Mach had called 'inertial' and with respect to which gyroscopes orientate themselves. That same time-datum would then be the 'cosmic time', or 'time of the universe' as Galileo and Newton had conceived it, and the present positions of bodies at every successive instant in that 'cosmic' or sidereal time would be what classical astronomers and physicists conceived as the now of the universe. In this way, he thought, in that eternal ongoing now, with its absolute proper-time sequences of spatial

[128] Phipps, T. E. Jr., Heretical Verities, Classic Non-Fiction Library, Urbana, Illinois, USA (1987) p. 341.

[129] See also T. Van Flandern (above, Ch. XII.5 FN [88]).

distributions, evolutionary continuity is maintained unproblematically throughout all the relativities of time and space.

In this analysis, he felt, the intuition of pre-Einsteinian, commonsense was restored. As he himself had intuited, prior to his contact with relativity, there is a universal now of physical existence in which it makes sense to talk about the present positions in relation to one another of distant bodies and a universal time in which all those universal nows, or present-moments evolve. And it was satisfying to recall that none of this is contradicted the relativistic equations! The knock-on effect of this logical-empirical reappraisal of phenomena, however, was profound. Not only did it support what Mach had said about mass, that nothing has mass in and of itself but only in relation to other bodies, it also said the same about consciousness. This implied that when consciousnesses cease to interact they cease to exist. Like mass, consciousness had to be something which a person (or any other organism) possessed only in relation to other consciousnesses and other things within the interaction-communicational nexus called the now, so there could be no question of consciousness existing anywhere on its own, outside that narrow interrelational time-band. In that case, there could be no conscious immortality in the traditional sense of being transported somewhere after death, because if there was no existence other than in this one and only uniquely defined time-band, then to where might that consciousness be conceivably ‘transported’?

XV. 2 The ‘Eternal Now’

He could therefore well understand the sentiment expressed by a woman at talk he gave on Science and Religion to a local discussion group, ‘When we die,’ she said, ‘that’s the end, finis!’ To this he had replied, ‘Let’s hope so, Madam, let’s hope so!’ Now why, he wondered, had he given her that guarded response? What reservations had been lurking at the back of his mind that had prompted this irony? Having reflected upon it he remembered something he had learned in his Sociology course at university about the social functions of even personal beliefs. If everyone had thought in the same way that this woman did, that all consciousness ended at death, then there would, surely, never have been any society to speak of. For if it was indeed a case of finis for consciousness with bodily death, then one might wonder why there had been any form of morality at all, in any human society past or present. With no survival of consciousness after death there can be no final calling-to-account for deeds done in life. No rewards and no punishments, the anticipation of which all religions throughout history had sought to instil in us. Do whatever you can get away with would be the order of the day, according to the advice of sceptics like Hobbes, Machiavelli and Nietzsche, and anyone not availing himself of the opportunities that such advice offered would be a fool, a mere slave to social conditioning.

Not that such purely pragmatical considerations —if that, indeed, was what they were— should be allowed to bias an objective philosophical analysis. But, he speculated, it might just be worth thinking about for a moment. Complete objectivity demanded that the language of ‘mortality’ and ‘immortality’ in terms of which the woman at his talk had couched her opinion, should not be judged hastily, on pure face-value. This was not least because it was based on precepts involving vestiges of old thoughts and exploratory ideas about existence and time which were no longer absolutely reliable. For instance, we tend to think of time, nowadays —as he himself had, in fact— in the way that was expressed in an old hymn he had learned as a child $^{[130]}$:

Time, like an ever-rolling stream,

$^{[130]}$ Isaac Watts (1674-1748), Number 165, Hymns Ancient and Modern.

They fly forgotten as a dream

Indeed, in the view of the twentieth-century American philosopher, William James, ‘A “river” or a “stream” are the metaphors by which it (the flow of time) is most naturally described’. This, however, was characteristically modern thinking, which would have been very foreign to the fifth-century B.C. philosopher Parmenides. For him there would have been no thinking in that way of anything that presently exists being ‘swept downstream’ from present to past, from being into non-being, like a leaf over a waterfall. For Parmenides and his Eleatic associates, there was, by definition, no non-being. Everything was being. ‘Non-being’ was just a noise. Everything that truly is at any time, these thinkers would say, is for all time, with no escape into non-being. In that way of thinking, there could be no sensible way of saying, simply, ‘X is not’. To say ‘X is not A’ or ‘X is not (or is no longer) B, C, D, ... or whatever, meant that A, B, C, etc., were qualities or properties used to describe X. In other words, the only truly meaningful use of words such as ‘is’, ‘being’, ‘exists’, and so on, was in ascribing some property, quality or set of relations to something else, as in ‘Socrates is short and stout’, or ‘Socrates is not handsome’ —or even, ‘Socrates no longer lives,’ or ‘is now dead’. Otherwise, to say, simply, ‘Socrates is not’ was a metaphysical convolution. Even nowadays, if you said that to someone nowadays, you would be asked, and quite sensibly, ‘Socrates is not ...What?’

As our odysseyist had discovered, then, the fact of relativistic time-staggering which had prompted his correspondence with Einstein, made the ‘stream’ (i.e., time-continuum) metaphor of Isaac Watts and William James as inappropriate, nowadays, to describe the existence of observers relative to one another as it would have been in those ancient times. Rather than think of things (forms) being borne away down a stream of time, it now seemed more suitable, in view of relativity and indeterminacy, to think of them remaining where they were, in the now and being transformed, in a continual ‘on-the-spot’ process, more like a candle-flame or a kaleidoscope pattern than the leaf in the stream. In this new/old way of thinking, time ‘flows’ from and into nowhere but simply evolves in and around that permanent and permanently changing event we call the present. Nor, he reasoned, could existence sensibly be thought of in the Einsteinian way, as something that advances ‘through time’ like a needle along a gramophone record, with the rest of the record extending both ahead of and behind it as space-time ‘continuum’ or ‘substratum’. As the ‘time-staggering’ example showed, nothing exists either ahead of or behind that unique and completely indeterminate moment of relativistic interaction and communication called the now. Time was therefore, he told himself, not something in itself, a ‘continuum’ in the sense of Einstein. It was not even a dimension that can be truly represented (other than purely schematically, of course) in diagrams of the sort he himself had drawn —far less whole sets or series of dimensional continua of the sort projected by the ‘many-worlds’ theorists. It had to be a whole set of relative dimensions projected in observation and communication from actual physical interaction, not a theoretical precondition of the same.

This, as he now saw it, changed the whole complexion of the ‘immortality’ issue. For instance, although it would logically dispose of any idea that there was any other state of being, outside the now, to which a soul might depart at death, it would not exclude the possibility of there being somewhere inside that existential now where the departed soul may be accommodated. Might this, he wondered, have been what Pythagoras had been trying to convey? If so, then that would explain why, for the Pythagoreans, a soul leaving a body had to appear straightaway in some other body somewhere else, as opposed to hanging about in some kind of Limbo or Purgatory, as later thinkers imagined.

As our explorer saw it, then, in order to accommodate, in these broader philosophical terms what he had discovered about relativistic time-staggering, he would have to revise all his old ideas of the sort he had inculcated, as a child, in the hymn about time being an ‘ever-rolling stream’. What he had seriously to consider was, as might have been argued in an Eleatic seminar, that whatever truly exists right now cannot at any time become non-existent but can only be transformed, so that everything that is and ever was exists and continues to exist in some changed form or other, in that eternal, existential now$^{[131]}$. That principle would certainly apply to those ‘conserved’, or ‘substantial’ measures like the angular momentum and mass-energy discussed in the previous chapters. Might it not also, he wondered, apply to what had been traditionally called the soul?

For the sake of consistency, he felt, this revision of our ideas of ‘existence’ and ‘time’, entailed by relativity, needed to be carried through, uninhibitedly and unselfconsciously, into the ancient question of the immortality of ‘self’ or ‘soul’ before any truly objective answer to that question could be contemplated. In other words, the concepts on which that question was based should, he felt, be properly studied in the light of these further logical and empirical considerations. Certainly the question was not to be summarily precluded on the tacit assumption that it was about whether or not at bodily death our souls are transported to some ‘other world’. Silly questions invite silly answers. At any rate, the basis of his reservations in answer to the woman’s statement were now plain. Like those ‘atheist’ students he had asked to describe the ‘God’ they did not believe in, the lady who said she didn’t believe in immortality had undoubtedly made that observation without any thought as to what, for her, ‘immortality’ meant. She may well have been right, he reflected, but any discussion of whether or not he could agree with her that consciousness ends with death would have to be conducted on grounds far more carefully considered than those she had —or, rather, had not — presented.

XV.3 Consciousness

But what, he asked himself, is consciousness anyway, and can it be thought of as something superior to and perhaps even separable from the physical body? This, for any convention-unencumbered thinker was a fair and sensible question to be tackled logically and objectively. To start with, then, consciousness is undoubtedly an empirical fact — surely, the first and most fundamental of empirical facts. As Descartes had noted in his famous statement: ‘I think, therefore I am’, consciousness definitely does exist in the world —at the very least in that bit of it which comprises oneself. It had always seemed to our lineman the height of absurdity that there were people prepared to doubt the reality of consciousness —even their own consciousness— on the basis of an inculcated (and entirely unjustified) belief that it was an epiphenomenon created and sustained by the movements of blind atoms. Surely these people could see that they had to be conscious in order to doubt that consciousness in the first place, hence that consciousness had to be the starting-point or datum-line of any enquiry —which, of course, was precisely the point that Descartes had made. Needless to say, it was also the inevitable datum-line of any truly scientific enquiry.

Besides, there could be no doubt that consciousness exists, not only in oneself but also in others who are every bit as capable of estimating, discussing and deciding courses of action and, on that basis, exerting a measure of true volition, all within that same narrow band of interaction and communication called the present moment. Defined ostensibly, then, (as opposed to theoretically or metaphysically), consciousness exists, is creative, purposive and can change things. Being no mere ‘chemical product’ it rises above automaticity. It can do things in its own right, for the sake of its own interests as well as those of others. And, as recognised by the science of psychology, it has its own peculiar integrity, acting in ways which are by no means reducible to those blind physical processes which are governed by entropy.

However, there was also something else that had to be considered, namely, those aspects of consciousness which modern psychologists had described as ‘unconscious’, ‘subconscious’,

$^{[131]}$ On discussing this with one of his friends he was informed that the phrase ‘eternal now’ had been used by someone else (See Ouspensky, P. D., ‘Christianity and the New Testament’, and ‘The Fourth Way’, in The Teachings of G. I. Gurdjieff, Penguin (1990).

'underconscious'... and so on. Where did these fit? There were definitely purely reflexive responses which are called autonomic, such as breathing, digesting and so on, which continue even when we are profoundly unconscious. To include all these different aspects, therefore, under the umbrella-term 'consciousness' seemed inappropriate. Perhaps, then, he thought, the old word 'psyche' might be pressed into service as a replacement for 'consciousness' because, after all, that psyche was the same both waking and sleeping. Even the archaic word 'soul' might serve as a variant so long as it was stripped of its modern-day mystical accretions.
