On the understanding that these words signified no more nor less than the entity that is fully recognised as the basis of the respectable science of psychology, he decided that the words 'psyche' and 'soul' might be used, without further apology, as alternatives to describe what everyone outside the narrow confines of mechanistic physics recognised as the essence of a human individual—and even of an animal.

Something, then, which well and truly is, he decided—during its life at any rate—is that essence of an individual personality traditionally called the soul. Recalling the premise he had enunciated previously, that whatever there truly is at any time has to remain in being for all time, with no escape for anything from being into non-being, he asked himself the question of whence, on that premise, the soul might disappear at death. What answer should there be, he reflected, but 'somewhere or other in the eternal now'. But how was that sort of present persistence to be conceived? Few people, he thought, were prepared to return to the Pythagorean view of the transmigration of souls—at least, not in the way the Orphic priests had originally presented it. There was, however, a more up-to-date version of that Pythagorean doctrine contained in the concept of Gaea (sometimes spelled Gaia), from the name of the earth goddess in Greek mythology. This, as he had read somewhere, was embraced by certain 'New Age' thinkers, so called, who regarded our planet as a living organism with its own agenda of aims and intentions, of whose 'consciousness' we partake as individuals. This is in the way that, say, ants and termites, although they are physically separate and mobile, nevertheless seem to partake of the environmental awareness of the whole colony. Some people saw our planet, in all its growing forms of communication, developing a nerve-net similar, at its present stage, to that of a starfish, but now evolving rapidly towards the encephalisation characteristic of the higher organisms. In any event, it seemed that our planet was now well on its way towards becoming an organism—or, perhaps, a super-organism—in its own right. Scarcely anything could now come within a parsec of it without being detected. Day and night, the earth's antennae were out there, scanning its environment like a beast in the forest. Its motorways and roads were becoming more like the arteries, veins and capillaries of a living organism, of which we and other animals are analogous to the 'cells'. And our apparently independent activities—as many naturalists and sociologists present them, at any rate—seemed more and more to be bound-up with some functional need of the planet as a whole than with our own interests as individuals.

How localised, then, was the human psyche? Descartes had supposed that it was purely personal, located in the pineal gland, from which it was supposed to navigate the body as from the bridge of a ship. Modern psychology and physiology, however, had shown that there can be no question of such precise location. These sciences had ascertained, that the organisation which forms the psyche has no specific centre. Rather, it operates as a whole (i.e., holistically) in a way which is not entirely reducible to autonomous activities of its cells and their electrochemical interconnections. Large—indeed huge—though the body is in atomic dimensions, its self-awareness is spread all over, even into limbs that have been amputated, as testified by the so-called 'phantom limb' phenomenon. So how far, he wondered, did this non-specificity or non-locality of the psyche extend? He was aware that some people claimed actual experience of disembodiment, as in claims of so-called 'out-of-body' and 'near-death' experiences. Indeed, his own wife claimed to have once experienced such a disembodied awareness under anaesthetic at a dentist's. Again, the fact that he himself had never had experiences of that kind was no evidence against them. It was altogether too facile for those like himself who had no claim to such experiences to assume that all those who did were simply lying or deluded. But also, he cautioned himself, one should not be so gullible as to accept all such claims as ‘evidence’ because honesty, even at its best, was no absolute guarantee against self-delusion. Moreover, objectivity demanded that empirical evidence had to be replicable, that an experience reported by any one person should be experienceable in the same or similar manner by others or should at least be verifiable in some way or another by the use of instruments. However, by the very nature of subjective experience, these criteria might not always be relevant. One day, whilst fishing with a friend, the atmosphere, the ambient light, the blue sea and sky suddenly invoked a feeling which overwhelmed him. After a long and enraptured silence, he was just about to say something when his friend stated it exactly: ‘This,’ the friend breathed, ‘is like the “Boyhood of Raleigh”,’ (a painting by Millais which, as it turned out, they had both known from school). Was this experience objectified by the fact that two people had experienced it at the same time? It was undoubtedly replicated. But what ‘instruments’ could have been considered relevant in that case? And were there, perhaps, other experiences of that sort, perhaps more vivid and even possibly of a more ‘paranormal’ kind, which were replicable in that same way but communicable only in verbalised or ‘anecdotal’ form? All he could do, from his point of view of someone singularly lacking in such ‘paranormal’ experiences was to try to sort out for himself which of these various anecdotal claims were simply beyond logical reason and which were definitely contrary to it. Like Sherlock Holmes, he would have to say that once you’ve logically eliminated the impossible, anything else is probable.

In this systematically open-minded or agnostic frame of mind our delving ex-lineman continued to examine the logicality, if such there was, of the age-old claims for immortality of the self or soul. He remembered that there was something else again to consider. This was the fact that in purely practical terms, the health and well-being of some individuals and sections of society can be secured at the expense of others. No society, as such, therefore, could ever function in the way that people like Jeremy Bentham (1748-1832) had envisaged with his ‘felicific calculus’ according to which the aim of all morality was to secure ‘the greatest happiness of the greatest number’. Unfortunately, for any realistic interpretation of that theoretical ideal, the ‘greatest number’ for all too many people was ‘number one’, the irrefutable maxim of plain unadulterated selfishness. On that basis of pure selfishness, no society could ever have existed—at least, not for long. Any society imbued with that ‘selfishness’ ethic would require severe measures of policing in the interests of some ruling faction, generating the inevitable anarchistic reaction which brings such regimes to an end, so that others may be instituted (no less precariously) in their place.

Clearly, then, no moral formula could ever work without some conviction among its members of a personal accountability which was inescapable even by death. It seemed, therefore, that there was no way in which morality could be reduced to calculations of any purely political or ‘humanist’ sort. Without some moral premiums placed on posthumous and therefore inescapable accountability, no society, he decided, could ever survive. It was undoubtedly for this reason that every society throughout history had its built-in ideas of a ‘hereafter’, of one kind or another. Only in our modern Western society, it seemed, had these ideas been dismissed, in the way that was now fashionable, as ‘political ploys on the part of the ruling classes’. However, even if the ruling classes had operated these ideas to their own advantage—as, indeed, they ought, according to such as Hobbes and Machiavelli—that did not make those ideas necessarily and basically false. And if this pre-mechanistic awareness of the inescapability of continuous conscious existence and posthumous moral accountability should be somehow rationally restored to our Western society, then at the very least, that might reinstate some of the lost premiums on moral behaviour which have undoubtedly made this last century the nastiest on record.

But, of course, these moral premiums could not now be restored pragmatically, by decree, as a matter of purely political expediency—though they might well be enforced, in some places at least, by sheer irrational (e.g., ‘fundamentalist’) reactions, as a policy of desperation against an increasingly amoral trend. Personally, however, he had seen enough of the evils of that kind of belief-addiction among those who, with their stylised and stilted modes of expression had managed to give religion such a bad name. This was not only in well-known cases like that of the Manson murders, the Waco tragedy and the suicides during the appearance of the Hale-Bopp comet, all in the last decades of the twentieth century. There were also some difficulties with religious fundamentalism, not only within his own family but also, as this personal history attests, in his contacts with scientists entrenched in their own peculiarly deistic beliefs and superstitions. Far better, then, he thought, if these moral premiums—or something like them—could be restored rationally, by removal of the barriers which the last few centuries of mechanistic science had erected against them. At least, on the basis of a new relationist natural philosophy, the rational and well-intentioned people that there are always to be found in any society might be allowed to continue the moral dialogue of the ages without further interference from the bogus ‘authority’ of either the mechanists or the reactionary ‘fundamentalists’.

But, he asked himself, even supposing that there is posthumous personal accountability, what difference should that make to the way we conduct our lives? How should that conviction direct us as to what moral code to follow? Do we, as individuals, have any more chance of calculating that than Bentham had of establishing his felicitic calculus? Did this, he wondered, mark the need for some kind of divine inspiration mediated to us ‘from on high’ by such gurus as Jesus, Mahomet or Buddha? But, as he reminded himself, history attested the extent to which such ‘divine inspirations’ are flawed by their capacity to create as much ‘evil’ in their own right as anything they are directed against. Was there, then, some moral common factor among these various teachings which might be winnowed-out as a rational basis for a workable world-morality? If so, then the extended aim of a truly natural philosophy should surely be to sort out the natural logos of that morality from the various idiosyncratic and purely evangelical doctrines of it, in the same way that in science the aim should be to sort out the same natural logos, in contexts like those of physics, from all the various conflicting theoretical dogmas about it.

Otherwise, as he saw it, without that natural and rational morality the control of our world-population would, with the failure of natural religion fed by the virulence of an amoral science, fall automatically to the function of wars and other culling processes, in the way that a tree sheds leaves and sometimes whole branches in times of stress. Might not our recent world-conflicts, then, be the effect of a similar stress caused by mechanistic science’s blocking of the sap of natural philosophy?

So what, he wondered, might the missing moral factor be? Or was it, perhaps, that there simply was none and that with global proliferation our species had reached its natural ‘sell-by’ date and was now headed for natural extinction, like so many species before us which had failed to match pace with a changing environment? Or might it be that it does not matter whether or not our society or even our species survives, or indeed whether it is naturally doomed, so long as from that cauldron of chronic social ineptitude our individual souls are saved in the way that was projected by John Bunyan in his book Pilgrim’s Progress?

How much of our ideas of morality, anyway, should be concerned with our own personal salvation and how much with that of our fellow beings? What, if anything, does the one concern have to do with the other, and if so, what proportion should there be of each? How, and on what rational basis, was any of this to be truly assessed? These sorts of questions crowded in on our ex telephone-lineman as he sat with his wife watching the scudding view from a fast train on their way to a holiday in Italy. There remained, throughout, the realisation that that no matter how necessary soul-survival might be for the functional purposes of human society, there was no evidence of it that could possibly be garnered by empirical science. Certainly there was none of which he knew that survived careful objective inspection. Yet neither could he find any logical contradiction in the claims by other people of ‘out-of-body’ experiences and of psychic experiences during periods of suspended animation and even clinical death. The mere fact that one saw no empirical evidence for it was obviously no argument against it, any more than the fact that one sees no astronomical evidence of extra-terrestrial life means that life on our planet is the only life in the universe.

It occurred to him, moreover, that a consciousness cannot, obviously, come into being and then non-exist relatively to itself. That is to say, a consciousness cannot be conscious of its own non-existence. What can happen is that in relation to the consciousness in question, ordinary time-processes in the world, as others experience these, can stop—as, for example, in cases of mental ‘blackouts’, deep sleep and anaesthesia, or coma—and then resume in what, to that consciousness, may be a disappearingly small instant. In that case, so far as concerns the consciousness in question, it is the world that has ceased to exist, not the consciousness. Only in communication and interaction with others in a new and entirely different physical situation might that consciousness ever become aware of anything having occurred ‘in the interim’ as the world sees it.

Nor was there, he noted, even in these circumstances any evidence of that consciousness being put out of joint with the conscious consensus. So how, he wondered, according to the premise of permanent existence in the now, was that suspension of consciousness between one world-time and another to be envisaged? Where, if anywhere, could that consciousness reside in order to cease in the world’s time and rejoin it later in what to itself was no time at all? On a sunny morning beside Lake Maggiore (in Italy), whilst enjoying the view he sat and thought about it all. These were questions which were begged by the scientific fact of relativistic time-staggering. They were there, crying out to be answered, yet it seemed that no one had thought these things. Why was that? It had to be, he decided, that physicists were conventionally shy of following trails, no matter how logical, into territories where all talk of ‘existence’, ‘consciousness’, ‘immortality’ and so on were, for them, ritually tabu. Yet those implications were there for the taking. For instance, the question of how might a consciousness-time cease relative to the existential time and rejoin it later was plainly answered by relativity. It was precisely what would happen in the original clock non-paradox example if B travelled a distance $s$ away from A at the speed $c$ and returned immediately at that speed, having recorded zero time for the journey. But of course, for B to travel away and return in that zero time would be physically impossible due to his bodily mass, which would require an infinite acceleration to reach that speed $c$. Logically, then, on the premise that the psyche of an observer might exist separately from that observer, for a disembodied observer, or pure psyche, the mass would present no such problem. With zero mass, that psyche might travel any distance and back in non-zero time, just like a quantum of light-energy. In any length whatsoever of the world’s time, that psyche might leave the body and be transported any distance, in no time at all relatively to itself, and re-embody itself back at source—or, perhaps, without returning, lodge itself in some other corpus somewhere else, in the way Pythagoras had envisaged. No doubt, if the modern fact of relativity had been known to Pythagoras, he would have found this the obvious way of presenting his theory of metempsychosis. That, however, he realised with a lurch, would have meant identifying the psyche with light!

XV. 4 The Psyche and the Psi Function

Looking at the light reflected off the lake, the intricate patterns of sparkles off the wavelets, the mountains in the misty distance, our philosophical freethinker could see nothing but light. It was there all around. He was literally immersed in it. Might the normally embodied psyche, then, he mused, be conceived as being in some way a condensate of that light? Somehow, in that particularly luminous setting, the suggestion did not seem entirely absurd. Surely, he thought, if our present knowledge of encephalography had been available to the Pythagoreans they might well have argued that since the ‘brain-waves’ that are recorded on the encephalograph are of the same physical nature as light (i.e., what is currently called ‘electromagnetic’), that identification of the psyche with light would be far from implausible. That is to say, if those waves which the encephalograph registers are associated with light they are also associated with mind—or, at least, with those brain-patterns which are the external evidence of that psychic activity. So, those Pythagoreans might have claimed, there would be no logical contradiction in surmising that the psyche was a form of light. Light, they might have said, is a kind of informational 'soup' in which everything is steeped. Moreover, had they but known it, in a definite sense we really are all condensates out of light. Sunshine produces, by photosynthesis, the food we eat, which forms all the ingredients in our bodies and those of other organisms. And even the atoms of minerals interact in terms of light by releasing and storing mass-energy in fractions of $mc^2$ in the manner described in Chapter VII.1 (equation (8)). The very geometry and substance of the world, those Greeks might therefore have claimed—and certainly so far as relativity is concerned—is in the light. Light is what gives shape and form to things, with emphasis on form. If logos is anything, therefore, they might have argued, it is light. So, he speculated, in the context of modern relativity, the idea represented in the various ancient religions, of the psyche condensing out of something like a logos at birth and returning to it at death did not seem such a wild idea after all.

Sitting there beside the lake, with the light reflecting off the water, watching the perspective and parallax-changes of the fast-moving ferries against the island backgrounds, it was easy to picture those ancient thinkers, in surroundings which would surely have been no less compelling than these, musing the way they did. For some time, he sat and savoured that feeling of empathy with those philosophers in a way he had never experienced in his readings of books. On the premise that the psyche truly exists and the further premise (from relativistic time-staggering) that what truly exists in the now cannot non-exist but must appear in some form or other somewhere else, when the psyche of an observer X ceases relatively to the world-time of other observers A, B, C, ... etc., the only logical place for it to go had to be somewhere or another in the evolving now. And the only way in which anything can remain temporally suspended in that way (that is, in its own zero proper-time) was for it to be transported 'luminiferously', in a completely disembodied state, like a quantum of light, at the 'speed' $c$ in the clock non-paradox situation. (He remembered that people reporting 'near-death' or 'out-of-body' experiences described these, typically, as being bathed in or drawn to a 'great light'.)

However, for X's disembodied psyche to remain temporally suspended in that way it would have to keep on travelling relative to the corporeal world at the 'speed of light' for as long as was required by the relative period of suspension, which might be over any distance-time whatsoever. To think in that way of the psyche shooting-off into the great beyond, as a form of light-energy, perhaps never to return, or else, maybe, re-embodying itself when, say, the brain-functions of X returned after a coma, did not seem credible. There was, though, another way of looking at it which seemed not quite so incredible. This was to remind oneself that the distance $s$ over which light is transported at the relative 'speed' $c$ may be split into as many immediate to-and-fro segments as can be imagined. This would confine the psyche waves in something like what wave-theorists call a resonance cavity; analogous to a vacuum flask in which heat-radiation is contained by reciprocal reflection within its silvered inside surfaces. In this way, a perfectly efficient resonance-cavity (perfect 'vacuum-flask') might be thought of, with suitable caution, as accommodating a whole set of encephalographic waves, in suspended resonance, like a genie in a bottle. This reminded him a little bit of 'Schrödinger's Cat', whose existence as an uncollapsed wave-function could not be known until the box in which it was imprisoned was opened $^{[132]}$. After all, he reminded himself, resonance was what his thesis was all about and it was also what quantum physics was about. Perhaps, he reflected, the two were, in their different ways, saying the same sort of thing. The whole angular momentum discretum, as he had described it, was a resonance-cavity; and so, in quantum angular momentum terms, was every length distancing every atom from every other. Somewhere in the those resonant interstices, he mused, the psyche might possibly exist in suspended, disembodied form.

$^{[132]}$ Gribbin, J. In search of Schrödinger's Cat, Black Swan (1984).

The idea of the brain-waves identified as the psyche as resonating reciprocally in some way or another in the logos was therefore not too difficult to think about, much less so for an ex telecommunications engineer like himself—and especially compared to the traditional ‘other-worldly’ alternatives. He felt sure that had he been a member of a Pythagorean seminar but equipped with his modern knowledge of ‘wave-dynamics’, that ‘luminiferous’ transportation idea was a possibility he might have suggested as a model of transmigration.

For a while he remained intrigued with this ‘luminiferous’ model of transmigration. But then it occurred to him that there was a rather obvious objection to it. If the psyche was to be identified with light, then on departure from the body it should be detectable in the same way that light is detectable, as, for instance, with some encephalographic version of a thermal imaging camera. That is to say, the departing ‘luminiferous’ psyche should be radiated, reflected, refracted, diffracted, scattered, distance-diminished, cast shadows ... etc., in at least some of the ways which are instrumentally measurable. So far as he was aware, however, neither physics nor psychology had produced any such evidence—other than, of course, to establish that the highly organised electrochemical brain-function that psychologists study as the psyche dissipates entropically at death by radiating away in the completely randomising, purely physical form of heat. But although this supported the ‘luminiferous’ hypothesis of psychic identification with light, it would scarcely suffice for the Pythagoreans as a satisfactory model of what they called metempsychosis.

Intriguing, therefore, though that ‘luminiferous’ model of psychic transportation might be, it would have to be abandoned—in that particular form, at least. Plainly, the living entity that is studied by physiologists as ‘brain-function’ and psychologists as ‘psyche’ could not, as such, be regarded, at death as radiated away as any coherent form of energy, in anything like the complete and wholesome manner of a picture beamed by a slide-projector or laser. However, as he reminded himself, in his thesis there was not just that one aspect to light. There was that other aspect in which it had none of those radiational characteristics, hence none of the associated objections. This was, of course, its non-radiational, angular momentum or ‘quantum still’ aspect which is customarily called—or, rather miscalled—‘gravitational’, as previously discussed (in Part 3 of this volume). So whereas the radiational aspect of light is sequential, or ‘cinematic’, this other aspect is static. The difference, in other words, was that the radiational process was what physicists call local, while the static connection was non-local (or, as Einstein had dubbed it, ‘spooky superluminal’). This was the way, already described, in which, due to angular momentum conservation, the form of an orbit at one place is correlated, holistically, with the forms of all other orbits in the system. Might it be, then, he speculated, that in the logos this overall connexity, or correlation, of the forms of orbits extends also to the forms, or brain-patterns, of the psyche? If so, then it seemed that the luminiferous model of transmigration he had thought of abandoning could be recovered by its Pythagorean proponents, and all its resonance characteristics rescued from the ‘radiational’ objections.

Accordingly, as our speculator now conceived it, an up-to-date member of a Pythagorean seminar might have claimed that the theory of psychic transmigration was not so silly after all. What was silly, he might have said, was any idea that transmigration should be detectable in the form of radiant energy. And what was even sillier, he might have added, was to assume that the sheer absence of any such en route evidence of metempsychosis in radiational terms was evidence that it could not take place in non-local, action-at-a-distance terms. After all, he might have pointed out, there is no possible en route detection, in local-process terms, of angular momentum correlations between distant orbital systems, but that does not mean that such correlations do not take place.

Besides, he might have argued, the psyche is essentially a form, a character-complex—what some modern information-physicists, such as Marcer$^{[133]}$, have described as a quantum hologram. And

---132

forms, as already discussed (see Chapter XV) can maintain their identity independently of their particulars. Whether or not a form such as the psyche can transmigrate in the way the Pythagoreans envisaged was therefore not a matter of whether its particulars (its physical concomitants) can be transported from one place to another. It was a matter of whether or not as a form it can be accommodated immediately (i.e., instantly and without mediation) somewhere within the overall pattern of resonance that was the angular momentum discretum or Heraclitan logos. This was in the way that —to employ a typically Pythagorean simile— certain harmonics fit into a tuned string, or an instrumental piece of music into a whole orchestration.

At this point, then, he felt he had to acknowledge a vague similarity between these theoretical projections of his and those of the ‘collapse of the wave-function’ idea of conventional quantum wave mechanics. He had already incorporated into his theory this purely mathematical ‘probability function’ of de Broglie and Schrödinger —stripped, of course, of all bogus local-process implications regarding ‘waves’ and ‘fields’. Was it credible, he asked himself, that the logos could be thought of (with these linguistic reservations) as an unimaginably intricate cosmical ‘wave-function’ —in pure resonance terms, consisting of forms such as (on its most rudimentary level) angular momentum stills and sequences of stills and, on its highest levels, forms analogous to and perhaps including the human psyche? Had he, then, he wondered, been unjustified in his scorn for the unscientific character of that standard-model ‘wave-functional’ view, which he now found barely distinguishable, except in its modern terminology, of course, from the Pythagorean musical concept of the ‘logos’ as an overall-connected cosmic harmony? Should he not have regarded the ‘wave-function’ as just another legitimately speculative and exploratory notion on the way towards a full understanding of nature? Linguistically dusted-off, it would no doubt go to swell the historical fund of no less ingenious ideas subscribed by the likes of Pythagoras, Parmenides and Plato.

Anyway, he thought, in order to accommodate it to his relationist philosophy, the unnecessary analog implications of the ‘wave-functional’ concept could be avoided by referring to it simply as the psi function, from the Greek letter $\psi$ (psi) as customarily used for Schrödinger’s probability function. This, he felt, would be a more parsimonious and therefore more efficient term for describing, in this new context, that function which he had already embraced into his angular momentum system. To think of the logos (Nature, Gaea or whatever) as a Heraclitan flux, like a candle-flame —a highly structured musical/mathematical ‘flame’, to be sure— was not altogether too taxing to contemplate for an adventurous thinker. It was certainly what some might regard as ‘far out’, but scarcely more so than the ideas of some modern theoretical physicists who, in the view of many commentators, had become ‘more mystical than the mystics’.

So far, then, as our adventurer was concerned, having cast off all the old ‘mechanistic’ inhibitions, how else should a consistently relationist science proceed other than by speculations of this far-reaching and free-ranging kind? At least it should allow the new-age science to merge seamlessly with commonsense in that ongoing search for the verities of our existence which was once called natural philosophy. With those mechanistic dreads and superstitions removed, it all seemed to make logical sense —at least, it raised no obvious contradictions or paradoxes. It was outlandishly strange, to be sure, but logical nonetheless. He thought back to the incipient ‘God’s time’ that he, like so many others, had automatically thought of as the universal background-time, the time of all physical existence. It was against that, that Einstein’s relative-time had seemed so paradoxical. It now occurred to him that working-through the full implications of relativity had more or less reaffirmed that original naïve conception of an objectively real universal now of the existence of things. So perhaps, he mused, these new developments were not so ‘outlandish’ after all, since they had done no more, in fact, than to reaffirm something of the commonsense way in which he had originally conceived it. The wheel,

$^{[133]}$ Marcer, P. ‘From the Self-Creation to the Creation of the Self’. Proceedings of ANPA 18, Cambridge (1996), pp. 172–183.

in that sense it seemed, had turned full circle —albeit, so far as he was concerned, having, in the process, rolled his own philosophical wagon out of its original traditionalist rut.

XVI Beyond but not Contrary to Logic

In pursuing his course of enquiry towards the full philosophical implications of the issue of time-staggering, which had been raised in his correspondence with Einstein, our odysseyist had steered perilously close to the rocks of religious subjectivism. But so, in their various ways, had many scientists, the wreckage of whose mystical ideas littered the populist shoreline of scientific literature. He was confident, however, that in his odyssey, those subjectivist rocks had not even scraped his keel. He felt he had dispassionately analysed and listed all the various logical possibilities without actually committing himself to any of them.

However, in response to that disinterested approach an eminent scientist chided him for ducking the issue of theological commitment, of acting ‘like Pilate’ as this commentator had put it. Admittedly, being deeply suspicious of all forms of unrestrained subjectivism he had perhaps overstated the logic of his enquiry at the expense of personal belief and opinion.

But then, he reminded himself that his thesis had emphasised that there can be no approach to science, philosophy, theology or whatever, from which belief can be entirely excluded. In that case, he reflected, to satisfy his critic, some mention of his own personal beliefs in regard to theology and religion should be entered somewhere in the log of this philosophical odyssey.

On that score, so far as he was concerned, he could not be sure that it made sense to personify —far less deify— the logos (Nature, Gaea, or however one chose to name it). Should it be worshipped for all its wonder and beauty or despised for its endemic cruelty —for its brutal principle of ‘red in tooth and claw’ on which our presence on this planet is based? But what point was there in getting either ecstatic or cynical about it? It is what it is, and we have no alternative but to accept it on its own terms, regardless of any kind of humane evaluation.

These sorts of evaluative questions were, as he saw it, for theologians to address, being well outside the writ of science as such. In his view, the very best that any new scientific effort might ever hope to achieve in that direction was to pass on cleanly to natural theology the logical baton of natural philosophy that had been fumbled by mechanistic physics. He, personally, had never been conscious of feelings of religious awe or dread, far less of dull-minded indifference. His most intense feeling had been, from the start, that of sheer curiosity at the wonder of it all and the extent to which, by his thoughts and actions he could become involved in it. However it might be evaluated, the question remained as to why was there anything at all. Why was it that we could see for such great distances, savour the seasons’ scents, hear and make sense of sounds from near and far, pick things up and examine them, some to bend and shape as one wished? These abilities were, for him, great gifts, beyond any evaluation he could think of as ‘religious’. So he enjoyed them to the full, exercising them as they came, with all the energy he possessed, without any conventional restraint save by anything he was convinced were entirely reasonable. And as for any ‘religious’ kind of evaluation: ‘Look not a gift horse in the mouth’, as his grandmother used to say.

This same attitude permeated right through to his adult life, despite the many pressures of his upbringing towards religious conformity. If these pressures had not been sufficient to gain him any religious conviction, then neither had they been sufficient to make him react against it. If there was anything of true religious import to be discovered, then he was with Omar Khayyám in the view: 'Better one glimpse within the tavern caught than in the Temple lost outright'.

Yet, as he reminded himself, he was not unaware of the social necessity of some sort of conviction that might, without prejudice, be described as 'religious'. Purely doctrinal religion, however, left him cold, which left him in two minds about 'religion'. On the one hand he could see the social—and perhaps even personal—necessity of it, while on the other hand he was acutely aware of its addictive and inhibitive effects on man's search for true philosophical understanding.

In this somewhat ambivalent frame of mind, he was forced to wonder where the modern Church stood in all of this? Why had it not come 'up to speed' with our modern knowledge of nature? Why had it not capitalised on the obvious support that modern relativity had provided for the relationist (anti-mechanistic) sort of philosophy which Coleridge had indicated? Why did it insist on standing still, vainly trying to fit its traditional 'square pegs' into the ever more rounded political holes?

Once, though, on his travels as a telephone lineman, he had been most impressed to find, in the ancient halls of a local Catholic priory, a well-equipped laboratory in which priests were working with what was then the very latest in communicational technology. In the short time that was afforded (as he was at work) he had some very interesting discussions with these priests. Given more time he would have quizzed them as to where they stood in the matter of the changes in their philosophy that those latest information-theoretical developments must have entailed. Where, he would have asked them, were those brothers who would have argued these philosophical issues? Shouldn't the Church Authorities now be taking some kind of positive lead in this new-age science-philosophy direction instead of merely following in the wake of developing Industry and Economics?

Some years after his working visit to the Catholic priory he remembered a suggestion made by one of the novices to initiate such a dialogue with the Church and he wrote a letter (an over-long and complicated one, unfortunately) to the office of Cardinal Hume, the then Archbishop of Westminster. The gist of it, as expressed in its final paragraphs, was as follows [134]:

My researches have made it clear to me that the choice, nowadays, is not between science and faith but between two kinds of faith, one in a ubiquitous 'Presence' whose perceptions are entirely alien to man (deism or naturalism) and the other in God as a Logos or Mediator between human perspectives in the terms of human language. In Science, as I see it the implicit dogma of deism has become impenetrable. The form it gives to the theories of relativity and indeterminacy is fixed and no longer open to question.

In order to develop my thesis I need contact with people who are alive to the need for our understanding to evolve, for it to grow out of scientific esoteric into common knowledge. If theories like relativity and indeterminacy are true, then they are for the contemplation of people in general, as aspects of the cosmos, not simply rituals to be used in the initiation of students into the profession of Physics.

This was followed with a request to be put in touch with some scholar who might be interested in discussing this issue. The response which it elicited was from the Vicar General, who, having thanked our lineman for his letter, wrote [135]:

I remain a Thomist and have never found any difficulty in reconciling religion and science. I have always believed that science cannot tell us about God. However, I am sure that you are not very interested in my views on this matter. The difficulty is to find a scholar who would be able to help you, but I am not sure whether any of them would have the same interest as yourself.

[134] Phil. Glim. Vol. 5, p. 1370.

[135] Op. Cit. p. 1375.

And that was where the matter ended—except for an abortive discussion with a novice Catholic priest who became most unprofessionally enraged at any suggestion that the synthesis which Thomas Aquinas had instituted between the Church and 13th century Science could ever be improved upon—even though science had moved-on seven centuries since!

But why, he wondered, was that prospect of an ongoing synthesis between religion and science so steadfastly resisted by the Catholic Church from the Vicar General right down to the novice priest? Nor was there any better response to approaches to the Church of England. (A paper on the subject presented through the local vicar to a Church authority, at about the same time as the one to the Cardinal’s office, was returned without comment.) Why, then, did there seem to be, in the Churches, no one prepared even to think about these things? How could these people keep on talking about ‘souls’ without even trying to account, in modern terms, for what, if anything, ‘soul’ now means and what happens to these entities at death? For souls to survive they have to survive somehow and somewhere. Modern hardheaded thinkers should no more be asked to believe in the old unplaceable ‘heaven’ and ‘hell’ than to believe in the ancient Greek myth of the river Styx across which souls are ferried, at death, by the boatman Charon.

A modern synthesis along the lines projected by Thomas Aquinas should therefore, surely, he thought, give some logical account in properly understandable language of where, if anywhere in relation to real space and time, those ‘departed souls’ might be accommodated. Should they return to something like the ‘Gaea’ of the New Age thinkers, to the secular logos of the Greeks or into the divine Logos of Coleridge and the traditional Christian faith? Or, to follow the logical implications of relativity, should those brainwaves which are the physical manifestation of our psyche become re-absorbed into the luminiferous ‘soup’ from which they perhaps emerged? Or might it be that in the last analysis these answers were all basically the same, with the only differences being due to persistent partisan choices of what Wittgenstein called ‘language games’? In that case the confusions created by the sheer proliferation of these ‘games’ would need to be sorted out by linguistic analysis.

Had he been allowed, then, to infiltrate the apparently closed ranks of the Church seminary groups he would have asked members how, exactly, did they picture psychic immortality. Might it be that at the very lowest level of consciousness as, surely, in the case of death, we become disembodied yet still existing—to paraphrase the words of M. Scott Peck: like the newborn infant, at one with the logos but incapable of any action that might make its presence felt in the world$^{[136]}$? These scholars might also have been invited to consider the definite sense in which, since one can never be directly conscious of one’s own non-existence, one’s consciousness has to be, by default, eternal and indestructible—not in the world’s time, of course, but certainly in its own. Was this, perhaps, they might have been asked, what those original philosophers such as Pythagoras and Plato, were trying to express; Plato, no matter how ineptly, with his transcendental World of Ideas, and Pythagoras with his theory of transmigration? Such notions, he would have argued, seem ridiculous to us nowadays not because those who thought of them were stupid—far from it—but simply because that was not the way, historically, along which our conventions evolved. Not knowing what we now know about time in relativity and quantum theory, those thinkers could surely be excused for having confused us (and, of course, themselves) with their rudimentary and probing attempts to express their philosophical intuitions in the only available analogues of their day. In that case, a modern, updated version of the Thomistic Synthesis might need to be conducted more along the lines of a commonsense ‘linguistic analysis’ of the languages of science and religion than confined to in-group ecclesiastical wranglings.

Besides, he would have argued, the fact that society could scarcely survive without some such synthesis needed seriously to be considered. In its functional need for those moral premiums on social survival, mankind seemed to have produced and depended upon people like Pythagoras and Plato, Jesus and Mahomet, the Buddha ... etc., to articulate that moral need. This was similar to the way that

$^{[136]}$ The Road Less Travelled. Quoted in Marvels and Mysteries of the Human Mind, publ. Readers Digest 1992, p.63.

our Western society had produced people like James Watt and the Wright brothers to satisfy its need for transport. To think of a modern society functioning without these sorts of people was just about impossible. But the particular shape and form of their contributions very much depended on the circumstances at the time as well as, of course, on the peculiarities of the characters involved. For instance, if someone other than Pythagoras—someone, say, with our modern knowledge of relativity and untainted with our mechanistic Western tradition had expressed Pythagoras’ ideas of metempsychosis, our Western social history might have been as different as if the petrol engine and the computer had been discovered before steam.

However, as things are, he reflected, it is difficult for us Westerners, with our mechanistic tradition of science, to contemplate in fairness the Pythagorean theory of metempsychosis. We cannot think of the psyche as being transported, at death, anywhere other than into the environment in the form of dissipated heat. Even throughout life, much of that electrochemical energy is continually dissipated in that way. We have no difficulty with that. Overall energy is conserved (i.e., remains in the eternal now) and the law of entropy is obeyed, in ways which we feel we fully understand. If this kind of transportation of brain energy by dissipation into the surroundings had been what the Pythagoreans had meant by ‘transmigration’, there would have been no problem. The logical identification of the psyche with energies in the light-spectrum—i.e., with energies in general—might have been acceptable as a matter of course. Indeed, he reflected, his arguments for the ‘luminiferous’ representation of the psyche would then have expressed no more than a truism of Western philosophy—which did at least, however circuitously, confirm the validity of his ‘remaining in the now’ argument, regardless of how trivial the result.

There was, though, he told himself, more to it than that. What we Westerners balk at is any suggestion, as in the Pythagorean way of thinking, of the psyche as transported as a whole; that is, coherently, in anything like the form of a consciousness or individual identity. But the psyche is, after all, a form, and forms as such, as he had already ascertained, are not necessarily subject to entropy, or dissipation. His answer to the second question of Porphyry had been that forms may persist independently of their particulars. Can they, though, he asked himself, persist without any particulars at all, as ‘pure idea’ (i.e., as bits of pure disembodied logos)? However, in view of the argument that there is no describable ‘other realm’ of being, apart from the eternal now, in order for a form to survive transmigration it would have to appear in the new place with some different but similar set of parts or particulars. This was, of course, the way the Pythagoreans had conceived it (i.e., in transmigration from the molecules of a man, say, to those of a dog or a horse). But of course, in the angular momentum discretum, as he had described it, that transmigration would have to be conceived in terms of some kind of resonance between where the psyche ceased at the one place and resumed at the other place, or places. In this resonance-type of transportation, the identity of the original form would not, therefore, necessarily be retained intact. It might be transferred to as many different places as there were resonant subdivisions (harmonics) of the original entity.

It seemed, then, that in that distributed fashion the psyche so far defined may be conserved, not as a single conscious identity but as different parts, perhaps, of some higher, overall consciousness (Gaea, Logos or whatever one might choose to call it). But how, he wondered, would that relate to his earlier deliberations about moral accountability—of posthumous rewards and punishments for virtues and transgressions? For that to be relevant, the subject of the moral evaluation had to be aware of those consequences, which it could be only as a single conscious entity, not as a collection of dissociated bits. But then, he asked himself, in the logos as previously described, are those bits ever truly dissociated? If not, then our ‘punishment’ for moral transgressions in life might be for us to have excluded ourselves from becoming part of that higher-order consciousness—by becoming morally ‘out of synch.’ with it, so to speak. This, he recalled, was precisely the point of social philosophies, such as those of the Hindus, in which Nirvana is conceived as freedom from mortality and absorption into Brahma, the soul of the universe; and Buddhism where the aim of all religious effort is the final assimilation of the individual soul into the Supreme Spirit. Indeed, belief in a Supreme Being with which it should be the soul’s aim to become integrated by loss of self, or ego, seemed to be the common factor in all the main-line religions such as Buddhism, Islam, Judaism, etc., and he could think of no society which did not have its basic religion. Western society was plainly no exception, since it was based on Christianity—even though that might now be considered little more than vestigial, its authority having been usurped by that of Science. However, for any culture based on nothing more than (that kind of) Science, the days of that society would surely be numbered because being amoral (i.e., explicitly without mores$^{[137]}$) that science as such could never, in itself, sustain a society. All it could ever do would be to serve the whimsical interests of commerce and industry which, without any moral controls, could be dedicated to nothing more than self-interest, if not pure greed.

Could it be, then, that the common factor among the social religions was that behind all their differences in language and culture they shared the same logical conviction, as opposed to that of reductionist science, that the whole must always be greater than the part, so that man, as something obviously a part of a greater whole, can be neither self-made nor a product of blind chance? But that, surely, was no more than plain commonsense because from any point of view other than the one which is peculiar to mechanistic physics, to regard human consciousness as the pinnacle of a blind mechanical evolution had to be the ultimate in silliness. In this, it seemed, commonsense and religion were automatically allied against mechanistic science. Was it, then, that in the nature of man, but for his base promptings towards individual self preservation and personal aggrandisement, there is an instinctual and overriding moral imperative, common to all the various religions, towards seeking, in common with one’s fellows, a final integration of the self with the logos (Brahma, God, Tao, or whatever)? If so, then all moral failure would revolve around the sort of ‘selfishness’, or egotism, which served to elevate the self, or soul at the expense of its association with the Whole—causing that soul to ‘self-destruct’, as one might put it, or relegate itself to lower levels. Moral admonitions against this sort of disintegration seemed to constitute the ‘bottom line’ of all the main religious teachings.

At university, he had witnessed the contortions with which ethical philosophers had sought to rescue moral words like ‘good’, ‘ought’, ‘duty’, etc., from pure subjectivity by listing the ways in which these words were used in all the various social contexts (‘language-games’ in the jargon of the linguistic philosophers). Nothing had ever seemed to him quite so barren as discussions of that kind. This was because without any objective reference to the cosmos as science knows it, they could have no significance other than with regard to our own preferences. And what could these preferences possibly be, in the absence of any superhuman dimension, but for thoughts and actions which provide us with pleasure over those which cause us pain? But that, he felt, could never explain the call for the self-negation and moral sacrifice in the face of pain and suffering that was such a typical feature of all the various natural religions.

It seemed to him, then, that the only way of making sense of this natural—perhaps even instinctual—tendency towards evaluating human behaviour in terms of morality and immorality was to link it with these concepts of cosmic integrity and disintegrity (Cosmos or Supreme Being, however identified). As he could see it, nothing else but that cosmical connection could provide the context for moral words such as ‘love’, ‘hate’, ‘right’, ‘wrong’, ‘good’, ‘evil’, ‘ought’, ‘duty’, ‘justice’, ... etc., etc., in which they cease to be just names for purely subjective ‘emotions’ and become words truly reflecting what all religions regard as moral verities..

However, it occurred to him that an ability to estimate what actions might serve that purpose of moral integration, as opposed to those that serve only to thwart it, could scarcely be regarded as something for us, with our finite intellects, to judge for ourselves on each and every occasion. Perhaps,

$^{[137]}$ Mores: a term used in sociology to describe the customs and conventions which embody the fundamental values of a group or society.

then, he thought, this is why, as a species, we have evolved a moral language in the way that, by trial and error, other animal species have adopted modes of behaviour which have proved advantageous to them in the matter of survival. The evolution of general rules, directing human behaviour towards those cosmical ends would therefore relieve us, to a large extent, of the otherwise impossible task of having to judge, as individuals, the full moral consequences of our every action. As compared with the stereotypical responses of other animal species, our moralising judgements which make us rationally adaptable to changing circumstances might be seen as the peculiar advantage which our own species has over others in life's negentropic climb towards this assumed goal of cosmical integration. However, the fact that this 'advantage' still entails having to make personal choices of a moral sort, which other animals do not have to make could scarcely be regarded as an unqualified advantage. That it can be regarded as a burden—a curse rather than a blessing—is expressed in classical literature. For instance, there was, as he recalled, the story of Prometheus, who was punished for stealing from the gods the holy fire (the logos). And in the biblical legend of Eden, Adam and Eve had brought punishment on mankind for the 'original sin' of illicitly learning, from the Tree of Knowledge, the difference between Good and Evil.

This, analysis, though, raised many further questions. For instance, there was still the question of how to reconcile the sort of morality required for the survival of man as a living species with the sort required for the posthumous salvation of his own soul. The only answer he could think of was that supplied by studies of the social insects. In such insect societies, the selfless sacrifice of the individual for the sake of the community is as moral for the individual as for the community, so that there is no conflict between the two on that primitive social level. Was it, then, perhaps, that in much the same way, the moral ends of man as a species and as an individual were coordinate? Certainly, that seemed to be the message of the New Testament, with its social exhortation to 'love thy neighbour as thyself'. Did this, then, imply that the end-aim of social evolution and of what in religious terms was called the attainment of 'personal salvation' were, in essence, one and the same? In that case, there could be no distinction between the moral goals of man as a society and man as an individual, as indeed Socrates had taught.

Nor, for the same reason could there be any true distinction, of the sort projected in some religious doctrines, between 'this world' and some metaphysical 'world beyond'. As his reasoning had already convinced him, if the soul truly exists as a real entity, then the 'world' in which it exists has to be the same as that in which physical things exist. That is, any existence the psyche might be accredited as an entity, both in and beyond the life of the body, had to be in 'this' one-and-only world of the eternal now, in which physical and spiritual existed together and in which evolution (negentropy) and dissolution (entropy) operate as conflicting principles. 'Natural selection', as Darwin defined it, was thus the negentropic selection of those agents and actions we call organic which are best fitted to a continually changing environment. This reminded him of many encounters with door-to-door peddlers of 'fundamentalist' religions in which he had argued that 'evolution', as these anti-evolutionists had misrepresented it, was not mechanical evolution, as they invariably assumed and which, in any case, barring the assumption of mechanical miracles, was a contradiction in terms. It was logical evolution ('logical' as in 'of the logos') and therefore by no means hostile to religion as these people claimed.

Of course, whether these principles of negentropic evolution and entropic dissolution (logos and physis, cosmos and chaos, or whatever) were ultimately balanced, or in so-far-unresolved conflict—like the 'God' and 'Satan' of classical Christianity—was a moot point for theologians to argue, being an issue on which no truly empirical science could sensibly pronounce. Not quite so intractable, however, was the question of moral intention, with which the 'road to hell' is said to be paved. From his reasoning so far, he could see no way in which good moral intention, in itself, could secure for a soul a place in the eternal logos. For that, moral intentions and actions would need to be effective. But, given the natural exigencies of circumstance, that effectiveness could never be guaranteed, not even on the basis of complete obedience to any known moral code. For those, then, who personalised the Logos as a God ‘in whose image’ they supposed man is made, it might be expected that having blessed/handicapped humanity in that way, he would have offered his creation some sort of helping hand. This would make sense of the Christian belief in the Saviour (Messiah) and the Buddhist, Hindu and Islamic doctrines of divine inspiration on the part of their respective ‘prophets’. It might also explain the Christian emphasis on individual and communal supplication for divine assistance in the form of prayer, for wisdom, strength and forbearance in the face of so much temptation, pain and suffering.

This brought to mind once again the problem of suffering. What purpose, he had wondered, could so much suffering have in the scheme of things. Some insight into that problem, he now thought, might be gained by regarding pain and pleasure as the sorts of natural guiding principles supplied for keeping life-forms on their upward (i.e., negentropic) evolutionary track. Pain and suffering might thus be regarded, not so much as some kind of divine punishment, arbitrarily visited on man for some imagined ‘original sin’ committed in a garden, but as the automatic accompaniment of the divine gift given to man of a freedom to choose, hence to choose wrong or even disastrous courses of action which create pain and discomfort. Moreover, much of the sort of suffering which is peculiarly human might be seen as stemming from man’s ability, which other animals seem not to possess (at least not to anything like the same extent), of evaluating their pleasures, pains and sufferings in subjective terms such as ‘justice’ or ‘injustice’. For these sufferings and so on, other animals seem more instinctively prepared, so that they endure them as a matter of course. (This was not to imply that animals other than human are ‘soulless mechanisms’ and therefore incapable of feeling pain, as the Cartesian physicians had supposed. It simply meant that such pains as they may suffer are the ones designed to avoid certain actions which might be inimical to their survival and with which they do not become overmuch preoccupied in the way that we do.) It might sensibly be maintained, then, it seemed, that in the natural statistical up-drifting in logical order that we know empirically as ‘evolution’, we have the unfolding of the intentionality or purposiveness of the logos —not in some ‘other world’ of metaphysical projection, like the ‘heaven’ of doctrinal religion but in ‘this world’, the one-and-only world of the eternal now.

However, some of his commentators found this offensive. To them, making morality something adjustable to the circumstances of ‘this world’ made it a matter of casuistry, indistinguishable from pure political expediency. ‘Do you mean to say,’ one of these people asked him, ‘that morality evolves?’ To this he had replied that in some ways it has to, but that the verities on which morality is based are, by definition, constants. For instance, truth is truth, no matter what, yet it may not always be right, or moral, to tell the truth. As Socrates had argued, if a friend who is pursued by brigands hides himself in your cupboard, it would scarcely be right for you to report that fact to his pursuers. In such instances, it would surely be one’s moral duty to lie or, at any rate, hide the truth. Nor is that moral necessity for deception confined to man, as witnessed by the fact that animals instinctively disguise themselves and hide their young from predators. But when situations change —as, for instance, when cats and dogs become household pets (as the bible puts it, when ‘the lion lies down with the lamb’), or when enemies become friends and the ‘brigands’ become benefactors, the mores may also change. In that sense, morality may rationally evolve while the verities on which it is based remain constant. Indeed an uncommon ability to interpret those eternal verities into temporal morality might well be regarded as the mark of the truly virtuous person or even, in exceptional cases, ‘prophets’. As Socrates saw it, in an ideally moral society, the virtue (in Greek, areté) of the individual and that of his society would be in perfect accord, so that there would no longer be need of deceit of any kind (Whether that ideal society might include animals in general, in soul or in body, was wide open for discussion.)

It seemed, then, that by dumping all precepts of any religious kind (both pro and contra), one could arrive at something like a logically coherent, purely descriptive and truly scientific (i.e., non-evaluative) account of the social phenomena of religion and morality, as pursued by mankind in so many different forms throughout human history. This seemed to indicate very clearly that only with reference to some kind of instinctual or intuitive awareness of posthumous accountability can religious and moral language make any (common) sense. Most people feel this moral awareness naturally in what is called ‘conscience’, which might be regarded as the human equivalent of what, in animals, is called ‘instinct’. In man, however, this ‘conscience’ can be overridden by his intellect—which, while it gives man his advantage over other animals, also defines his predicament in being thereby deprived the sort of natural instinct on which other animals can depend. This, undoubtedly, is why man, uniquely among animals, is thrown on the necessity of creating systems of religion and superstition.

The subject matter of this particular part of his analysis was, of course, very much with issues that most people would nowadays regard as ‘purely speculative’ Nevertheless, once again, it all seemed in so many ways to fit. However, on this issue of religion and transcendental states of being it was a case, once again, so far as he was concerned, of the need for open-mindedness or rational agnosticism in refusing to be prejudicial in our modern assessments of these traditional theological speculations about immortality, morality and so on. For himself at least, these were no longer justly to be ridiculed as ‘unscientific’, especially in view of the new and liberating, information-theoretical and anti-mechanist advances in science and technology. Did these advances, he wondered, portend a new intellectual democracy or a new anarchy? Who was to say? Had he rediscovered the essential connection of morality in his relationist rewrite of Einsteinian relativity? He would have to leave that for others to decide. In any event, it was bizarre, he felt, that extant Education had sought to suppress his own personal explorations along these lines as socially unsettling in the same way that originally, and for this same reason, free-minded scientific thought had itself been suppressed by the Church. What was it, he wondered, that had gripped our present Science, that made it now so suspicious of those very same intellectual freedoms it had demanded for its own early survival? But that, he reminded himself, was a rhetorical question. His personal experiences had already provided the answer. It was that in these ‘heretical’ developments, the custodians of the Educational norms saw the same sort of threat to their capped-and-gowned authority that the upholders of dogmatic Christianity had perceived in the beginnings of empirical science.

Regardless of that, however, from his own essentially secular, religiously uncommitted standpoint, he began to feel, uncomfortably, that all this talk of ‘souls’, ‘immortality’ and so on, was leading him into endless vistas of ratiocination. He would gladly have accepted any excuse to ‘cop out’ from it all, as gracefully as possible, through any logical loophole that might have presented itself. However, try as he might, his philosophical rendering of relativity had not allowed him to separate physics from psychology in the traditionally approved way. But now, as a belated concession to that academic tradition, he reminded himself that his business, in this particular enterprise was not with religion but with science. The only reason that these ‘religious’ issues had arisen, he recalled, was that the relativistic ‘time-staggering’ problem with which he had originally approached Einstein had led to the logical realisation of how inadequate had been our separatist philosophical and scientific traditions in dealing with questions of time and physical existence. This was why, in all honesty, he would have to declare, in answer to the woman who had attended his talk, that for those reasons of which he was now aware, he could not be as certain as she was that when we die, ‘that’s the end, finis!’ The sheer ineptitude of some of the theories of immortality with which our traditions had provided us, he would say, made it unsafe for anyone to respond so summarily to that ‘immortality’ question. The question as usually posed had, he was convinced, become far too simplistic and distorted. Besides, he could not happily write-off those hosts of people who, on the basis of their beliefs in the transcendental nature of the human soul, had lived lives which, deep down, and no matter how we might rationalise it, we just had to admire and perhaps even take as exemplary.

Moreover, the fact that these moral exemplars had based their lives on pure faith rather than on logical and mathematical certainty made them seem, in some ways, all the more admirable. As he had sometimes felt, in contemplating for himself the sheer irrationality of human existence, it was as though life were somehow made systematically irrational as a test of faith. And if that were so, then one shouldn't be able, by any logical, mathematical or other means, to spy out the answers, because of course, being able to obtain that kind of certainty would short-circuit the whole point and purpose of true faith, hence of any form of theistic religion.

In sum, then, so far as he was concerned, that was where natural science and natural religion had to rest their corporate case, in the full understanding that in neither sector can anything about 'Nature', 'God' or the 'Universe' be known with absolute certainty. That, he felt, should serve at least to offset both religious and scientific dogmatism, hence to eliminate the entrenched differences among the various sects of religion and science. In that case, surely, what they should be doing, instead of jealously guarding their separatist dogmas, was dispassionately to pool their rational resources in a common search for whatever religious and moral verities may be uncovered in those areas where the arbitrary divisions once imposed by mechanistic science no longer apply. In such combined efforts, he would have urged, the emphasis should fall far more on plain philosophical argument than on the usual doctrinal and political 'horse trading'.

For himself, however, he had to confess no ambitions whatever in serving that political aim of religious 'unification'. The nearest he had ever come to having any personal experience that could even remotely be called 'religious' was whilst sitting alone in the little church by the sea, as recounted earlier. Had that been a truly religious episode? He couldn't say for certain whether it had or had not. It was what it was, regardless of how he sought to explain it. And certainly, the episode had been followed by some remarkably unforeseen developments which were no less inexplicable! Once again he was reminded of his Grandmother's old saying: 'Look not a gift horse in the mouth'.

Could he, then, really and truly claim that he had no personal evidence of religious experience, or was he fooling himself that this was so? It all depended, he supposed, on the way you looked at it. Some might say that the experience of being alive, of being able to see and hear, to move one's muscles and direct with them whole chunks of nature, was as convincing a religious experience as might ever be expected. That, basically, was how he felt; and if that was called 'religious' then to that extent he would have to admit to being religious. Moreover, it was obvious that there were so many people who naturally persisted, against all the odds, in dedicatedly seeking and defending goals that they knew were well beyond any hope of personal attainment in their own lives. This natural phenomenon of personal self-sacrifice, among humans and animals alike, seemed a complete negation of any idea that 'this life is all'. In his own philosophical pursuit of truth he had gained undeniable first-hand knowledge of how one's efforts may be sustained by some such natural prompting, of which one knew no more than a weed would know as to why it pushed its way up through a crack in concrete. Could obedience to this drive be counted as 'religious'? Perhaps that was the most paramount, basically 'religious' experience of all, which we all have in one way or another but of which we are no more normally aware than fish are of water. In any event, so far as he himself was concerned, it was that unrelenting personal drive, call it wonder, curiosity or whatever, towards searching out order in the face of chaos, reflecting other similar tendencies he had discerned in other people and in nature generally, that convinced him more than anything that life is future-directed and therefore no mere mechanism. Perhaps, then, he thought, the truest form of religious experience, for those capable of recognising it, was life itself; and perhaps the truest evidence of soul was that recognised by those who were fortunate enough to possess one. Other than acting in best obedience to that personal imperative of life, consciousness and reason, he had no inclination to analyse it further, far less codify it and express it in any kind of stylised language or 'jargon'—which was why organised religion had no appeal for him. And as for worship? How could anyone be more worshipful of any power, real or imagined, than to spend one's life in obedience to it, as he had done, albeit without any form of ritual, religious or political, verbal or behavioural!

Well, alright, he told himself, to have reached that personal conviction was not as objective and socially significant a conclusion as some might wish from all those years of dedicated philosophical effort. Some friends who had joined him in his venture near the start, carried along by his then youthful enthusiasm (which some called ‘charisma’), were therefore disappointed that it had not secured for them any logical assurance of personal salvation; and as it became plain to them that such was not and never had been the agenda, they gradually dropped out. Any progress the venture might have made in replicating Einstein’s theory in commonsense terms and in solving some real problems of science—to say nothing of restoring agnosticism in the face of so much burgeoning mysticism, both deistic and atheistic—left these people cold.

However, he was satisfied that he had, logically at least, done his best to rescue from the clutches of classical physics some small seeds of natural philosophy which might just conceivably take root, even if that might be way over his own life’s horizon. Moreover, the fact that he had long since dismissed any possibility of getting his thesis as such academically accredited, left him profoundly relaxed. So he decided that unless he was mad—and he had checked that out (see above)—he felt he should speak as he had found, that beneath its cloak of obscurantism the ‘Emperor’ of modern Theoretical Physics was, indeed, most unprepossessingly nude. His more particular discoveries, ‘heretical’ though the Establishment had regarded them had been, for him, very much their own reward, and he would not have swapped that personal satisfaction for umpteen Chairs in Philosophy—or, as his father would have said, ‘a gold watch as big as a frying pan’. Philosophy meant, literally, ‘love of wisdom’, so to be ‘qualified’ in that love had, for him, overtones of intellectual prostitution. As he had experienced it, like love itself, philosophy is spontaneous. You either felt it or you didn’t; and if you did, then you would defend its integrity to the death. And how much more dedicated than that could be any prompting that was called ‘religious’?

Imbued with that philosophical curiosity and logical integrity by his free-minded, non-academic father, a telephone-lineman like himself (a man whose insights concerning this modern space-age had been as outlandish to his contemporaries as they were now commonplace to ours) our adventurer had spontaneously and unwittingly fallen into the tracks of Socrates, naturally preferring genuine understanding to monetary reward and social acclaim. Also in that same spontaneous, ‘Socratic’ way, rather than genuflect to Scholarship, he had challenged it, following instinctively the same method of critical commonsense dialogue or dialectic which, as he had since learned, others had followed before him. And the conformist reaction which that persistence had produced was also typical. ‘So you think you are the only one in step!’ was the way one conventionalist spokesman had put it to him. In reply he had pointed out that as people like Copernicus and Galileo had shown, it may well turn out, on occasions, that just one person may be proved to be ‘in step’ with truth and reality where millions may be marching wrong. Reflecting on this, it occurred to him that despite having ‘bucked the system’ in this way and foregone so many of society’s rewards for conformity, he had retained his wife’s loyalty and empathy throughout. Moreover, the fact that in the face of it all, their family had managed to survive reasonably well—mostly due to her managerial skills—might be counted as yet another ‘religious’ experience.

Of course, whether he would eventually prove to be right in anything like the manner of history’s most illustrious ‘heretics’ he could not presume to judge. But certainly, the possibility was not to be precluded by any purely conformist opinion or authoritarian edict about being in or out of step with the Establishment. Whichever way you looked at it, he felt, it was only right and proper that authority should be challenged, that for any form of authority to remain properly accountable there should always be some conscientious opposition to it of the sort he was offering. He was sure that Einstein, who had been the first to set him on his philosophical odyssey would have wholeheartedly approved that sentiment. But being unable to discern any other such challenge to orthodox Modern Physics—at least, none which had sought to shift it without standing full square upon it—this telephone-lineman, from his natural stand-apart position had sought to restore, so far as he could, some measure of the proper balance. In doing this he had simply followed those original 'lines from Einstein' to wherever they led in the various directions of natural philosophy. From his very first attempt in that undertaking he had been appalled at the extent to which the ruling system, with its academic 'chalk lines' had sought to circumscribe and confine natural thought. And the more he continued, the more incredible it seemed to him was the extent to which that system had succeeded, among so many ostensibly free-minded people, in establishing scholarship and jargon-mongering at the expense of clear-minded criticality.

He was thankful, however, that in his own experience there had been a few fortuitous exceptions. Launched into philosophy at the instigation of Einstein, and with the private help of one or two no less liberally minded academics, he had survived the stultifying heat of academia and continued to breathe the extramural fresh air of pristine natural philosophy. In that untainted atmosphere he felt he had sniffed-out the present situation for what it really and truly is, a scholastic state of conceptual paralysis. He was therefore satisfied that regardless of whatever he himself had made of it philosophically, he had, at the very least, repaid his debt to Einstein by revealing that the division which he, Einstein, had inadvertently created between relativistic and quantum accounts of nature was illusory. What more was there to be said than that, except that it had taken a self-confessed ignoramus like himself to enjoy the freedom to spot that fundamental philosophical mistake and have the gall to state it so plainly.

SUMMARY

When a young telephone engineer learned, in a lecture at his local Astronomical Society, that according to Einstein, time is not the same for everyone, he found it difficult to believe. As he reasoned, since our consciousnesses span only a very short time, then if our living times become staggered in the way Einstein claimed, in time those consciousnesses would cease to synchronise, which seemed nonsensical.

Einstein's claim also seemed paradoxical in another way. Since the view of an observer A travelling relative to another observer, B, is symmetrical with the view of B travelling relative to A, then the relativistic time dilation due to the relative motion should be the same for both, so that the overall effect would be nil. Naively, the telephone engineer wrote to Einstein about this 'paradox'. He received a reply to the effect that in order to come together again the symmetry between A and B would be broken by the one or the other reversing his motion, so that in the end there would be not just the two time frames but three: the one frame of the stay-put observer and the two separate, out and back frames of the traveller, which were by no means 'symmetrical'.

After much discussion with several distinguished experts in relativity theory, the telephone-lineman finally understood what Einstein was saying.
